xii
Terms of reference
Reappointment
That
(a) a. the Select Committee on PFAS (per and polyfluoroalkyl substances),
appointed by resolution of the Senate on 22 August 2024, be reappointed
with the same powers and provisions for membership, except as otherwise
provided by this resolution;
(b)
(c) b. the committee and any subcommittee have power to consider and use
the evidence and records of the select committee appointed on 22 August
2024; and
(d)
(e) c. the committee present its final report on or before 19 November 2025.
Appointment
(1) That a select committee, to be known as the Select Committee on PFAS (per and
polyfluoroalkyl substances), be established to inquire into the extent, regulation and
management of PFAS, with particular reference to:
(f) the extent of data collection on PFAS contamination of water, soil and
other natural resources;
(g) sources of exposure to PFAS, including through environmental
contamination, food systems and consumer goods;
(h) the health, environmental, social, cultural and economic impacts of PFAS;
(i) challenges around conducting and coordinating health and exposure
research into PFAS, including the adequacy of funding arrangements and
the influence of the chemicals industry over the evolving body of scientific
evidence on the health effects of PFAS, including in respect to First Nations
communities;
(j) the effectiveness of current and proposed federal and state and territory
regulatory frameworks, including the adequacy of health based guidance
values, public sector resourcing and coordination amongst relevant
agencies in preventing, controlling and managing the risks of PFAS to
human health and the environment;
(k) the role, liability and responsibility of government agencies and industry
in the production, distribution, contamination and remediation of PFAS,
xiii
including obligations under the Stockholm Convention on Persistent
Organic Pollutants and other relevant principles and international
conventions;
(l) international best practices for the environmentally sound management
and safe disposal of PFAS;
(m)the adequacy and effectiveness of government engagement with and
support for communities disproportionately affected by PFAS
contamination, including fair and appropriate compensation schemes;
(n) the effectiveness of remediation works on specific sites and international
best practices for remediation and management of contaminated sites;
(o) international best practices for environmental and health risk assessments,
reduction and management of PFAS contamination and exposure;
(p) areas for reform, including legislative, regulatory, public health and other
policy measures to prevent, control and manage the risks of PFAS to
human health and the environment, including the phasing out of these
harmful substances; and
(q) any other related matters.

List of recommendations
Recommendation 1
11.37 The committee recommends that Food Standards Australia and New Zealand
(FSANZ) reviews the Tolerable Daily Intakes for PFAS against the latest
international research and regulatory settings and updated Australian
Drinking Water Guidelines, and presents a report to the Australian
Parliament—no later than October 2026—which outlines the findings of the
review.
11.38 As part of this review, FSANZ should reconsider the need to establish
regulatory limits on PFAS in food, taking into account a holistic
quantification of population PFAS exposure.
Recommendation 2
11.59 The committee recommends that, as part of the current review of the enHealth
guidance statement—Per- and polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS), the
Australian Government considers the appropriateness of the statement that
health effects associated with PFAS exposure 'have generally been small and
are unlikely to be important to health outcomes' in the context of leading
international research and health advice.
Recommendation 3
11.78 The committee recommends that the Australian Government considers
providing subsidised PFAS blood testing, enhanced health and cancer
screening, and targeted mental health support for those people with the
highest levels of PFAS exposure, including some firefighters, people exposed
at work, and residents of heavily contaminated areas (especially those with
exposure to home-grown produce, contaminated seafood, plants and animals,
river and creek waters, and/or bore water).
Recommendation 4
11.82 The committee recommends that the Australian Government consults with
the Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences and other relevant
stakeholders on designs for a national, longitudinal chemicals biomonitoring
program (similar to existing programs in the United States, South Korea,
Japan, and several European countries) and associated longitudinal PFAS
health research projects.
11.83 A national biomonitoring program should incorporate a focus on monitoring
exposure levels of children and pregnant women and identifying any
associated health effects or trends.
xix
11.84 In designing the program, the government should consider using leftover
blood held by pathology laboratories.
Recommendation 5
11.92 The committee recommends that the review of the enHealth PFAS guidance
statement consider whether the risks to human health from exposure to PFAS
are dose dependent. Should the review determine that to be the case, the
guidance should acknowledge that the risks are likely increased for those
who have been heavily exposed and are lower for those with low levels of
exposure to PFAS.
Recommendation 6
11.107The committee recommends that the Australian Government considers
providing PFAS blood testing, cancer screening and general health
monitoring for all impacted residents of Wreck Bay who wish to take part,
with a particular focus on children and pregnant women.
11.108These services must be provided in a culturally appropriate and easily
accessible manner, in collaboration with the Wreck Bay Aboriginal
Community Council and relevant Aboriginal Community Controlled Health
Organisations.
Recommendation 7
11.109The committee recommends that the Australian Government considers
commissioning longitudinal research into the health impacts of exposure to
PFAS contamination on the community at Wreck Bay.
11.110This research should utilise existing data from available sources as well as
collecting new data through the testing and health monitoring outlined in
Recommendation 6.
Recommendation 8
11.117The committee recommends the Australian Government:
 works with the states and territories to ensure that workers are protected
from unnecessary PFAS exposure in all states and territories;
 reviews relevant Commonwealth work health and safety legislation,
regulations, and official advice with the aim of ensuring a consistent
approach to occupational PFAS exposure—and actively encourages states
and territories to do the same;
 amends the Australian Code for the Transport of Dangerous Goods by
Road & Rail to classify PFAS as a 'dangerous good'; and
 develops a publicly available online resource to provide state regulators,
employers, and employees with information about PFAS as a workplace
xx
hazard, including how to initiate and progress remediation of
contamination.
11.118Where necessary, work health and safety regulations should also be
amended to extend provisions to employees of private companies working on
PFAS remediation, such as for Defence.
Recommendation 9
11.119The committee recommends the Australian Government:
 works with relevant state and territory regulators to ensure that all
veterans and firefighters exposed to PFAS—including members of the
Australian Army Fire Service and Retired Firefighters' Group—are
considered and eligible as part of any remediation, research, testing, and
support programs; and
 includes Airservices Australia Emergency Vehicle Technicians in its
workers compensation scheme for firefighters, by amending the Safety,
Rehabilitation and Compensation Act 1988 (Cth).
Recommendation 10
11.120The committee recommends the Australian Government works with state
and territory work health and safety regulators to develop a national
regulatory framework which enables state and territory WorkSafe agencies to
issue notices and progress regulatory responses to PFAS exposure in
Australian workplaces.
Recommendation 11
12.23 The committee recommends the Australian Government:
 fast-tracks the deployment of PFAS-free firefighting foams to replace all
legacy PFAS-containing foams;
 moves to quickly replace all legacy firefighting equipment that still
utilises or stores PFAS-containing foams, and ensures the safe disposal of
existing stocks;
 bans or severely restricts the use of firefighting foams containing PFAS
by Commonwealth entities; and
 works through cross jurisdictional bodies to achieve an urgent national
ban on the use of PFAS-containing firefighting foams, including by
private sector entities, major hazard facilities and B Class foam
extinguishers in Australia.
xxi
Recommendation 12
12.26 The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides
funding for toxicological studies of fluorine-free foams so as to ensure that
legacy PFAS-containing foams are not replaced by the next harmful products.
Recommendation 13
12.30 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
states and territories to establish a fund and taskforce to support the
remediation of severely PFAS-contaminated sites, including current and
former fire stations (and surrounds), firefighting training facilities, and
nearby water catchments that have been impacted, and ensures the
availability of uncontaminated drinking water at all fire stations.
12.31 To complement this initiative, the Australian Government should establish
an expert tripartite panel comprised of government, private sector and unions,
to:
 advise on reducing PFAS exposure risk to workers;
 advise on effective remediation of PFAS-contaminated sites;
 advise on new and established techniques and technologies; and
 encourage further innovation through supporting site remediation
research and development.
Recommendation 14
12.33 The committee recommends the Department of Infrastructure, Transport,
Regional Development, Communications, Sports and the Arts assumes full
responsibility for remediation and management of PFAS contamination on
and around Norfolk Island airport.
Recommendation 15
12.41 The committee recommends that the Australian Government funds and
coordinates a strategic national PFAS environmental monitoring program to
track contamination levels and trends in the Australian environment,
including key waterways and native wildlife species, and publish emerging
data.
Recommendation 16
12.42 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with state
and territory and local governments to improve data-sharing on PFAS
contamination and remediation activity, and develop a publicly available,
interactive online PFAS map which identifies PFAS contamination locations
and remediation activity.
xxii
Recommendation 17
12.49 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with state
and territory government entities to establish a mechanism to fund and
support conservation and remediation of PFAS-contaminated environments
that have significant social, cultural, recreational, heritage and/or
environmental value.
12.50 This initiative should include the development and provision of information
to assist organisations to undertake remediation activities safely, and safety
information for school groups and other recreational users.
Recommendation 18
12.71 The committee recommends that the Australian Government commences
negotiation with the aim of formalising its partnership with the Wreck Bay
Aboriginal community for ongoing remediation and restoration of PFAS-
affected lands and waters at Wreck Bay. The agreement should be designed
to provide self-determination for Wreck Bay people in managing and
remediating PFAS contamination on their land.
Recommendation 19
12.78 The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides
immediate access to clean drinking water for the community at Wreck Bay as
a temporary measure until the pipeline connecting Wreck Bay to the
Shoalhaven City Council water supply is completed and operational.
Recommendation 20
12.89 The committee recommends that Australian Government agencies involved
in remediation efforts, including the Department of Defence, work together
with local First Nations community and language groups, and Aboriginal
Community Controlled Organisations, to review PFAS management plans
and update them to incorporate actions to safeguard the cultural, social,
nutritional, and economic wellbeing of First Nations people and communities
that are significantly impacted by PFAS contamination.
12.90 PFAS management plans for sites with significant Aboriginal and Torres
Strait Islander communities should be aligned with the priority reform areas
in the National Agreement on Closing the Gap and the United Nations
Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.
Recommendation 21
12.91 The committee recommends that the Australian Government urgently
considers options to improve and maintain ongoing access to healthy and
traditional food sources for Aboriginal communities around Katherine. This
xxiii
may include facilitating safer fishing, subsidising, and/or improving the
supply of affordable uncontaminated seafood.
Recommendation 22
12.101The committee recommends that the new PFAS National Coordinating Body
prioritises establishing community-based working groups, and/or other
collaboration mechanisms, to facilitate the involvement of affected
communities in decision-making processes relating to remediation efforts.
Recommendation 23
12.102The committee recommends that the Australian Government, through the
Department of Defence, reaches out to local health services, such as Wurli
Wurjlinjang in Katherine, to codesign culturally appropriate and effective
signage and educational material for Aboriginal communities impacted by
the PFAS contamination of the Katherine River.
Recommendation 24
12.105The committee recommends that the Australian Government investigates the
proposal made by La Trobe University to develop Indigenous-led field-
deployable PFAS detection technologies.
Recommendation 25
12.119The committee recommends that Airservices devotes additional resources to
its National PFAS Management Program to expedite the remediation phase
of the program.
Recommendation 26
12.125The committee recommends that the Department of Infrastructure,
Transport, Regional Development, Communications, Sport and the Arts
works with the Australian Airports Association, Airservices, and the
Department of Defence to identify airports that may have been exposed to
significant PFAS contamination which fall outside the scope of the three
Commonwealth PFAS investigation and remediation programs.
12.126Options for remediating these airports should then be considered by the
Australian Government, in collaboration with the relevant state, territory, and
local government authorities.
Recommendation 27
12.135The committee recommends that the Australian Government works through
the new PFAS National Coordinating Body, the Heads of Environmental
xxiv
Protection Authorities Australia and New Zealand, and other relevant bodies
to:
 conduct a national audit of PFAS contamination sites and remediation
activity;
 develop a strategic, risk-based national remediation plan;
 develop a common national framework for remediation; and
 develop national standards for reporting on remediation activity and
outcomes.
Recommendation 28
12.139The committee recommends that the Australian Government produces a
detailed cost estimate for the remediation of Australia's airports impacted by
PFAS contamination and works with stakeholders to develop a cost-sharing
model.
Recommendation 29
12.149The committee recommends that the Australian Government establishes an
interdisciplinary joint council, comprising regulators, industry experts, and
researchers, to evaluate PFAS treatment and remediation technologies.
Recommendation 30
12.150The committee recommends that the Australian Government continues to
invest in a variety of PFAS remediation, removal and destruction technologies
and techniques as part of its remediation programs. Where possible,
Australian Government PFAS remediation programs should seek to support
locally developed solutions and invest in environmentally responsible
remediation technologies.
Recommendation 31
12.165The committee recommends that the Australian Government amends the
National Water Quality Management Strategy to incorporate a coordinated
approach to pollutants and contaminants, including PFAS. As part of this
initiative, governments should create a taskforce, comprising regulatory
agencies and industry bodies, to:
 plan and coordinate the management of PFAS in water supplies around
Australia;
 develop standardised PFAS monitoring protocols and a national register
of results; and
 progress the implementation of the revised Australian Drinking Water
Guidelines.
xxv
Recommendation 32
12.171 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
state and territory governments and the Water Services Association of
Australia to develop funding mechanisms that will support water service
providers to meet upgraded PFAS testing and removal.
Recommendation 33
12.175The committee recommends that the Australian Government actively
pursues legal action against 3M and any other manufacturer of PFAS-
containing firefighting foams used in Australia, with any settlement used to
help fund remediation of contaminated sites.
Recommendation 34
12.184The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
stakeholders from the textiles, clothing, floor coverings, and furniture sectors,
and other relevant stakeholders, to:
 measure the scale and distribution of PFAS in clothing, textiles, floor
coverings, and furniture across Australia;
 identify key sources of PFAS-contaminated clothing, textiles, carpets and
furniture, including import pathways;
 develop strategies for reducing the import of PFAS-contaminated
clothing, textiles, floor coverings, and furniture; and
 develop mechanisms for more safely disposing of legacy PFAS-
contaminated textiles waste.
Recommendation 35
12.188The committee recommends that the Australian Government fast-tracks
regulatory reforms to remove PFAS from all food contact packaging imported
into and/or used in Australia and considers encouraging the establishment of
a domestic, toxic-free packaging industry.
Recommendation 36
12.204The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
state and territory governments, local councils, and the waste management
sector to develop a plan for dealing with legacy PFAS contamination in
Australian waste streams, including consideration of funding, options to
reduce disposal of PFAS contamination in landfill facilities, and cost-sharing
mechanisms.
xxvi
Recommendation 37
12.210The committee recommends that the Australian Government fast-tracks the
development and publication of new Health Investigation Levels to inform
risk assessments for the suitability of PFAS contaminated land for
agricultural purposes, and publishes advice on the threshold over which
produce containing PFAS should not be sold.
Recommendation 38
12.211The committee recommends that, in line with Recommendation 16, the
Australian Government provides publicly available information identifying
the presence of PFAS contamination on farming land, and proactively
informs landholders.
Recommendation 39
12.228The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides
additional resources to the Australian Industrial Chemical Introduction
Scheme to fast-track chemical assessment, including for PFAS and other
persistent organic pollutants (POPs) to ensure toxic chemicals are not allowed
on the market.
Recommendation 40
12.235The committee recommends that the Australian Government prioritises
work to develop legislative and policy mechanisms to enable the Schedule 7
listing of PFOA, PFOS and PFHxS (and related chemicals) to be effectively
monitored and enforced, especially in relation to imported products.
12.236Detection, disclosure and monitoring mechanisms should include internal
assessments and self-reporting by businesses along with independent
product testing where appropriate.
12.237Enforcement mechanisms should be risk-based, focussing on industries and
sectors where businesses may be unintentionally or unwittingly importing
products containing restricted PFAS.
Recommendation 41
12.238The committee recommends that, as a priority, the Australian Government
works to enforce the PFAS ban in relation to cosmetics and personal care
products, including period products.
Recommendation 42
12.239The committee recommends that the Department of Climate Change, Energy,
the Environment and Water works with the Department of Health, Disability
and Ageing and the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission to
xxvii
develop a mandatory product labelling regime aimed at retailers, wholesalers,
distributors, importers and manufacturers who import consumer products
containing PFAS chemicals.
Recommendation 43
12.244The committee recommends that, as a priority, the Australian Government:
 provides adequate resourcing for the development of legislation and
associated regulations to give effect to the ban on PFAS;
 works with states and territories to encourage and support adoption of the
Industrial Chemicals Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS)
into state and territory chemical regulations;
 urgently progresses the treaty making process to enable the ratification of
the Stockholm Convention listings of PFOS, PFOA and PFHxS; and
 works across government to develop a clear and simplified framework to
enable faster ratification of future amendments to the Stockholm
Convention and domestic implementation of toxic chemical bans.
Recommendation 44
12.245The committee recommends that the Australian Government develops a
standardised approach to domestic chemical classification and regulatory
changes for new hazard classifications made by the International Agency for
Research on Cancer.
Recommendation 45
12.254The committee recommends that the Australian Government works through
intergovernmental decision-making forums, such as National Cabinet and
the Heads of Environmental Protection Authorities Australia and New
Zealand, to encourage and support jurisdictions to incorporate the standards,
practices and regulatory limits outlined in the PFAS National Environmental
Management Plan (NEMP) into their own regulatory regimes, including new
limits for biosolids.
12.255The Australian Government should consider providing additional
resourcing and support as jurisdictions implement NEMP 3.0, including by
funding the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and
Water to provide departmental staff to oversee and support the
implementation.
Recommendation 46
12.262The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with:
research organisations including the CSIRO, Australian Academy of Science
and Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences; industry bodies;
xxviii
and independent research centres like crcCARE, to develop a strategic PFAS
research strategy and roadmap.
12.263A national PFAS research strategy should focus investments towards
developing novel remediation technologies, supporting longitudinal
research—particularly in relation to health and environmental impacts—and
streamlining ethics approval for research involving multiple jurisdictions.
Recommendation 47
12.267The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides user–
friendly, up-to-date public information on PFAS which incorporates:
 a 'single source of truth' on PFAS, updated regularly according to the most
recent evidence, with publication dates included on all pages;
 a single point of contact for members of the public to ask questions about
PFAS; and
 in due course, a map showing PFAS contamination 'hotspots' and
remediation activity (see Recommendation 16).
12.268This information could be provided on a new website or the existing website
hosted by the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and
Water (pfas.gov.au).
xxix

Chapter 1
Introduction
Appointment of the committee and conduct of the inquiry
1.1 On 22 August 2024, the Senate of the 47th Parliament of Australia appointed the
Select Committee on per and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) to inquire into
the extent, regulation and management of PFAS in Australia. The committee
was required to present a final report by 5 August 2025.1
1.2 After receiving 127 submissions, conducting four public hearings, a site visit to
Launceston Airport, and a community visit to Wreck Bay in the Jervis Bay
Territory on Australia's south-east coast, the committee tabled an interim report
on 14 March 2025, which included a summary of the committee's visit to Wreck
Bay.
1.3 The committee's interim report aimed to provide an initial overview of PFAS in
Australia and present some of the evidence the committee had received at that
time. The report included a focus on the impacts of PFAS contamination in the
Aboriginal community of Wreck Bay and made two recommendations aimed at
improving the government's remediation response at Wreck Bay. Before making
any further recommendations, or drawing further conclusions, it was the
committee's intention to seek more evidence and continue to deliberate on these
complex matters.
1.4 The committee's first four public hearings were held in Canberra on
13 November 2024; in Nowra, New South Wales (NSW) on 21 January 2025; in
Penrith, NSW on 22 January 2025; and in Launceston, Tasmania on 12 March
2025. Information about those hearings, including a list of witnesses, is provided
in Appendix 4.
1.5 The committee held three more hearings during the 47th Parliament—one in
Melbourne on 4 June 2025, and two hearings in Canberra, on 10 and 11 June
2025.
1.6 The committee also conducted additional two site visits. The first at Launceston
Airport on 11 March 2025 involved an inspection of the remediation site at the
former Fire Training Ground. The second was to the SOLVE hazardous waste
facility in Altona, Victoria on 3 June 2025.
1.7 Summaries of the committee's site visits to Launceston Airport and the SOLVE
hazardous waste facility in Altona are provided in Appendices 1 and 2
respectively.
1 Senate Journals, No. 127, 22 August 2024, pp. 3840–3841.
1
2
1.8 The committee continued to receive and publish submissions, responses to
questions on notice, and additional information right up until the eve of the
47th Parliament. At that time, the committee had received 148 submissions.
1.9 In its interim report, the committee recommended that it be reappointed to
continue its work in the 48th Parliament. On 28 July 2025, the Senate reappointed
the committee, granting access to the documents and records from the
committee of the 47th Parliament, and extending the reporting date to
19 November 2025.2
1.10 Following the appointment, the new committee set up a new website for the
48th Parliament. The committee conducted one more hearing, in Canberra on 5
September 2025, and continued to receive evidence. Evidence received in the
48th Parliament is published on the committee's website for the 48th Parliament.3
1.11 A total of 170 submissions were received during 47th and 48th Parliaments, along
with a substantial number of additional documents, including answers to
questions on notice. These are available on the committee's websites for the
47th and 48th Parliaments respectively.
1.12 The committee also received a petition from Ms Sophie Barrett, regarding the
impact of chemicals on childhood cancer. The petition had 63 signatories and
has been published as additional information to the inquiry.
1.13 All submissions, additional information, and answers to questions on notice
received across both Parliaments are listed in Appendix 3. Information about
hearings across the 47th and 48th Parliaments, including a list of witnesses, is
provided in Appendix 4.
1.14 At the time of writing this report a government response had not yet been
received to the committee's interim report.
Acknowledgments
1.15 The committee thanks all the individuals, organisations, government
departments and agencies who appeared as witnesses or made submissions to
the inquiry, including international witnesses.
1.16 The committee especially values the courage and generosity of witnesses who
have been personally impacted by PFAS, including through occupational or
environmental exposure, and appreciates their testimony.
Notes on terminology and references
1.17 Throughout this report, the term 'PFAS' is used as a shorthand for the group or
class of chemicals that can also be known as 'perfluorinated chemicals',
2 Senate Journals, No. 4, 28 July 2025, p. 150.
3 Select Committee on PFAS (per and polyfluoroalkyl substances), About this inquiry (accessed 10
November 2025).
3
'perfluorochemicals', or 'perfluoroalkyls'. PFAS are also commonly referred to
as 'forever chemicals', due to their persistence in the environment and in living
organisms. A detailed explanation of PFAS was included in Chapter 2 of the
committee's interim report.
1.18 The terms 'Indigenous', 'First Nations' and 'Aboriginal' and/or 'Torres Strait
Islander' may all be used at various points in this report.
1.19 To differentiate between the primary Commonwealth departments involved in
Australia's PFAS response (the Department of Defence, the Department of
Health, Disability and Ageing, the Department of Infrastructure, Transport,
Regional Development, Communications, Sports and the Arts, and the
Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water) the
committee uses the shorthand terms, 'Defence', 'Department of Health',
'Department of Infrastructure', and 'Department of Environment', throughout
this report.
1.20 The committee also notes the 'Department of health and Aged Care' became the
'Department of Health, Disability and Ageing' part way through this inquiry.
1.21 References to 'Committee Hansard' are to Proof Committee Hansards, which
were available at the time of drafting. There may be minor differences between
Proof and Official Hansards, including page numbers.
AICIS clarification
1.22 Following the release of the committee's interim report, the Australian
Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme (AICIS) wrote to the committee with
a request that it clarify two statements that were made in the interim report
which were not an accurate representation of AICIS obligations.4
1.23 Paragraph 2.26 of the committee's interim report stated:
All importers and manufacturers of industrial chemicals listed under this Act are
required to register their business and the categories of their chemical importations
or manufacture with AICIS before they introduce industrial chemicals into
Australia.
1.24 AICIS suggested that the following would be a more accurate representation:
All importers and manufacturers (introducers) of industrial chemicals are
required to register their business before they introduce industrial chemicals
into Australia. They must also categorise their chemical introductions before
introducing the chemical into Australia for the first time, and meet the
obligations associated with the relevant category.
1.25 Paragraph 2.27 of the committee's interim report stated:
4 Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme (AICIS), clarification of AICIS' obligations
(received 30 June 2025).
4
AICIS enforces import and export controls on PFOS and specified PFOS precursors
listed under an international agreement called the Rotterdam Convention.
Importers and manufacturers of PFAS must comply with legal obligations under
the Act and are prohibited from importing or exporting PFAS without approval
from AICIS.
1.26 AICIS suggested that the following would be a more accurate representation:
AICIS enforces import and export controls on chemicals that are listed under
an international agreement called the Rotterdam Convention, which
includes high concern PFAS chemicals such as PFOS, PFOA and related
chemicals. Importers and manufacturers of chemicals that are listed on the
Rotterdam Convention must comply with legal obligations under the Act
and are prohibited from importing or exporting such chemicals without
approval from AICIS.
1.27 The committee thanks AICIS for these clarifications.
Structure of the report
1.28 The committee's final report is divided into two parts—evidence received, and
committee views and recommendations. Part 1 presents the evidence received
throughout the course of the inquiry and has 10 chapters:
 Chapter 1 provides an introduction to the report.
 Chapter 2 presents further evidence on PFAS and human health, including:
 Australian research on health impacts;
 international research on health impacts, including recent
international findings;
 Australian Government guidance and plans for review;
 views of inquiry participants on the health science; and
 discussion on biomonitoring and health screening for exposed
cohorts.
 Chapter 3 presents evidence relating to firefighters and other groups
exposed to PFAS at work, including a discussion of exposure management
strategies.
 Chapter 4 discusses social and ecological impacts of PFAS, including
impacts on lifestyle and recreation, and impacts on Australian wildlife and
habitats.
 Chapter 5 presents evidence on the unique and disproportionate impacts of
PFAS contamination on First Nations communities, including Wreck Bay
and Katherine in the Northern Territory.
 Chapter 6 discusses PFAS remediation technologies and projects and
provides a case study on Launceston Airport. It also provides an update on
PFAS remediation programs, including:
 Defence's PFAS Investigation and Management Program;
 Airservices' National PFAS Management Program; and
 the Australian Government PFAS Airports Investigation Program.
5
 Chapter 7 provides an update on PFAS in water supplies, including
drinking water and other water systems. The chapter incudes a case study
update on water supplies in the NSW Blue Mountains.
 Chapter 8 discusses PFAS in consumer goods and products, including:
 Prevalence of PFAS in consumer goods, including imported goods;
 methods of human exposure; and
 views of inquiry participants on regulation, labelling, food packaging and
research.
 Chapter 9 presents evidence about PFAS in waste streams and agricultural
impacts of PFAS. It covers:
 PFAS in waste streams such as biosolids, food organics and garden
organics (FOGO) and landfill;
 PFAS in agriculture, including application of biosolids on agricultural
land, PFAS in stock water and PFAS in pesticides, and their impact on the
food chain; and
 approaches to managing PFAS in waste streams and agriculture,
including suggestions from these sectors.
 Chapter 10 outlines PFAS policy, regulation and coordination in Australia.
This chapter incorporates discussion of:
 chemical assessment and regulation;
 national PFAS policy and coordination, including the PFAS National
Environmental Management Plan;
 views of state, territory and local governments, and other inquiry
participants;
 lessons from international jurisdictions; and
 potential options for phasing out PFAS in Australia.
1.29 Part 2 comprises Chapters 11 and 12 and presents the committee's views and
recommendations.

Part 1
Evidence received

Chapter 2
PFAS and human health
Introduction
2.1 Over the last 25 years, a growing body of research has raised concerns about the
potential impacts of some per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) on
human health.1 However, the significance of these impacts remains a contested
subject.
2.2 In its interim report, the committee outlined current Australian Government
health advice regarding PFAS and discussed PFAS exposure in Wreck Bay,
Richmond, and the Blue Mountains in New South Wales. The interim report also
summarised evidence regarding the mental health impacts of living in an area
that is known to be contaminated with high levels of PFAS.
2.3 In this final report, the committee presents the remaining evidence on PFAS and
human health that was received throughout the course of the inquiry. This
chapter begins by outlining major Australian PFAS research, considers the
impact of historical delays in commencing independent research on the health
effects of PFAS, and then looks at recent international research and findings.
This is followed by the Australian Government health guidance and current
reviews of that guidance, and witness views on the adequacy of this advice.
2.4 Next, the chapter presents the testimony of submitters and witnesses on the
science around PFAS and human health, including bioaccumulation in animals
and humans, and the strength and quality of the epidemiological evidence. The
chapter then presents testimony about the health effects of PFAS and potential
safe levels of PFAS exposure, and views on approaches to dealing with
uncertainty. The chapter concludes with a consideration of biomonitoring.
Australian research on health impacts
2.5 This section sets out several Australian studies and reviews of evidence, as well
as summarising the conclusions of a number of Commonwealth agencies.
Australian Expert Health Panel on PFAS: 2017–18
2.6 In 2017, the Australian Government established the Expert Health Panel on
PFAS (the Panel) to advise it on the evidence for potential health impacts
associated with PFAS exposure.2
1 See, for example, Professor Denis O'Carroll, Submission 1 (47th Parliament), p. 1.
2 Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, Expert Health Panel on PFAS,
7 June 2024 (accessed 8 July 2025).
9
10
2.7 The Panel's 400-page report, published in May 2018, was based on 'a
comprehensive review of recent literature reviews regarding Australian and
international evidence on potential human health effects of PFAS exposure', as
well as a public consultation process.3
2.8 In its report summary:
The Panel found that although the scientific evidence on the relationship
between PFAS exposure and health effects is limited, current reports,
reviews and scientific research provide fairly consistent reports with several
health effects.4
2.9 These health effects were:
 increased levels of cholesterol in the blood;
 increased levels of uric acid in the blood;
 reduced kidney function;
 alterations in some indicators of immune response;
 altered levels of thyroid hormones and sex hormones;
 later age for starting menstruation (periods) in girls, and earlier menopause;
and
 lower birth weight in babies.5
2.10 However, the Panel stated that for the health effects listed above, 'the differences
reported in the scientific studies between people who have the highest exposure
to PFAS and those who have had low exposure, are generally small'.6
2.11 Further, the Panel stated that 'the level of health effect reported in people with
the highest exposure is generally still within the normal ranges for the whole
population'.7
2.12 Therefore:
The Panel concluded there is mostly limited or no evidence for any link with
human disease from these observed differences. Importantly, there is no
current evidence that supports a large impact on a person's health as a result
of high levels of PFAS exposure. However, the Panel noted that even though
the evidence for PFAS exposure and links to health effects is very weak and
3 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS – Report,
pp. 1–2 (accessed 9 July 2025).
4 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 1 (accessed 9 July 2025).
5 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 1.
6 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 1.
7 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 1.
11
inconsistent, important health effects for individuals exposed to PFAS
cannot be ruled out based on the current evidence.8
2.13 The Panel also 'concluded many of the biochemical (for example, higher
cholesterol and uric acid levels in the blood) and disease links reported in the
studies may be able to be explained by reverse causation or confounding':
Reverse causation is when there is a link between the exposure to PFAS and
a health effect, but it is not clear whether the exposure has caused the health
effect or whether the health effect causes increased exposure. Confounding
is where a third factor (for example, age, smoking, or socio-economic status),
could influence the findings of the study.9
2.14 In terms of cancer, 'the Panel concluded there is no current evidence that
suggests an increase in overall cancer risk':
The Panel did however note that the most concerning signal reported in the
scientific studies for life-threatening human disease is a possible link with
an increased risk of two uncommon cancers: testicular and kidney cancer.
However, these associations were only found in one cohort, and the Panel
believes they were possibly due to chance, as they have yet to be found in
other studies. Additionally, the Panel noted that the limited amount of
evidence which is available on cancer relates to the [perfluorooctanoic acid
(PFOA)] chemical, not [perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (PFOS)] (which is more
common in Australia).10
2.15 In concluding, the Panel stated:
After considering all the evidence, the Panel's advice to the Minister on this
public health issue is that the evidence does not support any specific health
or disease screening or other health interventions for highly exposed groups
in Australia, except for research purposes.11
ANU PFAS Health Study: 2016–21
2.16 Research on the health impacts of PFAS on the Australian population is limited.
One study that investigated the potential health effects of PFAS exposure was
the Australian National University's (ANU) PFAS Health Study. The study was
commissioned by the Department of Health and conducted by Professor Martyn
Kirk and a team of researchers from the National Centre for Epidemiology and
Population Health at ANU between 2016 and 2021. The study focussed on three
Australian communities with known PFAS contamination: Katherine in the
8 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 2.
9 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 2.
10 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 2.
11 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Expert Health Panel for PFAS:
Summary, May 2018, p. 2.
12
Northern Territory, Oakey in Queensland and Williamtown in New South
Wales.12
2.17 The study started with a systematic review of the available scientific and health
literature to identify potential health effects related to exposure to PFAS
chemicals. The study was conducted in four parts:
(1) Focus group study—examined the three PFAS management areas
communities' social and health related experiences and
perceptions.
(2) Blood serum study—examined the blood PFAS levels, biochemical
markers of health, and PFAS exposure in the communities of the
three PFAS management areas and in people living in three
similar communities that had no known environmental PFAS
contamination (referred to as 'comparison communities').
(3) Cross-sectional survey—examined the exposure history, physical,
and mental health of the communities in the three PFAS
management areas and the three comparison communities and
compared the prevalence of self-reported physical and mental
health outcomes between them.
(4) Data linkage study—comprised three studies that used historical
health records to determine if the rates of adverse health outcomes
were higher among those living in the three PFAS management
areas than among those living in the comparison communities.13
2.18 Overall, the PFAS Health Study found:
 elevated blood serum concentrations of PFAS in residents and workers of
the exposed communities.
 PFOS, perfluorohexane sulfonic acid (PFHxS) and perfluorooctanoic acid
(PFOA) were detected in more than 80 per cent of blood samples in each
community.
 Blood PFAS concentrations were higher in residents of the exposed
communities than in residents of comparison communities, except for PFOA
where concentrations were similar. PFOS and PFHxS were the main active
ingredients of the firefighting foams that contaminated the environment of
the exposed communities.
12 Australian National University, PFAS Health Study: Overall summary, December 2021, p. 2, in
Professor Martyn Kirk, Dr Kayla Smurthwaite and Ms Susan Trevenar, Submission 85 (47th
Parliament).
13 Australian National University, PFAS Health Study: Overall summary, December 2021, pp. 1–5, in
Professor Martyn Kirk, Dr Kayla Smurthwaite and Ms Susan Trevenar, Submission 85 (47th
Parliament).
13
 Clear evidence of increased psychological distress in the three exposed
communities in the PFAS Health Study.14
2.19 In their submission, Professor Kirk et al. noted that, apart from the findings on
psychological distress, the evidence for other adverse health outcomes was
generally limited, and the findings were consistent with international studies:
An extensive data linkage study provided limited support for adverse
health effects from living in an exposed community. While there were
higher rates of some adverse outcomes in individual areas, they were rarely
present in more than one community. Across different components of the
PFAS Health Study, we observed associations between blood concentrations
of PFAS and higher cholesterol levels, and between living in an exposed
community and increased deaths due to heart disease.15
ABS National Health Measures Survey 2022–25
2.20 The Australian Government provided funding to the Australian Bureau of
Statistics (ABS) to include PFAS biomarker testing as an indicator in the
National Health Measures Survey (NHMS) as part of the Intergenerational
Health and Mental Health Study. Collection of samples occurred over 2022–23,
and results were released on 27 May 2025.
2.21 Mr James Eynstone-Hinkins, Head of Health Statistics, explained that the data
'will help to create a national baseline for levels of PFAS in the Australian
population', will enable future monitoring, and will 'support further research
into PFAS and the potential impacts of these chemicals on human health'.16
2.22 The ABS tested for 11 types of PFAS in people aged 12 years and over.17 Three
types of PFAS were detected in the blood of over 85 per cent of the Australian
population, while six types were detected in less than 10 per cent of people. The
remaining two types of PFAS had detection rates between 15 per cent and 45 per
cent. In general, older people had higher PFAS levels, and PFAS levels were
generally higher for males than for females.18
14 Professor Martyn Kirk, Dr Kayla Smurthwaite and Ms Susan Trevenar, Submission 85 (47th
Parliament), p. 1.
15 Professor Martyn Kirk, Dr Kayla Smurthwaite and Ms Susan Trevenar, Submission 85 (47th
Parliament), pp. 1–2.
16 Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS), Media release: New ABS data on PFAS levels, 27 May 2025
(accessed 9 July 2025).
17 PFAS tested were: Perfluorooctane sulfonic acid (PFOS); Perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA);
Perfluorohexane sulfonic acid (PFHxS); Perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA); Perfluoroheptane sulfonic
acid (PFHpS); Perfluorodecanoic acid (PFDA); Perfluoroundecanoic acid (PFUnDA);
Perfluoroheptanoic acid (PFHpA); Perfluorobutane sulfonic acid (PFBS); Perfluorohexanoic acid
(PFHxA); and 6:2-Fluorotelomer sulfonic acid (6:2-FTS). See: ABS, Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances,
27 May 2025 (accessed 10 November 2025).
18 ABS, Media release: New ABS data on PFAS levels, 27 May 2025.

2.23 As shown in Figure 2.1, the ABS found levels of the three most common PFAS
(PFOS, PFHxS and PFOA) were 'generally highest in Europe, followed by the
US, then Canada, and then Australia'. The ABS noted that these studies 'were
undertaken at different times and have different methodologies', so any
comparisons should be 'done with caution'.19

(ng/mL) from the NHMS and other international studies—Adults

2.24 According to the ABS, mean total PFOS levels were higher for older age groups,
with persons aged 75 years and over recording the highest mean total PFOS
level of 3.62 ng/mL. Amongst people aged 18 years and over, males had a higher
mean total PFOS level than females in every age group except people aged 75
years and over. The ABS also observed that 'PFAS levels have been reported to
be declining in humans in the last two decades in North America and Europe',
and the Australian results support this trend.20
2.25 As part of its research, the ABS looks for associations between high PFAS levels
and chronic disease biomarkers. The ABS was careful to point out that an
'association' to a chronic disease biomarker does not mean a 'direct cause', and
that its analysis did not control for all factors known to influence PFAS levels.
Nevertheless, the ABS provided the following information regarding PFOS and
PFOA levels, and biomarkers of chronic disease:

In 2022–24, the mean total PFOS level for people aged 18 years and over was

 an abnormally high total cholesterol level, compared to those with a

 diabetes compared to those without diabetes (2.40 ng/mL of total PFOS

In 2022–24, the mean total PFOA level was higher for people aged 18 years
and over with an abnormally high total cholesterol level than those with a
normal total cholesterol level (0.94 ng/mL of PFOA compared to 0.81
ng/mL). This was also true for the following age groups:
 people aged 45–54 years (0.89 ng/mL of PFOA compared to 0.77 ng/mL)
 people aged 55–64 years (1.16 ng/mL of PFOA compared to 0.97 ng/mL).23
2.26 While some associations may be observed, the ABS analysis could not control
for all factors known to influence PFAS levels, nor for all factors known to
influence chronic disease. As such, the ABS concluded 'it is not possible to
determine from this analysis if higher PFAS levels are related to an increased
risk of chronic disease'.24
FSANZ guidance values and total diet study
2.27 Food Standards Australia New Zealand (FSANZ) is a statutory authority
responsible for developing food standards for Australia and New Zealand. It is

a standards-setting body, rather than a regulator and has no jurisdiction over
food harvested or grown for personal consumption.25
2.28 FSANZ undertakes risk analysis based on hazard (the potential of a substance
to cause harm or toxicity) and exposure (the levels of the substance in food and
how much food containing the substance is consumed in the diet). FSANZ is
responsible for developing PFAS guidelines for food intake.26
2.29 In June 2016, the Department of Health commissioned FSANZ to develop
Health Based Guidance Values for PFOS, PFOA and PFHxS. The Australian
Health Based Guidance Values, 'expressed as tolerable daily intakes [TDIs],
indicate the amount of a chemical in food or drinking water that a person can
consume regularly over a lifetime without any significant risk to health'.27
2.30 The TDIs for PFAS were derived from the results of toxicity studies in laboratory
animals.28
2.31 FSANZ established the following tolerable daily intakes for PFAS chemicals
measured as nanograms (one billionth of a gram) per kilogram of body weight
per day, or ng/kg bw/day:
 PFOS: 20 ng/kg bw/day;
 PFOA: 160 ng/kg bw/day;
 PFHxS: FZANZ noted that while there was insufficient toxicological
information to establish a TDI for PFHxS, the tolerable daily intake
established for PFOS would be sufficiently protective for PFHxS and
therefore can be applied to the sum of PFOS and PFHxS.29
2.32 For comparison, in 2020, the European Food Safety Authority's (EFSA)
published their Tolerable Weekly Intake (TWI) at 4.4 nanograms per kilogram
of body weight per day for the combined sum of 4 PFAS compounds (PFOS,
PFOA, PFHxS and PFNA). This converts to 0.63 nanograms per kilogram of

body weight per day for the combined consumption of all four compounds
(PFOA, PFNA, PFHxS, and PFOS) in food.30

by the EFSA are much lower than the amounts established by FSANZ. While
FSANZ did not comment directly on these differences, it did note that it 'does
not routinely consider non-dietary exposure pathways because food standards
are established specifically for food', suggesting that Australia's tolerable daily
intakes for PFAS do not take into account potential accumulation of PFAS
exposure from a variety of sources.31

2.34 The trigger points established by FSANZ could be used to assist 'relevant
authorities in undertaking site assessments and providing advice to
communities impacted by localised PFAS contamination'.32
2.35 Noting that Australia's tolerable daily intakes for PFAS are set significantly
higher than those of the European Union, FSANZ analysed levels of PFAS in the
Australian food supply. FSANZ submitted that its 27th Australian Total Diet
Study released in December 2021 'showed PFAS levels in the Australian food

supply are very low and there are no public health and safety concerns for the

2.36 FSANZ also submitted that 'based on the scientific evidence gathered to date,
there is no need to establish regulatory limits for PFAS in any foods'.34 However,
some submitters noted that this research was not focused on food products
emerging from PFAS contamination hotspots, where food production may still
be occurring, and where locals may be exposed to a higher volume of
contaminated produce.35
2.37 To support the 27th Australian Total Diet Study, FSANZ also reviewed studies
of the potential for PFAS to affect the human immune system (the PFAS and
immunomodulation review). FSANZ 'evaluated the relationship between PFAS
and immune response to vaccinations, susceptibility to infections, and
hypersensitivity responses, including allergy'. FSANZ published its findings in
December 2021. It concluded that, despite some statistical associations, there
was 'a lack of consistent evidence that PFAS at levels of environmental exposure
were harmful to the human immune system'.36 A number of inquiry
participants, including toxicologists, disputed this assertion. Their evidence is
discussed further on in this chapter.
2.38 Some submitters argued that FSANZ's tolerable daily intakes were outdated
and did not align with stricter international standards set out by bodies such as
the European Union or the United States Environmental Protection Agency (US
EPA). For example, the Cooperative Research Centre for Contamination
Assessment and Remediation of the Environment (crcCARE) submitted that the
tolerable daily intakes established by FSANZ are, 'almost three hundred (300)
times higher' than the limits established in 2020 by the EFSA.37 (See also the
comparison in Table 2.1 above).
2.39 Environmental campaigner, Ms Tracey Anton claimed that 'the biggest threat to
victims of contamination in food growing areas is FSANZ's declaration that
PFAS is safe at a ridiculously higher level than global levels'.38

2.40 FSANZ responded to Ms Anton's criticism of its Health Based Guidance Values
(HBGVs) by noting:
HGBVs are not standards but guidance values used specifically to
characterise food-related health risks. FSANZ continues to undertake
regular reviews of new scientific evidence to ensure the HBGVs for PFOA,
PFOS and PFHxS remain health protective for consumers.39
2.41 In June 2025, the committee asked FSANZ if it intended to conduct an updated
review of acceptable levels of human PFAS consumption through food (the last
review being in 2021). FSANZ responded:
FSANZ is confident the TDIs for PFAS chemicals in food remain health
protective and notes that the outcomes of the 27th Australian Total Diet
Study demonstrated Australian consumers' exposure to PFAS through food
is very low and poses no food safety concerns.40
2.42 Nevertheless, FSANZ reiterated its commitment to continually reviewing
scientific evidence as it is made available, affirming that it would consider
establishing regulatory limits on PFAS in food if the evidence supported such
an action.41
2.43 FSANZ also outlined its intention to contribute to the 'future independent,
international review of PFAS by the Joint Food and Agricultural Organisation
of the United Nations (FAO)/World Health'. The review will examine if there is
a need to provide international HBGVs for PFAS.42
2.44 Finally, FSANZ noted that the values it derives for acceptable PFAS
consumption solely consider dietary exposure pathways. In other words,
FSANZ has not considered non-dietary exposure pathways of PFAS (such as
through dermal absorption or inhalation) as 'ingestion via food and water' is
considered the primary exposure route for PFAS. While other exposure routes
are deemed less significant, FSANZ stated that if the data is available, it is
willing to consider other sources of exposure to aid in a holistic quantification
of PFAS exposure.43
Minderoo Foundation research
2.45 Some inquiry participants spoke about research and reviews their organisations
have conducted. For example, Emeritus Professor Sarah Dunlop, Head of
39 Food Standards Australia and New Zealand, Response to Submission 128 (47th Parliament), [p. 2].
40 Food Standards Australia and New Zealand, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe
(received 11 July 2025), [p. 1].
41 Food Standards Australia and New Zealand, Response to Submission 128 (47th Parliament), [p. 2].

43 Food Standards Australia and New Zealand, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe

20
Plastics and Human Health at the Minderoo Foundation, described the process
of conducting an 'umbrella review':
By looking at systematic reviews, which statistically combine all the data,
and then doing an umbrella review, which systematically reviews the
systematic reviews, we finally got there after four years work … What that
does is look at the level of the literature of the systematic reviews—someone
else has already synthesised them—and then it looks at the balance sheet of
those systematic reviews. It's essentially a sort of vote-counting method
where you say, 'Yes, there is an impact,' and, 'No, there isn't.' … The other
really important thing about this methodology is to make sure that you take
all the evidence into account for harm, no harm or even being protective of
health. … That's how, after about four years, we got to publishing the
umbrella review.44
2.46 The Minderoo Foundation umbrella review found:
… consistent and statistically significant evidence of harm to health from
PFAS: reduced birth weight and length—these are adverse outcomes which,
in turn, lead to poor outcomes later in life—poor cognitive development;
chronic diseases such as respiratory disease, heart disease and diabetes;
lower lifetime earnings; an increased risk of ADHD in girls and of hay fever
and obesity in children; and altered thyroid function in adults. There's other
research which has identified risks to kidney and testicular cancer and has
also identified, concerningly, reduced response to vaccines.45
International research
2.47 This section briefly charts the history surrounding research into the health
effects of PFAS, including the role of industry research. It then presents some of
the international research on PFAS exposure and health effects. Several inquiry
participants noted that a range of international bodies using several lines of
evidence have now classified certain PFAS as carcinogenic.
Delayed start to independent research on the health effects of PFAS
2.48 The committee received evidence that industry reluctance to fully investigate
and publish potentially adverse findings with respect to the health effects of
PFAS was an important factor in the decades-long delay before independent
scientific studies began.
2.49 Senior Research Fellow at the School of Pharmacy at the University of Sydney,
Dr Nicholas Chartres told the committee that empirical studies have 'shown that
the main manufacturers and sellers of PFOA and PFOS in the United States
44 Emeritus Professor Sarah Dunlop, Head, Plastics and Human Health, Minderoo Foundation,
Committee Hansard, 12 March 2025, p. 21.
45 Emeritus Professor Sarah Dunlop, Head, Plastics and Human Health, Minderoo Foundation,
Committee Hansard, 12 March 2025, p. 21.
21
(US)—3M and DuPont—suppressed the science from regulatory agencies for
decades'.46
2.50 Professor Philippe Grandjean from the University of Southern Denmark and the
University of Rhode Island submitted that it was remarkable how late industry
knowledge on PFAS toxicity and environmental dissemination was disclosed to
the public and how few in-depth studies the industry had undertaken to reveal
and understand any risks associated with PFAS products and hazardous
exposures:47
Manufacturers of PFAS and PFAS-containing products knew already
decades ago about the highly toxic qualities of PFASs, their environmental
and biological persistence, and their capacity to inflict harm on human
beings at low levels of exposure. Because internal information was
eventually released only two decades ago, public knowledge on
dissemination and adverse effects happened at a substantial delay.48
2.51 For example, in 1956 researchers at Stanford University detected PFOA bound
to human serum proteins. In 1968, the first report suggesting 'the general
population was exposed to fluorine-substituted organic compounds' was
published in Nature.49
2.52 Although 'a few major producers of PFAS undertook or sponsored some
exploration of PFAS properties in the 1970s and 1980s', Professor Grandjean
submitted that certain findings remained unpublished and follow-up studies
were not commissioned.50
2.53 Apparently, 3M, a major producer of PFAS first discovered that PFAS was
transferred in milk in a study on lactating goats in 1993. However, it appears
that 3M decided not to publish the results and 'did not follow up with analyses
of human milk to establish the potential implications of this discovery'.51
Further, when company-based articles began appearing in the late 1990s and
after 2000, 'they tended to reach optimistic conclusions that appear not to be
justified by the data underlying the study'.52
2.54 The committee received submissions from US-based multinational,
3M Company, and 3M Australia. While praising the beneficial qualities of PFAS

47 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 4.

in its submission, 3M Company acknowledged the reality of 'changing
stakeholder expectations' and 'regulatory trends' against the continued use of
PFAS. 3M Company submitted that it is completely phasing out the
manufacture of PFAS—expecting to achieve this by the end of 2025—and has
since invested in research on remediating and treating PFAS.53
2.55 Similarly, 3M Australia argued that PFAS have many uses in society; some of
which are essential. 3M Australia noted that PFAS in the environment may come
from many sources—not only firefighting foams. Some of these other sources
included the fabric and carpet protection products, Scotchgard and Scotchban,
which were sold by 3M Australia.54
2.56 Neither 3M Company or 3M Australia acknowledged the existence of early
company research on the risks of PFAS in their submissions.
2.57 US attorney, Robert Bilott—who has been researching and litigating around the
impacts of PFOS since 1998—told the committee he has read 'literarily millions'
of pages of internal documents from the makers of PFAS chemicals 'which make
it pretty clear that the dangers and risks of these chemicals were well known
decades and decades ago'. Mr Bilott explained that early animal studies, worker
studies, and human data 'going back decades' were withheld by the companies
that produced PFAS, while the chemicals were 'pumped out into the world into
an incredible array of products', such as Teflon, Scotchgard and firefighting
foams.55
2.58 According to Mr Bilott, manufacturing company DuPont had to be compelled
to support a large-scale independent study into the health impacts of PFOA.
This was finally done in 2004 as part of the litigation process for a case on behalf
of thousands of people exposed to PFOA from a manufacturing plant in West
Virginia. Mr Bilott described the process:
We and DuPont jointly selected three independent world-class scientists
who were then tasked with looking at all the existing data—the animal
studies, the worker studies and not just what was published and peer
reviewed but also these internal studies that had been withheld and covered
up—and were tasked with doing new studies of the community that was
drinking this in their water out in West Virginia. We ended up being able to
do new studies of tens of thousands of people in that community in the
United States. Almost 69,000 people participated in those massive human
health studies that were created, some of the largest human health studies
ever done on any chemical anywhere … After seven years of this study, this
panel conclusively confirmed in 2013 that drinking PFOA had probable
links with six different diseases, including testicular cancer and kidney
cancer. When that data was confirmed we provided it to the top regulatory
53 3M Company, Submission 71, pp. 1–3.
54 3M Australia, Submission 87, pp. 2–3.
55 Mr Robert Bilott, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 11 June 2025, p. 1.
23
authorities in the United States, and, at that point, we finally started the
process of regulating these chemicals in the US—but that took another 10
years.56
2.59 Consequently, even though PFAS production began more than 70 years ago,
independent publication on PFAS toxicity only started in earnest about 20 years
ago.57 According to Professor Grandjean, the public health implications of a
failure to conduct and publish early studies only became apparent many years
later when independent research was published.58
Recent international findings
2.60 The International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) at the World Health
Organization uses a hazard classification to indicate the level of certainty that a
substance can cause cancer. For example, the Group 1 category indicates an
agent is carcinogenic to humans: there is sufficient evidence for cancer in
humans. The Group 2B category indicates an agent is possibly carcinogenic to
humans: there is limited evidence in humans and less than sufficient evidence
in experimental animals.59
2.61 Amongst others, the Department of Health noted that, in November 2023, the
IARC classified PFOA as Group 1—carcinogenic to humans, and PFOS as Group
2B—possibly carcinogenic to humans.60
2.62 In its submission to the inquiry—provided in December 2024—the Department
of Health and Aged Care Interim Australian Centre for Disease Control
maintained that 'the detailed information needed to interpret the International
Agency for Research on Cancer's findings' has not been made available, but the
department 'will consider the updated classification once the full article is
available as part of its existing work on updating Australian advice in line with
the new scientific evidence'.61 The IARC published this detailed research in

57 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 4.
58 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 5.

Classification, 16 June 2023 (accessed 11 November 2025).

February 2025. The committee is unaware whether the Department of Health
has since considered it.62
2.63 Dr Chartres told the committee about the growing global scientific consensus
on the human health effects of PFAS exposure and the multiple streams of
evidence used to support the conclusions:
In the last four years, three world-leading authoritative bodies—the
International Agency for Research on Cancer, the National Academies of
Sciences, Engineering and Medicine and the US Environmental Protection
Agency—have all established that PFOA and PFOS are carcinogens and are
associated with multiple health effects, including heart disease, neurological
effects and developmental effects. Many of these reviews relied on state-of-
the-art systematic review methods to evaluate the evidence that had been
widely used in clinical sciences throughout the world to inform billions of
dollars of healthcare spending. Importantly, these agencies use multiple
streams of evidence—human, animal and in vitro studies—to come up with
these conclusions.63
2.64 Professor Denis O'Carroll also pointed to negative health findings by
the Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry (ATSDR), a US
government, non-regulatory, environmental public health agency overseen by
the US Department of Health and Human Services.64
2.65 While the US ATSDR noted that research is ongoing to understand the
mechanisms of PFAS toxicity, the epidemiological evidence suggests
associations between increases in exposure to (specific) PFAS and certain health
effects. However, the risk of health effects associated with PFAS depend on:
 Exposure factors (for example, dose, frequency, route, and duration).
 Individual factors (for example, sensitivity and disease burden).
 Other determinants of health (for example, access to safe water and quality
healthcare).65
2.66 The US ATSDR also notes that studies on laboratory animals may help scientists
understand whether PFAS will harm people. Studies have found PFAS can
cause damage to the liver and immune system, as well as low birth weight, birth
defects, delayed development, and newborn deaths in laboratory animals.
62 International Agency for Research on Cancer, IARC Monographs Volume 135: Perfluorooctanoic acid
(PFOA) and perfluorooctanesulfonic acid (PFOS), 14 February 2025 (accessed 22 October 2025). The
research is available here: https://publications.iarc.who.int/636.
63 Dr Nicholas Chartres, Senior Research Fellow, School of Pharmacy, Faculty of Medicine and Health,

64 Professor Denis O'Carroll, Submission 1 (47th Parliament), p. 1; see United States Government,

However, there are caveats to these studies. First, the dosage levels in most
laboratory animal studies are higher than human environmental exposure.
Second, humans and animals react differently to PFAS, and not all effects
observed in animals may occur in humans.66
2.67 Mr Bilott said it is 'frustrating' for him to have spent decades working with US
regulators, 'and have finally gotten to this point where the science is pretty clear'.
And yet other countries, including Australia, continue to say:
'We just don't know enough yet. There needs to be more science. It's not clear
whether there are really any links with disease.' That's simply not accurate.
It's simply inconsistent with what the science has actually shown and what's
gone on in the last 20 years.67
2.68 Mr Bilott observed that, while the US and Australia are dealing with the exact
same chemicals, Australian regulators continue to insist that Australian data is
required. He added that PFOS and PFOA are:
… in the water in Australia. The science is the same. I see these submissions
from people who are saying, 'Well, we actually need to collect more data
from people in Australia to see if we're seeing this here.' You've already got
studies of tens of thousands of people from the United States that show
this.68
2.69 Ms Anton, made a similar observation, saying:
Australia does not need to conduct our own scientific studies to prove
adverse health impacts are consistently associated with high PFAS
exposures because the independent international science fraternity has
already established that and only Australia appears to be disputing it. Both
cannot be correct so don't waste any more time and resources on research
that has already been done.69
2.70 Asked to comment on recent international findings around PFAS and human
health, Assistant Secretary, Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, Mr
Stephen Bouwhuis said the department is 'looking at the international evidence'
including the IARC report, 'looking at what other countries are doing', and
feeding this information into Australia's formal PFAS guidance.70

Australian Government guidance and plans for review
2.71 The Australian Government Department of Health, Disability and Ageing
(formerly the Department of Health and Aged Care) provides high level
national health guidance on PFAS.71
2.72 Several health portfolio agencies contribute to the development of national
PFAS health advice and policy, including:
 the National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC);
 Food Standards Australia New Zealand (FSANZ);
 the Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme (AICIS); and
 the Therapeutic Goods Administration (TGA).72
2.73 In its submission, the National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC)
summarised the current Commonwealth consensus on evidence relating to the
health effects of PFAS:
To date the evidence on health effects of PFAS has been uncertain, draws on
a mix of animal and human studies, and can be interpreted in different ways
depending on the choice of health outcome (endpoints) used,
methodologies, assumptions and other factors. PFAS exposure in humans
has been associated with health effects such as mildly elevated levels of
cholesterol (confirmed by the 2021 Australian National University (ANU)
PFAS Study), effects on kidney function and on the levels of some hormones.
However, these effects are small and largely within ranges seen in the
general population. The ANU PFAS study confirmed the findings of the
Australian Government Expert Health Panel which determined that a range
of public health impacts had been identified in international studies, but the
impacts were generally small.73
2.74 In its interim report, the committee outlined the Environmental Health Standing
Committee (enHealth) PFAS Guidance Statement which was provided as part
of the submission by the then Department of Health and Aged Care. The
enHealth Guidance Statement represents Australia's 'official position' on the
health impacts of PFAS; it states:
PFAS exposure has been associated with the following effects:
 increased levels of cholesterol in the blood
 increased levels of uric acid in the blood
 reduced kidney function
 alterations in some indicators of immune function
 altered levels of thyroid hormones and sex hormones
71 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Submission 100 (47th Parliament), p.
3.
72 Australian Government Department of Health and Aged Care, Submission 100 (47th Parliament), p.
3.
73 National Health and Medical Research Council, Submission 57 (47th Parliament), p. 3.
27
 later age for starting menstruation (periods) in girls, and earlier
menopause
 lower birth weight in babies.
However, these differences have generally been small and are unlikely to be
important to health outcomes.
…
An association means that there is a relationship between PFAS exposure
and the above health effects. This does not mean that the PFAS exposure
caused the health effect. A causative relationship between the above health
effects and PFAS exposure has not been established to date.
…
Whilst PFAS has not definitely been shown to be a cause of disease in
humans, enHealth notes that the science and our understanding of these
issues will continue to evolve.74
2.75 In contrast, the US and many European countries have a different interpretation
of the scientific evidence on PFAS and human health than the Australian
Government. For example, the US Environmental Protection Agency (US EPA)
lists the following as 'what we know about health effects':
Current peer-reviewed scientific studies have shown that exposure to
certain levels of PFAS may lead to:
 Reproductive effects such as decreased fertility or increased high blood
pressure in pregnant women.
 Developmental effects or delays in children, including low birth weight,
accelerated puberty, bone variations, or behavioral changes.
 Increased risk of some cancers, including prostate, kidney, and testicular
cancers.
 Reduced ability of the body's immune system to fight infections,
including reduced vaccine response.
 Interference with the body's natural hormones.
 Increased cholesterol levels and/or risk of obesity.75
2.76 Mr Bouwhuis told the committee that state-based regulators have reported that
they rely upon the enHealth Guidance Statement on PFAS (updated in February
2024), which consolidated the Australian Government's PFAS health guidance
and included updates from the ANU PFAS Health Study and the FSANZ 27th
Australian Total Diet Study.76
74 Environmental Health Standing Committee, enHealth guidance statement—Per- and polyfluoroalkyl
Substances (PFAS), 2024, p. 4.
75 US EPA, Our Current Understanding of the Human Health and Environmental Risks of PFAS, last

76 Mr Stephen Bouwhuis, Assistant Secretary, Department of Health, Disability and Ageing,

28
2.77 Mr Bouwhuis noted that, despite the current consensus that the potential health
impacts of PFAS are likely to be small, the enHealth Guidance Statement on
PFAS advises 'a precautionary approach, recommending that exposure is
minimised where possible because PFAS persists for a long time in humans and
the environment'.77
2.78 The enHealth guidance, and the assumptions that underlie it, informs many
decisions made by government departments and entities. For instance, when
asked why the Commonwealth Health Department does not provide explicit
advice to people to avoid consumer products containing PFAS, Mr Bouwhuis
said:
… that ABS study goes to the levels found in people's blood and that was
generally below one nanogram per millilitre, off the top of my head, which
is not enough to cause any discernible level of risk to people. That's a very
low level of PFAS within a blood supply, so it shouldn't cause concern, and
that is bioaccumulated over time—so, obviously, that's a person's lifetime
exposure to PFAS.78
2.79 Evidence received over the course of the inquiry indicated that the government
is considering updating its advice on PFAS and human health.
2.80 The NHMRC recently reviewed and updated the 2018 Guideline
Recommendations for PFAS concentrations in drinking water. The update
incorporates 'a comprehensive review of the latest scientific evidence on PFAS
in drinking water for the Australian context, including both national and
international scientific reviews'. The new guidelines—published 25 June 2025—
stipulate significantly lower allowable concentrations of PFOS, PFOA and
PFHxS in Australia's drinking water (see Chapter 7 for discussion).79
2.81 Asked to comment on the role of emerging evidence, the Department of Health
submitted, 'emerging evidence has not changed enough to state with a degree
of confidence that any particular form of PFAS causes any particular disease'.80
2.82 Nevertheless, the committee was told that the Department of Health is also
conducting a review of PFAS evidence. Mr Bouwhuis said he anticipates that
this review will 'feed into a revised enHealth guidance', likely to be finalised
later in 2025:
