The enHealth guidance was updated recently in February 2024 but there's a
recognition by the enHealth committee of the need to update that further.
We are pulling together some analysis of the literature on PFAS and the
different studies to inform enHealth ahead of their consideration, should
there be a need to update the guidance—which that committee has
recognised there is a need to do.81
2.83 The Department of Health confirmed this on notice, saying:
 Since the release of the Expert Health Panel's report in 2018, there has
been ongoing public interest in PFAS exposure, and recent international
developments such as the outcomes of the International Agency for
Research on Cancer (IARC) evaluation of PFOA and PFOS highlighted
the need to review the latest developments in the health science on PFAS.
 In addition, the 2022 Parliamentary Inquiry into PFAS remediation in and
around Defence bases and the 2024 Independent Review of land uses
around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS contamination both
reference the need for expert advice.
 To address these ongoing concerns, the department engaged Allen +
Clarke Pty Ltd to conduct a review of domestic and international PFAS
health advice since the release of Expert Health Panel report in 2018,
taking into account international health advice on PFAS, the work done
by the NHMRC for the review of the Australian Drinking Water
Guidelines, and the work produced by the IARC.
 The Environmental Health Standing Committee (enHealth)
independently decided to review the enHealth Guidance Statement of
PFAS given developments since February 2024, including the revised
PFAS values in the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines and the work
produced by the IARC. It is anticipated the enHealth review will be
completed by the end of 2025.82
Views of inquiry participants
2.84 Many inquiry participants argued that Australia's official stance on the science
surrounding PFAS and human health is outdated and fails to incorporate recent
international findings. Current Australian government guidance on PFAS and
human health notes that PFAS exposure has been associated with a number of
effects (outlined earlier in this chapter), but states that these effects 'are unlikely
to be important to health outcomes'. Nevertheless, the enHealth Guidance
Statement acknowledges that 'the science and our understanding of these issues
will continue to evolve' and recommends PFAS exposure be minimised.83
81 Mr Stephen Bouwhuis, Assistant Secretary, Department of Health, Disability and Ageing,
Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 38.
82 Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe—
blood testing and enHealth guidance (received 6 August 2025), [p. 1].
83 enHealth, Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS) enHealth Guidance Statement, updated February
2024, p. 4.
30
2.85 The committee received a range of views from inquiry participants—many of
whom are scientists—on:
 the accumulation of PFAS in human and animal bodies over time;
 epidemiological and other evidence regarding the health effects of PFAS;
 levels of human PFAS exposure currently deemed safe; and
 the need to establish a range of monitoring programs and comprehensive
longitudinal studies.
2.86 This section presents submitters' and witnesses' views on bioaccumulation,
epidemiological evidence about the health effects of PFAS exposure, human
health effects of PFAS exposure, and potential safe levels of PFAS in the
environment.
PFAS bioaccumulation in animals and humans
2.87 Several submissions drew attention to the build-up, known as bioaccumulation,
of PFAS in human and animal bodies.
2.88 Dr Cameron Shearer, lecturer in chemistry, submitted that because PFAS does
not have 'a natural degradation pathway', it has been accumulating in the
environment since it was first made so that contamination is now globally
pervasive.84
2.89 Dr Chartres spoke to the pervasive nature of PFAS exposure, its
bioaccumulation, and the unknown cumulative effects on human health:
It is likely that every single person in this room has PFAS in their blood at
this moment in time. Governments around the world cannot tell you what
the likely source of that exposure is, how much is in our environment or
what the cumulative effects of these chemicals are. PFAS are persistent. They
don't leave our environment for several decades, and they bioaccumulate,
building up in our bodies over time.85
2.90 The Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences explained that the
durability and persistence of PFAS chemicals in the environment means they
can accumulate in human and animal bodies. The resulting bioaccumulation
means that PFAS 'concentrations within a person's body can be higher than is
measured in their surrounding environment'.86 Further, 'the effects of individual
84 Dr Cameron Shearer, Submission 49 (47th Parliament), p. 1; see also Professor Philippe Grandjean,
Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 2.
85 Dr Nicholas Chartres, Senior Research Fellow, School of Pharmacy, Faculty of Medicine and Health,
University of Sydney, Committee Hansard, 22 January 2025, p. 1.
86 Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences, Submission 35 (47th Parliament), p. 2; see also
Dr Cameron Shearer, Submission 49 (47th Parliament), p. 1.
31
chemicals cumulate even when they occur in low concentrations, often as part
of complex chemical mixtures'.87
Views on the epidemiological evidence
2.91 The science about the health effects of PFAS is evolving rapidly. The committee
heard that, 20 years ago there were one or two papers a year on the subject of
the interaction between PFAS and human health; whereas now, more than 100
papers are published annually.88
2.92 However, the committee also heard there are substantial obstacles to obtaining
causal scientific evidence that could prove PFAS causes adverse health effects,
including cancers, but that a precautionary approach is warranted.
2.93 Professor Kirk, whose team conducted the ANU study, told the committee that
trying to assess health effects associated with chemical exposure is 'quite
difficult', as health effects may occur long after exposure. Further complications
arise because many other factors that determine health may also be involved.89
2.94 The Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences noted that 'confirming
and quantifying' the potential risks of PFAS is challenging and complex:
Identifying the extent of PFAS bioaccumulation across the Australian
population and then investigating the connection between PFAS exposure
and health outcomes, requires adequate monitoring of the levels of a wide
range of chemical contaminants that co-accumulate in the human body.90
2.95 Professor Kirk also emphasised the complexity in determining causal
relationships between exposure and health effects, including demonstrating a
dose response (that is, the more exposure, the greater the risk of disease) and
being able to repeat the findings.91
2.96 Much of the uncertainty around the health findings arises because the small
number of people highly exposed to PFAS precludes the typical large-scale
epidemiological studies comprising many tens of thousands of individuals.92
87 Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences, Submission 35 (47th Parliament), p. 3.
88 See Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025,
p. 32.
89 Professor Martyn Kirk, National Centre for Epidemiology and Population Health, Australian
National University, Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, p. 27.
90 Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences, Submission 35 (47th Parliament), pp. 2–3.
91 Professor Martyn Kirk, National Centre for Epidemiology and Population Health, Australian
National University, Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, p. 32.
92 Professor Martyn Kirk, National Centre for Epidemiology and Population Health, Australian
National University, Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, p. 29; see also Associate Professor
Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 36.
32
2.97 This 'Catch-22' was summarised by Professor Bernard Stewart, Member of the
Occupational and Environmental Cancer Committee, Cancer Council Australia,
who was asked if the Cancer Council had considered—or would consider—
conducting research to try to establish if there is a cancer cluster in Wreck Bay.
Professor Stewart said the Wreck Bay population 'may not be enough to do
meaningful cancer statistics'. He added:
I'm not dismissing the sincerely held beliefs of the Wreck Bay population for
one moment, and I'm not dismissing the burden of anxiety they have. I
sympathise and can readily recognise that burden of anxiety and concern,
and I wish I could enunciate a medico-scientific procedure that would bring
a degree of reassurance to them in the sense that they have a burden of care
and anxiety at the moment.93
2.98 In their submission, Professor Kirk and colleagues stated that the ANU PFAS
Health Study 'was not designed to investigate whether PFAS exposure was the
cause of any health effects. These types of studies require investigation of people
over time'.94
2.99 Professor Deborah Glass concurred that epidemiological studies require
studying large cohorts of people over time. She noted that, generally, PFAS
exposure in the community is 'fairly low', while exposures around the PFAS
manufacturing plant were probably higher, and higher again for those working
in the plant. However, the cohort with high exposure to PFAS was still very
small. Professor Glass noted that epidemiology is not useful at finding answers
from small cohorts of people, because minor random variations can easily alter
the findings and affect the conclusions. She explained that scientists use
toxicology studies with laboratory animals because this may provide answers
in instances where epidemiological studies cannot.95
2.100 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne noted that conducting PFAS exposure
studies on humans is unethical because it risks creating harm. He also explained
the limitations of animal studies. While scientists can conduct PFAS exposure
experiments on laboratory animals such as mice and rats, a 40-year study with
a rat is impossible because they do not live beyond one or two years. In general,
however, people are exposed to low doses of PFAS over many decades. To
compensate, scientific studies tend to give laboratory animals larger doses in
expectation of a larger and quicker effect. Therefore, many animal studies are
93 Professor Bernard Stewart, Member, Occupational and Environmental Cancer Committee, Cancer
Council Australia, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 29.
94 Professor Martyn Kirk, Dr Kayla Smurthwaite and Ms Susan Trevenar, Submission 85 (47th
Parliament), p. 4.
95 Professor Deborah Glass, private capacity, Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, pp. 32–33.
33
trying to compare, for example, a higher dose given to an animal over a year
with a much lower dose experienced by people over 40 years.96
2.101 Similarly, Dr Shearer noted that most current research on animals looks at 'the
toxicity of very high levels of PFAS contamination in order to elicit a response'
with the result that the findings do not replicate natural processes.97
2.102 Consequently, Associate Professor Osborne stated that scientists tend to rely on
epidemiological studies that examine large populations and compare people
who have been exposed to either high or low doses.98
2.103 Associate Professor Osborne noted that because epidemiological studies are
observational studies, scientists 'can only really draw associations, not
causations, about that relationship between exposure to PFAS and health
outcomes'.99
2.104 Associate Professor Osborne observed that because an association is a
mathematical equation, scientists then need to explain a pathological pathway
between PFAS exposure and disease noting that, for example, 'many things are
known to cause kidney and testicular cancer'.100
2.105 In terms of low-dose chronic-exposure toxins, Associate Professor Osborne,
noted the gap in understanding the health effects of PFAS. He observed that
while most Australians are exposed to very low doses of PFAS, what is
occurring at hotspots next to an air force base is not understood. For example, if
a person buys all their food from the supermarket, their drinking water is piped
in from another area, and it is not dusty, their exposure to PFAS may not differ
significantly from Australians living away from hotspots. By contrast, people
consuming poultry, livestock, eggs, and vegetables produced on contaminated
land, using contaminated water, harvesting fish, seafood, and plants from
contaminated waterways, or swimming in contaminated creeks, may have
higher exposure and higher levels of PFAS in their bodies. However, research
that may demonstrate such findings has not yet been done.101
96 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 31.
97 Dr Cameron Shearer, Submission 49 (47th Parliament), p. 1.
98 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 31;
see also Dr David Goldberg, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 20; Professor
Martyn Kirk, National Centre for Epidemiology and Population Health, Australian National
University, Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, p. 29.
99 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 31.
100 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 32.
101 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025,
pp. 33–34.
34
2.106 Associate Professor Osborne considered that 'some Australians have lost their
lives due to exposure to PFAS' and PFAS 'has increased the risk of a range of
diseases' including 'increased cholesterol, kidney dysfunction and kidney and
testicular cancers'. That being acknowledged, it is nevertheless difficult to
conclusively attribute disease to PFAS because, in general, the exposure levels
are 'very low', many of the diseases or symptoms of disease, like increased
cholesterol, can be caused by a range of factors such as smoking cigarettes or
being overweight, and people have different genetic makeups that may increase
or decrease their risk.102
2.107 Consequently, epidemiologists aim to study large numbers of people, typically,
50 000, to be able to discern the risk, especially if that risk is relatively low. For
example, even though kidney cancer is the seventh most prevalent cancer in
Australia, it is still a relatively rare disease. Therefore, a study needs large
numbers of people to be able to detect that disease in the population, and more
than one or two cases to be able to make calculations and draw robust
conclusions around exposure to PFAS and health risk.103
2.108 Nevertheless, American toxicologist, Ms Jamie DeWitt, cautioned that the
difficulty in establishing a causative link should not lead to complacency when
it comes to regulating PFAS chemicals:
Just because there is not a causative link between PFOA or PFOS or PFHxS
exposure and a disease—we still have very strong evidence about those
links from epidemiological studies with support from experimental animal
studies. That means that the biological plausibility of these effects occurring
in people being from PFAS exposure is increased. … it can be very
challenging to uncover causative links between diseases and exposure when
you're dealing with an environmental contaminant. ... But I think, if you look
at different epidemiological studies and include the experimental animal
studies, that the evidence overwhelmingly supports that PFAS are hazards
and that exposure to PFAS increases the risk of experiencing those
hazards.104
Views on the human health effects
2.109 The committee received a range of views and conflicting interpretations of the
available scientific evidence on the health effects of PFAS exposure.
2.110 Ms DeWitt reported that she has been involved with PFAS research since 2005.
Research on PFOA at that time found that it 'suppressed vaccine antibody
response in rodents'. This particular study concluded that 'PFOA was the most
potent immunosuppressant [one of the authors had] ever observed'. Not long
after the findings of this research were published in 2008, human
102 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 35.
103 Associate Professor Nicholas Osborne, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 36.
104 Ms Jamie DeWitt, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, pp. 6–7.
35
epidemiological studies were published, indicating that this effect could also be
seen in 'children who were exposed to PFAS in the womb'.105
2.111 Since then, Ms DeWitt told the committee, many studies in both animals and
humans have been completed, and:
… the overwhelming majority of these studies have reached the conclusion
that exposure to PFOA and other PFAS can suppress the vaccine antibody
response. You might be thinking: 'That doesn't really seem like much of a
change. It seems mild to moderate.' But even mild to moderate immune
suppression, such as these studies have demonstrated, can really have
profound effects on the health of populations. It can increase the risk of
communicable diseases as well as other diseases such as cancer, and the
immune system is really a key player in keeping us healthy. When it's
impacted, it can lead to lack of health.106
2.112 Ms DeWitt also noted that the health effects of PFAS are not limited to the
immune system:
… we call PFAS multisystem toxicants because these agents can impact
many different parts of the body. As you probably well know, the list of
health effects is growing. Those types of health effects with the strongest
body of evidence include kidney and testicular cancer, elevated cholesterol,
liver damage, developmental effects such as low birth weight in babies,
endocrine disruption such as thyroid disease, and immune suppression.107
2.113 Dr Shearer noted that PFAS are detected in the ovarian fluid of women,
'demonstrating that these compounds can directly access the developing eggs in
female animals'. Dr Shearer also argued that adverse health effects have already
been documented:
Developmental defects are documented in children of women exposed to
high levels of PFAS; however, even low levels of PFAS, currently deemed
safe, can exert toxicity.108
2.114 The Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences noted that PFAS are
endocrine disrupting chemicals that can 'impact hormone functioning':
Preliminary research has proposed potential links between the
bioaccumulation of chemical contaminants and health risks such as
disruptions to the immune and reproductive systems, inhibited childhood
neurodevelopment, and increased risk of some cancers.109
2.115 Dr David Goldberg explained that certain chemicals can suppress the immune
system which has implications for how susceptible a person becomes to cancer.
105 Ms Jamie DeWitt, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 2.
106 Ms Jamie DeWitt, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 2.
107 Ms Jamie DeWitt, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 2
108 Dr Cameron Shearer, Submission 49 (47th Parliament), p. 1.
109 Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences, Submission 35 (47th Parliament), p. 2.
36
He noted that immune system population data on autopsy suggests that, for
every person who dies of a cancer, a person's immune system kills eight cancers.
One mechanism of cancer is immunosuppression. This means that something
can have a direct carcinogenic effect which is genetic and damages the cell, but
it can also cause immunosuppression. For example, if a person has a replication
error in a cell, even in the absence of a carcinogen, in the early stages of that
cancer the immune system will recognise it as an abnormal cell and kill it.
Dr Goldberg's view was that PFAS is 'probably a carcinogen but it's definitely
an immunosuppressant'.110
2.116 Professor Stewart told the committee that the Cancer Council accepts evidence
that PFOA 'is implicated as causing testicular cancer and renal cancer in
humans'. Professor Stewart acknowledged that 'the evidence is not definitive'
but noted there is evidence that PFOA causes cancer in rodents. PFOS, on the
other hand, is 'not known to cause cancer in humans' or in animals. However,
Professor Stewart noted that PFOS 'is ranked as possibly carcinogenic on the
basis of the mechanistic evidence by IARC'.111
2.117 It was Professor Stewart's view that 'the data are not there to establish a causal
relationship between PFAS exposure and cancer development as yet, but such
findings could emerge'. However, he suggested that, if preventing cancer is the
aim, governments should prioritise monitoring and reducing proven
environmental sources of cancer:
We're talking about prevention now. In terms of the preventive budget—in
terms of cancer prevention specifically—I respectfully submit that the main
polluting cause of cancer in Australia is atmospheric pollution, causing lung
cancer, and it's mainly from vehicular emissions and industry. If I had to
choose between monitoring atmospheric pollutants on the one hand and
monitoring PFAS chemicals in water on the other, I would have no difficulty
in saying that you monitor definitively established causes of cancer as a first
priority and suspected causes of cancer as a second priority, possibly with a
view to gaining more evidence.112
2.118 Professor Stewart summarised the Cancer Council's view that the risk of cancer
in humans from some PFAS chemicals 'is at least partially demonstrated' for
occupational exposure (those exposed while working with, or around, PFAS).
110 Dr David Goldberg, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, pp. 20–21. Dr Goldberg,
a practising medical practitioner, was a visiting medical doctor at Wreck Bay Aboriginal
Community between 2000 and 2008. Wreck Bay was exposed to PFAS contamination from the
historic use of firefighting foam at two Defence sites above Wreck Bay. Dr Goldberg's evidence is
included in the committee's Interim Report.
111 Professor Bernard Stewart, Member, Occupational and Environmental Cancer Committee, Cancer
Council Australia, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 26.
112 Professor Bernard Stewart, Member, Occupational and Environmental Cancer Committee, Cancer
Council Australia, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, pp. 28–29.
37
However, this risk has not been demonstrated in relation to exposure through
'contaminated water in the wider community'. In contrast with other evidence
received by the committee, Professor Stewart argued that the fear around PFAS
causing cancer may be 'misplaced':
Despite that, anxiety has been generated because of reports of people being
convinced that they have developed particular cancers because of their
exposure to these compounds. That anxiety is, to some extent, misplaced
because certain perceptions about cancer causation are wrong. These
include the ideas that cancer is sometimes self-evident from a particular
carcinogen; that the community can identify, apart from familial cancers, an
increased risk of cancer in humans; that any exposure to a known carcinogen
causes cancer; that cancer can arise throughout the body in response to a
particular carcinogen; that blood levels of a particular carcinogen indicate
risk of cancer; and that people have been deliberately withholding the truth.
All of those perceptions are, in fact, wrong.113
2.119 Professor Stewart also commented on suggestions that the government should
support PFAS blood testing, saying that blood levels of PFAS, or any other
carcinogen, do not give an indication of the risk of cancer:
If they did, we could, for example, establish the 16 per cent of lifelong
smokers who get lung cancer as identifiable by the tobacco based
carcinogens in their blood. They're not. The reason we have to take on
board—to the government's credit—a complicated scanning system for lung
cancer is that we can't determine which smokers will get lung cancer by
looking at their blood levels. So—although it's counterintuitive—in an
exposed population, a person with no detectable blood levels of PFAS and
a person with the highest recorded blood levels of PFAS have an equal
probability of being at risk of cancer. The blood levels do not tell you who's
going to get the disease.114
2.120 Professor Grandjean submitted that because PFAS compounds are transferred
to the foetus through the placenta and are excreted in human milk, 'PFAS
exposure reaches the youngest and most vulnerable population' and 'may exert
greater and lasting adverse effects if exposure happens in early life, when organ
functions develop'.115
Views on safe/unsafe levels of PFAS exposure
2.121 Professor Grandjean argued that, even with the current state of scientific
evidence, there are reasonable grounds for stating that PFAS cause adverse
health effects in the human population:
113 Professor Bernard Stewart, Member, Occupational and Environmental Cancer Committee, Cancer
Council Australia, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 26.
114 Professor Bernard Stewart, Member, Occupational and Environmental Cancer Committee, Cancer
Council Australia, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 28.
115 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), pp. 2 and 5.
38
Based on the weight of the epidemiological evidence and supporting
laboratory toxicology evidence, and stated with reasonable scientific and
medical certainty, exposures to PFASs (including, but not limited to PFOS,
PFOA, PFNA, and PFHxS, and their precursors) are causative of important
adverse health effects in humans in Australia and globally.116
2.122 Consequently, Professor Grandjean maintained that the current evidence is
sufficient to deem any additional exposure to PFAS as potentially adverse to
human health:
Given that virtually all people already have measurable quantities of PFASs
in their bodies, and that adverse effects have been documented within the
range of background exposures, any additional PFAS exposures should be
considered potentially adverse, whether from water sources, food or air
related to or originating from the specific contamination sources.117
2.123 He acknowledged that while comprehensive scientific evidence is still lacking,
prudent measures based on probability are necessary to manage preventable
adverse health outcomes:
In principle, it would be desirable to rely on evidence that is as complete as
required for decisions on evidence-based medicine. However, in a real
world situation, where the goal is to avoid risks from preventable hazards,
and where human experimentation is unethical, less comprehensive
documentation is generally accepted as the basis for prudent decision-
making on environmental risk factors, preferably based on high-quality
epidemiological studies and complementary experimental evidence that
supports the plausibility of observed associations being causal.118
2.124 In this regard, Professor Grandjean drew attention to the most recent assessment
by the US EPA which 'regards both PFOS and PFOA as human carcinogens and
therefore recommends a maximum human intake of zero as the level that will
not result in adverse effects in human beings'. He told the committee that the
weight of research is showing PFAS to be 'more toxic than previously thought',
leading to the lowering of exposure limits in a number of jurisdictions:
However, I understand that PFAS are useful; they are financially important.
They're fairly cheap to produce but highly expensive to remove after they
have been disseminated. I have participated as a medical expert in several
court cases in Australia, so I'm quite familiar with exposures in various
Australian communities, and it's clear to me that you have in front of you a
serious public health problem. I'm therefore very eager to help you in
116 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 1.
117 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 1.
118 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), pp. 5–6.
39
finding the best decision, the most ethical and prudent decision, for now and
for the future.119
2.125 Similarly, Dr Chartres argued that 'there is no safe level to any chemical, based
on the fact that we have huge amounts of human variability':
… people may come from low-income communities, they may have
background exposures to other chemicals, and they may have other
comorbidities that they're experiencing which exacerbate the chemical's
harm.120
2.126 By contrast, Dr Stuart Khan, Professor and Head of the School of Civil
Engineering at the University of Sydney expressed a different view about how
the term 'no safe level' is derived and the disputed concept of zero risk in
toxicology:
The model that they use—and this is where you hear the term 'no safe
level'—is a widely used model in toxicology, but toxicologists understand
that it's a serious oversimplification of the real world. They assume that any
carcinogenic or genotoxic chemical presents some risk of causing cancer. It
doesn't matter if it's one molecule; there's some risk of causing cancer there.
That's a hotly disputed point in toxicology, but that's how the model is
actually presented. So this is where 'no safe level' comes from. In order to
have zero risk, you have to have zero exposure.121
2.127 Dr Khan stated that, as yet, there is no toxicological data or cancer slope factor
that would enable the setting of safe levels of PFAS exposure. He therefore
argued that the US EPA has ignored the current scientific data and simply
declared that there is no safe level for PFAS, 'which is a purely aspirational
statement'. He added, however, that the US EPA does not use 'no safe level'
because it is neither measurable nor achievable and that is how the US EPA
arrived at four nanograms per litre for PFAS.122
119 Professor Philippe Grandjean, Submission 51 (47th Parliament), p. 7; Professor Philippe Grandjean,
Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 46; see also Mr Keiran Assheton, Private
capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 12.

University of Sydney, Committee Hansard, 22 January 2025, p. 9.

122 Dr Stuart Khan, Professor and Head of School, School of Civil Engineering, University of Sydney,
Committee Hansard, 22 January 2025, pp. 10–11. A cancer slope factor, also known as a cancer
potency factor, is a value used in toxicology and risk assessment to estimate the increased cancer
risk associated with a specific chemical or agent. It represents the estimated upper bound of the
increased cancer risk from a lifetime exposure to a substance, typically expressed as the risk of
cancer per unit of daily exposure. See: Suji Jang, Kan Shao, Weihsueh A. Chiu, 'Beyond the cancer
slope factor: Broad application of Bayesian and probabilistic approaches for cancer dose-response
assessment', Environment International, vol. 175, 2023, p. 107959 .

2.128 Dr Khan explained that the World Health Organization uses a tolerable level of
risk with respect to chemical exposure:
The World Health Organization uses a one in 100 000 excess lifetime cancer
risk—so, for one person, one additional cancer case by the end of their life
occurring as a consequence of exposure to this particular chemical—and it
extrapolates effectively what that level of exposure relates to in terms of a
risk and what that risk relates to in terms of exposure. That's also the
approach that has been taken in Australia for lots of genotoxic and
carcinogenic chemicals.123
2.129 Dr Khan noted that Australia has adopted the same approach as the World
Health Organization, which is to set conservative health-based guidelines based
on the current health and toxicological data:
If the toxicological data, the science that's available, doesn't take you down
to four nanograms per litre, they don't set the guideline at four nanograms
per litre. They set it where the toxicological data takes you. But there are
significant safety factors built into that.
There are a lot of conservative assumptions all the way through, including
the selection of the toxicological end point, including the calculation of the
tolerable daily intake and including when the guidelines are developed.124
Approaches to dealing with uncertainty
2.130 Professor Peter Sly, Fellow at the Australian Academy of Health and Medical
Sciences, said the Academy believes that 'scientific uncertainty' must not delay
action on PFAS, and the 'precautionary principle' must be paramount:
I think I'd take that one step further and say one of the problems we have in
science, science communication and environmental health in general is that
we have the onus of proof on the precautionary principle backwards.
Companies and manufacturers are allowed to introduce chemicals into our
environment, and it's up to us later on to prove there's something wrong
with them. It should be the other way around. They should not be able to
introduce something into the environment, particularly for children, unless
they have proven that it is safe.125
2.131 Senior Advisor to the International Pollutants Elimination Network, Dr Mariann
Lloyd-Smith argued that:
Successive Australian governments have downplayed the dangers of PFAS
exposure. A 2021, international review of epidemiological studies confirmed
associations between exposure to specific PFAS and a variety of health
effects, including altered immune and thyroid function, liver disease, lipid
123 Dr Stuart Khan, Professor and Head of School, School of Civil Engineering, University of Sydney,
Committee Hansard, 22 January 2025, p. 10.
124 Dr Stuart Khan, Professor and Head of School, School of Civil Engineering, University of Sydney,
Committee Hansard, 22 January 2025, p. 11.
125 Professor Peter Sly, Fellow, Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences, Committee
Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 15.
41
and insulin dysregulation, kidney disease, adverse reproductive and
developmental outcomes, and cancer. These findings were supported by
experimental animal data for many of these effects.126
2.132 Ms DeWitt disputed the idea that the science around PFAS and human health is
uncertain, saying 'the level of uncertainty is actually quite low if you look at
consistency across studies and if you look at the data from experimental animal
studies that are supportive'. Ms DeWitt referred to a 2023 study outlining 'the
epidemiological findings for PFAS'. This 'systematic review and meta-analysis'
looked at five different PFAS and considered their effects on 'antibody responses
across different vaccines in adults and in children':
They found that the results of these different studies were actually very
homogeneous, or consistent. There was agreement among the studies, even
across different types of vaccines, that these PFAS were all able to reduce
the responses to vaccines and that children were more sensitive.127
According to Ms DeWitt, studies related to cholesterol elevation, and those
that indicate decreased birthweight, also show consistent results. Studies
may find 'differences in terms of the magnitude of the outcome, but we see
that there are still reductions in birth weight and in antibody responses, and
there are elevations in cholesterol'.128
2.133 Ms DeWitt concluded that, while scientific data for PFAS 'are still evolving',
there is sufficient evidence to justify phasing out non-essential uses:
Many have been understudied with respect to their toxicity, but there's
really no reason to continue to allow exposures to these PFAS. Absence of
evidence isn't the same as evidence of absence, so why should we as a
society wait for people to get sick before we take action to reduce or
eliminate exposures or risks?129
Reducing blood PFAS levels
2.134 The committee considered evidence from people exposed to large amounts of
PFAS, including evidence on PFAS blood testing and reduction methods.
2.135 Mr Mick Tisbury, Assistant Chief Fire Officer with Fire Rescue Victoria,
reported that he developed, co-authored and commissioned a world-first study
which established that regular plasma and blood donations were successful in
significantly reducing blood PFAS levels.130 The study did not examine the

important questions are worthy of further research to better understand health
outcomes from exposure and treatment'.131
2.136 Mr Christopher Howe, who was exposed to PFAS at work, discussed how he
had volunteered to give blood to a University of Queensland study into PFAS
exposure with his results showing higher levels of PFAS than in the general
Australian population. Mr Howe undertook his own research to find out his
options for reducing the 'extreme levels of PFAS contamination' in his blood and
now has his own 'voluntary and self-funded schedule of donating plasma and
blood' fortnightly.132
2.137 As Mr Howe lives in regional Australia, this involves a three to four hour, 100-
kilometre round trip to his closest LifeBlood donation centre, despite restrictions
and challenges in giving blood or plasma, such as inability to find a vein, high
ferritin levels, illnesses such as a cold, and certain overseas travel which may
restrict donations.133
2.138 The United Firefighters Union Australia Aviation Branch underscored the
difficulties associated with plasma donation, noting that exclusions exist for
some people, and plasma donation facilities are not available on Norfolk Island.
It suggested:
The Commonwealth Government and NIRC [Norfolk Island Regional
Council] are jointly responsible for administering the island's affairs and
must implement and fund opportunities for affected fire fighters and
members of the public to access the most effective method of ridding the
body of PFAS chemicals.134
2.139 Mr Howe's most recent blood test results showed a decrease in PFAS levels;
however, he acknowledged 'it is likely to take many more years—most likely
decades—before my blood levels return to average'.135

There is a precedent for subsiding venesection solely for treatment purposes

reduce blood PFAS levels, 'noting the lack of clinical usefulness, an application
would require a comprehensive dossier justifying clinical need and financial
cost-effectiveness'. The Medical Services Advisory Committee would need to
assess the 'comparative safety, clinical effectiveness, cost-effectiveness, and total
cost, using the best available evidence' before any such amendment could be
made.140
Biomonitoring
2.144 Scientific and medical organisations who gave evidence throughout the inquiry
emphasised the benefits of large-scale biomonitoring to track PFAS levels, and
impacts, over time.
2.145 The Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences recommended:
 establishing a national biomonitoring program to track the accumulation of
PFAS and other chemical contaminants in Australia;

 immediately begin monitoring pregnant women's exposure levels, as an

 expand research into the health effects of PFAS exposure, 'especially the
impact during early life'; and
 'regularly review and update the legal limits for PFAS exposure based on
emerging evidence'.141
2.146 Professor Sly suggested leftover blood samples held by pathology providers
could be used to conduct wider population biomonitoring:
We've developed relationships with pathology groups in Queensland—
with Sullivan Nicolaides, for example—and we can get leftover blood and
urine samples which are pooled and de-identified, and we've used these to
monitor levels of plasticisers in the population in Queensland. This is a
technique that is cheaper because, in pooling samples, firstly, you protect
individuals' identities, but, secondly, you only have to measure one sample
rather than 10 or 100, and you can get levels across the population. … These
same things could be applied here, because, while the concentrations of
PFAS and things are low, they are measurable.142
2.147 According to the Academy of Health and Medical Sciences, Australia 'stands
out among comparable nations such as Canada, the US, Germany, South Korea,
and Japan for not operating a population-level human biomonitoring (HBM)
program'. This is despite Australia having 'some of the highest PFAS exposure
levels globally'. The Academy suggested:
To identify the extent of PFAS bioaccumulation across the population, and
then investigate possible connections between PFAS exposure and health
outcomes, Australia must begin tracking levels of PFAS and related
chemical contaminants that co-accumulate in the human body.143
2.148 Asked to comment on proposals for national biomonitoring, Mr Bouwhuis said:
There's not currently a biomonitoring program at the Commonwealth level.
That isn't something that's been introduced by successive Australian
governments. But we did pay for the ABS to do a study on levels of PFAS in
the Australian community generally—so 7½ thousand volunteers donated
their blood to sample to see what the PFAS levels were like. That does give
us a baseline population level for population exposure to PFAS, and that's
similar to biomonitoring.144
2.149 The Cancer Council recommended:

Health screening and biomonitoring for exposed cohorts
2.151 The enHealth Statement advises that individual blood tests are 'not
recommended' and 'have no current value in informing an individual's clinical
management'. It states:
… there is at present insufficient scientific evidence for a medical
practitioner to be able to tell a person whether their blood level will make
them sick now or later in life, or if any current health problems are related
to the PFAS levels found in their blood.147
2.152 Ms Jayne Newgreen—whose partner has high blood PFAS levels after
unknowingly drinking contaminated water for a number of years—
recommended free health monitoring be made available for people who have or
have had high blood PFAS levels; and that free mental health support be made
available 'for people who are contaminated'.148
2.153 The committee heard that Commonwealth agencies provided access to targeted
mental health and counselling services in PFAS-contaminated communities in
the past. However, this is reported to have ceased in 2019–20.149
2.154 Community members impacted by PFAS contamination at Wreck Bay similarly
expressed a strong desire for government-funded blood testing, cancer
screening, and general health monitoring for all impacted residents, but
especially for children and pregnant women.150

2.158 This claim has been refuted by Mr Mick Tisbury, from Fire Rescue Victoria, who
told the committee that his organisation provides blood testing for exposed
firefighters and has worked with physicians to develop health advice which is
provided to firefighters who seek advice following their PFAS blood testing.155
2.159 The advice aims to 'dispel myths and promote management options, aiming to
reduce the risk of those health conditions that have been associated with
elevated serum PFAS'. The advice outlines what constitutes a 'normal' blood
PFAS level, and lists possible health effects, saying:
A few of these health effects have been well established and found
repeatedly in research studies showing robust evidence that the relationship
cannot be questioned. For many more conditions, the evidence of

Chapter 3
Firefighters and others exposed at work
Introduction
3.1 Given the extensive use of foams containing per- and polyfluoroalkyl
substances (PFAS) in fighting fires, firefighters are among the most
PFAS-exposed groups in the Australian population.
3.2 Evidence to the inquiry indicated that cohorts exposed to PFAS contamination
at work include firefighters, persons responsible for maintaining, repairing and
decontaminating fire trucks, equipment and training facilities, and persons
working at civilian airports.
3.3 This chapter examines considerations specific to firefighters and other
occupational groups exposed to PFAS in the course of their work and outlines
suggestions for reform proposed by these cohorts.
Firefighters
3.4 Over the course of the inquiry, the committee heard that firefighters are one of
the most PFAS-exposed occupational groups due to their substantial, 'direct
frequent and occupational' exposure to firefighting foam containing PFAS.1
3.5 Firefighters include members of state and territory firefighting services, as well
as volunteer firefighters, aviation, Defence, private and contractor firefighters,
including those in industrial settings, such as oil and gas rigs and plants.2
3.6 Exposure to PFAS-containing foam has occurred during regular training
activities, active firefighting duties and emergency responses—through dermal
contact, as well as inhalation.3 At the time, firefighters claim they were
'completely unaware of the risks' and had been told that the firefighting foam
was safe.4
1 Also known as Aqueous Film Forming Foam (AFFF). Australian Army Fire Service, Retired
Firefighter's Group, Submission 141 (47th Parliament), pp. 1 and 6; United Firefighters Union of
Australia, Aviation Branch, Submission 113 (47th Parliament), p. 5.
2 See: United Firefighters Union of Australia, Aviation Branch, Submission 113 (47th Parliament), pp.
3 and 5; Australian Army Fire Service, Retired Firefighter's Group, Submission 141 (47th Parliament),
p. 1; Department of Defence, response to written questions on notice from Senator Thorpe (received
17 February 2025), p. [6]; Mr Greg McConville, National Secretary, United Firefighters Union of
Australia, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 18.
3 United Firefighters Union of Australia, Aviation Branch, Submission 113 (47th Parliament), p. 5.
4 Australian Army Fire Service, Retired Firefighter's Group, Submission 141 (47th Parliament), pp. 2
and 5.
49
50
3.7 Retired members of the Royal Australian Army Fire Service (and successor
units)—a cohort which has been largely unacknowledged to date—explained to
the committee that:
We routinely handled it—often without protective gear—coming into direct
contact with it on our skin, eyes and mouths, we inhaled its fumes, and it
saturated and contaminated our clothing and equipment.5
3.8 Retired members described other ways in which PFAS-containing foam was
used:
AFFF [Aqueous Film Forming Foam] was a regular part of everyday life for
our members, at every Army Fire Station, not just Oakey. We were told that
it was safe, and for example we:
 used it to wash trucks,
 we washed dishes and clothes in it
 we soaked our mates in it to celebrate a birthday,
 on barracks open days we would fill the vehicle bays with AFFF foam
and let children run through it, playing hide and seek,
 we trained with it regularly, getting it on our skin and in our eyes,
 we took our AFFF soaked uniforms home and washed them with the rest
of the families clothing,
 At Oakey, where married quarter water was supplied via bore water,
members would carry rain water home, for the family's consumption, in
rinsed out AFFF containers.6
3.9 National Secretary of the United Firefighters Union of Australia (UFUA),
Mr Greg McConville, stated, 'the fact of the matter is that our members are the
crash-test dummies and the lab rats in this kind of occupational exposure
because they experience it before anyone else does and they experience it at
greater doses'.7
Other occupations
3.10 Other occupations have also experienced exposure at work. For example,
emergency vehicle technicians (EVTs) responsible for maintaining, repairing
and decontaminating fire trucks, equipment and training facilities have also
been significantly exposed to PFAS.
3.11 The Community and Public Sector Union (CPSU) reported that an occupational
exposure study detected higher levels of PFAS in certain EVTs compared to
firefighters. It concluded 'it is possible that EVTs were exposed to AFFF
5 Australian Army Fire Service, Retired Firefighter's Group, Submission 141 (47th Parliament), p. 2.
6 Australian Army Fire Service, Retired Firefighter's Group, Submission 141 (47th Parliament), pp. 5.

Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 19.
7 Mr McConville, United Firefighters Union of Australia, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 18.

concentrate more frequently than firefighters, whereas the firefighters were
more likely to be exposed to the foam (which is less concentrated)'.8
3.12 Occupational exposure also includes those exposed to PFAS because of their
place of work. For example, air traffic controllers at Avalon Airport were
exposed to PFAS in drinking water in the course of their employment.9
3.13 The committee heard evidence from Mr Christopher Howe and his partner
Ms Jayne Newgreen. Mr Howe worked for a private company which operated
out of a sub-leased building at Avalon Airport. He advised that, by chance, he
became aware of PFAS contamination of the drinking water at the site.10
3.14 Mr Howe estimated he consumed two to three litres of water a day over the
15 months he worked at Avalon. Mr Howe described his exposure to PFAS:
… results show the water I was drinking daily while employed at Avalon
Airport was contaminated with PFOS [perfluorooctane sulfonate] + PFHxS
[perfluorohexane sulfonate] almost 200 times the NHMRC [National Health
and Medical Research Council] 2018 guidelines for safe drinking water
…
Under the proposed new guidelines, laboratory results show the water I was
drinking while employed at Avalon Airport was contaminated with PFOS
at 3,000 times the new guideline, and PFHxS at over 56 times the new
guideline … To think this all could have been easily avoided if I knew the
risk, and simply took my own drinking water to work …11
3.15 Ms Newgreen explained the impacts on Mr Howe and their family. This
included the need for Mr Howe to do much of his own research and advocacy,
without occupational supports:
I understand that there are a lot of other people affected on this Avalon site.
We don't know them; we're not connected and associated. I hope others have
their own peer support or unions or people around them that are supporting

each other. We're very much alone, because there's no structure to help
people who are in a similar boat to us.12
3.16 Similarly, Mr Roger Hart wrote of his time in the Jervis Bay area, as an employee
of the now Booderee Botanic Gardens and as a firefighter, for various periods
between 1979 and 2005. Mr Hart relayed his potential exposure to PFAS as part
of his work operating the irrigation system, including the pump, pipes, and
sprinklers. He described local waterways with run-off containing PFAS after
heavy rain, flowing down from the Jervis Bay airfield causing foaming. Mr Hart
and his colleagues also used PFAS foam during controlled burns to put out
smouldering stumps, logs and roots. Mr Hart would sometimes swim and bathe
in contaminated water at work, causing concern for the potential impacts on his
health.13
Exposure pathways
3.17 PFAS exposure pathways in different populations are not yet fully understood.14
Given some PFAS' longevity in the environment, and their ability to migrate
through soil and water, firefighters and other people working in facilities
contaminated with PFAS face ongoing exposure.15
3.18 Submitters identified a variety of workplaces (and their surrounds) which are
contaminated with PFAS, including current and former fire stations, firefighting
training facilities, airports and Defence facilities.16
3.19 The UFUA Aviation Branch reported that:
Despite [the] use of PFAS-containing fire fighting foams ceasing, aviation
fire fighters remain at high risk of acute PFAS exposure, including through
the water they drink and use at work. This is due to high levels of legacy
contamination arising from decades of use of PFAS foams in and around the
fire stations where they work.17
12 Ms Jayne Newgreen, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 4; Ms Jayne Newgreen,
Submission 92, [p. 6].
13 Mr Roger Hart, Submission 139 (47th Parliament), [pp. 1–2].

3.20 In other instances, the use of PFAS foams in firefighting continues, with new
workers exposed, as discussed later in this chapter.18
Potential impacts of occupational exposure
3.21 As discussed at length in Chapter 2, evidence about the impacts of PFAS on
human health is contested, with the committee hearing a range of views.
3.22 Generally, evidence from firefighters and other exposed groups submitted that
the health impacts of occupational exposure to PFAS and other chemicals are
significant:
Professional firefighters go to work every day with the risk that they will be
exposed to a cocktail of harmful chemicals; exposures that are shown to
significantly impact their health and wellbeing. The impacts vary based on
the type and extent of the exposure, but overall, the physical and
psychological injuries that result are felt by thousands of firefighters in
Australia.19
Physical health effects
3.23 Firefighters told the committee that they are aware of:
… findings [that PFAS] exposure is associated with serious conditions
including reduced immunological function, liver and kidney disfunction,
altered reproductive function and higher cholesterol. They are particularly
concerned about established associations between PFAS exposure and
higher levels of testicular and kidney cancer.20
3.24 The Retired Firefighters Group submitted that:
Today members of our Group are suffering from many PFAS related
diseases including:
 kidney failure
 throat cancer
 testicular cancer
 bladder cancer
 non-Hodgkin lymphoma
 nasopharyngeal carcinoma
 infertility

3.28 The Department of Health's Expert Panel on PFAS notes that knowledge around
PFAS is still evolving and concludes, 'there is no current evidence that supports
a large impact on a person's health as a result of high levels of PFAS exposure'
and 'mostly limited or no evidence for any link with human disease', and, in
addition, that there is 'no current evidence that suggest an increase in overall
cancer risk'.25

3.29 Current Australian Government PFAS guidance still recommends exposure to
PFAS be minimised wherever possible, in line with the precautionary
principle.26
3.30 A number of submitters expressed concern at potential health impacts of PFAS
based on research and conclusions about the safety of PFAS developed overseas
(for example the International Agency for Research on Cancer).27 Submitters
also acknowledged that the scientific understanding of firefighter exposure to
PFAS and potential health impacts is constantly evolving.28
Elevated PFAS blood levels
3.31 It is well recognised that firefighters and other highly exposed groups have
higher levels of PFAS in their blood than the general Australian population.29
However, the Department of Health advised that routine blood testing of PFAS
levels is not recommended because results cannot currently be used to provide
any specific health advice.30
3.32 Blood testing for PFAS levels has been undertaken for certain populations—
including firefighters—for research purposes to determine whether
communities have been exposed at higher than general population levels and,
over time, to determine the success of PFAS exposure reduction strategies in
reducing PFAS blood levels.31

3.33 For example, Fire Rescue Victoria has established regular health monitoring for

To this day, the government tells people not to test for PFAS and that testing

Mental health
3.35 The Australian Government recognises research showing people living in PFAS
affected communities are more likely to experience psychological distress.34
Indeed, the mental health impacts of PFAS exposure on firefighters and other
exposed groups was borne out in evidence received by the committee.
3.36 Mr Tisbury explained the concern felt by firefighters because of PFAS exposure
and the uncertainty associated with their future health.35 Mr Jay Aspros from
the Aviation Rescue and Fire Fighting Service at Avalon Airport spoke of the
worry he felt after PFAS exposure at work, noting some staff still felt unsafe and
were unable to return to work, and others had sought employment elsewhere:
I can personally attest to the stress, anxiety and fear that Avalon staff and I
felt when we were advised that we'd been drinking, bathing in and washing
in water that had PFAS levels 44 times higher than the human health
guidelines … What caused and is still causing the most stress and anxiety is
the fact that we have no information of the following: exactly what we've
been ingesting, how long this has been occurring for, how the chemical got
into our drinking water and where the excessive levels of contamination
were coming from.36
3.37 Mr Howe, who undertakes regular health monitoring on the recommendation
of his health care team, described his fear and anxiety about his future health,
and impacts on his family, saying:

Exposure management strategies and impacts
3.38 Firefighters and other exposed occupational groups discussed a range of
regulatory and management strategies to reduce the current and future impacts
of PFAS exposure associated with firefighting, as well as issues with these
strategies.
Regulation of PFAS in Australia
3.39 Australia does not have nationally consistent legislation prohibiting the
occupational use of PFAS. Regulatory frameworks—particularly in relation to
firefighting foams containing PFAS—vary across state and territory
jurisdictions.38
3.40 While Australian governments have agreed to work towards phasing out
ongoing use or sale of industrial and commercial products containing long-
chain PFAS, at present, the position is that:
Where short-chain PFAS are used in aqueous film forming foam (AFFF),
they should only be used in emergency situations and in accordance with
all relevant regulations. Any releases should be fully contained and wastes
managed in accordance with the PFAS National Environmental
Management Plan (NEMP).39
3.41 In response to the prohibition on the import, manufacture and use of PFAS
chemicals under the Industrial Chemicals Environmental Management
Standard (IChEMS), Mr Tisbury warned that transition periods would still put
firefighters at risk:
Now, there's a lot of publicity about IChEMS. From 1 July, there's going to
be a ban on PFAS-containing foam. It's smoke and mirrors … If you're
currently using PFAS-containing foam, right now, you can keep using it.
You're going to get five years to transition, because, apparently, 25 years
hasn't been long enough; you need longer. And there are some accepted
uses, like emergencies. Who do you reckon goes to the emergencies? It's us.

Who are the ones who keep getting exposed? It's us, all because the industry

3.42 At a state level, to date three states have introduced bans on the use of
firefighting foams which contain PFAS: South Australia in 2018, Queensland in
2019, and New South Wales (NSW) in 2022.41
3.43 Other submitters noted that local exemptions mean that PFAS continues to be
used; for example, in Class B foams used for fighting liquid fuel fires.42
3.44 According to UFUA, a piecemeal approach to regulation has meant that 'PFAS
foams are still being used in operational and training scenarios in some
jurisdictions across Australia'. As a result, the Union states there is 'significant
concern … that the regulatory environment is failing to ensure firefighter and
community safety'.43
3.45 As a national operator, Airservices Australia highlighted its frustration with
inconsistencies in regulation and implementation between jurisdictions, stating:
Airservices has also observed inconsistencies in approach by some
jurisdictions of other non-Commonwealth agencies that have also
historically used PFAS containing foams, who do not appear to be expected
to meet the same timeframes and requirements for management and/or
remediation by their own regulators.44
3.46 PFAS policy, regulation and coordination are further discussed in Chapter 10.
Flow on effects of Commonwealth regulatory treatment of PFAS to the states
3.47 The committee heard evidence that the Australian Government's regulatory
treatment of PFAS has flow on effects to states and territories, particularly in
terms of work health and safety regulation.
3.48 Mr Samuel Jenkin from WorkSafe Victoria (WorkSafe) outlined that, when it
comes to PFAS, the Australian Code for the Transport of Dangerous Goods by Road

& Rail does not classify PFAS as a 'dangerous good', which means that the
Victorian Dangerous Goods Act 1985 also does not recognise PFAS as a
'dangerous good'.45
3.49 Furthermore, Mr Jenkin explained that 'PFAS is [also] not included in the
nationally agreed workplace exposure standards, which are called up directly
by the Victorian legislation'.46
3.50 As a result of this lack of classification, WorkSafe's role as a regulator is reduced
to merely expecting duty holders in a workplace to take a 'precautionary
approach' to the use of PFAS, with little scope to conduct investigations or
enforcement against workplaces suspected of PFAS contamination.47
3.51 Similarly, in a response to questions on notice provided to the committee, the
Tasmanian Department of Health stated that it follows the Australian
Government on the classification of PFAS:
The Department of Health adopts the position of the Australian
Government Department of Health and Aged Care and of its Environmental
Health Standing Committee (enHealth) with regards to the health impacts
of PFAS. The current view is that whilst PFAS have not definitely been
shown to be a cause of disease in humans, enHealth notes that the science
and our understanding of these issues will continue to evolve.48
Workers' compensation legislation and PFAS
3.52 While the link between PFAS and cancer is disputed, since 2011, the Australian,
state and territory governments (except the ACT) have enacted presumptive
workers' compensation legislation that presumes firefighters (or volunteer
firefighters) who develop certain cancers or asbestos related diseases have done
so because of their firefighting duties and are therefore entitled to compensation
under the relevant scheme.49
3.53 The extent of cancers covered and the relevant qualifying periods of
service―that is, the minimum length of time a firefighter must have served to

classified under the globally harmonised system as a 'hazardous substance'.

PFAS exposure and workers not covered by workers compensation schemes
3.54 Despite the presumptive workers' compensation legislation, the committee
heard certain groups of firefighters are not covered in the relevant legislation.
Groups which are not covered by presumptive workers compensation include
private sector firefighters engaged at military bases across Australia,51 as well as
firefighters on Norfolk Island whose workers compensation legislation lacks
any presumption provision.52
3.55 The CPSU also advised that Airservices Australia EVTs are not covered under
the Commonwealth workers' compensation scheme, in the Safety, Rehabilitation
and Compensation Act 1988, despite research indicating they are subject to greater
levels of PFAS exposure than firefighters.53
3.56 The Retired Firefighters Group submitted that the criteria for workers
compensation schemes have been 'inconsistently applied' with some exposed
veterans being excluded despite meeting the relevant qualifications.54
Conditions not covered by workers compensation
3.57 Moreover, the committee heard that the conditions which are presumptively
compensated depends on the jurisdiction in which a firefighter serves.
3.58 For example, NSW, South Australia and Victoria do not provide presumptive
compensation for lung, skin, pancreatic or thyroid cancer, while only
Queensland and the Northern Territory provide presumptive compensation for
asbestos-related disease and liver cancer. Commonwealth legislation does not
provide presumptive compensation for uterine cancer, asbestos-related disease
or liver cancer.55
3.59 The Department of Health confirmed that most firefighters are covered under
state and territory workers' compensation schemes and that no two schemes are
the same. Commonwealth and Australian Capital Territory government
employees are covered under Commonwealth legislation, as are a 'small

Submitters proposals for addressing gaps in workers compensation legislation
3.62 Submitters recommended changes to workers' compensation legislation to
extend the diseases and workers covered. The UFUA called for the committee
to recommend that the Australian Government:
(a) give consideration to the addition of liver cancer to the list of cancers
covered by the Safety Rehabilitation and Compensation Act 1988;
(b) work cooperatively with state and territory governments to facilitate the
inclusion of liver cancer in their presumptive workers compensation
legislation; and
(c) secure presumptive workers compensation rights for firefighters engaged
at defence facilities, most particularly those employed by Ventia.59
3.63 The UFUA Aviation Branch recommended the Australian Government amends
the Employment Act 1988 (Norfolk Island) to incorporate presumptive legislation
for firefighters based in Norfolk Island and that this cover a number of cancers
and diseases, with specified qualifying periods.60

Sector-led mitigation and remediation strategies
3.65 The committee was told that 'the voice of firefighters is an important one, and
one that has played a leading role in advocating for domestic and international
bans, regulation and remediation of PFAS'.62
3.66 As representatives of a community with a vested interest in its own health and
safety, Fire Rescue Victoria has taken the initiative and shown leadership in
world-leading practices and research to address PFAS exposure, writing:
Every PFAS mitigation strategy FRV have implemented has been not only
measurable and successful, but prior to implementation, the 'experts' have
said our goals were impossible to achieve. The fire appliance
decontamination process, the PFAS blood reduction treatment, and the fire
station remediation processes focussing on immobilisation, were all deemed
impossible, until we did them. Never tell firefighters that something is
impossible, because that's what we do every day.63
3.67 This section looks at sector-led mitigation and remediation strategies, including
phasing out PFAS -containing foams, and research and data collection. Evidence
on reducing blood PFAS levels is presented in Chapter 2 of this report.
Phase out of PFAS containing firefighting foams
3.68 The Department of Defence (Defence) started phasing out PFAS-containing
foams in 2004 and no longer uses foams containing perfluorooctanoic acid,
perfluorooctane sulfonate or perfluorohexane sulfonate. It has moved to using
foam which contains only trace amounts of other PFAS and has indicated the
phase out of PFAS foams may take up to 10 years. These PFAS foams are only
used in emergency situations where lives or Defence assets are at risk or in
controlled testing environments, and with pollution control measures.64
3.69 By 2020, Defence had completed the transition of 'all handheld fire extinguishers
to fluorine free foams' and later transitioned all firefighting vehicles in 2022.
However, the program to transition 'fixed infrastructure' that utilises PFAS-
containing firefighting foam—including hangars, fuel installations and 'hot

(received 17 February 2025), [p. 6]; Department of Defence, response to written questions from
Senator Thorpe—questions to Defence (received 17 July 2025), [p. 3].

refuelling' (the process of refuelling an aircraft while its engines are running)—
is still in the planning stages, with Defence estimating it could 'take up to 10
years'. Navy vessels will continue to be equipped with PFAS-containing foams
until the 'United States Navy updates their requirements for inter-operability'.
Defence stated it:
… only uses these products in emergencies where lives and/or Defence
assets are at risk, or in controlled testing situations and not used for training
purposes. Pollution control measures are in place to manage any use of
foam.65
3.70 The Australian Government's position statement on PFAS states that certain
PFAS may need to be used 'until effective and economically feasible non-PFAS
alternatives are developed'.66 Defence is continuing to research the use of
alternative non-PFAS foams and alternate solutions.67
3.71 However, the UFUA contended that:
Despite there being tested and approved PFAS-free alternatives, continued
lobbying efforts from the manufacturers and suppliers of these PFAS foams
have allowed exemptions to be carved into regulation to allow for the
continued sale of these harmful products.68
3.72 Submitters noted that PFAS continues to be used in some Class B foams.69
Firefighters and others recommended a nationwide ban on the use of any
firefighting foam which contains PFAS, including Class B foam extinguishers.70

64
3.73 Mr Tisbury pointed out that Fire Rescue Victoria had successfully transitioned
to PFAS-free alternative foams even for B-class fires:
We wouldn't be advocating for the use of fluorine-free foam if it didn't work,
because we'd be at risk not just of disease but of immediate death … Not
only did we transition a decade ago to the fluorine-free foam and not only
did we do extensive testing; every single B-class fire that we've fought in
Victoria … has been extinguished using only fluorine-free firefighting foam.
So it works.71
3.74 Professor Bogdan Dlugogorski, an expert in fluorine-free firefighting foams,
told the committee that 'fluorine-free foams are approaching the performance of
PFAS-containing foam' and should replace PFAS-containing foams:
In all applications on land, the fluorine-free foams can be employed in
exactly the same situation as the foams that contained PFAS in the past. So
there's no excuse, in my view, not to employ fluorine-free foams on land.
The situation is a little bit different on ships, where there is a requirement to
use salty sea water for generating foams. Those fluorine-free foams are not
yet fully compatible with sea water. Except of that application, we should
be using fluorine-free foams everywhere else.72
3.75 Mr Tisbury disagreed with the suggestion that fluorine-free foams do not work
with saltwater, noting that offshore oil rigs utilise fluorine-free foams, as do
Victorian firefighting services when fighting fires at major hazard facilities on
docks (using saltwater).73
3.76 Professor Dlugogorski noted that fluorine-free firefighting foams still contain
chemical surfactants—just not PFAS. They still have an impact on the
environment. However, unlike PFAS, they biodegrade within 28 days.74
Workplace remediation and innovation
3.77 Fire Rescue Victoria advised 'Major Hazard Facilities and Industrial areas across
the country are heavily contaminated due to the ongoing use and testing of
PFAS containing foams in fire protection systems'. 75
3.78 Firefighters contended that deficiencies in managing risks, significant delays
and failure to remediate PFAS-contaminated work sites 'is creating new and

3.79 Submitters spoke to the contamination at both the Avalon and Norfolk Island

… the failures at Avalon Airport are not an isolated incident. It is
symptomatic of a much wider disregard for worker safety and
environmental accountability in PFAS management. Immediate action is
required to ensure comprehensive site remediation, long-term health
monitoring and policy reform to protect firefighters and Australians from
continued exposure.77
3.80 The CPSU and UFUA recognised the significant remediation actions undertaken
across numerous PFAS-contaminated worksites, while Airservices Australia,
the Department of Infrastructure, and Defence highlighted their ongoing
investigations and remediation of contaminated facilities.78
3.81 Firefighters acknowledged that a combination of technologies, products,
equipment and processes is required to effectively remediate contaminated
sites, with no single solution able to meet the requirements for all sites and
exposure pathways.79

Chapter 6 of this report. Fire Rescue Victoria discussed its work on developing
remediation strategies for fire stations and surrounds, including use of products
and processes which immobilise PFAS onsite, as well as soil treatment and

Research and data collection on exposed groups
3.84 The committee heard of studies and data collected on PFAS-affected occupation
groups, particularly firefighters. Notable studies which the committee received
evidence about included:
 Assessing Effectiveness of PFAS Exposure Control in Exposed Communities and
Firefighters (University of Queensland) which aimed to 'assess the changes
over time in PFAS blood serum concentrations, particularly in those
identified as having elevated serum PFAS concentrations, to investigate the
effectiveness of exposure control and identify factors governing successful
exposure reduction'.83
 Effect of Plasma and Blood Donations on Levels of Perfluoroalkyl and
Polyfluoroalkyl Substances in Firefighters in Australia (Macquarie University)
which conducted a clinical trial of 285 firefighters from Fire Rescue Victoria
and found both blood and plasma donations resulted in significantly lower
PFAS levels.84
 Evaluation of per- and poly- fluoroalkyl substances (PFASs) in Airservices
Australia's Aviation Rescue Fire Fighting Service (ARFFS) Staff (University of

3.85 Despite the various studies that have been conducted on PFAS-affected
workforces, the Cancer Council submitted that research to-date on affected
groups—and PFAS contamination more generally—is limited in its usefulness:
The quality of available research evidence is a significant barrier to
interpretation, with limits in sample size, information about confounders,
and a lack of consistent methodology in primary studies … Studies also
differed in the populations studied, including PFAS manufacturing
workers, communities exposed to contaminated water supplies, and specific
cancer patient populations. These inconsistencies make it difficult to
compare studies and draw robust conclusions.87
3.86 While the Cancer Council approved of the Australian Government's investment
into the potential health effects of PFAS via the National Health Research
Program and the Australian National University's epidemiological study in
PFAS-exposed communities, it recommended that environmental and human
biomonitoring be implemented for the 'general population, and specific groups
at high risk of exposures, such as firefighters'.88
3.87 The Retired Firefighters Group identified limitations in data collection
regarding former firefighters who were exposed to PFAS:
There is a critical lack of historical data on PFAS exposure among Australian
Army firefighters who served from the 1970s through the 1990s … Research
into PFAS-related illnesses among defence personnel remains fragmented
and insufficient. A robust, independent study focused specifically on all
veterans who handled AFFF is urgently needed.89
3.88 In 2019, the NHMRC provided over $10.8 million for nine research applications
through the PFAS 'Targeted Call for Research' (TCR) scheme. The scheme was

Department stated they would need to be 'peer-reviewed, published and

3.90 Further, projects which are funded under the TCR scheme are expected to
conclude by June 2026,92 and there is no indication the scheme will be extended
beyond this date.
Further proposals for regulation and remediation
3.91 Firefighters and those in other occupations with elevated exposure to PFAS
firefighting foams uniformly called for changes to prevent further exposure, to
mitigate legacy and ongoing exposure from contaminated sites, and address
potential health impacts from PFAS foam exposure.
3.92 There were calls for regulatory changes for PFAS. The Cancer Council
recommended that PFAS be recognised as an environmental pollutant 'across
the regulatory spectrum', while Ms Newgreen called for the designation of PFAS
as a 'hazardous substance' under the relevant legislation and for the polluter to
be held accountable for its dispersion.93
3.93 To improve the integration and availability of PFAS contamination information,
and to help workers and other exposed people reduce their exposure and health
risks, Mr Howe and Ms Newgreen called for mandatory reporting of
contaminations and a national public register of PFAS contaminated sites. It was
suggested the register include details of the sites—including those owned or
managed by the Australian, state, territory or local governments, as well as
privately owned or managed land.94

3.94 In recognition of the significant costs of remediation, other submitters advocated
for the establishment of an Australian Government fund to enable the
remediation of severely PFAS-contaminated sites. In particular, UFUA sought
funding to support remediation of current and former fire stations (and
surrounds), firefighting training facilities, airports and water catchments.95
3.95 There were calls from firefighters and others for the Commonwealth to
commence litigation against 3M and Dupont for the alleged harm their products
caused firefighters, other workers, and the environment, as well as for 3M's
alleged false marketing of harmful products.96 It was suggested that monies
from any litigation be used to partly fund the remediation of fire stations,
airports and related facilities.97
3.96 UFUA recommended the Commonwealth establish an expert tripartite panel,
with membership comprised of government, private sector, and unions, with
responsibility for reducing risks to workers, and to advise parties on effective
remediation of PFAS contaminated sites, providing:
… clear, well founded and genuine technical and logistical advice. We
recommend the establishment of an expert tripartite panel by the Australian
Government, to guide parties in addressing PFAS site remediation.
Importantly, the body should have a 'solutions focus', providing resources
and guidance from testing through to remediation.98
3.97 UFUA Aviation Branch recommended that the Department of Infrastructure
assumes full responsibility for remediation and management of PFAS
contamination on and around Norfolk Island airport.99

Addressing health concerns
3.98 There were calls from firefighters and other affected workers for equitable,
funded, long-term health monitoring for those exposed to PFAS at work—in
particular monitoring of blood PFAS levels.100
3.99 Affected workers underlined that access to testing would help them understand
their levels of exposure, mitigate future health risks (for example, through
plasma and blood donation and early detection of potential health issues) and
help address the anxiety associated with exposure.101
3.100 Submitters recommended that workers with elevated PFAS levels be made
eligible to access Medicare benefits for therapeutic plasma/blood donations,
where their blood is not accepted by blood banks.102
3.101 Some submitters went further, requesting access to plasma donation facilities
for those in isolated or regional areas (such as Norfolk Island) and cost
reimbursement for associated costs such as time and travel.103
3.102 Given the work-related nature of their PFAS exposure, and challenges to having
PFAS exposure recognised as an injury under compensation schemes, highly
exposed workers—including former members of the Royal Australian Army
Fire Service—recommended that they be given free and accessible medical and
mental health supports.104
3.103 The Australian Army Fire Service, Retired Firefighter's Group, recommended
that Australia adopt similar practices to those in international compensation
programs, such as health screening and lifetime medical support for all veterans

3.104 Submitters underscored the need for further data collection and funded research
