Chapter 4
Social and ecological impacts of PFAS
contamination
Introduction
4.1 Environmental studies have shown that per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances
(PFAS) are widespread in the Australian environment, including surface water
and groundwater—generally at low levels. However, due to historic industrial
and occupational practices, some environments are affected by significant PFAS
contamination levels. Figure 4.1 below indicates that several parts of Australia
have higher concentrations of PFAS in surface, ground, and drinking waters.
Figure 4.1 Global map of PFAS concentration in water (published 2024)
Source: Ackerman Grunfeld, D., Gilbert, D., Hou, J. et al. Underestimated burden of per- and polyfluoroalkyl
substances in global surface waters and groundwaters. Nat. Geosci. 17, 340–346 (2024).
Note: Sum of concentration of 20 PFAS subject to EU guidance in surface water, groundwater and drinking water
samples. Those above the EU drinking water limit of 100 ng l−1 (marked red on scale bar) are circled in red (for
known contamination sources (for example, AFFF or non-AFFF)) or black (unknown sources).
4.2 This chapter looks at the social and ecological impacts of PFAS contamination
in Australian environments, including impacts on lifestyle and recreation, and
impacts on Australian wildlife and habitats. Impacts on First Nations
communities and culture are considered separately in Chapter 5.
4.3 The chapter then reflects on national regulation and guidance, the collection and
availability of data, and compensation and funding. In doing so, the chapter
summarises evidence received throughout the inquiry and suggestions made by
submitters and witnesses.
73
74
4.4 A discussion of the legislative, regulatory and policy settings around PFAS—
including the Commonwealth's role in national coordination, environmental
monitoring, management, care and remediation of PFAS contamination—is
included in Chapter 10 of this report.
Impacts of PFAS on lifestyle and recreation
4.5 Numerous submitters to the inquiry were concerned about the impact that PFAS
contamination of soil and waterways is having on the Australian way of life.
Residents of areas with heavy contamination face restrictions on what they can
do, what they can grow, and how they can interact with the environment.
Swimmers, campers, sportspeople, hunters and others who interact with the
environment are also affected.
4.6 Cultural studies Professor at the University of Southern Denmark, Dr Charlotte
Kroloekke said the health consequences of PFAS are 'well documented', but the
cultural impacts are less well understood:
… beyond these biological effects, PFAS pollution also threatens cultural
life. It alters how communities live, work and relate to their environments.
It erodes traditions, practices and relationships that are essential to cultural
identity and to both individual and collective wellbeing.1
4.7 The Concerned Waterways Alliance highlighted the impact of PFAS
contamination on waterways that are 'highly valued for recreational activities',
such as the Moorabool, Yarra, Maribyrnong and Latrobe Rivers, and the
Gippsland Lakes, in Victoria. The Alliance stated:
PFAS contamination erodes community trust in water safety and diminishes
the rivers' roles as cultural and social hubs. Indigenous communities, who
have longstanding spiritual connections to waterways, face
disproportionate cultural losses if contamination disrupts traditional
practices.2
4.8 Field & Game Australia discussed the detrimental impacts of PFAS
contamination on the Heart Morass—a property in East Gippsland, Victoria,
popular with hunters. Having purchased the property for a total of $2.1 million,
the organisation's intention was to restore the 'degraded farmland' by creating
a wetland ecosystem which would support native wildlife and recreational
activities, 'including birdwatching, environmental education, and hunting'.
According to Field & Game Australia, PFAS contamination from RAAF Base
East Sale has 'wiped out the property's commercial value', hunters 'no longer
feel safe consuming game harvested on the property due to health advisories',
1 Dr Charlotte Kroloekke, Professor, University of Southern Denmark, Committee Hansard, 10 June
2025, p. 47.
2 Concerned Waterways Alliance, Submission 30 (47th Parliament), [p. 5].
75
and revenue losses from less people paying access fees 'are significant'. Field &
Game Australia maintained:
The local economy has also been affected. Activities like birdwatching,
hunting, and educational programs previously brought business to nearby
towns but have now declined substantially.3
4.9 Field & Game Australia was frustrated at the lack of authoritative public
information about the impacts of PFAS on the environment, and the safety of
interacting with contaminated sites. Mr Lucas Cooke, Chief Executive Officer
(CEO) of Field & Game Australia, noted that media attention on PFAS at the
Heart Morass has led to the cancellation of educational visits to the site. Asked,
'what risk does a daytrip to a wetland site have?', Mr Cooke said there is a lack
of adequate information they can provide to allay the fears of schools and
parents:
We can go and try to pull it off websites and out of scientific papers and that
sort of thing, but, as a parent sitting at home with a permission slip for your
kid to go somewhere, you just recognise: 'Hang on a minute. Heart Morass?
PFAS. I don't want them going there.' There's no nice little thing that goes,
'Yes, they're going to be there, but they're not going to be in the water.
They're not going to be eating the mud,' or, 'Maybe kids will eat a little bit
of the mud, but they're not going to eat a lot of the mud.' A nice educational
thing that actually gives some reassurance to people would be amazing, but
it just doesn't exist at the moment.4
4.10 A number of submitters talked about the impact of PFAS contamination on their
lifestyles. Mr Jim Mustica, a resident of Belmont Western Australia whose
property was impacted by PFAS from a former fire training ground, explained:
The dreams of mum and dad were to create a utopia for the family to all be
able to enjoy, their lives revolved around preserving and preparing produce
grown in the orchard and garden. Chickens were raised for meat and eggs
supplied to family and friends. From the late 60s to around the mid-80s the
fruit trees and produce thrived, this was very pleasing for dad as he enjoyed
following the family tradition of market gardening, we now believe when
the PFAS began entering the surface and groundwater, crops and trees
began to suffer …5
4.11 Years later when the property was being sold to a developer who intended to
build a childcare centre, Mr Mustica conducted testing of the water from the
bore that serviced the property and found elevated concentrations of 'PFOA
between 3.7μg/L to 6.1μg/L, PFOS at 37μg/L to 45μg/L and PFOS+PFHxS
between 64μg/L to 82μg/L' (current advice stipulates that the concentration of
PFOA in drinking water should not exceed 0.2 μg/L). After taking these results
3 Field & Game Australia, Submission 52 (47th Parliament), pp. 1–2.
4 Mr Lucas Cooke, Chief Executive Officer, Field & Game Australia, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025,
p. 13.
5 Jim Mustica, Submission 135 (47th Parliament), [p. 1].
76
to the developer, Mr Mustica noted that the developer ordered his own testing
and eventually abandoned the plan to build a childcare centre on site. The site
was then declared 'possibly contaminated—investigation required'. Two
investigations were undertaken. However, Mr Mustica argued they did not
accurately take into account the daily use of bore water on the property, or the
fact that home-grown produce was consumed and chickens were kept.6
4.12 The Cooperative Research Centre for Contamination Assessment and
Remediation of the Environment (crcCARE), which performs research, develops
technologies and provides policy guidance on PFAS, argued that PFAS
contamination 'has significant social, cultural and economic repercussions'.
crcCARE submitted:
Community anxiety and property devaluation are particularly high in
Australia and globally, impacting cultural and economic values linked to
affected sites. This economic burden will likely persist unless a risk-based
approach is integrated into PFAS management policies.7
4.13 Similarly, CSIRO noted that PFAS contaminated waterways may be 'closed or
restricted from recreational activities such as fishing', and that residents near
contaminated sites often face costs such as paying for alternative water supplies,
or switching from bore water to town water. Affected properties may 'be subject
to depreciation and/or face difficulty in selling and renting', and agricultural
producers in PFAS-affected areas 'can suffer reputational harm and income loss
from being unable to sell or export crops and/or livestock'.8
4.14 The Department of Defence (Defence) acknowledged that communities around
Defence sites affected by PFAS contamination have expressed concerns about
'impacts to their physical health, and wellbeing', restrictions on how they can
use their properties, contamination of drinking water, groundwater, surface
water and the environment, as well as 'reduced property values, both actual and
perceived'. Defence noted these concerns 'are ongoing despite the settlement of
legal claims' for compensation.9
4.15 While acknowledging the ongoing impacts of PFAS contamination on
communities, Defence submitted that its priority is to 'manage, remediate and
monitor PFAS contamination to mitigate the environmental impacts of PFAS
contamination and reduce the pathways that might expose people to
contamination, such as for example, the use of bores'.10
6 Jim Mustica, Submission 135 (47th Parliament), [pp. 2–3].
7 crcCARE, Submission 36 (47th Parliament), p. 8.
8 CSIRO, Submission 22 (47th Parliament), pp. 9–10.
9 Department of Defence, Submission 66 (47th Parliament), [pp. 2–3].
10 Department of Defence, Submission 66 (47th Parliament), [p. 3].
77
4.16 The PFAS Independent review of land uses around Defence bases (Independent
review)—discussed in Chapter 1 of the committee's interim report—
recommended a new national coordinating body work to develop 'additional
policy guidance' to address 'risks associated with food production in the context
of PFAS contamination'. This recommendation was agreed. Action will include
developing new Health Investigation Levels to 'inform risk assessments for the
suitability of relevant land uses'.11
4.17 Progress on the new national coordinating body and the recommendations of
the PFAS Independent review are further considered in later chapters of this
report.
Compensation for affected communities
4.18 Field & Game Australia recommended the development of a 'comprehensive
compensation package that reflects the loss of land value, revenue, and future
conservation opportunities', saying governments should establish a mechanism
to fund and support conservation of PFAS-contaminated land, such as the Heart
Morass. In addition, Field & Game Australia recommended funding be
provided for health monitoring for volunteers and regular site uses, and that
safety and risk assessment protocols be developed for volunteers working in
contaminated sites.12
4.19 crcCARE recommended governments implement 'fair and comprehensive
compensation schemes for affected communities'.13
Impacts of PFAS on Australian environment, ecology, wildlife, and habitats
4.20 Many inquiry participants were concerned about impacts of PFAS
contamination on the environment, ecology, wildlife and habitats. Submitters
highlighted the impacts of PFAS contamination of water supplies, with flow on
effects to soil contamination and ecosystems. Potential negative impacts could
include habitat degradation, biodiversity loss, and escalating toxicity and
ecological harm.14
4.21 Professor Denis Michael O'Carroll, from the University of New South Wales,
said his research demonstrates that 'PFAS is pervasive in the environment',
though current testing only identifies a limited range of PFAS chemicals, so
'there is more PFAS out there that potentially we should be concerned about'.
Professor O'Carroll's research 'found that a significant number of our surface
and groundwaters are at concentrations that would exceed drinking water
11 Govt response to independent review, [p. 12].
12 Field & Game Australia, Submission 52 (47th Parliament), [p. 4].
13 crcCARE, Submission 36 (47th Parliament), p. 23.
14 Dr Ian Wright, Submission 102 (47th Parliament), pp. 4 and 10–11.
78
levels, and the [proposed] new drinking water [Guideline] level for example in
Australia'.15
4.22 The Environmental Defenders Office (EDO) noted that PFAS have been detected
'in soil, household dust, groundwater, wastewater, and even rainfall', and
submitted that PFAS contamination has impacts 'beyond the site and may
remain in the environment over an unknown time period':
PFAS chemicals damage endocrine, reproductive and immune system
health of both humans and wildlife.
… PFAS contamination in land, food and water sources appears to be
increasingly identified across Australian communities.16
4.23 Concerned Waterways Alliance submitted that, because PFAS persist in soils,
groundwater, and surface water, they impact 'groundwater-dependent
ecosystems', interacting with other pollutants to 'amplify toxicity, exacerbating
ecological harm'.17
Wildlife
4.24 Several submitters and witnesses raised concerns about the bioaccumulation of
PFAS in wildlife such as invertebrates, waterbirds and fish, as well as higher-
order predators like ducks, platypus and dolphins.18
4.25 Dr Matt Landos, Director, Future Fisheries Veterinary Service Pty Ltd, argued
that animal studies 'across a whole range of biota', including fish, have identified
detrimental impacts from 'very low exposures during critical life phases':
This is often at the earliest of times, when the cells of the animal are being
organised as an embryo, that exposures can lead to permanent changes in
the trajectory of the health of that animal. It can lead to changes in the
behaviour of that animal that can be biologically important.
… These [studies] are adding evidence to the array of evidence that these
compounds are problematic at phenomenally low exposures when we see
them contacting human or animal cells.19
15 Professor Denis Michael O'Carroll, Professor, University of New South Wales, Committee Hansard,
13 November 2024, p. 18.
16 Environmental Defenders Office, Submission 55 (47th Parliament), pp. 6 and 13.
17 Concerned Waterways Alliance, Submission 30 (47th Parliament), [p. 5].

4.26 Dr Landos added that a major concern is the accumulation of PFAS in predatory
species, such as aquatic birds, which consume contaminated seafood:
We see similar patterns in seals—which was another of the papers that I
provided—such as the endangered Australian sea lion. These are of
significant concern over the way in which the PFAS chemicals accumulated
in an adult can particularly be passed and transferred in exposure to the
young—in seals via maternal milk; in penguins via the egg—leading to
critical exposures to the embryo.20
4.27 Submitters noted the impacts of PFAS contamination on marine ecosystems,
marine mammals, fish, and crustaceans:
 endocrine disruption in seals, dolphins, and other marine mammals;
 reduced fertility and population decline;
 impacts on coral reefs, such as 'reduced growth rates and weakened
structural integrity; and
 bioaccumulation in fish, crustaceans, and other seafood species, 'posing
risks to human consumers and marine food webs'.21
4.28 World Wide Fund for Nature—Australia (WWF—Australia) and the Australian
Marine Conservation Society (AMCS) submitted that exposure to PFAS 'is
having devastating impacts on wildlife as it can cause cancers, birth defects and
infertility, and places already vulnerable populations at greater risk'. According
to WWF—Australia and AMCS, research has identified 600 species at risk of
harm from PFAS, including species in Australia, such as freshwater turtles,
bottlenose dolphin, and platypuses.22
4.29 University of Tasmania researchers, Dr Melanie R Wells and Professor Mary-
Anne Lea submitted evidence regarding 'widespread dissemination of PFAS in
little penguins', which was associated with changes in 'blood parameters of
health'—which could be 'indicative of underlying health issues, such as anaemia
or nutritional deficiency'23—and 'abnormalities in blood cells'. The authors
noted a 'higher amount and number of PFAS detected in eggs' and proposed
that PFAS studies on wildlife should focus on egg-laying species as a priority.24

2025, p. 5.

80
4.30 Dr Wells and Professor Lea noted recently published research on PFAS in tiger
snakes from urban wetlands, and research on dolphins, which describes 'record
breaking high levels of PFAS'. They also noted the PFAS burden detected in
Australia's platypus populations.25
4.31 The committee also heard evidence from Professor Susan Wilson about PFAS
experiments on a bee colony that her team conducted at the University of New
England. The results showed that the PFAS transferred to the next generation
and many of the larvae died off:
We've run a series of experiments from about 2017 onwards to now. In this
most recent experiment, which isn't yet published, there were sublethal
concentrations. We found that the bee colony itself developed. It produced
eggs and larvae that hatched, but there were still concentrations in that
second generation. PFAS had transferred through the generations. Those
larvae just didn't do well. They died off. There were poor hatchling rates, et
cetera. … It was a very significantly different, much less improved hatching
of the offspring compared with the control that wasn't exposed to PFAS.26
4.32 Referring to findings of recent studies demonstrating impacts on metabolic
processes and reproductive success of species, CSIRO suggested PFAS
contributes to population decline. It argued that longitudinal studies around the
effects of PFAS need to incorporate 'environmental PFAS and effects data', as
understanding the impacts of PFAS on the environment 'is crucial':
Although PFAS effects have been demonstrated in short-term
ecotoxicological exposure studies, understanding the effects on aquatic and
terrestrial biota exposed to PFAS throughout their lifetime is limited.
… Additional research is essential to understand how PFAS exposure can
affect Australian wildlife (especially native and culturally significant
species) and ecosystems on a geographical and climatic scale, across
Australian states and territories.27
Fresh, estuarine and marine water habitats
4.33 Submitters and witnesses raised concerns about PFAS contamination of several
fresh, estuarine and marine waterways around Australia.
4.34 For instance, the Concerned Waterways Alliance submitted that PFAS
contamination 'disrupts aquatic and terrestrial habitats', impacting the food
chain, 'leading to cascading ecological effects, and a shift towards more tolerant,
25 Dr Melanie Wells and Professor Mary-Anne Lea, Submission 133 (47th Parliament), [p. 2].
26 Professor Susan Wilson, Professor in Environmental Pollution, University of New England,
Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, p. 19.

81
often invasive, species' in Australia's waterways. Because it impairs
reproduction, PFAS could also lead to biodiversity loss.28
The Heart Morass wetland, Sale, Victoria
4.35 Protection and conservation of wildlife areas in Australia is often dependent on
volunteers. Field & Game Australia submitted that its project at the Heart
Morass, a wetland near Sale in Victoria, relies on volunteers to regenerate the
land. However, PFAS contamination of the area 'raises serious concerns' for the
safety of those volunteers, particularly when handling contaminated water, soil,
or vegetation, or participating in 'clean-up, maintenance, and monitoring
work'.29
4.36 Field & Game Australia explained that the Heart Morass 'was intended to be a
flagship conservation project, enhancing biodiversity, water quality, and
recreational access'. As well as damaging the commercial value of the Heart
Morass, PFAS contamination originating from RAAF Base East Sale has
undermined 'critical conservation work, exposing volunteers to potential long-
term health risks, and limiting our future capacity to purchase and protect
additional land'.30
4.37 Mr Cooke said the discovery of PFAS contamination at the Heart Morass
'derailed' over a decade's work in establishing a sustainable conservation model:
In 2016, the contamination of the RAAF East Sale base was a known hotspot,
but it was confirmed that the only source of outflow of stormwater from that
property is directly onto ours. They have their eastern main drain, which
flows directly into the Heart Morass. It discharges only onto our property,
and the Heart Morass is a terminal wetland, which means the water that
flows onto our property stays on our property except in times of flood or
overflow. The water flows on; the water evaporates, but the PFAS does not.
It doesn't break down. It accumulates in the sediment and the base of our
wetland. With that announcement back in 2016, all of our plans for that
property have basically come to a standstill. We're now at a loss.31
4.38 Mr Cooke also noted that current PFAS testing practices tend to test only the
water. However, PFAS collects in mud and sediment, which is then dislodged
in heavy rain events, infiltrating clean water supplies.32

Estuarine and marine ecosystems at Cobaki and Cairns QLD
4.40 One submitter expressed concern about PFAS contamination from the Gold
Coast Airport's fire training ground impacting the Cobaki coastal lowland
estuarine ecosystems, at the New South Wales (NSW) and Queensland border.
According to the submitter:
Despite groundwater contours/flow vectors from the [fire training ground
(FTG)] being to the south, west and [south-west] to the estuary, the drainage
network downstream of the FTG which discharges to the estuary has never
been sampled on the [Gold Coast Airport] site between the estuary and M1
more than 16yrs on. Also, a further key PFAS exposure pathway to this
ecological and human receptor being the Coolangatta Creek overflow to the
west with another discharge point to the estuary has never been sampled
when surface water PFOS contaminant levels up to 26.4 ug/L were
confirmed 2011, more than 13yrs ago.34
4.41 The North Queensland Airports Group highlighted the critical role of
Queensland's airports in providing access to a number of areas of outstanding
natural beauty and world heritage, including the Great Barrier Reef and Wet
Tropics, and the Daintree Rainforest. Cairns Airport is positioned within 'a
delicate ecosystem considered critical to the maintenance of biodiversity and
cultural values in the bordering Barron River estuary', and Mackay Airport is
'adjacent to the popular Whitsundays tourist destination'.35
Swan river, Perth WA

… states that the lake is a stormwater drain disregarding any environmental
value, and the wildlife living in the area, including the southwestern long
necked turtles. There's no mention of the effect on the Ascot racecourse or
the river system, it's interesting to note that the Swan River dolphins have
been found to have more PFAS in their organs than dolphins in any other
state of Australia. The PFAS levels at the Swan River discharge point
(Belmont South main drain) are 188 times higher than the guideline.36

Botany bay, Sydney NSW
4.44 One submitter expressed concerns about PFAS contamination in Botany Bay,
Sydney, from Sydney Airport, former oil refinery Caltex Kurnell, and Botany
Industrial Park—sites at which PFAS aqueous film-forming foams were
historically used for fire training activities. This submitter was concerned that
inadequate information is available to the public, reports about PFAS
contamination in Botany Bay are 'withheld from public scrutiny', government
agencies will not engage with concerned citizens, and that the NSW
Environmental Protection Authority (EPA) lacks the power or the will to
'actually address PFAS issues'.38
4.45 The submitter maintained that, despite Sydney Airport being a major source of
PFAS contamination for Botany Bay, 'results and data from [Sydney Airport
PFAS] investigations have never been publicly released', and the NSW EPA is
not investigating the site because it is in the Commonwealth's jurisdiction:
The EPA's choice to omit Sydney Airport makes their PFAS investigations
map extremely misleading for people trying to assess risk in their area. It is
a significant site for contamination which makes its omission worse. The
EPA will not provide adequate reasons as to why they will not remedy this
anomaly and include the site together with the words 'not under NSW EPA
jurisdiction'. This EPA map is clearly not being transparent and the EPA is
not being accountable in providing adequate explanation.39
Belubula river, central west NSW
4.46 Dr Ian A. Wright, an Associate Professor at Western Sydney University,
reported that concerned landholders around Australia are conducting their own
PFAS testing. For instance, in 2024 landholders along the Belubula River in
central-west NSW, noted 'large accumulations of foam that were appearing',
collected samples of the foam and river water, and sent them to a commercial

testing laboratory. According to Dr Wright, 'results revealed that the foam
contained an extraordinarily large and hazardous amount of PFAS mixed with

I detected PFAS in the water, sediment and also in large accumulations of
floating foam accumulating on the Belubula River surface and edges. PFAS

water column (median 20 ng/L). The Belubula River is a high conservation-

… given that the Belubula River is an endangered ecological community
(Murray Cod and platypus habitat) it is appropriate that known or potential
PFAS and other impacts from the mining operations are thoroughly
investigated. Currently the PFAS pollution in this river is serious—but the
nature, source and magnitude of the pollution are unknown.41
Medlow Bath, Blue Mountains NSW
4.48 Similarly, STOP PFAS Blue Mountains was concerned about the impact of PFAS
contamination on the wildlife and marine life in Medlow Bath, NSW,
recommending that the Commonwealth Government mandates PFAS testing in
affected waterways across Australia (including waterways used for recreation
and those essential to ecological health), and governments at all levels assess the
potential to clean up contaminated sites:
As a result of the 1992 petrol tanker crash, we still have very high levels of
PFOS near Adams Creek at 2,200 ng/L. But we're not sure that anything is
happening to protect the wildlife and marine life that live in these PFAS
contaminated waterways in Medlow Bath NSW.42
Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality
4.49 The Australian and New Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality
provide guidance on managing PFAS contamination in fresh, estuarine and
marine water. It contains recommended Default Guidelines Values for

managing a range of contaminants, with guidelines in development for PFOS

4.50 In relation to water testing, the Water Services Association of Australia
confirmed that it was 'not aware of a nationwide co-ordinated approach to
survey or monitor for PFAS in waterways across Australia', but that some
coordinated monitoring is done in relation to the Australian and New Zealand
Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality.44
4.51 In some cases, testing for PFAS has been conducted at specified sites due to
known contamination, such as at Dutson Downs, from an explosion and fire at
the Esso Longford gas plant in 1998—an event 'recognised to be a primary
contributor of PFAS on and in the environment downstream'. Testing
commenced in 2017, PFAS levels before this time are unknown.45
4.52 Friends of the Earth Australia has suggested Dutson Downs may be a major
source of PFAS pollution into nearby waterways, including Lake Coleman and
Lake Reeve.46
4.53 EPA Victoria reported that it issued a Clean Up Notice to Esso Longford Pty Ltd
in July 2017, 'requiring an environmental audit that addressed contaminated
soil, sediment, surface water and groundwater on and from the premises'. The
audit was completed in 2020 and is publicly available. EPA Victoria also noted
that PFAS contamination at the site 'is attributable to long term fire fighting
training rather than solely the 1998 fire'.47

Australia and internationally around the potential environmental impacts of

4.55 The Alliance noted that a NSW Chief Scientist and Engineer report, first

PFAS within synthetic turf', including that concentrations of PFAS would be
'highly variable from each individual field', due to differences in materials and
manufacturing processes.49
4.56 Community lobby group, Friends of Callan Park, was also concerned about

life [synthetic turf] poses risks to marine and terrestrial creatures and their
environments'. In addition, Friends of Callan Park argued that installing
synthetic turf at Callan Park 'would be destructive of the cultural and social
values' of the park.50
4.57 The Independent review into the design use impacts of synthetic turf and public open
spaces made a number of recommendations aimed at better managing approval
and funding of synthetic turf fields; managing pollutant 'run-off' and 'walk-off'
risks; planning for 'end of life' and disposal of existing synthetic turf fields;
better data collection; and the implementation of a research program to better
understand human health and environmental impacts.51
4.58 The Alliance was concerned that the Federal Government 'has failed to
undertake or initiate any of the recommendations outlined' in the 2022 report,
despite the fact that the report:
… clearly identifies the significant risks and unknowns that surround the
impacts to human health, the environment and future generations given the
identification of PFAS contaminates; via leachates, pollutants and airborne

Research, monitoring, and testing
4.60 Several submitters pointed to the importance of further research, monitoring,
and testing of a range of environments to improve our knowledge and
understanding of the impacts of PFAS on environments, ecology, wildlife, and
habitats.
More research into the impacts of PFAS on ecosystems
4.61 There were various calls for more research into the impacts of PFAS on
ecosystems, and the mechanisms of transport:
Defining the mechanisms of PFAS transport through the atmosphere, soils,
and surface and groundwaters … The government has a number of
mechanisms to promote this research, including targeted grants, direct
commissioning of research, reviews of the existing PFAS literature,
collaboration with researchers and industry and/or employment of
government researchers.54
4.62 CSIRO submitted that further research is critical to understanding how PFAS
exposure affects Australian wildlife—'especially native and culturally
significant species'—and ecosystems across all Australian states and territories.55
CSIRO recommended the establishment of Australian PFAS 'anthropogenic
background levels in media (i.e., soils, waters, atmosphere and biota) across
geographic and climatic conditions to support site/regional risk assessments
and clean-up levels', proposing governments:
Expand research on Australian wildlife (especially native and culturally
significant species) including sub-lethal exposures and beyond traditional
endpoints by employing systems biology (multi-omics) techniques that
encompass comprehensive resilience, fitness, and health metrics.56

National monitoring program
4.64 The Australian Academy of Technological Sciences and Engineering
recommended the Commonwealth develops 'a standardised national
framework for monitoring PFAS levels across all states and territories based on
legally binding guidelines'.58
4.65 A similar recommendation was made by the Australian Academy of Science,
which called for a strategic national monitoring program to determine the extent
of PFAS contamination across the country. Such a program would assess
environmental and human risk and inform effective management and
remediation efforts. Supporting this recommendation, Dr Hayley Teasdale,
Head of Science Advice and Policy at the Australian Academy of Science said
the time has come for PFAS research in Australia to transition from 'academic
science' to 'regulatory science', which is concerned with 'providing certainty for
regulatory decisions':
It's not intended as a generic call for more funding for research; rather, it is
an investment in government scientific infrastructure to fulfil the
government's responsibilities towards its citizens. … building an evidence
base that's consistent, reliable and able to create informed decision-making
based upon it, whether that's regulatory decision-making or other decision-
making, is very critical at this point.59
4.66 Likewise, crcCARE recommended establishing 'a national PFAS monitoring
program to track contamination levels and trends', improved data sharing and
coordination between federal, state and local agencies, and enhanced public
access to PFAS contamination data and information.60
4.67 Dr Nathan Kilah, Senior Lecturer in Chemistry, University of Tasmania,
Australian Academy of Science, referred to the PFAS map created and
maintained by Friends of the Earth, which collates information about PFAS
contamination across Australia from media reports, FOI requests, and

4.70 STOP PFAS Blue Mountains recommended that, where PFAS contamination is

consistent with the National Environment Protection Measure 1999', to 'help inform

4.73 Defence submitted that it has collected 'a significant data set from sampling on

that residents can request 'property identification be kept private'; in this case,

contaminated sites across the Defence estate'.67
4.74 Asked if the government has considered creating a database or map of PFAS

of Defence; Department of Infrastructure; Airservices Australia; Department of
the Environment; Department of Finance; and Department of Industry, Science
and Resources. Implementing such a map would require, 'amongst other things:
an agreed lead agency and website host; data governance; IT design and build;
and access and sharing arrangements between Commonwealth and State
government agencies'.68

Chapter 5
Impact of PFAS on First Nations communities,
lands and waters
Introduction
5.1 In its interim report, the committee presented evidence of the substantial
impacts of PFAS contamination from Defence facilities on the community and
environment at Wreck Bay, in the Jervis Bay Territory on Australia's South-East
coast. Wreck Bay provides a striking example of the unique impacts of PFAS on
Australia's First Nations communities. Other Aboriginal communities that have
been substantially impacted include Williamtown (New South Wales (NSW)),
Oakey (Queensland) and Katherine (Northern Territory).
5.2 This chapter summarises evidence received during the course of the inquiry on
the unique and specific impacts of PFAS contamination on First Nations
communities, lands and waters. It also presents evidence from academics,
research and science organisations about the need for more investment and
effort in culturally sensitive research methodology and remediation practice.
Unique and significant impacts
5.3 There are First Nations communities in many locations impacted by PFAS
contamination. In these areas, First Nations people are often disproportionately
impacted due to restrictions on traditional and cultural practices, such as
swimming, birthing on Country, hunting, gathering bushfoods and fishing.
These practices have historically led to higher exposure to PFAS among First
Nations people. While the experiences of different communities vary, impacts
are often further compounded by socio-economic factors, including lower
incomes, geographical isolation, poorer access to healthcare services, education,
and the impacts of racial discrimination.1
5.4 The National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation
(NACCHO) noted that, along with increased exposure through traditional
practices, Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities 'may face
compounded risks [of PFAS contamination from consumer goods] due to
limited access to safe alternatives, higher reliance on second-hand goods, and
poor housing conditions that increase indoor contamination'.2
1 crcCARE, Submission 36 (47th Parliament), p. 10.
2 National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation, Submission 17 (48th Parliament),
p. 8.
91
92
5.5 According to NACCHO, traditional practices such as fishing, hunting and
gathering bush foods 'are central to cultural identity and wellbeing' for many
Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples. In this context, NACCHO
described the PFAS contamination in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander
communities, including Wreck Bay and Katherine, as a 'crisis', saying PFAS is
'not only an environmental and health issue, but also a stark example of systemic
exclusion'.3
5.6 NACCHO highlighted the psychological toll of PFAS contamination on Country
and sacred sites:
The trauma of environmental harm especially when it affects land and water
central to cultural identity has led to calls for culturally safe health
interventions, long term monitoring, and justice for affected communities.
… Culturally, the damage is deeply felt as traditional practices like fishing,
gathering bush medicine, and teaching children on Country have been
severely restricted, leading to a loss of cultural transmission and identity.4
5.7 NACCHO also observed that, while the Department of Defence (Defence) has
polluted 'land and water central to cultural practices' for decades, community
members have been excluded from decision-making processes and 'denied
timely health interventions':
This is a form of environmental racism—the disproportionate exposure of
racialised communities to environmental hazards, often due to systemic
neglect, lack of consultation, and unequal access to remediation and justice.
… Racism also manifests in the slow pace of government response,
inadequate compensation, and failure to recognise the cultural significance
of the land and water that has been contaminated.5
Testimony from Wreck Bay
5.8 Aboriginal people from Wreck Bay described the devastating impacts that PFAS
contamination from the Jervis Bay Range Facility has had—and continues to
have—on their community, including:
 prohibitions on traditional and cultural practices;
 restrictions on eating bushfoods, fish and crustaceans;
 prohibition on the cultural and recreational use of waterways, as well as the
use of plants and sediments for cultural purposes;
 a sense of profound loss and grief;
 a sense of being disconnected from their land;
3 National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation, Submission 17 (48th Parliament),
p. 4.
4 National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation, Submission 17 (48th Parliament),
p. 7.
5 National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation, Submission 17 (48th Parliament),
p. 5.
93
 fear and uncertainty about the health impacts of PFAS, including on
children;
 frustration around perceived poor consultation from the Defence; and
 a strong desire for self-determination in relation to the future of their lands
and waters.6
5.9 Their testimony is presented in detail in the committee's interim report and
briefly summarised below.
5.10 At the public hearing in Nowra, committee members heard that Wreck Bay
people consider themselves part of the ecosystem. Wreck Bay Traditional
Owner, Mr Clive Freeman, said that when the environment changes, 'we
change': 'If it changes too quickly and we can't keep up, that's what constitutes
the loss, because we can't keep up with the change'.7
5.11 Ms Annette Brown, Chairperson of the Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community
Council (WBACC), explained that the people's cultural practices 'relate directly
to land', including hunting, gathering, fishing and swimming. High levels of
PFAS in critical areas, such as Mary Creek and Mary Beach (otherwise known
as Bullock's Hoof) mean that parents can no longer 'take [their] kids on country'.8
5.12 Mrs Coral Ardler Yuke, who grew up in Wreck Bay when it was a Christian
mission, described the deep loss she feels that her grandchildren and great-
grandchildren 'have to live on this land that's been poisoned'. She asked how
older members of the community can pass on cultural knowledge when the
environment is contaminated.9
5.13 Mr Freeman pointed out that the precautionary message from government has
had as big an impact as the PFAS contamination itself, with community forced
to change many of its most important practices to minimise risk of exposure,
even while governments remain unsure of the health impacts.10
5.14 Community members, including Mr Darren Brown, highlighted the deep
archaeological and historical significance of the PFAS-affected areas. Mary
Creek—which is directly impacted by contaminated run-off from Jervis Bay
Range Facility—is the site of ancient axe grinding grooves which are over a
6 Senate Select Committee on PFAS (per and polyfluoroalkyl substances), Interim report, March 2025,
pp. 77–93.
7 Mr Clive Freeman, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 8.
8 Ms Annette Brown, Chairperson, Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community Council, Committee Hansard,
21 January 2025, p. 7.
9 Mrs Coral Ardler Yuke, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 3.
10 Mr Clive Freeman, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 8.
94
thousand years old. Mary's Beach, at the bottom of the creek, is another sacred
site where a number of significant cultural artefacts have been found.11
5.15 Community members questioned what the future would look like for their
children, living under the shadow of the contamination. Mrs Ardler Yuke said:
How are they going to sort out this problem that we have in our place for
our kids? We want our kids to grow up and live the way we grew up: loving
their place, enjoying our community, having pride in their place and being
able to do the things that we did and that we can show them. But we're
restricted in a lot of that stuff.12
5.16 Severely restricted in their ability to access their land and practice their culture,
Wreck Bay people sought compensation for cultural loss, with WBACC
launching a class action against the Commonwealth in 2021. In 2023, the Federal
Court of Australia approved a $22 million settlement and, shortly after,
approved the Settlement Distribution Scheme, which outlined how the funds
would be distributed. $5 million was used to pay legal fees to Shine Lawyers, a
significant portion went to WBACC, and the rest was distributed among
community members, with most receiving a few thousand dollars.13
5.17 Community members generally felt that the compensation was deeply
inadequate and that the Commonwealth had failed to recognise the profound
loss suffered by the community. Many community members objected to the
settlement, asking 'is that all our culture is worth?'. However, WBACC and
Shine Lawyers explained that, under Australian law, there is no formal
recognition of Aboriginal culture, and no way for the courts to value cultural
loss. As such, they believed the settlement obtained was the best possible
outcome under the circumstances.14
5.18 With the court case over, WBACC and Wreck Bay community members turned
their attention towards improving the remediation program. One concern
outlined by community members was poor communication and consultation
from Defence and other agencies. Committee members also noted the history of
infrequent community engagement and the inadequate and poorly designed
warning signage at Mary Creek and Mary Beach.15
11 Mr Darren Brown, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 7.
12 Mrs Coral Ardler Yuke, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 3.
13 Senate Select Committee on PFAS (per and polyfluoroalkyl substances), Interim report, March 2025,

5.19 In evidence provided to the committee, Defence described its efforts to improve
communication and increase the role of Wreck Bay people in testing and
remediation. However, community members were distrustful of government
broadly, and Defence specifically, with some believing and pointing to evidence
that suggested Defence and the Commonwealth Government had known about
the dangers of PFAS for decades before they informed the community.16
5.20 Confusing messages from government about the potential health effects of PFAS
have increased the sense of distrust among community members, who
described feeling as if they have little control or input into how the
contamination is managed. Ms Annette Brown said that the community does
not 'want to be fed second-hand information' and wants to be 'at the table',
monitoring the contamination, monitoring the health of children, and ensuring
they have a role in designing solutions. She encouraged the Commonwealth to
bring all the agencies involved in administering Jervis Bay Territory together at
the table with Wreck Bay people to find solutions that genuinely work for the
community.17
Committee’s interim report proposals and recommendations
5.21 In its interim report, the committee outlined a number of proposals from Wreck
Bay community members for reducing the negative impacts of PFAS
contamination on their culture, lifestyle, and well-being. These included: more
meaningful and genuine consultation with Aboriginal communities,
empowering them in the process of managing their country; funding programs
to train local community members to conduct monitoring and remediation
activities; and consideration of novel responses to contamination that allow First
Nations people to stay on Country while reducing the risk of further exposure.18
5.22 The committee recommended Defence and other agencies improve consultation
and cooperation with the community at Wreck Bay. The committee also
recommended that the Parliament considers referring an inquiry to the Joint
Standing Committee on the National Capital and External Territories which
examines governance, representation, and service provision in Jervis Bay
Territory (including the Wreck Bay Aboriginal community settlement).19
5.23 As discussed in Chapter 6 of this report, Defence provided evidence that it is
working to establish greater trust with the community, including by consulting
16 Mr Clive Freeman, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 21 January 2025, p. 6.
17 Ms Annette Brown, Chairperson, Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community Council, Committee Hansard,
21 January 2025, pp. 3 and 15.

pp. 89–91.
19 Senate Select Committee on PFAS (per and polyfluoroalkyl substances), Interim report, March 2025,
pp. 71 and 93.

on more appropriate signage, training local rangers to undertake testing and
surveillance of the waterways, and meeting with the community more often.20
5.24 The government has yet to formally respond to the committee's interim report.
5.25 Further recommendations, and the committee's views about Wreck Bay, are
included in the final chapters of this report.
Testimony from Katherine
5.26 Other Aboriginal communities heavily impacted by PFAS contamination
include the communities around the Katherine River, in the Northern Territory,
where PFAS contamination from RAAF Base Tindal has led to restrictions on
drinking local water, eating wildlife, and using the land and waters.
5.27 According to Aboriginal Medical Services Alliance NT (AMSANT)—peak body
for Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Services in the Northern
Territory—and Katherine-based Wurli Wurjlinjang Health Service (AMSANT
and Wurli Wurjlinjang), this contamination 'has created a set of challenges that
extend beyond the immediate health risks'. These include concerns around 'food
security, chronic disease risk, cultural continuity, and economic pressures'.21
5.28 AMSANT and Wurli Wurjlinjang highlighted the prohibitive cost of store-
bought foods in Katherine and the importance of consuming traditional foods
to health and chronic disease outcomes in Aboriginal communities, saying:
… bushfoods remain a staple of Aboriginal communities, including in the
Katherine region … By their disproportionate reliance on traditional bush
foods in the affected areas around Tindal, Aboriginal communities are
redirected to a greater consumption of cheaply available junk (calorie dense,
high sugar, high salt and high fat) food, given the higher costs of a Healthy
Food Basket in rural and remote areas.22
5.29 With a reliance on local crustaceans and fish, including barramundi, catfish and
black bream, First Nations communities are uniquely impacted by the PFAS
contamination of the natural waterways around Katherine. The health services
noted concerns about food crops irrigated by contaminated water and observed:
The risk to Aboriginal communities also exists in potential fishing from the
Katherine River between Donkey Camp Weir and Daly River, and Tindal
Creek. This was further confirmed by a Food Standards Australia and New
Zealand [FSANZ] report released in 2018 which detailed the appropriate
tolerable daily intakes of 12 fish samples from the Daly River. … Overall,
the report indicated liver and flesh consumption over time based on the
small sample is unlikely to present a public health or safety concern.
20 See Chapter 6.
21 Aboriginal Medical Services Alliance Northern Territory and Wurli Wurlijang Aboriginal Health
Corporation, Submission 13 (48th Parliament), p. 3.
22 Aboriginal Medical Services Alliance Northern Territory and Wurli Wurlijang Aboriginal Health

However, FSANZ recommended because of the uncertainty of health
concerns about PFAS that people in the affected area limit their fish
consumption to a few serves a week and even more so in children and
pregnant women.23
5.30 NACCHO noted that PFAS contamination 'threatens traditional food sources',
including finfish and crustaceans, 'fruits and roots gathered from land near
Defence sites, and hunted animals like kangaroos and goannas that may ingest
PFAS through water or vegetation'. This poses 'serious health risks' and disrupts
cultural practices which are central to 'community wellbeing and cultural
continuity'.24
5.31 NACCHO explained that around 17 per cent of Aboriginal and Torres Strait
Islander people already experience food insecurity, which can affect mental
health, childhood development and learning, and increase the risk of diseases
like type two diabetes and cardiovascular disease. These diseases account for
80 per cent of the mortality gap between Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander
people and other Australians.25
5.32 According to AMSANT and Wurli Wurjlinjang, initial consultations between
Aboriginal stakeholders, Defence, and Northern Territory Health failed to 'feed
into the development of health promotion materials and signage to prevent
excess consumption of at-risk bushfoods'. As such, the health services reported
anecdotal evidence of 'local and itinerant community members fishing and
obtaining volumes of bush foods over-and-above' the recommended levels.
They noted:
The attempt to reduce consumption of PFAS exposed food sources among
Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities around the Katherine
River is a complicated request to make when these communities already
experience a lack of fresh and healthy food options.26
5.33 AMSANT and Wurli Wurjlinjang acknowledged that Defence has provided
alternative water supplies, remediation works and treatment of the Katherine
River. However, they submitted that 'it remains uncertain whether the short and
long term effects of any existing PFAS are within traditional bush foods'. They

2018 PFAS Health Study—views from Katherine focus groups
5.34 The PFAS Health Study, conducted by the National Centre for Epidemiology
and Population Health at the Australian National University (ANU), included a
series of focus group discussions in Oakey (Queensland), Williamtown (NSW),
and Katherine (Northern Territory), held between January and August 2018.
The ANU published a focus groups report in March 2019, which incorporates
the views of 69 participants from Aboriginal communities in Katherine.
Participants expressed many similar views to those outlined by Wreck Bay
community members, including that they:
 were concerned about the potential health risks from exposure to PFAS,
particularly for children;
 were worried about PFAS aggravating existing health conditions and
possibly causing cancers;
 experienced 'psychological stress and anxiety' as a result of living in an area
with PFAS contamination, along with 'uncertainty about health and
financial implications'; and
 would like 'greater transparency and support in their interactions with
government representatives'.28
5.35 Aboriginal focus groups in Katherine represented the three main Aboriginal
groups in the area; the Dagoman, Jawoyn and Wardaman communities.
Aboriginal focus group participants, who were primarily women, reported that
there was 'a lot of sick people in the community' and a lot of people dying 'too
young'. Some suggested a link between PFAS and cancer, or other poor health
outcomes.29
5.36 PFAS blood testing had been provided via a free, voluntary blood testing
program available through the Wurli-Wurlinjang Aboriginal Health Service.
The health service reported 'a small but steady trickle' of people having blood
tests. However, participants in the focus groups suggested the testing was
inaccessible, as getting into town is difficult, and blood testing should be
organised on-site, in communities.30
27 Aboriginal Medical Services Alliance Northern Territory and Wurli Wurlijang Aboriginal Health
Corporation, Submission 13 (48th Parliament), p. 5.
28 National Centre for Epidemiology and Population Health, Australian National University College
of Law, Governance and Policy, PFAS Health Study: Reports (accessed 28 August 2025).
29 National Centre for Epidemiology and Population Health, Australian National University, Research
Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, pp. 32–33.
30 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 33.
99
5.37 Researchers from the ANU's National Centre for Epidemiology and Population
Health reported their view that Aboriginal communities in Katherine 'appeared
confused and uncertain' about pathways to PFAS exposure and the effects of
PFAS. However:
The one item of information that most people were aware of, was that they
should only eat one fish a day because the river and the fish in it were
contaminated by PFAS. They understood that it may be detrimental to their
health if they consumed greater quantities than this. Participants described
PFAS as though it was an infection that people could catch from contact with
river water or river food products.31
5.38 The researchers noted that this precautionary advice had 'considerable cultural
and economic significance', as people had previously fished frequently in the
river, and often consumed 'large quantities and varieties' of fish and other
aquatic animals. Community members did not seem to understand that certain
fish may be safer to eat than others and appeared to believe that PFAS was like
an illness they 'could catch' by eating infected wildlife.32
5.39 Community members reported spending a lot more money on supermarket
food to replace foods they were now advised to avoid or limit, such as fish and
game. At that time, many community members also reported buying bottled
water.33
5.40 The researchers noted that local people seemed unclear on whether their tap
water was safe to drink and whether it was safe to use river water and native
plants to make medicines. Researchers also noted a possible move away from
traditional diets, with focus group participants reporting an increase in eating
red meat from the supermarket.34
5.41 Participants in the ANU study expressed 'a strong attachment to, and concern
for, their country', saying those who caused the PFAS contamination had
'damaged the country' and 'poisoned the river'. Even though the river was
described as 'central to many of their activities', people felt uncertain about
swimming in the river, including whether it was safe for children and whether
they should open their eyes under the water.35
5.42 While the Departments of Defence and Health had delivered a number of
information sessions, Aboriginal focus group participants reported that they did
not feel comfortable attending these sessions. As a result, the researchers
observed that Aboriginal community members 'did not appear to have the same
31 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 33.
32 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 33.
33 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 34.
34 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, pp. 33–34.
35 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 34.
100
level of detailed and specific knowledge that many general community
members had obtained' about PFAS.36
5.43 As was the case in Wreck Bay, the Katherine focus group participants were
distrustful of Defence, questioning why it took so long for government agencies
to inform the community about the risks.37
5.44 At the time of the Katherine focus groups (2018), many community members
reported 'high levels of anxiety and stress'. Some reported feeling 'stuck' or
trapped and unable to get away from the contamination. Others wanted to stay
on their land and sought remediation or decontamination of that land to make
it safe. Many wanted greater certainty, and 'clear information' about the health
effects of PFAS, and 'greater transparency in government's response' and,
similar to Wreck Bay, many participants 'wanted to feel as though government
agencies were listening to them and treating them with respect'.38
5.45 Among other observations, the ANU study concluded that:
… the contamination of the environment with the PFAS chemicals, and the
subsequent prolonged and complex response from authorities, may have
had a pronounced psychological impact on members of the community.
Participants observed that they experienced substantial stress and anxiety
related to the duration of the PFAS contamination, delay in notification,
perceived lack of transparency and poor communication of risk, and
uncertainty of potential health effects.39
Findings of the Defence Independent Review
5.46 The Independent Review of land uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS
contamination, dated March 2024 (Defence Independent Review), was led by Mr
Jim Varghese AM (the independent reviewer) and considered land use options
around Defence bases with a view to assisting people and businesses who have
been affected by PFAS contamination.40 The report made numerous
observations and recommendations relating to the Commonwealth's
engagement with First Nations communities on PFAS.
5.47 The Independent reviewer noted that PFAS contamination in Australia 'has
typically been managed through the lens of environmental contamination',
while impacted communities generally view it 'through the lens of potential
human health impacts'. Mr Varghese maintained that '[m]anaging PFAS
36 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 34.
37 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 35; See also:
National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation, Submission 17 (48th Parliament),
p. 5.
38 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, pp. 35–36.
39 ANU, Research Report—PFAS Health Study: Focus Groups Study, February 2019, p. 37.
40 Defence, PFAS Independent Review (webpage) (accessed 19 September 2025).
101
contamination as a health issue would likely see quite different considerations
about how communities are supported'.41
5.48 As part of the review, the independent reviewer met with Traditional Owner
groups face-to-face to discuss responses to PFAS contamination around RAAF
Base Williamtown (NSW), the Army Aviation Centre Oakey (Queensland) and
RAAF Base Tindal. As was the case in 2018 with the ANU study, the
independent reviewer observed that First Nations people had varying levels of
understanding about PFAS exposure, with the community at Williamtown
considerably better informed than the others. The reviewer speculated that
enhanced understanding of PFAS in Williamtown was likely a result of the
'strong relationship' between the Traditional Owners and the local Defence
Indigenous Liaison Officer.42
5.49 The independent reviewer reported First Nations communities were seeking
'culturally-appropriate and tailored advice about the environmental and health
impacts of PFAS to Country that is significant to them'. Community groups near
Katherine talked about the need to protect cultural heritage sites 'associated
with songlines' near RAAF Base Tindal and told the reviewer they had offered
'to work with Defence to provide training in understanding and respecting
culture'.43
5.50 The independent reviewer recommended that Defence conducts 'bespoke
sampling of culturally significant flora and fauna', and collaborates with
Traditional Owners 'to provide advice on potential impacts of PFAS
contamination on cultural practices, and options for mitigating those impacts',
also saying:
Defence should conduct environmental PFAS sampling to provide
Indigenous communities with an increased understanding of contamination
in local soil, surface water, groundwater and biota; and work with
state/territory authorities to provide culturally appropriate information (for
example in relevant language) on how the results relate to cultural needs.44
5.51 Finally, the independent reviewer suggested that some PFAS-contaminated
land is unlikely to be able to be remediated in a way that would enable
unrestricted use of that land. The reviewer suggested there are 'opportunities
for repurposing of PFAS-impacted land' and suggested that the government

5.52 The independent reviewer noted that:
Traditional Owners across the three impacted communities emphasised the
importance of their connection to Country. They emphasised a desire for
any future development around the bases to include Indigenous cultural
and business opportunities that are compatible with the contamination
status of the land, and that could benefit their communities.46
Other submitter views
5.53 A number of other submitters noted the disproportionate impact of PFAS
contamination on First Nations people and communities. For instance, CSIRO
submitted that impacted Aboriginal communities 'can feel a disconnection to
Country, uncertainty around long-term health outcomes, access to culturally
significant sites, waters, and foods'. Toxics Free Australia argued that 'the
consumption of fish contaminated with PFAS levels harmful to human health
represents an environmental injustice and violates the rights of First Nations
people'.47
5.54 Environmental campaigner, Ms Tracey Anton, maintained that First Nations
communities 'need to know government are taking contamination of the
waterways seriously and [are] prepared to start prioritising the health of people
and environment'.48
5.55 Professor Susan Wilson, from the School of Environmental and Rural Science,
University of New England, described First Nations communities as 'critical
stakeholders' in PFAS management, saying 'their involvement and knowledge
transfer is essential'. Professor Wilson provided an example of a successful
government partnership with local communities in Kakadu National Park:
… to understand and manage risk of PFAS in aquatic bush foods in Kakadu
National Park downstream of Jabiru township and Ranger Mine. Key to the
program success was trust, transparency, open lines of communication and
deep involvement of indigenous communities in all aspects of the program
from inception to completion.49
45 Jim Varghese AM, Independent Review of land uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS
contamination, March 2024, p. 66.
46 Jim Varghese AM, Independent Review of land uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS
contamination, March 2024, p. 62.
47 CSIRO, Submission 22 (47th Parliament), p. 9; Toxics Free Australia, Submission 72 (47th Parliament),
[p. 3].
48 Tracey Anton, Submission 128 (47th Parliament), p. 13.
49 Professor Susan Wilson, Submission 84 (47th Parliament), p. 3.
103
5.56 The Environmental Defenders Office (EDO) noted that Australia's national
PFAS policy framework, the PFAS National Environment Management Plan
(NEMP), recommends that assessment and remediation of PFAS-contaminated
sites be conducted with 'due regard … to sites of cultural or spiritual
significance, in particular, the significance that Indigenous people attach to
land'. However, corresponding state-based legislation does not always reflect
this recommendation—for instance, it is absent from the Contaminated Sites Act
2003 (WA).50
5.57 La Trobe University argued that current laboratory-based PFAS analysis is slow
and costly, creating a barrier for communities to conduct their own monitoring
and screening, at the local level. La Trobe suggested that governments invest in
the development of 'fit-for-purpose', same-day, in-field screening technologies,
which would enable communities, including Indigenous communities, to
quickly identify PFAS hotspots and track environmental contamination levels.51
5.58 Dr Charlotte Kroløkke, a professor in cultural studies from the University of
Southern Denmark, described PFAS pollution as a threat to cultural life, saying:
'It alters how communities live, work and relate to their environments. It erodes
traditions, practices and relationships that are essential to cultural identity and
to both individual and collective wellbeing.'52
5.59 Dr Kroløkke argued that PFAS, and 'pollution in general', has done specific and
disproportionate damage to Indigenous communities and First Nations people:
I'm saying that in part because of the relationship that Indigenous
communities have with the land and country upon which they live.
Contamination of land and country has severe effects on the communities
that live upon this land and country where their ancestors have also lived
and where they have a cultural history of performing traditions and being
connected to land. I think not only do we have to be attentive to these issues,
we also have a moral obligation to view, regardless of today's political
sphere, the ways in which many of these pollutants are also implicated in
past colonial practices and thus accordingly have a potential to do harm to
First Nations people in a way that we need to be very attentive to.53
5.60 As such, it was Dr Kroløkke's view that governments need to recognise the
cultural impacts and intergenerational effects of PFAS contamination when
calculating the risk and harm. She concluded that:
50 Environmental Defenders Office, Submission 55, p. 8. See also: Government of Western Australia,
Western Australian Legislation, Contaminated Sites Act 2003 (accessed 10 November 2025).
51 La Trobe University, Submission 16, p. 2.
52 Dr Charlotte Kroløkke, Professor, University of Southern Denmark, Committee Hansard, 10 June
2025, p. 47.
53 Dr Charlotte Kroløkke, Professor, University of Southern Denmark, Committee Hansard, 10 June
2025, p. 47.
104
Addressing PFAS pollution therefore requires more than technical
solutions; it demands dialogue with affected communities in a broader
recognition of the social and cultural harm that it inflicts.54
Evidence from the Department of Defence
5.61 The Department of Defence acknowledged that First Nations people have
concerns about the impact of PFAS contamination on 'areas of cultural
significance and cultural practices'. Defence noted that its remediation program
includes direct engagement with First Nations stakeholders, including in
relation to the Jervis Bay Range Facility, RAAF Base Tindal, RAAF Base
Townsville and the Townsville Airport, RAAF Base Darwin and Robertson
Barracks.55

Wreck Bay, at a community meeting in Nowra in February 2023, and committed
to assisting the community preserve its cultural practices for future
generations.56

5.65 PFAS management at RAAF Base Tindal has primarily focused on minimising
the amount of PFAS that leaves the base and impacts the surrounding areas.
According to the Defence, most of the remediation actions outlined in the PFAS
management area plan for RAAF Base Tindal 'are complete', including soil
remediation works which were completed in November 2024. Groundwater
treatment commenced in 2019. Defence has treated over 3 billion litres of PFAS-
contaminated groundwater to-date, with groundwater treatment continuing at
two on-base water treatment plants.61
5.66 Despite this investment, Northern Territory Government warnings—which
were first issued in 2018—remain in place. This precautionary advice limits
locals’ consumption of fish, shellfish and crustaceans from the Katherine River,
Tindal Creek, Rapid Creek and Ludmilla Creek. It also advises to avoid eating
bushfoods from near the Katherine River and Tindal Creek, including wildlife,
native fruit, native vegetables and native plants.62

In consultation with States and Territories, the Government will work with
local residents and Indigenous communities to increase understanding of
contamination in local soil, surface water, groundwater and biota. This
includes tailored and culturally appropriate information materials,
including in traditional language, where appropriate. The Government will
apply the same approach for other relevant Commonwealth sites, if PFAS
contamination is migrating from the site and is affecting other properties.63

investigated' for PFAS contamination, rather than indicating confirmed
contamination.69

responsibility of Department of Infrastructure to install signage.70

Better supporting communities
5.80 Inquiry participants made a number of suggestions for better supporting First
Nations communities in dealing with the challenges of PFAS contamination.
5.81 The researchers who undertook the PFAS Health Study ultimately argued for
'[b]etter engagement with Aboriginal communities, through community visits,
among other activities, is needed to improve their knowledge and
understanding of the PFAS contamination and what this means for them and
their relationship with country'.77
5.82 AMSANT and Wurli Wurjlinjang recommended:
 'practical support from Government in the form of sponsored transportation
to uncontaminated river systems for fishing';
 subsidies or a price cap to enable Aboriginal communities to buy fresh,
uncontaminated fish at affordable prices;
 direct involvement of local First Nations people in the remediation and
long-term monitoring of PFAS;
 direct involvement of local health services, such as Wurli Wurjlinjang, in the
codesign of signs and educational material; and that
 '[f]urther education and remediation efforts on the effects of PFAS on
Katherine Aboriginal communities should be cognizant of traditional food
disruptions and be sensitive to the downstream impacts that may not be
easily recognised beyond what has already been publicised'.78
5.83 NACCHO argued there is an 'urgent need for adequately resourced, culturally
informed, community-led responses to PFAS contamination that address both

address 'health outcomes, site remediation, and food security while ensuring
