Chapter 6
Remediation of PFAS affected sites
Introduction
6.1 As discussed in the committee's interim report, remediation of PFAS-
contaminated sites usually comprises removing PFAS contaminants from sites
and reducing PFAS leaching into the surrounding environment. In addition to
addressing the source of contamination, remediation is intended to remove or
mitigate further contamination wherever it has spread, including soil,
groundwater and surface water. Remediation also encompasses the storage or
destruction of resultant waste.
6.2 PFAS contaminated waste cannot simply be moved to landfill, as this would
merely relocate the problem to a new location, resulting in leaching of PFAS into
the environment at a different locality.1 To address this, several entities have
developed remediation techniques and technologies designed to remove, safely
contain, dilute or destroy PFAS.
6.3 The first part of this chapter summarises the technologies and processes
currently used by organisations conducting remediation, as well as remediation
technologies that have been created or developed by submitters.
6.4 The second part of this chapter outlines the practices that are being used for
remediation programs led by Commonwealth and state agencies, including the
Department of Defence (Defence) and Airservices Australia (Airservices), which
are undertaking PFAS investigation and remediation projects across Australia.
This chapter builds on the programs discussed in the interim report using
additional information and evidence provided by some of these
Commonwealth, state and territory agencies, and presents the views of inquiry
participants.
6.5 In January 2025, the committee held a hearing for community members
impacted by PFAS contamination originating from Defence bases in the Jervis
Bay Territory. The committee has since received further evidence from Defence
in relation to the remediation project at Jervis Bay. This chapter provides an
update on Defence's engagement with the Wreck Bay Aboriginal community.
PFAS remediation technologies and projects
6.6 In its interim report, the committee discussed a range of strategies and
techniques used for remediation of PFAS contamination. Since then, the
1 SciDev, Submission 41 (47th Parliament), p. 16.
113
114
committee has received further evidence in relation to methods for destruction,
removal and containment of PFAS from contaminated sites.
6.7 The CSIRO submitted that 'PFAS destruction is very expensive and not widely
available yet'; therefore:
A combination of non-destructive PFAS removal with subsequent PFAS
destruction in the concentrated waste stream is often the most sustainable
and cost-efficient approach to dealing with PFAS.2
6.8 The CSIRO noted that 'best practices are still in development for most remedial
technologies, especially destructive ones'. This is why, in some cases, there is a
'stark contrast in remedial guidance and legislation across jurisdictions'. As an
example, CSIRO noted that the incineration of materials containing PFAS—
which is used in Australia for a small percentage of materials which contain the
highest proportion of PFAS contamination—is 'preferred' in Germany, but
'currently prohibited for the [United States] Department of Defense'.3
6.9 As well as describing and promoting a number of technologies, inquiry
participants emphasised the substantial difficulties faced by Australian entities
developing novel remediation technologies—particularly when it comes to
getting support for testing and research. These difficulties are outlined at the
end of the chapter.
Water remediation technologies
6.10 Veolia, a company specialising in water, waste and energy management,
operates several PFAS treatment and management technologies across
Australia, including 'mobile water treatment assets for treating PFAS-impacted
run-off from contaminated areas'. These assets are deployed 'on construction
projects, fire training schools and in emergency response situations, for example
in the case of foam release at aircraft hangers'.4
6.11 Veolia described its water remediation projects in Australia, which include:
 Installation of a mobile PFAS water treatment plant on a contaminated
former fire training ground, treating PFAS impacted water in a nearby
dam for compliant discharge into the environment.
 Management and treatment of PFAS impacted water from the flushing
of fire trucks during a changeover from AFFF to non-fluorine containing
fire fighting foams, with treatment residuals appropriately managed
offsite.
2 CSIRO, Submission 22 (47th Parliament), p. 12.
3 CSIRO, Submission 22 (47th Parliament), p. 12.
4 Veolia, Submission 58 (47th Parliament), pp. 1–2.
115
 Collection, management and treatment of neat AFFF concentrate from
former fire training facilities.5
6.12 The 3M Company (3M)—who initially developed and manufactured PFAS—
submitted that granular activated carbon is 'effective in removing a wide range
of PFAS and is particularly effective in removing longer-chain PFAS such as
PFOS and PFOA' from water. The resulting PFAS residues then need to be
thermally destroyed.6
6.13 One option for those concerned about PFAS in their drinking water supply is
home water filtration systems. However, Head of School of Civil Engineering at
the University of Sydney, Professor Stuart Khan explained that in-home water
filtration systems are 'highly variable'. While household activated carbon filters
are commonly available, and 'can be at least partially effective', there is no
practical or affordable means of monitoring the performance of PFAS removal
for these products.7
Thermal destruction of PFAS
6.14 In Australia, Enviropacific—which specialises in environmentally focused
engineering and applied science solutions—is the operator of the SOLVE
thermal desorption facility, located in Altona, a suburb in Melbourne.
6.15 SOLVE uses a two-part process of volatilisation8 and thermal destruction to
remove and destroy PFAS from heavily contaminated soil where:
… heat is applied to the contaminated soil to volatilise the contaminants,
capturing them as a vapour for further destruction, while allowing the
recovery of the soil matrix for beneficial purposes.9
6.16 The committee visited the SOLVE thermal destruction plant and was provided
with a presentation and a tour. The committee noted that thermal destruction is
highly effective at neutralising PFAS. However, it is only available at two
Victorian sites, requiring transportation of contaminated materials from around
the country to these facilities in Victoria. The technology also requires a
significant amount of gas to run and is thus costly and energy intensive. A
5 Veolia, Submission 58 (47th Parliament), p. 10.
6 3M Company, Submission 71 (47th Parliament), pp. 3–4.
7 Professor Stuart Khan, Head of School of Civil Engineering at the University of Sydney, responses
to written questions on notice from Senator Thorpe (received 26 February 2025), [p. 2].
8 'Volatilisation' refers to the process of converting a solid or liquid into a gas or vapor, which can be
described as the evaporation of lighter volatile fractions of binders during high temperature
heating. This process is influenced by the properties of the chemical and the soil, as well as climatic
factors, and can be defined as the act of passing into the atmosphere as a gas. (Definition from
Taylor and Francis).
9 Enviropacific, Submission 142 (47th Parliament), [pp. 4–5]. See also: Veolia, Submission 58 (47th
Parliament), pp. 1–2.
116
detailed report on the facility and destruction methods is included in Appendix
2 of this report.
6.17 The SOLVE facility is used by a number of government and non-government
organisations for their respective remediation programs. Enviropacific stated
that it has thermally destroyed PFAS in over 200 000 tonnes of contaminated
'soils, concentrate, washwaters, filter media, concrete and biosolids'.10
6.18 Enviropacific submitted that the main benefit of thermal destruction 'is an
immediate reduction in legacy risks associated with PFAS, enabling soil to be
beneficially used in construction, landfill rehabilitation, and topsoil'.11
6.19 Enviropacific also uses other technologies to remediate PFAS contamination,
like chemical stabilisation, engineered repositories or a 'cap and contain'
approach, as well as 'soil washing'.12
Hemp cultivation to remediate PFAS contaminated land
6.20 Hemp Farms Australia is Australia's largest family-owned breeder of low-THC
industrial hemp genetics.
6.21 Mr Lauchlan Grout, Co-Founder and Chief Executive Officer of Hemp Farms
Australia, stated that industrial hemp represents 'an unparalleled scientifically
proven method for remediating soils with contaminated PFAS'. According to
Mr Grout, Australian-bred hemp varieties 'have demonstrated the remarkable
ability to bioaccumulate up to and above 80 per cent of PFAS contaminants from
soil'.13
6.22 Mr Grout added that Hemp Farms Australia has partnered with a Belgian
bioengineering specialist that has developed 'a soil enzyme additive which,
when combined with [the] hemp varieties, can enhance the rate of PFAS uptake
by approximately 40 times compared to traditional methods'.14
6.23 The final part of the process involves placing the resulting biomass (the organic
material produced from the harvested hemp) in a mobile pyrolysis machine and
10 Enviropacific, Submission 142 (47th Parliament), [pp. 1 and 4].
11 Enviropacific, Submission 142 (47th Parliament), [pp. 4–5].
12 Enviropacific, Submission 142 (47th Parliament), [pp. 4–5]. Soil washing is a remediation process
which focusses on the aggregation of contaminants into fines (clays/silts) to produce recoverable
materials such as sand and gravels. This allows the secondary treatment of the consolidated and
concentrated PFAS materials, with beneficial reuse of the majority cleaner products. Enviropacific,
Treating PFAS across all waste streams, February 2024 (accessed 13 August 2025).
13 Mr Lauchlan Grout, Co-Founder and Chief Executive Officer, Hemp Farms Australia, Committee
Hansard, 4 June 2025, pp. 47 and 50.
14 Mr Lauchlan Grout, Co-Founder and Chief Executive Officer, Hemp Farms Australia, Committee
Hansard, 4 June 2025, pp. 47 and 50.
117
heating the biomass to 800 degrees Celsius. Mr Grout stated that this leaves
behind a '100 per cent carbon product', with no PFAS residue.15
6.24 Mr Grout noted that this technology could be used to remediate soils at airports
and former fire stations and would be particularly useful in remote locations.
Despite promising research, Mr Grout said 'Australia has yet to fully explore
and leverage' industrial hemp for PFAS remediation.16
Biochar
6.25 Mr Robert Tonks—a Queensland engineer with almost 20 years of research and
development in pyrolysis technology and biochar production—emphasised the
difficulties faced by purveyors of emerging technologies, including 'zero
pathways for field trial access, no dedicated funding for pre-commercial
technology validation, and regulatory frameworks that favour established
multinationals over Australian innovation'.17
6.26 Mr Tonks has developed a 'biological PFAS remediation technology combining
pyrolysis-produced biochar with proprietary microbial solutions'. Mr Tonks
believed this technology has 'the potential to destroy PFAS contamination in-
situ while improving soil health'.18
Immobilising PFAS in concrete
6.27 Mr Robert Lancaster, the Senior Business Development Manager at Fire and Test
Australasia (FTA)—a certified Aboriginal-owned special hazards company
based in Victoria—described FTA's solution to PFAS remediation at the
Melbourne public hearing. FTA is the sole 'global Indigenous distributor' of
Ambiolock, which is an 'Australian designed, developed and patented PFAS
mitigation technology'. Mr Lancaster stated that, 'unlike many remediation
methods that manage PFAS effects, Ambiolock targets the source, mechanically
immobilising PFAS compounds and preventing their migration through soil
and water'. He claimed Ambiolock had 'improved its effectiveness from a 60 per
cent to a 99 per cent effectiveness in encapsulating PFAS in concrete and other
substrates for reuse and repurposing'. FTA further argued that, by immobilising
15 Mr Lauchlan Grout, Co-Founder and Chief Executive Officer, Hemp Farms Australia, Committee
Hansard, 4 June 2025, pp. 47 and 50.
16 Mr Lauchlan Grout, Co-Founder and Chief Executive Officer, Hemp Farms Australia, Committee
Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 43.
17 Robert Tonks, Submission 140 (47th Parliament), [pp. 1–2]. Pyrolysis is the thermal decomposition of
organic materials in an oxygen-free environment to break them down into their simpler molecular
compounds. Biochar is a stable, carbon-rich charcoal produced from biomass like wood, manure,
or waste by heating it to high temperatures in a low-oxygen environment through a process called
pyrolysis.
18 Robert Tonks, Submission 140 (47th Parliament), [pp. 1–2].
118
PFAS at the source, Ambiolock would 'reduce future remediation needs and
associated costs'.19
6.28 Mr Lancaster outlined three different solutions for PFAS contamination in
concrete:
 The first is a substance that is mixed into new concrete 'to stop that concrete
substrate from becoming a contaminated waste product later on'.
 The second is a treatment for concrete rubble from impacted sites, such as
airports, chemical plants, firefighting grounds, etc. This rubble is crushed
into an aggregate, a substance is added, then the new concrete mix can be
used to construct new runways, or other hard landscaping.
 The third solution, Ambioseal, is applied at sites that 'can't shut down, like a
firefighting training ground where you need a live fire station in the
suburb'. FTA cleans the concrete, then applies 'a two-coat penetrating
system':
The first coat goes down and migrates into the slab in excess of 150
millimetres. It actually locks it down and stops the transfer of all the
moisture. So there's nothing going in and nothing coming out. Then we've
got a second-stage coating which migrates into the slab up to 50 millimetres.
It's not a surface coating; it becomes a part of the substrate. It grows in
amongst all the voids inside your concrete. As I think everybody would be
aware, concrete is like a hard sponge. It is porous. So, every time it rains, it
spreads the love. That's something that we can do.20
6.29 In addition, Mr Lancaster maintained that FTA can 'sectionalise' remediation,
meaning contaminated sites do not need to 'all shut down at once' while
remediation is undertaken.21
6.30 According to FTA, its products have been used on numerous projects around
Australia, including fire training grounds and truck wash bays, and 'have
demonstrated more than 99% reduction in PFAS leaching using the
AmbioSeal™ two-stage concrete treatment process'. Despite having
demonstrated effectiveness, Mr Lancaster submitted that FTA faces 'barriers to
swift implementation due to regulatory inertia and consultant-driven delays,
often focusing only on soil and groundwater impacts while overlooking
concrete remediation within the source areas'.22

Hazardous waste storage facility
6.31 Tellus owns and operates a non-destructive hazardous waste facility at Sandy
Ridge in remote Western Australia. The facility 'is licensed and equipped to
dispose safely of almost all forms of hazardous waste'. Tellus stated that the
'geological characteristics' of the site makes the facility 'one of the safest places
on earth to permanently dispose of hazardous waste, whether chemical or
radioactive':
Unlike a conventional landfill, which is open, with an artificial liner that
degrades over time, and collects leachate which can pollute groundwater,
the Sandy Ridge geological repository uses the natural barriers of the
environment to contain waste, supported by an additional engineered
barrier—an air dome—which prevents the build-up of leachate during
disposal.23
6.32 Tellus argued that its operation could play a much larger role in remediation, as
the scale of the facility at Sandy Ridge 'means remediation and clean-up projects
can be completed at far greater scale and speed than other current solutions'.24
6.33 Tellus stated that it has disposed of 'some PFAS material, including material that
originated from government agencies'. However, the organisation emphasised
that 'it has been constrained in disposing of higher volumes by current
concentration limits in applicable legislation', as current regulation 'prevents
acceptance of higher concentrations of PFAS contaminants due to the
interpretation of the Stockholm Convention in the current National
Environmental Management Plan'.25
6.34 Tellus acknowledged arguments that 'landfill disposal is inappropriate'.
Nevertheless, Tellus believed that this is due to 'current guidance and practice
about destruction of PFAS materials, for example through thermal
destruction'.26
6.35 Tellus acknowledged that thermal destruction is an 'effective method of
destroying PFAS', however, raised concerns about thermal destruction
continuing 'to be a valid method of PFAS management in Australia, including
in the population-dense Melbourne metropolitan region'. Tellus also cited the
United States Environmental Protection Agency's guidance, provided in
December 2020, which noted:
…the use of storage, deep well injection, permitted hazardous waste
landfills and solid waste landfills provide a more certain method of safely
23 Tellus, Submission 83 (47th Parliament), p. 2.

25 Tellus, Submission 83 (47th Parliament), pp. 2–3.
26 Tellus, Submission 83 (47th Parliament), pp. 2–3.

dealing with PFAS compared with hazardous waste combustors and other
thermal treatment.27
6.36 Therefore, Tellus encouraged government to amend the PFAS National
Environmental Management Plan 3.0 (NEMP 3.0) to provide 'for geological
repositories as a preferable form of waste disposal to take account of their
unique attributes for disposing of PFAS in an environmentally responsible
way'.28
Technology choices, funding, and development pathways
6.37 Several submitters recognised that treatment technologies may be site-specific,
and certain sites may require multiple treatment technologies.
6.38 SciDev Limited (SciDev), an Australian company specialising in water
treatment solutions including remediating PFAS contamination, submitted that:
… the process for selection and regulatory approval of appropriate
treatment technologies must be flexible and allow for a wide range of
treatment technologies that can be combined based on site-specific
conditions.29
6.39 Mr Steve Becker, Technical Director of Water Technologies at SciDev, noted that
the optimum remediation solution may involve a 'combination of technologies
to effectively address complex situations'.30
6.40 Similarly, crcCare submitted that a 'combination of technologies’ is required to
achieve optimal remediation outcomes'.31
6.41 However, submitters expressed varying views on the extent to which a range of
technologies have reached commercial maturity and the developmental and
regulatory hurdles facing smaller businesses developing remediation
technologies.
6.42 On the one hand, SciDev argued that the remediation industry in Australia has
'long understood the problems of PFAS, and there are numerous fully
commercialised solutions available that can be tailored to site-specific
conditions'.32

PFAS remediation programs
6.50 The following sections cover a range of remediation programs operated by
Commonwealth and state agencies, including Defence, Airservices, and the
Australian Government's PFAS Airports Investigation Program, administered
by the Department of Infrastructure, Transport, Regional Development,
Communications, Sport and the Arts (the Department of Infrastructure). This
section also provides an update on the PFAS management and remediation
program at Wreck Bay.
Defence PFAS Investigation and Management Program
6.51 Over 10 years, from the 2015–16 budget to 2025–26, Defence has had an
allocation totalling $817 million for investigation and remediation.40
6.52 Since 2015, Defence has delivered the PFAS Investigation and Management
Program (the Defence Program) to manage risks associated with PFAS
contamination on and around Defence sites, which was caused by the historic
use of materials containing PFAS, most notably firefighting foams.41
6.53 The use of PFAS-containing foams at Defence sites across the country means
high levels of PFAS remain present in the environment surrounding many
Defence bases. Since 2004, Defence has worked with a variety of
Commonwealth, state and territory agencies and local communities in
managing PFAS contamination around Defence sites.42
6.54 The Defence Program includes investigation, management, remediation and
ongoing monitoring of PFAS contamination at 28 Defence sites across the
country. This includes joint-user airports, where both Defence and civil aviation
operations occur, such as at Darwin and Townsville airports.43
6.55 Defence works collaboratively with the Department of Infrastructure and
Airservices. This involves pooling information, experience and knowledge,
including providing data analyses of historical work and previous sampling 'to
39 Mr James Comley, PFAS Technical Lead, Airservices Australia, Committee Hansard, 5 September
2025, p. 5.
40 Department of Defence, Submission 66 (47th Parliament), [p. 4]; Department of Defence, response to
questions on notice, public hearing 21 January 2025 (received 11 February 2025), [p. 7].
41 Department of Defence, Submission 66 (47th Parliament), [p. 2].
42 Department of Defence, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe—questions to Defence,
(received 17 February 2025), [p. 4].
43 Department of Infrastructure, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe—questions to
Infrastructure, (received 28 March 2025), [p. 2].
123
avoid duplication of effort and to ensure all PFAS sources and impacts on
airport are investigated'. In addition to sharing knowledge, the agencies
consider and use findings from other investigations across the program.44
6.56 Investigations have determined that 'PFAS is mostly concentrated in areas
where legacy firefighting foams (in use from the 1970s until Defence
commenced transitioning away from them in 2004) were previously used,
stored or disposed', which has led to these locations being designated as 'source
areas'. PFAS in these locations can be found in soil and groundwater. In addition
to these concentrated locations, Defence has acknowledged instances of PFAS
being distributed from source areas, via surface water transport, including
drainage lines and creeks, as well as groundwater flow, contaminating
surrounding waterways and properties.45
Site investigations and PFAS Management Area Plans
6.57 Defence argued that it is a world leader in PFAS investigation and remediation
through its largescale investigation and remediation program.46 However, the
Defence program highlighted the difficulties of removing PFAS from the
environment, as well as the size and complexity of the task.
6.58 According to Defence, the first stage of its program involved 'site investigations,
and human health and ecological risk assessments, conducted in line with the
National Environment Protection (Assessment of Site Contamination) Measure 1999
and PFAS National Environmental Management Plan'.47
6.59 Investigations for each base 'identified the areas where PFAS contamination is
concentrated, known as source areas, and where pathways from the source areas
may result in PFAS exposure for people, plants or animals'.48
6.60 Defence used this information to develop PFAS Management Area Plans for
each site, which set out the actions Defence is taking to manage and remediate
PFAS contamination. The investigation stage began in 2016 and is now complete
at all 28 bases included in the program. These sites were selected 'using an
assessment and prioritisation process based on how legacy firefighting foam
was used or stored'.49

Infrastructure, (received 28 March 2025), [p. 2].

6.61 Management and remediation activities at some sites started before the
completion of investigations. In many cases, this has included providing an
alternative drinking water supply to households where water was potentially
contaminated.50
6.62 Defence holds information sessions for communities affected by PFAS
contamination from Defence sites, with these sessions intended to provide
information on the progress of remediation and outcomes of ongoing
monitoring, while providing opportunities for community members to 'learn
more from the PFAS Investigation and Management Program team'.51
6.63 PFAS mass flux studies are used by Defence to measure levels of PFAS and
'groundwater and surface water flow rates'. These studies involve sampling and
analysis of PFAS in groundwater and surface water and 'hydrological
modelling' using samples 'collected over a 12-month period to measure seasonal
changes within the environment'.52
6.64 A mass flux study at Holsworthy Barracks began in September 2024 with
Defence anticipating that the report 'will be finalised around November 2025,
allowing for an independent technical review of the study'.53
6.65 In September 2025, Deputy Secretary, Security and Estate, Ms Celia Perkins told
the committee that Defence had now 'completed agreed remediation actions to
the extent reasonably practicable' at two sites—Singleton Military Area, in New
South Wales (NSW); and HMAS Cerberus, in Victoria.54
6.66 While Defence's investigation and remediation program may be one of the most
advanced in the world, impacted community members have previously
expressed anger and disillusionment towards Defence and the Commonwealth
for the slow pace of the program, limited scope of remediation, confusing and
unsatisfactory communication and community engagement, and ongoing
impacts on their land, its economic value and their lifestyles.55

RAAF Base Darwin and RAAF Base Tindal
6.68 Defence stated that it 'undertakes significant data collection as part of its
ongoing investigation and monitoring programs' in and around sites such as
RAAF Base Darwin and RAAF Base Tindal (located near Katherine). These
datasets are made available through the Defence PFAS website, which includes
information on surface water, groundwater, soil and biota.56
6.69 Defence has commenced remediation of source locations at RAAF Base Tindal
and RAAF Base Darwin with the removal of 'concentrated source material from
site' for destruction and disposal. Defence has determined that removing the
most highly contaminated material 'will reduce the overall load of PFAS
entering the environment in those locations'. However, additional remediation
'to stabilise lower level PFAS concentrations on these sites will likely further
reduce contaminant load'. Defence stated that the result of remediation works
'will require multi-year monitoring to determine contaminant trends both on
and off site'.57
Jervis Bay Range Facility
6.70 Forecasted expenditure for future works at the Jervis Bay Defence sites—
including the Royal Australian Navy School of Survivability and Ship Safety,
which is the primary site of PFAS contamination in Wreck Bay—is
approximately $80 million. This figure includes currently contracted
remediation works, ongoing monitoring and water treatment plant operating
costs through to 2035. Defence noted that the forecasted cost is subject to change
due to ongoing investigations as well as external factors such as the cost of
materials and labour.58
6.71 Remediation works commenced at the Royal Australian Navy School of
Survivability and Ship Safety facility and the headwaters of Mary Creek in early
2025. This work included 'capturing contaminated surface water and
groundwater; and diverting clean surface water to reduce flow over PFAS-
impacted soils'; and excavating and disposing of PFAS contaminated soils. The

Settlement of class actions
6.74 Defence has settled five class actions and paid out around 400 other legal claims.
The majority of class actions involved compensation to landowners for
'economic loss, including the diminution in value to their properties'.62 In
contrast, the Wreck Bay class action settlement was paid in recognition of
cultural loss and cultural impacts. Along with class actions, Defence has paid
out a number of non-litigated claims. With some exceptions, the majority of the
PFAS related claims received by Defence to date 'have claimed property value
diminution and inconvenience, distress and vexation'. These 'are assessed on a
case-by-case basis, in accordance with the Attorney-General's Legal Services
Directions 2017 (Cth)'.63
6.75 According to the Assistant Secretary of PFAS Investigations and Management,
Ms Kathleen Leane:
Wreck Bay was $22 million, settled on 19 June 2023. There were three class
actions at Williamtown, Katherine and Oakey; that was $212.5 million on
5 June 2020. The class actions relating to RAAF bases Pearce, Darwin,
Townsville, Edinburgh, Richmond and Wagga and to Bandiana military

Airservices National PFAS Management Program
6.78 As discussed in the interim report, the Airservices National PFAS Management
Program (Airservices' Program) targets PFAS within Airservices lease areas on
airports. Airservices' Program includes site investigations, monitoring,
management actions and, where appropriate, remediation.67
6.79 As at 30 June 2023, Airservices' PFAS-related activity had cost approximately
$129 million, with $77.8 million spent in 2022.68
6.80 In its 2024–25 Corporate Plan, Airservices stated that the costs and liabilities
associated with PFAS contamination and its remediation is a key risk to the
organisation, saying:
We are currently funding these activities through our operating budget;
however, we are actively working with our stakeholders to devise a more
suitable funding model.
We recognise that the full cost of remediation is beyond the capacity of our
current funding model. Consequently, it is likely that we will need support
from the Commonwealth Government and the airports.69

6.81 Airservices has already sought support from the Commonwealth, with at least
$20 million of public monies allocated for remediation at Launceston Airport, in
Tasmania, and around $25 million being sought for remediation at
Rockhampton Airport in Queensland.70
6.82 While some airports, like Launceston Airport, have reflected positively on the
Airservices program, other airports around Australia raised concerns regarding
the pace and depth of the program.
Australian Airports Association
6.83 The industry body for Australian airports, the Australian Airports Association
(AAA), outlined concerns that airport operators had with Airservices' Program;
most notably stating that the program 'is too little, too late'.71
6.84 AAA called on Airservices to expedite action 'to effectively address the ongoing
risks and impacts of legacy contamination', adding that Airservices must strive
to achieve a nationally consistent approach to the management of PFAS and
ensure that all airport sites are involved in a coordinated remediation effort.72
6.85 AAA noted that the current funding model 'focuses exclusively on federally
leased airports, leaving significant gaps in addressing contamination at other
affected sites'. AAA therefore argued that a nationally consistent framework
was 'essential to provide clarity and certainty for airport operators, local
governments, and communities'.73
6.86 AAA suggested Airservices' approach should also include 'collaboration with
state-based agencies to align remediation efforts and regulatory standards,
ensuring that all affected sites receive the attention and resources they need'.74
Polluter pays principle
6.87 AAA emphasised that the 'polluter pays' principle is 'a widely accepted
principle underpinning environmental legislation, which implies that those who
generate pollution and waste should bear the cost of its containment, avoidance
or abatement'.75 According to AAA, these costs extend to the costs of
'identification, assessment, remediation and management of land affected by the
relevant pollution'. AAA highlighted that the polluter pays principle is an

August 2025 (accessed 10 November 2025).

75 Australian Airports Association, Submission 39 (47th Parliament), p. 7.

important part of the Intergovernmental Agreement on a National Framework
for Responding to PFAS Contamination.76
6.88 The submission from AAA ultimately asserted that:
… even though the existing regulatory framework contains mechanisms to
hold the polluter to account, the polluter pays principle has not been
effectively implemented through the Commonwealth's current regulatory
approach to managing contamination at federally leased airports sites.77
6.89 AAA believed that a broader scope would ensure that the financial burden of
addressing PFAS contamination 'is borne by the polluters under the "polluter
pays" principle', arguing that federally leased airports 'should not be required
to shoulder these costs for contamination they did not cause'.78
6.90 The United Firefighters Union of Australia made a similar argument, suggesting
the Australian Government establish a PFAS remediation grant program to fund
remediation of the most heavily contaminated sites, and consider 'at least partial
funding of the remediation grant program through the pursuit of litigation
against manufacturers and suppliers of firefighting foams containing PFAS'.79
6.91 The PFAS independent review also suggested that Commonwealth legal action
against manufacturers of PFAS-containing products could provide an
opportunity to recover some of the costs of remediation.80
6.92 In May 2025, the NSW EPA ordered 3M Australia to address PFAS
contamination at Brogans Creek Quarry, where the company tested firefighting
foams between 1980 and 2000.81

North Queensland Airports Group
6.94 North Queensland Airports Group is the owner and operator of both Cairns and
Mackay airports. The Group outlined a brief history of its experience with the
Airservices program.84
6.95 North Queensland Airports Group submitted that the 'initial testing reports
confirmed PFAS contamination of soil and groundwater at Cairns Airport in
2008'. However, it was not until 2019 that Airservices 'conducted formal
Preliminary Site Investigations and targeted sampling at Cairns and Mackay
airports'.85
6.96 According to North Queensland Airport Group, the results for Mackay Airport
indicated PFAS contamination so high that it 'caused Queensland Health to erect
signage at nearby Shellgrit Creek' which warned the public 'not to eat fish from
Shellgrit Creek due to elevated levels of PFAS'.86
6.97 After the results were released in 2019, Airservices advised North Queensland
Airports Group that a Detailed Site Investigation would follow for both Cairns
and Mackay airports. It was not until five years later, in late 2024, that North
Queensland Airports Group received draft versions of the Detailed Site
Investigation reports from Airservices. North Queensland Airports Group
noted that the 'reports are not expected to be finalised until mid-2025'.87
6.98 Like AAA, North Queensland Airports Group submitted that the process has
been 'drawn-out' and 'highly frustrating'. The airport operator expressed its
desire for 'prompt, effective action and communication from Airservices',
adding that Airservices 'must act to expedite its investigations and reporting
and take responsibility for remediation at affected sites under the "polluter
pays" principle' and ensure 'PFAS management [is] nationally consistent'.88

civilian operations. It outlined similar frustrations, stating that Airservices has
'continuously attempted to shift accountability and liability for PFAS

Evidence from Airservices
6.103 Airservices noted that it is 'a tenant at each of the airports' involved, not a
landowner. As such, Airservices is 'dependent' on airport lessors, and
Commonwealth and state and territory agencies to provide approvals before it
can commence any investigations or work:
Securing agreement from these complex groups, who in some instances
have different objectives, often adds to the timelines within which
Airservices can act.93
6.104 Airservices also argued that the 'intensity' and pace of investigations differs
between airports based on the results of initial investigations, due to Airservices'
risk-based approach.94
Australian Government PFAS Airports Investigation Program
6.105 The Department of Infrastructure administers the Australian Government's
PFAS Airports Investigation Program at civilian airports 'where the

6.106 AAA argued that the Department of Infrastructure's PFAS Airports
Investigation Program 'focuses exclusively on federally leased airports, leaving

the committee notes that there are 37 airports in scope for the program but only
21 federally-leased airports (the total will be 22 once the new Western Sydney
Airport is operational).
6.107 As part of the PFAS Airports Investigation Program, a Memorandum of
Understanding (MOU) has been entered into between the Commonwealth and
some operators of federally leased airports for the investigation and
management of PFAS at airport-lessee companies' sites. AAA noted that the
Commonwealth 'has agreed to fund the roles and responsibilities set out in the
MOU'. However, 'memorandums of understanding are generally non-binding
in nature and in this instance, the MOUs do not deal with the question of liability
for PFAS contamination'.97

Infrastructure requesting an update on the program, which is expected to be
completed by 30 June 2027.98

 investigations are commencing at nine airports;
 five airports have been invited to participate, but have not yet provided a
response;
 six airports will not be participating in the program or are included in

be required. It becomes a matter for the airport to implement those
management plans and any actions that might be assigned to other parties—
for example, tenants on airports—would then flow from that work.101
Case study: Launceston Airport
6.112 Launceston Airport is the second busiest airport in Tasmania, linking the
northern areas of the state to the mainland. The airport is leased by the
Commonwealth Government to Asia Pacific Airports (Launceston) (APAL)
which maintains airport operations. In conjunction with APAL, Airservices
facilitates operations through air traffic control and provides firefighting
services. Launceston Airport used the same standard firefighting foams that
were utilised by other Australian airports and stopped using foams that
contained PFAS in 2010.102
6.113 In 2009, Airservices provided to APAL a contamination assessment report for
its Fire Training Ground (now the Former Fire Training Ground) at Launceston
Airport which identified contamination from Aqueous Film Forming Foams
(AFFF). APAL noted that, in 2010, 'Airservices wrote a letter to APAL, stating
that Airservices had become aware of potential contamination issues arising
from the use of AFFF at airports, with active ingredients which can include
PFOS and PFOA'. The letter also stated that Airservices had 'ceased the use of

Arts, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe (received 1 August 2025), [pp. 1–2].

September 2025, p. 18.

AFFF in training' and were undertaking 'extensive investigations and working
to understand the potential environmental issues'.103
6.114 APAL established roundtable discussions to coordinate a response to the
contamination. Stakeholders who attended the roundtable included:
 APAL;
 Airservices;
 EPA Tasmania;
 Tasmanian Government Department of Natural Resources and
Environment;
 Tasmanian Government Department of Health;
 Commonwealth Department of Infrastructure, Transport, Regional
Development, Communications and the Arts;
 the Airport Environment Officer; and
 the Australian Government PFAS Taskforce (which operated as a discrete
entity from 2017 to 2022 to support whole-of-government coordination and
oversight of Australian Government responses to PFAS contamination).104
6.115 APAL considered this 'an effective and representative group that was well
positioned to work together and advocate on behalf of diverse stakeholders'.
APAL stated that it was 'pleased with the process' which ultimately resulted in
'industry-leading remediation works taking place at Launceston Airport'.105
6.116 However, one submitter expressed concern that they, as property owners near
the airport, felt 'locked out of any direct communication', despite having 'asked
on several occasions to be included in the "round-table" meetings'.106
6.117 Asked to comment on these concerns, APAL said the airport is 'open to
including new voices and representatives as appropriate' to ensure that
'decision-making is as informed as possible'.107
6.118 In early 2024, remediation works for Launceston Airport received approval
through the Parliamentary Standing Committee on Public Works with a cost
estimate 'exceeding $20 [million]'.108
103 Launceston Airport, response to question taken on notice, public hearing, 12 March 2025 and

6.119 The works aimed to remediate an estimated 90 per cent of PFAS contamination
at the Former Fire Training Ground. APAL stated that the 'remediation
approach involves the excavation of PFAS-contaminated soil, mixing in
stabilising agents (such as activated carbon) where appropriate, and then
reinstating the stabilised soil'. However, where excavated soil exceeds a 'chosen
level' of PFAS contamination, the soil is not stabilised or reinstated. This heavily
contaminated soil is instead 'taken off-airport to a thermal destruction facility',
specifically the SOLVE facility in Melbourne.109
6.120 When soil has been stabilised, Airservices submitted that 'PFAS contaminated
soil in this area ceases to be an ongoing potential source of contamination to the
surrounding area', because stabilisation prevents PFAS from leaching out of the
soil and contaminating surface waters or groundwater.110
6.121 APAL agreed that Airservices' plan to immobilise PFAS contamination and
reinstate most of the soil is a 'reliable, reasonable, and practicable approach to
managing this type of contamination'. It added that the 90 per cent removal
target 'only applies to the Former Fire Training Ground, where long-term
exposure to PFAS has caused significant contamination'.111
6.122 The remediation works commenced in October 2024 and were expected to take
approximately 6 months to complete.112 At the committee's September 2025
hearing, Airservices confirmed that the remediation work at Launceston Airport
were completed on 30 June 2025. With 27 000 cubic metres of soil remediated,
Mr Comley explained that some had been 'treated, re-instated and stabilised',
and some had been disposed of 'off-airport'.113
6.123 Nearby landowners, Ms Kate Tucker and Mr David Hughes—whose land is
'utilised for agriculture (livestock and crops), recreational, and industrial
purposes (quarrying of basalt)'—were concerned that PFAS contamination has
already spread from the airport. A waterway, Kelly's Creek, which runs through
their property from the Airport was recorded as having 'combined PFHxS + POS
levels in several water samples as high as 24.5 [nanograms per litre (ng/L)]' by
Airservices in 2023.114

for the area which represents the main source of contamination on their land—

Authority (EPA)] Tasmania, Airservices, Launceston Airport Group and the

to who is responsible for this area and moreover who is responsible for stopping
the contamination coming onto our land'.115
Remediation standards and associated risks and costs
6.125 crcCare submitted that remediation works on specific sites 'have been
inconsistent in their effectiveness, with some efforts focused solely on
immediate contamination cleanup rather than long-term environmental
protection'. Accordingly, crcCARE recommended government entities adopt 'a
holistic approach to site assessments that consider long-term environmental
impacts' and that entities ensure 'ongoing monitoring and maintenance after
remediation is complete'.116
6.126 Australia's national peak body for soil science, Soil Science Australia, submitted
that management strategies for PFAS should incorporate both immediate and
long-term action plans. This could involve 'encouraging the development and
application of new technologies for the removal of PFAS from contaminated
sites' as well as 'engaging local communities in the development and
implementation of monitoring programs'. Soil Science Australia also argued
that 'instituting regular monitoring and transparent reporting of PFAS levels in
the environment’ would both foster trust between government entities and
affected communities and ensure accountability.117
6.127 Drawing on its experience with remediation projects, SciDev argued that
differing remediation standards across Australia reduce public confidence.
Therefore, SciDev emphasised the need to establish a common national
framework for remediation and 'national standards for reporting'.118
6.128 SciDev also recommended establishing a national peak body 'to deal with PFAS
management issues and concerns'. Such a body could be based on the Interstate
115 Kate Tucker and David Hughes, Submission 132 (47th Parliament), [p. 1].
116 crcCare, Submission 36 (47th Parliament), p. 22.
117 Soil Science Australia, Submission 111 (47th Parliament), [p. 2].
118 Mr Steve Becker, Technical Director, Water Technologies, SciDev Limited, Committee Hansard,
4 June 2025, p. 45.
137
Technology and Regulatory Council in the United States and the Network for
Industrially Co-ordinated Sustainable Land Management in Europe.119
6.129 Several submitters raised the issue of relative risk, and the costs associated with
stricter remediation standards.
6.130 Dr Askeland and Mr Mitchell argued that remediation needs to 'match the level
of risk' by considering 'how many grams of PFAS are removed per dollar spent
or per unit of CO2 emitted'.120
6.131 SciDev also noted that meeting stricter remediation standards requires more
sophisticated solutions.121
6.132 Airservices stated that stricter remediation standards may be 'neither
proportionate nor risk-based and, in the context of an operational airfield, … not
achievable without significant disruption to civil aviation'. Airservices
explained that if 'a more conservative remediation standard equal to that in the
PFAS NEMP for the protection of ecological health from indirect exposure were
adopted, this would result in a threefold increase in management and
remediation costs'.122
6.133 Airservices also pointed out that:
… regardless of the level to which remediation is performed, measurable
concentrations of PFAS will remain. It is simply impossible to remove all
PFAS that is now ubiquitously distributed throughout the developed world.
Indeed, there is a growing body of scientific evidence that suggests that
PFAS concentrations in rainfall globally exceed some of the more
conservative standards being proposed and used by regulators in Australia.
The PFAS problem is truly ubiquitous.123
6.134 Noting the widespread occurrence of PFAS in the environment, Airservices
believed that 'any potential solution should include a cross-industry approach
and be nationally consistent'. It further added that responses 'must be driven by
an understanding of the health and ecological impacts and be risk-based, giving
due consideration to cost-benefit analysis of remediation works versus natural
attenuation where appropriate'.124

Chapter 7
PFAS in water supplies
Introduction
7.1 This chapter builds on evidence explored in the committee's interim report on
per and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) contamination of water supplies—
particularly in drinking water.1
7.2 It looks at the guidance for, and regulation of, PFAS in drinking water and
recycled water, the extent of PFAS in water supplies, water treatment options,
and an update on the Blue Mountains case study considered in the interim
report.
7.3 The chapter concludes with an outline of views and suggestions from inquiry
participants.
Australia's drinking water
7.4 The committee heard that Australia's drinking water is, by and large, of high
quality and the risks from PFAS contamination are very low.2 However, given
that, 'for most people, ingesting food and drinking water contaminated with
PFAS is the primary source of exposure', there are community concerns.3
Australian Drinking Water Guidelines
7.5 As discussed in the interim report, the then Department of Health and Aged
Care (now Department of Health, Disabilities and Ageing) requested that the
National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC) undertake an
independent review of the Australian guideline values for PFAS in the
Australian Drinking Water Guidelines (ADWG or the Guidelines), issued under
the National Water Quality Management Strategy.4
7.6 On 25 June 2025, the NHMRC released updated ADWG with a significant
decrease in what are considered safe levels of PFAS in drinking water. The
changes to the values are outlined in Table 7.1.

7.8 The NHRMC's independent review of the 2018 recommended levels of PFAS in
drinking water found the existing levels to be 'adequate'. Still, in 2024 the
NHMRC decided to lower the threshold for what constitutes 'safe' PFAS levels.6
7.9 When asked about this change from 2018 levels, Executive Director of the
Research Quality Branch at the NHMRC, Mr Alan Singh explained that the

review's objective was only to provide evidence—not recommendations—to the
Water Quality Advisory Committee, with the committee itself using the
evidence and its expert judgement to determine the values.7
7.10 In making its decision, the Water Quality Advisory Committee considered the
strengths and weaknesses of the underpinning studies, the relevancy of
international advice to the Australian context, and consumer values and
preferences. Mr Singh remarked:
… the Water Quality Advisory Committee have considered the findings of
that report and have determined what they consider the most high-certainty
and most conservative precautionary values that we could use for our
drinking water guidelines for the public consultation draft.8
7.11 Other submitters suggested that the substantial tightening of guideline values
for PFAS were due to community concerns that PFAS 'compounds are
carcinogenic'9 or, at the very least, that the 'NHMRC feels that the revised value
has been carefully developed to protect human health'.10
7.12 In relation to PFOS, the NHMRC initially proposed a guideline level of 4 ng/L
(nanograms per litre) but eventually settled on 8ng/L.11
7.13 The NHMRC advised that the draft guideline value for PFOS of 4 ng/L was
'based on a benchmark dose selected by the United States Environmental
Protection Agency (US EPA) as the best available model on which to derive a
reference dose for PFOS for bone marrow effects'.12 However, Mr Singh
acknowledged that the NHMRC used a 'different approach' to the United States
when determining its final guideline value. The Australian approach 'is used by

7.14 On notice, the NHMRC expanded on this point, saying:
NHMRC guidelines typically use the relevant factors from the GRADE
evidence-to decision framework and the WHO-INTEGRATE framework to
guide the evidence-to decision process in guideline development. These
frameworks are widely accepted in the guideline development community,
including NHMRC, as best practice models.
… In line with best practice, NHMRC adopts the evidence-to-decision
process that was fit for purpose and considered the factors relevant to the
topic and guideline purpose.14
7.15 The GRADE and WHO-INTEGRATE frameworks are widely used but have
been criticised for not clearly articulating considerations that are relevant to
government decision-making, such as 'essentiality' or 'environmental justice'. To
address this, Dr Nicholas Chartres and colleagues at the Program on
Reproductive Health and the Environment, University of California, San
Francisco (UCSF), developed the Navigation Guide Evidence-to-Decision
Framework for Environmental Health (UCSF EtD) to provide 'a structured and
transparent framework incorporating a range of scientific information and
factors for decision-making'.15
7.16 Asked if the NHMRC considers the UCSF EtD to be 'best practice for prioritising
health in assessing and regulating chemicals', the NHMRC submitted:
While both NHMRC and UCSF EtD frameworks share similar criteria that
are considered during decision-making … the UCSF framework appears to
place greater emphasis on social and ethical dimensions, including
environmental justice, human rights, and sociocultural feasibility, as core
decision-making criteria alongside heath evidence.
NHMRC and the Water Quality Advisory Committee consider potential
impacts of different guideline values during the EtD process, but ultimately
the decision about the PFAS guideline values were based on what was

therefore further research was needed to understand the health impacts of other

Alignment with international standards
7.20 As discussed in the interim report, the proposed PFAS levels in the 2024 draft
ADWG faced criticism for falling short of the stricter limits established by
international jurisdictions, particularly the United States, which stated in 2024
that there was 'no safe level' of PFOS and PFOA in drinking water.20
7.21 Some witnesses thought the NHMRC should have had more discussion and
coordination with the US EPA when revising the ADWG. For example,
Dr Chartres stated that it was of 'huge concern that the NHMRC didn't
communicate with the US EPA on this'.21

7.22 Mr Singh explained that the NHMRC did not follow the US EPA's findings
because they were 'largely based' on human epidemiological studies which they
did not think were 'sufficient to derive a guideline value' from.22
7.23 Mr Singh also stated that automatically adopting guidelines from other
jurisdictions would ignore water quality considerations in a local context:
This is important because other countries, such as the United States, do issue
public health advice within their own legislative frameworks, which don't
apply to Australia. They can also use risk assessment approaches and
policies that may differ from Australia's, and these approaches can result in
different estimations of risks and differences in the basis for setting target
values. Hence, it's not unusual for guideline values to vary from country to
country due to these different methodologies and calculations and the
choice of endpoints used.23
7.24 Dr Stuart Khan, Professor and Head of the School of Civil Engineering at the
University of Sydney, argued that the US EPA's PFAS limits would not be
practical to implement:
So what the US EPA have done is do all the research, collect all the data and
throw it out. They say, 'There's no safe level.' So they have this maximum
contaminant level goal, which is a purely aspirational statement … to say
there should be no PFAS in your water, but they don't use it. It's not
implementable. So, what they do is say: 'Here's what we think is measurable.
Here's what we think is achievable.' That's how they come up with the four
nanograms per litre for the PFAS chemicals.24
7.25 Dr Khan contended that Australia's approach has been the same as the World
Health Organization, and the rest of the world, and has relied on health-based
data and toxicological data to set the Guideline, 'with significant safety factors
built into that':
… unlike the US EPA, which have effectively plucked a number out of the
air to say, 'This is what we think is … a measurable value, because we've set
the maximum contaminant level goal at zero,' as they do for all genotoxic
chemicals.25

145
Lack of consistent national approach
7.26 Another issue identified in the committee's interim report was the lack of
national consistency in the regulation of PFAS in drinking water across various
Australian jurisdictions.26
7.27 Cancer Council Australia stated that, when it comes to water quality
management, instead of 'national policies or frameworks for monitoring and
testing, reporting and clean-up', Australia has 'multiple authorities making
reference to different guidelines and standards, each variant deemed relevant in
the particular circumstances'. The Council argued this structure makes a
consistent approach to water quality management 'impractical'.27
7.28 Mr Steve Becker from SciDev Limited agreed, indicating 'state-by-state
standards, in our experience, have the potential to erode public confidence in
the individual standards because of the differences from one state to the next'.28
Regulating PFAS individually or as a class for drinking water
7.29 The revised ADWG continue to specify safe levels of individual PFAS, whereas
some other international jurisdictions have a total or sum value for PFAS in
drinking water.29 The NHMRC considered developing a single PFAS value but
concluded 'there wasn't enough evidence to do that and also [it] required further
review'. Further, the NHMRC argued 'there seems to be a lack of consensus
about, particularly, the operational values that might be helpful in terms of
testing for all the PFAS'.30
7.30 However, some submitters warned that current regulation of individual PFAS
variants is too prescriptive, and they should be regulated as a group or class of
chemicals. For example, Dr Chartres stated:
The NHMRC made a statement when they released these water value
guidelines saying that they know little about the other PFAS variants that
we're all currently being exposed to and that, as they find out more data on
whether those PFAS are causing cancer, they will let us know and they will
reduce the values. That's just not good enough. What we need to be doing

of chemicals.31
7.31 By contrast, Dr Oliver Jones countered that there are thousands of individual
PFAS compounds which 'vary greatly in their chemical, physical, and
toxicological properties and [they] should not be grouped together for
regulatory purposes'. Dr Jones recommended they be regulated according to
their chemical properties or an informed risk assessment.32
Flow on effects of the ADWG to other regulations
7.32 There were concerns that the revised ADWG could have flow-on health and
economic impacts on other regulations, given the Guidelines are sometimes
used to regulate other water sources such as recycled water (discussed later in
this chapter) or are considered when making other regulations such as the
Tolerable Daily Intakes for food.33
7.33 The Australian Academy of Science remarked that the stricter revised
Guidelines, which cost more to comply with, may be overly prescriptive when
used for these other applications.34
Increased compliance costs from more stringent ADWG
7.34 Submitters and witnesses told the committee that the new, more stringent
ADWG would likely incur higher compliance costs—both because treatment
infrastructure will need to be developed or upgraded and, at the very least, more
sensitive water testing and monitoring will be required.
7.35 Water utilities observed there would likely be increased compliance costs
because of the more stringent ADWG. The Queensland Water Directorate
(qldwater) noted 'there is a very large cost associated with meeting the lower
Guidelines values'. It questioned whether the additional protection was justified
against the potential additional costs.35

7.36 Not all water treatment facilities can remove PFAS.36 Dr Khan observed that this
will mean 'significantly greater' costs in Australia as 'we have very little existing

7.37 The Water Services Association of Australia (WSAA) outlined that new
guidelines would require 'a limited number of utilities' to implement treatments
to meet safe levels of PFAS, with smaller utilities in regional and remote
Australia likely to bear the highest costs.38
7.38 Professor Oliver Jones concurred:
This cost would be higher for remote communities with smaller treatment
plants than for large cities already using (or close to using) advanced water
treatment at a large scale.39
7.39 The WSAA thought the costs were 'likely to be significant' given advanced water
treatment infrastructure can be 'capital and energy intensive' for utilities already
under pressure.40 The WSAA cited costs of $100–$200 million for a new water
treatment plan and $300–$400 million for a new desalination plant.41
7.40 WaterNSW agreed infrastructure would likely need to be improved:
Most of the implications would, I imagine, come in the source of treatment

as some of some of the investigation work that we're doing in the Blue
Mountains system, where we're trying to understand what the source of that
is, to see if remediation is a viable option for that system, to try and correct
what has happened up there.42

Testing and monitoring
7.42 Testing and monitoring is conducted on treated drinking water but may also be
completed on raw water sources, wastewater, recycled water and biosolids, in
accordance with strict collection and analysis protocols and standards.44
7.43 The impact of the revised ADWG on testing regimes is still being assessed.
However, the WSAA warned that more sensitive testing methods would likely
be required and testing costs could increase by 30 to 100 per cent,45 with costs
likely passed on to consumers.46
7.44 Private laboratories routinely test for up to 28 different PFAS compounds, with
more specialised services in some capital cities.47 However, this represents only
a small proportion of PFAS variants. The NHMRC advised 'the technical
capability of Australian commercial laboratories offering analytical services for
PFAS has increased over time. However, routine analysis of around 15 000 types
of PFAS is not considered to be technically feasible at this time'.48
7.45 qldwater likewise raised questions about the technical feasibility of testing for
PFAS under the revised Guidelines given 'testing proficiency at the detection
limits required is not yet widespread' and testing at the limits of detection has
cost implications.49 It estimated the cost of testing at around $1200 per sample
site.50

PFAS in water supplies
7.47 The committee's interim report discussed the status of PFAS testing of water
supplies, as well as the prevalence and extent of PFAS in drinking water.52
7.48 The water sector pointed out that, while they are not the source of PFAS
contamination, they are required to respond to contamination from upstream
activities. The sector takes a risk-based approach to monitoring and developing
mitigation strategies, identifying potential contamination sources, and treating
contaminated water.53
7.49 Water supplies most at risk of PFAS contamination are those on or adjacent to
sites where firefighting foams containing PFAS have been used—typically
Defence bases, airports, fire stations and training sites.54 For example, testing of
water around the Williamtown Defence base detected high levels of PFAS
contamination.55 Some water supplies may be at risk from one-off
contamination events like fires, spills, or accidents.56
Testing for PFAS in water supplies
7.50 Water testing requirements for PFAS are set by state and territory authorities,
with testing typically undertaken by state, territory and local governments and
water operators.57

Range of PFAS variants tested for in water supplies
7.55 The range of PFAS variants tested for is limited, with the 'regulated collection of
drinking water quality data by water utilities … limited to the three PFAS
health-based guideline values currently listed in the Australian Drinking Water
Guidelines'.65
7.56 The NHMRC acknowledged that other PFAS data may be collected.66 Water
utilities confirmed they test for PFAS not specified in ADWG.67
7.57 Friends of the Earth indicated 'many other PFAS chemicals have been detected
in water supplies', including perfluorohexanoic acid (PFHxA),
perfluoropentanoic acid (PFPeA), perfluoroheptanoic acid, (PFHpA), and
6:2-fluorotelomersulfonic acid (6:2 FTS). It stated 'no guidelines are proposed for
these chemicals at the present time'.68

7.58 The WSAA submitted that 'water utilities across the country have been
publishing and updating data on PFAS monitoring in raw water and drinking
water on their websites'.69
7.59 Other evidence confirmed that testing for PFAS is conducted and published by
many of the larger capital city water utilities. However, the extent of testing
across Australia is less clear, with publication dependent on state and territory
requirements.70

Drinking water in Australia is generally compliant with the new guidelines
7.65 The WSAA highlighted that Australia's historical manufacturing, industrial,
and land use activities mean that our drinking water is of high quality with
relatively little PFAS contamination:
Currently, for the vast majority of Australian communities, testing has
shown that the risks from PFAS are very low and drinking water complies
with the current and/or draft guideline levels. While water utilities will need
to remain vigilant and continue testing, most are unlikely to need treatment
interventions at this point.76
7.66 Testing and studies of PFAS in Australian water systems have given mixed
results. In numerous cases, PFAS levels are below ADWG levels. However, in
other instances, levels are above—sometimes well above—those specified in the
new Guidelines.77
7.67 For example, TasWater highlighted that it had conducted sampling of 70 raw
water sites in 2024 for 28 PFAS compounds at half the levels of the new
Guidelines—no PFAS was detected. TasWater confirmed that it tests quarterly
for sites where PFAS may be present but below ADWG, or annually where there
is little chance of PFAS being detected.78 Other utilities follow different testing
regimes, depending on their risk assessment.79
7.68 In November 2024, the NHMRC highlighted that most water supplies are below
the now current Guidelines, 'but that the existence of other water supplies with
higher PFAS levels cannot yet be ruled out'.80
7.69 Mr Anthony Amis from Friends of the Earth summarised PFAS testing results
from around the country, telling the committee:
… [SA Water] didn't find any in the major reservoirs, but I think the smaller
towns where this issue mainly comes up still haven't been tested at all.
Tasmania came up clear. In Victoria, the detections are sporadic. The higher

Testing of water supplies has been limited
7.70 Despite this, testing has been limited and in numerous cases water utilities and
operators have done no testing for PFAS in water supplies. The extent of
potential PFAS contamination is unknown.82 The NHMRC advised, in these
cases, 'water providers and regulators aim to manage unknown potential risks
to health from drinking water (such as the presence of unknown PFAS) by using
preventative risk management approaches as outlined in the risk management
framework of the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines'.83
7.71 Some submitters were distrustful of regulators and water utilities, especially
after being told water supplies were safe before there had been any PFAS
testing. Mr Jon Dee from the STOP PFAS community group elaborated:
… these entities should not have been assuring our communities that our
tap water was safe because there was no PFAS testing undertaken before
June 2024. So it's our view that they had no idea about it. They can't tell us
what were the levels of PFAS chemicals that we were exposed to …84
7.72 A national overview of PFAS detections in drinking water has not been
published since April 2011.85 More recent data compiled by Friends of the Earth
suggested that PFAS levels exceeded the previous Guidelines for certain PFAS
in drinking water supplies across certain chronically impacted communities,
impacting over 34 000 people.86 The group reported that short-term breaches to
the revised Guideline levels have occurred in raw water across a number of
communities, impacting over one million people.87

and resistant to degradation, which makes them particularly challenging to

7.74 The committee's interim report outlined several treatment technologies for
remediating PFAS found in water supplies, and their current limitations.89
7.75 A range of solutions are in use. These include sorption technologies which
capture PFAS from the water, such as granular activated carbon and ion
exchange resin; as well as filtration technologies, like nanofiltration and reverse
osmosis membranes.90
7.76 Representatives from the water industry highlighted that treatments vary in
effectiveness at removing PFAS from water supplies depending on
contamination levels, the type of PFAS, and other site-specific issues. For
example, the WSAA indicated that treatment facilities using reverse osmosis
and advanced oxidation demonstrated greater than 99 per cent removal of
PFOS, while other processes resulted in variable or even increased PFAS
concentrations.91
7.77 TasWater use activated carbon and nanofiltration at various sites, with
nanofiltration being 'one of the more effective technologies'.92
7.78 Veolia acknowledged that addressing contaminants close to their origin is
'generally more efficient and cost effective than relying on end-of-line treatment
processes'.93 It further explained that a range of treatment options currently
exist, and relevant expertise is available in Australia.94
7.79 Australia is trialling new PFAS treatment options to improve the quality of
drinking water, such as foam fractionation and new absorbents. Submitters

 two separate fuel tanker accidents on the Great Western Highway near the
Medlow Bath township in 1992 and 2002; and
 the Medlow Bath Rural Fire Service station.101
7.86 Secondary PFAS contamination was also likely to have occurred from soil,
sediment and concrete.102
7.87 Sampling and testing showed PFAS concentrations consistent with the use of
legacy firefighting foam, and suggested contamination moving downstream
from the Medlow/Greaves catchment 'via rainfall and surface runoff, and
previous water transfers between dams'.103
7.88 The water operator confirmed that Medlow Dam and Greaves Creek Dam
would remain disconnected from the Blue Mountains water supply system and
would only be reconnected 'once WaterNSW is confident appropriate
permanent mitigation measures are in place'.104
7.89 The NSW Environment Protection Authority recommended a more detailed site
investigation in line with the National Environment Protection (Assessment of Site
Contamination) Measure 1999 (Cth) (NEPM). WaterNSW confirmed this
investigation will help inform potential remediation options.105
7.90 Submitters noted that governments are increasingly focused on regulation of the
key risk of PFAS in water systems, with water infrastructure operators and other

Recycled water
7.91 While the Australian Guidelines for Water Recycling (Recycled Water Guidelines)
provide overall guidance, the WSAA indicated that there are no national
guidelines for PFAS in recycled water. The Concerned Waterways Alliance also
highlighted the lack of guidelines, writing 'no threshold levels exist for recycled
water, which is a major gap in the regulatory framework'.107
7.92 Submitters confirmed the Recycled Water Guidelines are used for some recycled
water purposes (e.g. purified recycled drinking water), along with the PFAS
National Environmental Management Plan (NEMP) and Australian and New
Zealand Guidelines for Fresh and Marine Water Quality.108
7.93 The Department of Environment advised the Recycled Water Guidelines are
being updated as a priority and confirmed that work on PFAS in drinking water
and freshwater, estuarine and marine water 'will be crucial in the work to
include PFAS into renewed recycled water guidelines'.109 However, further
work needs to be done to manage the risks in non-drinking recycled water
applications.110
7.94 Friends of the Earth reported that widespread testing of recycled water in
Victoria in 2023 found '21 different PFAS chemicals were detected'.111 The EPA
reported that 'none of the PFAS concentrations detected exceeded human health
guidelines'.112

Views of inquiry participants
7.95 Water utilities, community advocates, regulators and researchers put forward a
range of regulatory, monitoring, reporting, funding and research
recommendations to address contamination of water supplies by PFAS and the
potential flow-on health and environmental impacts.
Improve national regulation, leadership and collaboration
7.96 Submitters and witnesses called for a range of regulatory changes aimed at
improving national consistency, providing clear leadership, and improving
collaboration to improve Australia's water supplies.
National regulation
7.97 A number of submitters recommended enforceable national regulations for
PFAS in drinking water and other water supplies to overcome the current
regulatory uncertainties and inconsistencies.113 Mrs Tracey Anton expressed
frustration with the current system, saying 'we've got to stop this delineation of
state and federal, because it's not helping. There's no continuity'.114
7.98 A range of solutions were proposed. Dr Khan argued that this can be achieved
through an act to regulate safe water:
… the Commonwealth government should seek to take a much more active
role in setting drinking water quality and risk management requirements.
It's time for an Australian safe drinking water act. Such an act would enable
the Commonwealth to mandate consistent application of best practices for
managing drinking water quality, as defined by the Australian drinking
water guidelines nationally.115
7.99 Professor Jones supported legally binding regulation of PFAS, implemented
through a national Council of Ministers for the Environment, with consideration
of potential unintended effects before implementation.116
7.100 Consistent and enforceable national regulation for PFAS in recycled water was
also recommended, to help inform risk management and treatment processes.117

7.101 However, Cancer Council Australia thought there was no need for standalone
legislation for PFAS, pointing out it:
… is of the view that Commonwealth legislation to protect the environment
in various contexts has been designed to address, where necessary,
carcinogenic hazards. Cancer Council acknowledges that general legislation
deemed limited in providing a framework to respond to PFAS monitoring
and management may be amended or revised to ensure Australian's
exposure to PFAS remains below the allowable limits.118
7.102 Other submitters, such as STOP PFAS thought that the Guidelines need to be
more stringent, and follow the levels set by the US.119
Source control
7.103 There was widespread support for improved source control measures to totally
or significantly reduce the use of PFAS, including in consumer goods. Water
utilities were particularly in favour of source control, given they bear the burden
of water treatment. For example, the WSAA wrote:
Effective source control is widely recognised as the best strategy for PFAS
management and requires clear, strict, and enforceable regulations and
monitoring for PFAS in consumer products. Global evidence demonstrates
that implementing robust source control measures significantly reduces
exposure and associated risks.120
7.104 Water utilities and their peak body argued that source control would be more
sustainable, and improve outcomes for consumers:
The improvement of source control measures would alleviate the
operational burden on water utilities, reduce treatment costs, and lessen the
financial impact on customers and communities. This further enables the
broader objectives of the water sector to deliver sustainable, safe, clean, and
affordable water drinking supplies and wastewater services.121
7.105 Similar concerns about the importance of source control raised by the waste and
wastewater industries are covered in Chapter 9.

National leadership, coordination and collaboration
7.106 Inquiry participants recognised the need for stakeholders responsible for
reducing PFAS contamination and ensuring safe water supplies to work
together in coordinated and collaborative ways.122
7.107 The WSAA submitted that consistent application of the national guidelines and
a shared understanding of PFAS contamination risks will only occur where
there is coordination between regulators and frameworks are enforced.123
7.108 There were various calls for:
 strengthened leadership, coordination and collaboration, including through
a revised National Water Initiative;124
 a nationally coordinated approach to pollutants and contaminants
(including PFAS);125
 a taskforce comprised of regulatory agencies and relevant peak industry
bodies;126 and
 a national peak body to deal with PFAS management issues.127
7.109 The WSAA thought improved coordination and collaboration could be achieved
through the Australian Government's new national environment protection
agency—Environment Protection Australia. It envisaged the agency could
'significantly enhance coordination on PFAS source control, regulatory
consistency, and strategic oversight' and help ensure that the water sector is
actively engaged in shaping solutions to PFAS and other contaminants.128 The
WSAA envisaged an expanded role for the new agency:
… Environment Protection Australia could play a key role in this effort,
aligning the objectives of both the NEMP and [Industrial Chemicals
Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS)] processes and addressing

Increase government investment and support
7.119 Water utilities highlighted the increasing costs associated with managing PFAS
in drinking and wastewater—inclusive of both capital and operating costs for
treatment—particularly considering the more stringent Water Guidelines.141
The WSAA explained:
Given the substantial costs of PFAS management, there is an increasing need
for additional government support and investment. The water sector is
actively exploring opportunities to partner and collaborate with
governments and other sectors to address PFAS risks. Expecting water
utility customers alone to shoulder this financial burden is neither equitable
nor sustainable.142
7.120 Other submitters highlighted that smaller regional and remote water suppliers
and communities would need government assistance:
It is well-known that they often lack the finance, technology and expertise
that is available in capital city water agencies … For the small regional water
suppliers, the additional burden of tighter PFAS guidelines is likely to be
another overwhelming problem when they are already struggling to deliver
the best water quality they can with limited resources and often already
contaminated source water. All three levels of Government need to work
together to address this problem and help provide clean and safe drinking
water to all Australians, not just to people in big cities.143

membranes, you've still got a contaminated product. That destruction aspect
is really important and needs further investment. Our services are fully
