existence, and the likelihood that, if PFAS are banned individually, other PFAS
will simply be brought in and used in place of those that are banned.32
10.37 Director of Ecology Databank Services, Dr Kate Hughes argued the risk
associated with banning individual PFAS is that 'PFAS continues to be
manufactured, with new chemical formulae regularly replacing older ones that
are due for bans, phase out or restrictions'.33
10.38 Conversely, Chemistry Australia argued against 'adoption of definitions and
approaches that indiscriminately capture substances based on their chemical
structure alone'. In Chemistry Australia's view, banning PFAS as a class of
chemicals would be 'counterproductive':
It is widely acknowledged that the risks associated with PFAS substances
are not equal. It is, therefore, neither scientifically accurate nor appropriate
to group all PFAS together or take a one-size-fits-all regulatory approach.
Doing so risks diverting resources to low-risk substances and increasing the
complexity of regulatory activities.34
10.39 Environmental Risk Sciences Pty Ltd argued that Australia has 'well established
Government guidance on how to undertake a human health and ecological risk
assessment' and there is 'no reason for PFAS to be assessed differently to other
chemicals':
In fact, it is our experience that treating PFAS differently to other chemicals
can create practical, logistical, financial and risk communication issues.
These issues can outweigh any positive effects and benefits that may be
gained from applying an overly cautious approach to PFAS management.35
10.40 The National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC)—a portfolio
agency of the Department of Health, Disability and Aged Care—stated that
Commonwealth agencies responsible for classification and regulation of
chemicals have considered different approaches for grouping PFAS chemicals
and have not yet agreed on an approach. NHMRC has researched different
approaches undertaken by key overseas jurisdictions, including the European
Union, and determined that, at this stage, there are concerns 'about the
feasibility of implementing a guideline value for a PFAS sum/mixture with the
current options available, given the limited health evidence available for other

PFAS'. A method for grouping PFAS together for regulatory purposes 'may be

10.41 Mr Barden said that AICIS 'is able to and does group chemicals together for
assessment', which creates 'efficiencies', allowing AICIS to 'bring together
different datasets on chemicals that have similar characteristics, and … build
recommendations about risk management that can deal with those as a group'.
However, Mr Barden noted that under existing laws, regulatory changes (such
as restrictions) must still be enacted on a 'chemical-by-chemical basis'.37
10.42 Ms Burgess said that the Department of Environment groups chemicals together
in relation to scheduling decisions, wherever 'possible and appropriate', but
does not believe it would be appropriate to 'consider all PFAS at once'.38
10.43 The department expanded on this response, submitting:
PFAS are a diverse group of chemicals that exhibit a range of structural,
behavioural and functional properties. This means that their risk
characteristics vary.
From an environmental risk management perspective, not all PFAS would
meet the requirements for listing in Schedule 7, which would prohibit their
import, export, manufacture, or use.
For example, the PFAS perfluorobutanesulfonic acid (PFBS), is commonly
used for its water and oil repellent properties in cleaning products, waxes,
polishes and surface coatings.
The Commonwealth Risk Assessment for PFBS concluded that it was
persistent in the environment, not bioaccumulative and not toxic under the
Australian Environmental Criteria for Persistent, Bioaccumulative, and/or
Toxic Chemicals. This means that under the principles, PFBS would be a
schedule 4 or 5 chemical and that activities relating to PFBS would not be
prohibited.39
Details of the IChEMS listing
10.44 Submitters raised concerns with some of the detail of the IChEMS listing for
PFAS, including the current limit for 'unintentional' PFAS contamination in
imported products under the Schedule 7 regulation.
10.45 The Queensland Water Directorate noted that the limit for 'unintentional' PFAS
is 25 μg/kg for PFOS, PFOA, PFHxS, and suggested this is too high, considering

10.57 Noting that Australian companies already do not manufacture PFAS

on importation, storage and use of PFAS, saying this 'could provide valuable

National PFAS policy and coordination
10.58 State and territory governments are responsible for day-to-day regulation of
pollution prevention and contamination management. However, the
Commonwealth Department of Environment sets national standards aimed at
achieving 'better protection of the environment through improved management
of environmental risks and achieving nationally consistent outcomes across the
Commonwealth, states and territories'.53
10.59 The Department of Environment explained that standards and guidelines are
developed which 'support those responsible for managing chemicals to apply
risk proportionate mitigation measures, where necessary', and that the
department works closely with states and territories to ensure these standards
are implemented consistently across jurisdictions.54
National Environment Protection Measures
10.60 The Commonwealth government provides regulatory guidance through the
National Environmental Protection Council Act 1994 (Cth) (NEPC Act) and the
National Environment Protection Council (NEPC). The NEPC has two primary
functions:
 to make National Environment Protection Measures (NEPMs); and
 to assess and report on the implementation and effectiveness of NEPMs in
participating jurisdictions.55
10.61 The Department of Environment oversees the National Environment Protection
(Assessment of Site Contamination) Measure 1999 (ASC NEPM) which 'aims to
provide adequate protection of human health and the environment where site
contamination has occurred, through implementing a national approach to
assessment of site contamination'.56
52 Australian Academy of Science, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe (received 30
June 2025), p. 2.
53 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33 (47th
Parliament), pp. 6–7.

10.62 While the current version of the ASC NEPM does not include explicit guidance
on PFAS, the Department of Environment explained that 'non-mandatory
guidance consistent with the ASC NEPM is available in the PFAS National
Environmental Management Plan' (PFAS NEMP). Commonwealth agencies,
including the Department of Defence and Airservices, utilised both the ASC
NEPM and the PFAS NEMP when conducting PFAS investigations and
planning remediation activity.57
10.63 The Department of Environment submitted that existing standards and
guidance for industrial chemical management, water quality and contamination
management 'set a clear pathway for consistent national action to respond to
PFAS, including by industry as the main users of chemicals'. The department
indicated it 'works closely' with chemical regulators across the Commonwealth,
and states and territories, and concluded:
[PFAS] Interventions should be taken with the understanding that results
may take many decades to achieve. Strong policy and regulatory
frameworks that are fit-for-purpose, modern and align with community
expectations help to protect the environment. Governance and coordination
mechanisms should ensure clear oversight, accountability and consistency
in decision-making, response and management of not just PFAS, but other
chemicals of concern and contamination issues.58
10.64 The Environmental Defenders Office (EDO) recommended reform of the
National Environment Protection Measures framework to better regulate PFAS.
EDO noted that NEPMs made under the NEPC Act 'are legislative instruments
which are given effect by individual legislation and guidelines in each state and
territory'.59
10.65 Further, while NEPMs provide 'nationally agreed guidance in relation to a
variety of prescribed environmental matters … there is no legal requirement to
enact NEPMs into enforceable national or subnational law'.60
10.66 According to EDO, the ASC NEPM has been used as a guide for investigation,
assessment and management of PFAS contamination on Commonwealth land,
such as Defence bases and airports. However, 'uptake of the Site Contamination
Policy Framework and enforceability of the ASC NEPM between jurisdictions is
uneven'.61
57 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33 (47th
Parliament), p. 7.
58 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33 (47th

10.67 EDO therefore recommended:
In order to ensure a consistent regulatory and legislative approach, NEPMs
such as the ASC NEPM must be wholly integrated into enforceable state and
territory laws. This will ensure that the enforcement, mitigation and
prevention of PFAS contamination is addressed evenly throughout
Australia.62
PFAS National Environmental Management Plan
10.68 Inquiry participants expressed a range of views about national coordination of
PFAS management and remediation in Australia, including the PFAS NEMP.
10.69 The complexity of dealing with PFAS contamination was acknowledged by key
submitters, including leading PFAS research organisation, crcCARE:
In Australia, the challenges are particularly pronounced from policy, risk
assessment, and remediation perspectives. Currently, Australian policies
regarding PFAS management are fragmented and lack comprehensive
national standards. This inconsistency creates gaps in addressing
contamination, leading to delays in effective risk communication and
mitigation strategies.63
10.70 As discussed in the committee's interim report, the PFAS NEMP is Australia's
national guide for managing PFAS contamination in the environment. It has
been jointly developed by the Commonwealth, state, territory, and New
Zealand governments through the Heads of Environmental Protection
Authorities Australia and New Zealand (HEPA), to provide nationally
consistent guidance.64
10.71 The NEMP has been updated multiple times to 'reflect new scientific evidence
and guidance'. The current version—NEMP 3.0—was adopted on 10 December
2024 and includes new and additional guidance and standards on priority areas,
including:
 new guideline values for investigation and risk assessment;
 guidance around remediation of contaminated land;
 guidance on re-use of resource recovery products; and
 risk-based criteria for re-use of biosolids contaminated with PFAS.65
10.72 The Department of Environment also noted that with respect to the
management of wildlife (which is primarily the responsibility of state and
territory governments and land managers), NEMP 3.0 'provides nationally
62 Environmental Defenders Office, Submission 55 (47th Parliament), p. 8.
63 crcCARE, Submission 36 (47th Parliament), p. 2.
64 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, PFAS National
Environmental Management Plan 3.0, last updated 4 March 2025 (accessed 27 June 2025).
65 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, PFAS National
Environmental Management Plan 3.0, last updated 4 March 2025.
215
agreed guidance for managing the impacts of PFAS contamination on matters
of national environmental significance' under the Environment Protection and
Biodiversity Conservation Act 1999 (Cth).66
10.73 According to EDO, the updated NEMP 'complements' the standards established
under the IChEMS, which 'ban, severely restrict, or reduce the environmental
impact of three PFAS along with over 500 related substances'. The NEMP
'provides a prescriptive framework for the community, environmental
regulators, governments and industry to address PFAS contamination'.
However, the NEMP is 'not enforceable':
Accordingly, the National PFAS Plan affords significant discretion to
decision-makers and regulators in the extent to which they implement the
National PFAS Plan framework. The National PFAS Plan therefore does
little to create concrete standards or enforceable mechanisms by which to
hold polluters to account.67
10.74 Environmental consultants, Dr Matthew Askeland and Mr Andrew Mitchell
were concerned that existing regulatory tools are addressing 'only three of the
thousands of known PFAS' and coordination between agencies 'is limited'. They
argued that 'poor coordination between regulatory agencies' causes
'inefficiencies and uneven enforcement', with the majority of 'cleanup' falling to
water authorities and waste processors, who do not create PFAS contamination,
but are 'left to manage it'.68
10.75 Toxics Free Australia criticised the NEMP as failing to address the source of
PFAS pollution, saying the NEMP is 'essentially a mechanism that defines the
allowable levels of PFAS pollution in our Australian environment' but does not
attempt to '"turn off the tap" to this ongoing source of harm'.69
10.76 Section 1 of NEMP 3.0 confirms that addressing 'current use and management
of PFAS-containing products and articles' is not within the scope of the NEMP.
It also states that environmental regulators 'may take action to restrict and
manage the use of PFAS-containing products and articles under national or their
jurisdictional legislation'.70
10.77 Dr Askeland and Mr Mitchell contended that, while the NEMP 'has been
pioneering', the latest draft 'mixes basic "PFAS 101" material with regulatory
66 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, response to written questions
from Senator Thorpe (received 21 July 2025), p. 2.
67 Environmental Defenders Office, Submission 55 (47th Parliament), p. 9.
68 Dr Matthew Askeland and Mr Andrew Mitchell, Submission 60 (47th Parliament), p. 4.
69 Toxics Free Australia, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe (received 2 May 2025),
[p. 9].
70 Heads of EPA Australia and New Zealand (HEPA), PFAS National Environmental Management Plan
Version 3.0, 2025, p. 5 (accessed 27 June 2025).
216
guidance, potentially confusing end-users'. They proposed that the NEMP
should 'be concise, clear, and risk-proportionate, and consider international best
practices, such as adopting holistic regulatory frameworks'.71
10.78 Dr Wells and Professor Lea reported seeking 'regulatory information' during the
conduct of their research but found the NEMP was 'severely lacking in resources
pertaining to wildlife or risks to wildlife'. They also submitted that the NEMP
was 'difficult to understand and interpret as a lay person'.72
10.79 Toxics Free Australia was concerned that the NEMP does not address the issue
of airborne PFAS emissions.73 Dr Askeland and Mr Mitchell echoed this concern,
saying:
PFAS exposure through dust, aerosols, and volatilised forms is often
overlooked. PFAS can bind to dust particles, travel through the air, or
volatilise under certain conditions. This contributes to both direct human
exposure and broader environmental spread. For example, PFAS-
contaminated dust settling on roof catchments can impact rainwater
collection systems.74
10.80 Friends of the Earth was critical that the NEMP allows PFAS in biosolids 'to be
"diluted" by mixing in soil that does not contain PFAS'.75
10.81 Asked how Australia is 'tracking the reduction of PFAS through various
sources', the Department of Environment submitted that states and territories
'undertake the majority of environmental monitoring'. However, the
government's initiative to include PFAS as an indicator in the ABS National
Health Measures Survey will 'strengthen PFAS exposure'.76
PFAS NEMP and fresh, estuarine and marine water
10.82 Overarching environmental frameworks and measures exist to manage PFAS in
rivers, lakes, groundwater, wastewater, and recycled water:
 Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act 1999 (Cth);
 National Environmental Protection Council Act 1994 (Cth) and related NEPM;
and

10.83 Local regulatory tools and measures are in place to help manage PFAS
contamination in water supplies in Australia, with state, territory, local
governments and other stakeholders generally responsible for management of
the actual water resources.78
10.84 The overarching National Water Quality Management Strategy is a voluntary
framework, that provides nationally agreed policy, process, and guidelines on
the management of water quality across all water types, for the productive and
sustainable use of water for a variety of purposes, including drinking,
environment, industry, recreation, cultural and spiritual purposes.79 A range of
guidelines for water quality management have been issued under the Strategy.80
10.85 However, there were criticisms of the water regulation framework. For example,
EDO noted there is 'currently no NEPM which relates to marine, estuarine and
freshwater quality'. EDO recommended the Australian Government develops 'a
water quality National Environment Protection Measure to establish
enforceable water quality standards, objectives and goals to protect our water
systems from PFAS contamination'.81
10.86 The Concerned Waterways Alliance recommended the introduction of
'enforceable national limits for PFAS concentrations in soil, water, and biota to
standardise management efforts across states', and the alignment of Australian

Views of state, territory and local governments
10.87 State, territory and local government entities that participated in the inquiry
recognised PFAS contamination as a significant ongoing concern. State and local
governments described supporting and participating in the NEMP, though
some had concerns about the impacts of stronger regulation, including more
restrictive drinking water guidelines, and the resource requirements of
implementing NEMP 3.0.83
10.88 The Tasmanian Government submitted that implementing the revised NEMP
will create 'significant challenges and costs' to Tasmania, with at least one expert
needing to be hired for three years 'to develop an understanding of Tasmania's
requirements in relation to the PFAS NEMP 3.0, and to commence the first stages
of implementation'. Environmental Protection Authority Tasmania (EPA Tas)
expects to prioritise reviewing PFAS monitoring and treatment requirements,
with a view to imposing:
… limits of PFAS in effluent/wastes being discharged to land or waters) at
wastewater treatment plants and associated reuse schemes and biosolids
application, and monitoring (and treatment) of PFAS levels in leachate from
landfills.84
10.89 EPA Tas also noted that its primary legislation 'requires the EPA to regulate by
individual activity/premise', meaning monitoring and reporting requirements,
treatment requirements, and 'imposition of discharge limits' have to be
considered individually. EPA Tas expects to apply 'a risk-based approach … in
determining which activities should be addressing PFAS issues as a priority'.85
10.90 The Northern Territory Department of Lands, Planning and Environment (NT
DLPE) submitted that state and territory governments have a role to play in
supporting national PFAS initiatives, including by incorporating national
guidance documents into jurisdictional management approaches, but need to be
resourced to do so:

With detailed and clear guidance from national level guidelines,
jurisdictions have a firm basis to adopt more uniform measures to prevent,
control and manage the risks of PFAS to human health and the environment,
using their existing legislation. However adequate resourcing at
jurisdictional level is necessary to drive successful outcomes.86
10.91 Brisbane City Council highlighted a number of issues it faces in implementing
national PFAS policies, including:
 financial and resource implications
 closed landfill leachate management
 project management at parks and closed landfill sites
 regulatory and policy issues
 household waste collection, treatment and disposal
 reuse of soil contaminated with low levels of PFAS
 use of pesticide and herbicide products.87
10.92 The Council noted a lack of 'clear and consistent national standards', including
in relation to acceptable levels of PFAS in biosolids reuse and argued there is a
need to shift from addressing 'end-of-life/contamination issues towards limiting
the manufacturing and importing of PFAS -containing products'. Brisbane City
Council also proposed that policies be developed which acknowledge 'legacy'
contamination, such as by introducing 'a reduced landfill levy when
remediating public space'. Addressing these issues 'requires urgent national-
level action and support'.88
10.93 The Tasmanian Government expressed its intention to 'work collaboratively to
develop broadscale monitoring programs and policy in relation to PFAS' but
said it would 'welcome Australian Government support for funding the
implementation of the PFAS NEMP 3.0'.89
10.94 The Local Government Association of Queensland recommended
Commonwealth and state governments work collaboratively with local
government 'to ensure a pragmatic and phased transition approach to the
introduction of any new standards relating to PFAS'. It also recommended an
'appropriate level of funding and support [be provided] to local government
where increases in PFAS standards are required'.90
10.95 The Department of Environment submitted that the NEMP is implemented by
individual jurisdictions 'under their own legislation and regulations', the

Views of inquiry participants
10.96 Inquiry participants acknowledged the valuable role played by the NEMP in
providing national guidance for PFAS management and coordination, while
making a number of suggestions for improving the NEMP and its application
across jurisdictions.
10.97 Loop Organics noted that existing mechanisms, including the NEMP, allow
'thresholds [to] be determined by each states' regulatory authority', leading to
'different regulations State by State'. This is confusing for industry and
consumers. Loop Organics recommended 'nationally agreed thresholds and
regulations [to] provide a level playing field and greatest public confidence'.92
10.98 EDO recommended that the Commonwealth seeks to develop the National
PFAS Plan 'into a distinct PFAS Environment Protection Measure to be created
under and in accordance with the National Environment Protection Council Act
1994'.93
10.99 The Water Services Association of Australia recommended a 'collaborative
approach, led by a taskforce of regulatory agencies and relevant peak industry
bodies' to 'enhance coordination and information-sharing'. It suggested a new
national body—Environment Protection Australia, which is being established
by the Commonwealth government—'could play a key role in this effort,
aligning the objectives of both the NEMP and IChEMS processes and addressing
regulatory gaps'.94
10.100Dr Matthew Askeland and Mr Andrew Mitchell submitted that the NEMP
'must be refined'. While initial versions 'were groundbreaking', they argued
NEMP 3.0 'should undergo an independent review to ensure it is risk-
proportionate, clear, and actionable':
Separating technical "PFAS 101" information from regulatory guidance
would improve usability. Better collaboration with industry and more

10.101Enviropacific suggested there is a need to 'harmonise PFAS management

10.102Some submitters outlined concerns that state environmental protection
agencies are hampered by the Commonwealth, with one submitter arguing:
We will only get effective PFAS contamination management if the [state]
EPA is given complete independence from Government and is given the

Governments, and other organisations to account. It cannot happen whilst
the EPA is beholden to other Government departments. Ideally another

Public information, health messaging, and precautionary signage
10.103A number of submitters proposed that PFAS contamination should be
monitored and publicly reported by an independent national body.98
10.104EDO also argued for 'greater transparency' around notifying communities of
PFAS incidents and possible risks to human health, recommending that the
government mandates 'monitoring and reporting on PFAS contaminated sites
and exposure events be made publicly available, for example through the
creation of a consolidated national contaminated sites register'.99
10.105This recommendation was echoed by the Australian Academy of Science which
proposed:
A public PFAS exposure map, drawn from government data collected using
published, consistent, methodology and data standards, would provide a
clear, accurate, and nationally coordinated picture of PFAS concentrations
in the Australian environment. This would be of value to lawmakers,
policymakers, regulators and the general public. The map of exposure sites
against environmental guideline values produced by the UK Royal Society
for Chemistry is a useful model. Professor Oliver Jones at RMIT University

10.109The Australian Academy of Science recommended that public health
messaging about PFAS more clearly delineate between communities that are
exposed and the wider community, where PFAS contamination levels are low.
Professor Oliver Jones from RMIT University said:
We want to be very careful about worrying people, because there will be
people, such as firefighters and those at the Wreck Bay community, for
example, who have been exposed to very high concentrations that,
potentially, have a health effect. The everyday person has probably got a
small—85 per cent, according to the latest data, have some PFAS in their
blood—amount in them, but you don't want to worry people
unnecessarily.103
10.110Representatives from the Department of Defence noted that the department

impacted by PFAS contamination from Defence facilities, including the
Katherine River. In addition, Defence has recently undertaken work with the
Victorian EPA and the Field & Game Association to install signage at the Heart
Morass.104

Department of Defence stated that the body is being established 'with senior
representation from all key portfolios, including health, environment and
infrastructure, to promote a stronger and more integrated whole-of-government

10.116In its response, the Government acknowledged that 'complete removal of PFAS
from the environment is not possible'. As such, remediation should aim to
'render a site acceptable and safe for long term continuation of its existing use
or proposed use'. The Government outlined its intention to work with states and
territories to review approaches to the assessment of site contamination in the
National Environment Protection (Assessment of Site Contamination) Measure
1999 and the PFAS National Environment Management Plan, 'with a view to
streamlining standards and guidance for PFAS in the environment'.109
10.117The Government will also work across jurisdictions to develop national
guidance to 'address situations where PFAS has migrated across jurisdictional
boundaries' and to increase information-sharing about contaminated sites:
The Government acknowledges that State/Territory and local governments
control how information about PFAS contamination is recorded and
communicated to property owners in their jurisdictions. … The National
Coordinating Body will work with the NSW Government (as a pilot) to
assess existing guidance in the NSW state and local government planning
and environment frameworks. The Government will seek to identify
opportunities to improve consistency and transparency in how the presence
of historical contamination is communicated to potentially affected property
owners.110
Data and research
10.118This section considers Australian research and data collection on PFAS,
including in areas such as: PFAS distribution and scale; impacts on human
health; impacts on wildlife in the environment; remediation strategies and
technologies; and waste management and destruction.
10.119The CSIRO noted that while an extensive body of data and information exists,
'significant knowledge gaps' remain in relation to 'PFAS distribution and scale
(i.e., waters, soils, sediments and biota)'. The CSIRO submitted there is 'likely an
underestimation of the future impacts of PFAS in Australia'.111
108 Department of Defence, Independent Review: Government response to the Independent Review of land
uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS contamination, December 2024, [pp. 9–11].
109 Department of Defence, Independent Review: Government response to the Independent Review of land
uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS contamination, December 2024, [p. 9].
110 Department of Defence, Independent Review: Government response to the Independent Review of land
uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS contamination, December 2024, [pp. 10–11].
111 CSIRO, Submission 22 (47th Parliament), p. 4.
226
10.120While there is research on PFAS in Australian wildlife, most of the data has
been collected from deceased animals or blood samples and therefore 'lacks key
health information'. CSIRO aims to close these gaps by:
… evaluating PFAS burdens in conjunction with health and fitness measures
using a systems biology and multi-omics ecotoxicology approach. This
approach aims to identify major molecular pathways with a significant
association to survival, reproduction and fitness.112
10.121Although ad hoc data is collected by water authorities, health and
environmental agencies, and academic researchers, there is currently no
comprehensive data collection on levels of PFAS in humans, water, or the
environment in Australia. The Australian Government has recently invested in
further data collection and research, including:
 Provision of $800 000 to the Australian Bureau of Statistics to include PFAS
biomarker testing in the Intergenerational Health and Mental Health Study
to 'help us to understand PFAS exposure levels in the Australian
population'. The main collection of samples occurred over 2022–23, with
study results to be released in 2025.
 Provision of $11.7 million for health and medical research through the
NHMRC Targeted Call for Research scheme to 'increase our understanding
of the acute and long-term potential human health effects from PFAS
exposure'. Nine grants have been awarded with research to be presented by
2025.113
 Provision—through the Department of Defence—of approximately $26
million in funding for 21 research and technology programs to support
PFAS investigation and remediation activities, and $12.5 million to establish
a National Research Program to study the potential effects of PFAS
exposure on human health. Since 2018, Defence and CSIRO have been
working together on a PFAS research program. The outcomes of this
research are applied to remediate and manage PFAS contamination at
Defence bases, and to develop better processes for construction and
maintenance work where PFAS is encountered.114
10.122The NHMRC clarified that, in relation to its $11.7 million Targeted Call for
Research:
Applications are investigator-initiated and therefore based on the expertise
and research interests of those applying for funding. It is also important to
note that grants are awarded based on competitive peer review with the best

science and most significant research proposals funded based on the

Contamination Research when led by crcCARE in Australia ensured a

launched the Australian National Remediation Framework and also

10.125The Australia & New Zealand Biosolids Partnership advocated for more
research into understanding environmental and human exposure to PFAS,
recommending 'expansion from "extent" to "extent and effects"' of PFAS

HREC should be then nationally accepted without further paperwork. Data
custodians should accept that HRECs have properly considered data
privacy. Annual reporting could similarly be to one HREC.127
Role of research organisations
10.135Due to the complex and technical nature of the PFAS problem, scientific,
engineering, and medical research organisations have a sizeable role to play in
the development of public policy and regulatory frameworks. Relevant
submitters highlighted their willingness to contribute to these processes and
made suggestions for how governments can utilise their expertise.
10.136CSIRO submitted that it 'recognises the scale of the problem, and the urgent
need to prioritise and act so Australians and their environments can be safe'.
While the CSIRO 'does not have a role with establishing or operationalising
federal or state regulatory frameworks', it acknowledged that it can 'contribute
appropriate science outcomes' to 'underpin their establishment and revision':
We partner across the breadth of regulatory agencies, industry and
community groups to understand and fill knowledge gaps and advance
technologies that would assist with defining, prioritising and controlling
risks from chemicals such as PFAS.128

Potential options for phasing out PFAS in Australia
10.150Many inquiry participants recommended phasing out, or banning the use of,
PFAS in Australia. However, not all submitters agreed with a blanket ban. This
section considers options for phasing out PFAS in Australia, including how any
such ban could be implemented.143
10.151Many submitters, including water utilities, scientific bodies, and state and local
government groups, highlighted the benefits of addressing PFAS at the source,
rather than attempting to remediate contamination once it is in the environment.
The Australia & New Zealand Biosolids Partnership advocated for 'root-cause
source control' of PFAS chemicals, noting it is cheaper to prevent PFAS entering
the environment than it is to clean it up. It recommended:
 Bans on industrial use,
 A phasing out of domestic use,
 A requirement for labelling of products that contain PFAS (such as
makeup, insecticides, clothing, non-stick pans, and food packaging etc),
 A need to communicate proportionately the effect of PFAS, and the
exposure pathways, because the mention of PFAS—which is

10.152CSIRO reported on 'increasing calls' to regulate or phase out PFAS
internationally:
The European Chemicals Agency (ECHA) is currently evaluating a
submission by European Union (EU) Countries (Germany, Denmark, the
Netherlands, Norway, and Sweden) to restrict the 'manufacture, placing on
the market, and use of PFAS' in Europe. The persistent organic pollutants
regulation in the EU restricts the use of PFOS, PFOA, PFHxS and related
compounds. The EU REACH list of Substances of Very High Concern
included other PFAS chemicals (e.g., PFHpA, PFBS), as having probable
serious effects to human health and the environment, to be replaced with
safer alternatives. ... The USA has initiated or is considering numerous bills
or restriction on PFAS manufacture and use to protect human and
environmental health. In 2021, Maine was the first US state to bring into law
a phase out of the sale and use of products containing intentionally added
PFAS by 2030, except for 'currently unavoidable' uses. The law was modified
in 2024 to a phase out of products by 2032 and ban of PFAS in key products
such as cookware, textiles, children's products, cosmetics, and menstrual
products by 2026 instead of 2030, and in artificial turf, outside apparel for
extreme weather conditions and fluorinated containers by 2029. Minnesota
state has since introduced in 2023 a law to restrict the uses of PFAS by 2032,
ban PFAS in products such as cookware, dental floss, and menstrual
products by 2025, and require companies to disclose if they are using PFAS
in any product.145
10.153The Minderoo Foundation provided further international examples:
 In 2020 Denmark implemented a ban on the use of PFAS in paper-, board
based and cellulose-based packaging.
 In January 2024 New Zealand announced a ban on the use of PFAS in
cosmetic products from the end of 2026.
 In April 2024 the US the U.S. House of Representatives introduced 'the
Forever Chemical Regulation and Accountability Act of 2024', a proposal
to phase out all non-essential uses of PFAS in ten years.
 In August 2024 Canada announced a precautionary, group-based
objective for drinking water, establishing limits based on a sum of 25
PFAS.
 For 2025: The European Union (EU) has committed to extending its phase
out of PFAS, moving from restricting select subgroups towards a
complete ban on PFAS in consumer products (aside from specific
industrial applications of PFAS where no viable substitutes are available
and the chemicals are deemed critical for health or technology).146

the precautionary principal would be upheld. Exceptions to bans should
only be made in rare 'essential use' applications where no alternatives exist
and PFAS compounds are used in controlled and closed environments with
insignificant risk for environmental leakage or human exposure.159

species, including humans. The health impacts of these ubiquitous
contaminants were significant, and they were finally (well almost) banned.
Bans and restrictions on other POPs followed during the ensuing years but

Part 2
Committee views and recommendations

Chapter 11
Committee views on health and related issues
Introduction
11.1 PFAS represents a wicked problem for Australian governments, including the
Commonwealth. While PFAS chemicals have never been manufactured in
Australia, products containing PFAS have been ubiquitous across the economy,
with some used heavily before, and even after they were known to be hazardous
to the environment and living things.
11.2 A number of PFAS variants—including PFOS (perfluorooctane sulfonic acid),
PFOA (perfluorooctanoic acid) and PFHxS (perfluorohexane sulfonic acid)—are
now present in almost every part of the environment and almost every human
being on earth, albeit generally at low levels.
11.3 An ever-expanding body of international research warns that these, and
potentially other, PFAS can act as endocrine disrupters, impacting hormones,
reproductive health, and the immune systems of living organisms—including
human beings. At least one PFAS—PFOA—has now been classified as
carcinogenic by an international cancer body, and PFOS has been listed as
potentially carcinogenic.
11.4 While scientific evidence around the health impacts of these chemicals continues
to grow, more research—particularly around long-term impacts—remains to be
done and, in Australia, debate continues as to the significance of any human
health effects.
11.5 Throughout the inquiry, the committee heard from regulators, scientific bodies,
academics, doctors, and toxicologists from Australia and overseas jurisdictions.
There were differing views and interpretations among scientists and
organisations, showing that reasonable people exposed to all sides of the
argument may reach different conclusions. Indeed, members of the committee
hold differing views and perspectives on the evidence received, making it
difficult to reach consensus on these matters.
11.6 This chapter summarises the debate around PFAS and human health,
presenting the committee's views on the evidence it has heard throughout the
inquiry and drawing conclusions where possible. The next chapter provides the
committee's analysis of evidence across other policy areas, including
remediation, technology and national regulation.
11.7 Across these two chapters, where a majority of committee members could agree,
the committee has drawn conclusions and made recommendations. Where
consensus could not be reached, matters have been addressed by individual
senators in their additional comments.
241
242
Current state of the science on the health effects of PFAS
11.8 Much of the evidence received by the committee spoke to the difficulties in
establishing a conclusive causal link between PFAS exposure and adverse health
effects, and the different lines of evidence required to establish such a link. Two
lines of evidence on which the committee received information were
epidemiological studies and experiments on laboratory animals; this evidence
is considered below.
Epidemiological studies and experiments on laboratory animals
11.9 Epidemiological studies, particularly randomized controlled trials, are often
considered the 'gold standard' for informing health policy.
11.10 The committee heard that there are significant challenges to conducting
epidemiological studies on the health impacts of chemicals like PFAS.
Epidemiological studies are typically conducted with a large population group
of around 50 000 people over long timeframes. However, inquiry participants
identified several complicating factors when attempting to use these types of
studies for determining the health effects of PFAS exposure:
 First, epidemiological studies have to account for a range of other factors
contributing to disease, such as lifestyle, diet, family history, smoking, and
alcohol consumption.
 Second, adverse health effects from PFAS may occur long after exposure.
This means the studies need to be very long-term. It also means adverse
health effects may be occurring and even perpetuated long before scientific
studies can conclusively identify them as causally related.
 Third, because epidemiological studies are observational studies, scientists
can only really draw associations, not causations, about the relationship
between exposure to PFAS and health effects.
 Fourth, while most of the population may have chronic low exposure to
PFAS, a much smaller cohort has high exposure. Epidemiology is less useful
for finding answers from small cohorts of people because minor random
variations can easily alter the findings and affect the conclusions.
 Fifth, studies need to be replicable, and therefore robust results are
generally drawn from multiple studies producing similar findings. This is a
time-consuming and resource-intensive process and one for which, the
committee was told, the appropriate funding pathways in Australia do not
currently exist.
11.11 With a shortage of large-scale epidemiological studies on PFAS, scientists have
often relied on findings from experiments on laboratory animals, and
observations from smaller-scale studies.
11.12 Animal studies also have limitations. Animal studies tend to give short-lived
laboratory animals larger doses in expectation of a quicker effect. Therefore,
many animal studies try to compare a higher dose given to an animal over a
243
year with a much lower dose experienced by people over decades. In addition,
humans and animals react differently to PFAS, and not all effects observed in
animals may occur in humans.
11.13 Nevertheless, the weight of evidence has swayed some of the world's regulators
to impose tight restrictions on PFAS chemicals, seeking to phase out their use to
protect the environment and human health. Further, an eminent international
body—the International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC) at the World
Health Organization—has now decided there is sufficient evidence to classify
PFOA as carcinogenic, and PFOS as possibly carcinogenic.
Commonwealth government agencies' position and Guidance Statement
11.14 In evidence to the committee, the general position of Commonwealth
government agencies, as well as the reviews that they rely on for formulating
their health advice, is that there is no current evidence that supports a large
impact on a person's health, even as a result of high levels of PFAS exposure,
and that the health differences between people who have the highest exposure
to PFAS and those who have had low exposure are generally small.
11.15 The enHealth guidance statement on PFAS represents Australia's official
position on the health impacts of PFAS. enHealth states that the effects of PFAS
exposure have generally been small and are unlikely to be important to health
outcomes. It also states that a causative relationship between health effects and
PFAS exposure has not been established to date.
11.16 The enHealth statement, as revised in 2024, draws on the Australian National
University (ANU) PFAS Health Study, which was funded by the
Commonwealth and included voluntary blood testing in three communities.
11.17 This study found elevated blood serum concentrations of PFAS and clear
evidence of psychological distress in impacted communities, along with
elevated cholesterol levels. It noted a number of other associations, including
increased levels of uric acid, reduced kidney function, and lower birth weight
in babies. However, evidence of these other adverse health impacts was either
inconsistent across communities, or not strong enough to establish a clear
connection to PFAS exposure.
11.18 The committee acknowledges that the ANU study provided limited evidence of
adverse health outcomes. However, the committee notes the study's authors
said it was not designed to investigate whether PFAS cause health effects, as that
would require investigation of people over time (and the ANU study was point-
in-time).
11.19 Other evidence that contributed to the 2024 revision of the enHealth statement
included the 27th Australian total diet study, discussed in Chapter 2 of this
report.
244
11.20 Based on its review of the science, enHealth determined that the health impacts
of PFAS are likely to be small. enHealth also stated that specific health and
disease screening, other health interventions, and lifestyle modifications are
unnecessary, even for highly exposed groups in Australia.
11.21 For example, while the enHealth statement advises pregnant women to
minimise their exposure to PFAS as a precaution, enHealth does not recommend
that mothers living in or around sites contaminated with PFAS cease breast
feeding.
11.22 The statement does, however, acknowledge that local authorities have advised
people in certain locations to limit consumption of PFAS-contaminated water,
home-grown produce, home-produced livestock, and locally harvested food
such as fish, to reduce PFAS exposure.
Other perspectives on the scientific evidence
11.23 The committee received submissions and witness testimony that challenged the
position of Commonwealth government agencies on the inconclusive nature of
the scientific evidence regarding the health effects of PFAS.
11.24 Several inquiry participants argued that sufficient evidence already exists to
indicate with reasonable scientific certainty that PFAS exposure—probably even
at low levels, but certainly at high levels—has adverse health effects in humans.
This included respected scientists from Australia, the United States and
Denmark who gave evidence before the committee.
11.25 Scientists including Dr Nicholas Chartres and Professor Philippe Grandjean,
along with United States Attorney, Mr Robert Billot, argued that the dangers of
PFAS were known to the manufacturers for decades, but the research was
suppressed. It was the view of these and other scientists that there is a growing
international consensus on the dangers of PFAS to human health.
11.26 American toxicologist, Ms Jamie DeWitt pointed to studies which have found
that PFOA suppresses immune responses, and others that provide strong
evidence for a connection with kidney and testicular cancer, among other
effects. Ms DeWitt argued that, when looked at together, the existing evidence
from animal and human studies overwhelmingly supports that PFAS are
hazardous to human health.
11.27 Several scientists pointed out that PFAS compounds are transferred to the foetus
through the placenta, meaning the foetus is exposed to PFAS when its organs
are developing. The committee was told that developmental defects have been
documented in the children of women with high levels of PFAS exposure.
11.28 While it took a comparatively conservative view of the risks of PFAS as a cancer-
causing agent overall, the Cancer Council Australia agreed that PFOA is
implicated in causing testicular cancer and renal cancer in humans.
245
Views on safe levels of PFAS
11.29 Inquiry participants also discussed what may reasonably be deemed to be safe
levels of PFAS exposure in humans.
11.30 The Health Based Guidance Values for PFAS, developed by Food Standards
Australia New Zealand (FSANZ), provide tolerable daily intakes for PFOS,
PFOA and PFHxS.
11.31 Commonwealth health agencies state that PFAS levels in the general food
supply are well below Australian guidance values and there are no concerns
from overall dietary exposure for the general Australian population.
11.32 Commonwealth health authorities argue it is extremely unlikely that specific
foods consumed over a period would all be sourced from a contaminated site.
Indeed, the assumption underlying the tolerable daily intakes for the general
population is that, for example, meat from contaminated livestock would be
distributed widely into the food chain through the supermarket system. And
yet, on the ground, the committee received evidence of contaminated food being
sold on a regular basis in a much more concentrated fashion at a small
community market.
11.33 Despite this evidence, health agencies argued the tolerable daily intakes do not
need to be reviewed. While the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines have
recently been revised, with new, much more restrictive guideline values
imposed, the tolerable daily intakes have not been updated since 2022 and there
are no plans in place to update them.
11.34 Some inquiry participants agreed with the Commonwealth position, noting that
there is no toxicological data or cancer slope factor that would enable the setting
of safe levels of PFAS exposure at a lower level and that, based on current
scientific knowledge, the current Australian guidelines are conservative.
11.35 By contrast, other inquiry participants argued that there is no safe level of
additional PFAS exposure. They argued that PFAS exposure guidelines are now
set much lower in the United States and Europe and suggested Australia is
lagging behind those jurisdictions.
11.36 Committee members hold a variety of views on this matter but agree that the
update of Australia's Drinking Water Guidelines may justify a review of the
tolerable daily intakes, which were established almost five years ago.
Recommendation 1
11.37 The committee recommends that Food Standards Australia and New Zealand
(FSANZ) reviews the Tolerable Daily Intakes for PFAS against the latest
international research and regulatory settings and updated Australian
Drinking Water Guidelines, and presents a report to the Australian
246
Parliament—no later than October 2026—which outlines the findings of the
review.
11.38 As part of this review, FSANZ should reconsider the need to establish
regulatory limits on PFAS in food, taking into account a holistic
quantification of population PFAS exposure.
International regulators
11.39 The committee notes that the United States and many European countries have
a different interpretation of the scientific evidence on PFAS and human health
than the Australian government.
11.40 The committee appreciates that this difference may be confusing and frustrating
for people. Australians are given precautionary advice to reduce their exposure
to PFAS while simultaneously being told that effects associated with PFAS
exposure are 'unlikely to be important to health outcomes'. Meanwhile, advice
in other countries appears more definitive.
11.41 The committee understands that this may be especially frustrating for
firefighters, residents in heavily exposed communities, and others whose PFAS
exposure is significantly higher than the average Australian.
11.42 These inquiry participants argued that, particularly in light of recent research
and reviews conducted by international regulatory authorities, there is now
sufficient evidence that certain PFAS, including PFOS and PFOA, tend to
disrupt the immune system of living organisms and likely contribute to human
disease.
11.43 However, asked if recent evidence is sufficient to state with a degree of
confidence that high levels of exposure to PFAS likely cause negative human
health effects and/or disease, the Department of Health stated the emerging
evidence was not sufficient to say that PFAS causes any particular disease.
Mental health and psychological distress
11.44 The Australian government acknowledges that people living in PFAS-
contaminated areas are more likely to experience psychological distress,
irrespective of their PFAS blood serum concentration.
11.45 The committee heard evidence that demonstrated this distress and saw it first-
hand in Wreck Bay.
11.46 The committee acknowledges that individual circumstances may vary widely
both within and between communities. For example, if a person buys all their
food from the supermarket and drinks water brought in from another area, their
exposure to PFAS may not differ significantly from Australians living away
from hotspots. By contrast, people consuming food from contaminated land and
waterways, and drinking contaminated water, may have much higher levels of
PFAS in their blood than the general Australian population.
247
11.47 Nevertheless, the committee witnessed the distress associated with the
uncertainty and fear around the unknown health effects of PFAS exposure and
high levels of PFAS in the blood.
11.48 The committee understands that Commonwealth agencies provided access to
targeted mental health and counselling services in PFAS-contaminated
communities in the past but have not done so since 2019–20.
11.49 While exposed cohorts would have access to mainstream services, generalised
mental health service providers lack knowledge of PFAS and may be unable to
provide the kind of reassurance people are seeking.
11.50 The committee acknowledges that PFAS affected communities are concerned,
are suffering from psychological distress, and are seeking answers. Existing
public rhetoric and information about PFAS is contributing to the psychological
distress that people experience.
11.51 It is incumbent upon governments to provide honest, transparent, up-to-date
information and demonstrate a commitment to the mental and physical health
and wellbeing of the most heavily exposed communities.
A 'precautionary approach' to health risks
11.52 One of the most difficult and contentious matters that committees face in an
inquiry such as this is that sometimes, science can only take us so far and
judgments have to be made about the nature and extent of any actions to be
recommended in the face of uncertain and conflicting evidence. This necessarily
entails careful consideration and the weighing up of many factors. One
approach that governments can adopt in these cases is the precautionary
approach.
11.53 The precautionary principle—or a precautionary approach—enables decision-
makers to adopt protective measures when scientific evidence about an
environmental or human health hazard is uncertain, but the stakes are high.
11.54 Australia takes a precautionary approach to managing the environmental
impacts of those PFAS that it has recently banned, though the committee notes
this does not extend to the broader group of PFAS chemicals, which may
potentially have similarly damaging impacts. However, inquiry participants
argued that Australia may not be taking a precautionary approach to managing
the possible health impacts of PFAS.
11.55 Despite a lack of conclusive evidence that PFAS cause disease, enHealth
recommends exposure to certain PFAS be minimised wherever possible, 'as a
precaution'. enHealth does not recommend any testing or health interventions—
even for those with the highest levels of exposure.
11.56 While there may be insufficient evidence for governments and regulators to say
that PFAS directly cause disease in humans, it could also be argued there is
248
insufficient evidence to claim the impacts of PFAS are 'unlikely to be important
to health outcomes', as is currently claimed in Australia's official guidance
material.
11.57 Massive investments by the Australian government into PFAS remediation and
the provision of alternative drinking water in heavily contaminated areas
appear incongruous with the public health messaging that PFAS are 'unlikely to
be important to health outcomes'. Recent revision of the Drinking Water
Guidelines dramatically limiting allowable levels of PFOS, PFOA and PFHxS
also appears incongruous with this advice.
11.58 In light of the above—along with the United States', United Nations', and
European regulatory approaches and findings of the IARC on PFOA—this
statement may be too definitive and should be reviewed.
Recommendation 2
11.59 The committee recommends that, as part of the current review of the enHealth
guidance statement—Per- and polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS), the
Australian Government considers the appropriateness of the statement that
health effects associated with PFAS exposure 'have generally been small and
are unlikely to be important to health outcomes' in the context of leading
international research and health advice.
Testing and health interventions
11.60 The enHealth guidance statement does not recommend individual blood tests
on the basis that there is currently insufficient scientific evidence about the
health effects of PFAS to inform clinical management by a medical practitioner.
11.61 In line with this position, government agencies have not offered any blood
testing, health or cancer screening, or specialised medical advice to impacted
communities or cohorts (aside from in the context of research).
11.62 People exposed to high levels of PFAS who gave evidence were deeply unhappy
with this approach. These witnesses observed that governments have invested
heavily in monitoring the soil, water, and biota at PFAS contamination hotspots,
but have chosen not to monitor the human populations in these areas. For many
inquiry participants, this felt like a wasted opportunity and contributed to a
sense of being abandoned or betrayed.
11.63 The committee understands that authorities may be reluctant to conduct
monitoring, such as blood tests, because the Commonwealth health advice is
that individual blood tests cannot determine whether any medical condition is
attributable to exposure to PFAS and, therefore, blood tests cannot inform
medical advice or health management. However, the committee heard evidence
from individuals with heavy PFAS exposure that blood testing was worthwhile
for them, and that taking measures to reduce their blood PFAS levels gave them
249
a sense of control and improved their mental health. The committee also notes
that organisations in some international jurisdictions, including the United
States, have conducted work towards establishing relevant PFAS blood levels
and clinical advice.
11.64 Firefighters, as an example, have been proactive in researching, developing and
implementing solutions to reduce their PFAS exposure, test and reduce blood
serum PFAS levels, and also to address the impacts of PFAS on the environment.
11.65 Committee members appreciated the heartfelt testimony of firefighters,
including Assistant Chief Fire Officer (ACFO) Mick Tisbury and Commander
David Hamilton, who have 'skin in the game' due to years of PFAS exposure. In
the absence of official government health advice, these individuals have chosen
to have their PFAS levels tested, and in many cases to undertake PFAS reduction
through blood and/or plasma donations.
11.66 Committee members respect and understand these decisions and are grateful
for the work—world leading in many respects—that ACFO Tisbury,
Commander Hamilton and other firefighters have done in relation to PFAS.
11.67 When asked about testing during the inquiry, health authorities suggested that
PFAS blood testing would simply lead to unnecessary stress, because there is
nothing that can be done for those with high blood serum PFAS levels.
However, the committee notes that Fire Rescue Victoria disputed this
suggestion.
11.68 Fire Rescue Victoria offers subsidised blood testing to its firefighters and
provides 'Clinician's advice' to those who are seeking to understand what their
results mean, and what they can do about them. This advice is upfront about the
scientific uncertainties surrounding PFAS and human health. It provides a
schedule of suggested health screening, based on specific PFAS blood serum
levels, and outlines proven reduction methods.1
11.69 The committee observes that Australian health authorities have not developed
or made available any such advice and have no plans to do so, only advising
people to limit their exposure to PFAS—a suggestion that provides little comfort
to those who have already been heavily exposed.
Concluding comments on a precautionary approach
11.70 The committee notes that, in taking a precautionary approach to legacy
contamination from Defence facilities and airports, the Australian government
has:
 invested over $1 billion in environmental investigation and remediation;
1 Mr Mick Tisbury, Assistant Chief Fire Officer, Fire Rescue Victoria, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025,
p. 17. See also: Fire Rescue Victoria, PFAS Clinician's Advice, provided by ACFO Tisbury and
Commander Hamilton, 10 September 2025.
250
 paid out almost half a billion dollars in class action settlements and other
compensation payments; and
 invested millions in research on PFAS and human health and remediation
technologies.
11.71 Meanwhile, investment in testing, screening and/or health interventions for
heavily exposed populations has been modest (and only for research purposes).
11.72 Some committee members feel there is an uneasy disparity between the
quantum of investment in environmental PFAS investigation and remediation,
and the characterisation of the health impacts as 'small'.
11.73 In considering this evidence, some committee members feel that the Australian
government should extend the precautionary approach to PFAS health policy,
as it is to PFAS environmental policy. This could involve enhanced health and
cancer screening of heavily exposed cohorts, and greater support for health
interventions like PFAS blood serum level reduction.
11.74 However, other committee members supported the government's view that
there is insufficient scientific evidence for medical practitioners to provide
meaningful health advice, screening, or intervention to those with high PFAS
blood serum levels.
11.75 The committee was unable to reach a consensus on this issue but remained
sympathetic to people's desire to have information about their own health and
options for taking action—even if that action is simply more regular and
proactive monitoring for certain health effects and diseases.
11.76 The committee acknowledges proactive health monitoring is unlikely to
produce negative health effects and may improve mental health and wellbeing
for relevant cohorts. The committee also notes that the number of Australians
that have been heavily exposed to PFAS is small, meaning that precautionary
health monitoring is unlikely to be cost-prohibitive.
11.77 As such, in line with the precautionary principle, the committee suggests that
PFAS blood testing and enhanced health monitoring should be considered for
the most heavily exposed groups.
Recommendation 3
11.78 The committee recommends that the Australian Government considers
providing subsidised PFAS blood testing, enhanced health and cancer
screening, and targeted mental health support for those people with the
highest levels of PFAS exposure, including some firefighters, people exposed
at work, and residents of heavily contaminated areas (especially those with
exposure to home-grown produce, contaminated seafood, plants and animals,
river and creek waters, and/or bore water).
251
Population biomonitoring
11.79 Australia's top scientific and health organisations recommended establishing a
national biomonitoring program to track the accumulation of PFAS and other
chemical contaminants in the bodies of Australians, with a focus on children and
pregnant women.
11.80 The committee acknowledges the government's recent investment in PFAS
biomarker testing through the Australian Bureau of Statistics' National Health
Measures Survey. This is a positive initiative.
11.81 Regular, ongoing testing, and testing for a wider variety of chemical
contaminants that may impact human health, should now be considered, with
any future biomonitoring to include a focus on children and pregnant women.
Recommendation 4
11.82 The committee recommends that the Australian Government consults with
the Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences and other relevant
stakeholders on designs for a national, longitudinal chemicals biomonitoring
program (similar to existing programs in the United States, South Korea,
Japan, and several European countries) and associated longitudinal PFAS
health research projects.
11.83 A national biomonitoring program should incorporate a focus on monitoring
exposure levels of children and pregnant women and identifying any
associated health effects or trends.
11.84 In designing the program, the government should consider using leftover
blood held by pathology laboratories.
Public health messaging and a dose–response relationship
11.85 Almost all Australians have PFAS in their blood, albeit at low levels. The
committee notes evidence from the Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) PFAS
blood testing that in the Australian population, levels of the three most common
PFAS are falling. This is a positive sign.
11.86 For most Australians their level of PFAS exposure is low. For some people,
however, their level of PFAS exposure is high. It is reasonable that a person with
a high level of exposure to substances that the government recommends
avoiding would be concerned about that exposure. Evidence to the inquiry
clearly indicated a heightened level of concern among people who have been
exposed to high levels PFAS.
11.87 The committee understands there is still uncertainty around the degree and
mechanism of impacts of PFAS on human health. However, Australia's current
public health messaging about PFAS does not acknowledge a dose–response
relationship. That is, it does not acknowledge that high levels of PFAS exposure
are more likely to be detrimental to human health.
252
11.88 This is perhaps because governments are not currently able to specify what
constitutes a 'high' or 'risky' level of PFAS exposure. As noted earlier, the general
position of enHealth is that the current evidence does not support a large impact
on a person's health, even as a result of high levels of PFAS exposure. Further,
the health differences between people with the highest and lowest exposure to
PFAS are generally deemed small.
11.89 At the same time, despite scientific uncertainty, regulators have relied on
available evidence to set allowable limits of PFAS in drinking water and food.
In practical effect, this would appear to be the same as estimating a safe versus
unsafe dose of PFAS in any given source of food and drinking water.
11.90 Therefore, the committee considers there would be a benefit in enHealth
estimating what constitutes 'high risk' PFAS exposure. Public health messaging
about PFAS would be more meaningful if it differentiated between heavily
exposed cohorts and the wider community, where PFAS contamination levels
are low.
11.91 The committee notes that the PFAS enHealth Guidance is currently being
reviewed. While not wanting to pre-empt the review and its outcomes,
committee members believe there would be value in enHealth reviewing the
extent to which the risks to human health are likely increased for those who have
been heavily exposed to PFAS. Depending on the outcome of the review, the
committee considers that enHealth should acknowledge if high levels of PFAS
exposure are likely to be riskier than low levels. In the committee’s view, this
would increase public confidence in health advice that low levels of exposure to
PFAS are likely to be safe.
Recommendation 5
11.92 The committee recommends that the review of the enHealth PFAS guidance
statement consider whether the risks to human health from exposure to PFAS
are dose dependent. Should the review determine that to be the case, the
guidance should acknowledge that the risks are likely increased for those
who have been heavily exposed and are lower for those with low levels of
exposure to PFAS.
Health of the residents of Wreck Bay
11.93 The committee notes evidence from residents of Wreck Bay and their supporters
about the impacts of PFAS on their health and wellbeing. Community members,
a doctor who worked at Wreck Bay for many years, and journalists researching
premature mortality among Wreck Bay residents all believe there is a 'cancer
cluster' in the community.
11.94 In contrast, the Department of Health has argued that health and cancer
statistics for Jervis Bay, which encompasses Wreck Bay, do not indicate an
increased incidence of cancer or mortality.
253
11.95 Committee members acknowledge that these conflicting positions offer up more
questions than answers.
11.96 There is no doubt the community has endured unimaginable loss, losing a large
number of comparatively young people to cancer over recent years—including
rare cancers.
11.97 Community members sincerely believe that these deaths and cancers are caused
by PFAS.
11.98 The committee acknowledges the stress, confusion, pain and suffering that the
Wreck Bay community has endured and continues to endure. The community
is frustrated that government agencies have not chosen to investigate what
community members and others see as a disproportionate occurrence of cancer
and other health effects among the population.
11.99 People are scared and confused; nothing that government agencies have told
them has reduced or alleviated this fear and confusion. Coupled with the
profound cultural loss and grief for the human lives lost in the community in
recent years, this unresolved fear represents a communal trauma.
11.100Wreck Bay people are searching for answers.
11.101Committee members have questions too. What data is the government relying
on when it says there is no increase in cancer or mortality in Wreck Bay? Does
that data look at Wreck Bay as a discrete population—separate from the rest of
Jervis Bay Territory? Has the government looked into the incidence of specific
cancers that have been related to PFAS (such as kidney, testicular and breast
cancer) in the community? Does the government have any theories on why so
many people, including young people, have died from cancer at Wreck Bay in
recent years?
11.102Around 152 people live in Wreck Bay. Access to health services is limited,
especially for those with mobility or transport challenges. Estimates provided
by the Department of Health during this inquiry suggested testing for some of
the health effects most commonly associated with PFAS exposure might cost a
few thousand dollars per person. Even if tests are conducted periodically, this
would not represent a large investment.
11.103Committee members suggest that the government considers providing
publicly funded blood testing, enhanced cancer screening, targeted general
health monitoring, and longitudinal data collection for impacted residents at
Wreck Bay who wish to take part.
11.104Connected to this, the government should consider commissioning
longitudinal PFAS health research on the population. The geographically
contained nature of the Wreck Bay community provides a potential opportunity
for targeted longitudinal research and monitoring, noting that the small size of
the population may limit the conclusions that can be drawn.
254
11.105Any testing, monitoring and research should be delivered in partnership with
the Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community Council, the community, and relevant
Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisations.
11.106The committee also notes the proposal from La Trobe University for its Gabra
Biik, Wurruwila Wutja Indigenous Research Centre and Biomedical and
Environmental Sensor Technology (BEST) Research Centre to help facilitate
monitoring and engagement across First Nations communities. This should be
considered.
Recommendation 6
11.107The committee recommends that the Australian Government considers
providing PFAS blood testing, cancer screening and general health
monitoring for all impacted residents of Wreck Bay who wish to take part,
with a particular focus on children and pregnant women.
11.108These services must be provided in a culturally appropriate and easily
accessible manner, in collaboration with the Wreck Bay Aboriginal
Community Council and relevant Aboriginal Community Controlled Health
Organisations.
Recommendation 7
11.109The committee recommends that the Australian Government considers
commissioning longitudinal research into the health impacts of exposure to
PFAS contamination on the community at Wreck Bay.
11.110This research should utilise existing data from available sources as well as
collecting new data through the testing and health monitoring outlined in
Recommendation 6.
Work health and safety
11.111Work health and safety regulators across Australia rely on the enHealth
Guidance to determine whether PFAS exposure constitutes a workplace hazard.
The existing enHealth guidance arguably describes the risks associated with
heavy PFAS exposure as being minimal.
11.112This contrasts with the workplace practices of the Department of Defence, for
instance, which takes considerable action to protect its personnel from PFAS
exposure. The committee saw similar practices being employed by Enviropacific
at the SOLVE hazardous waste facility, where workers follow strict safety
protocols to avoid unnecessary PFAS exposure.
11.113While some workers, such as firefighters at Fire Rescue Victoria, have access to
information, testing and advice, the committee heard that a patchwork of
workers' compensation legislation in place around Australia leaves some PFAS-
exposed workers falling through the cracks, such as private sector firefighters,
255
Emergency Vehicle Technicians, retired Army Fire Service personnel, and—
until recently—firefighters on Norfolk Island.
11.114The committee was pleased to hear that, in August 2025, the Government
(through the Department of Infrastructure) extended the disease provisions
relating to firefighters under the Federal Comcare Scheme to firefighters on
Norfolk Island. This is a positive step, but it remains critical that no relevant
group of exposed workers is left behind.
11.115PFAS is a workplace hazard and workers in Australia deserve to feel and be
safe at work. Employees in any industry who have been exposed to large
amounts of PFAS should be eligible for support under nationally consistent
policies and regulations.
11.116In the context of this broad inquiry, the committee was unable to delve deeply
into the issues of PFAS, work health and safety and workplace entitlements.
However, the committee supports calls from inquiry participants for the
Commonwealth to take a leadership role in reviewing national work health and
safety regulations to ensure they are protective and nationally consistent, and
that no group of heavily exposed workers is left out.
Recommendation 8
11.117The committee recommends the Australian Government:
 works with the states and territories to ensure that workers are protected
from unnecessary PFAS exposure in all states and territories;
 reviews relevant Commonwealth work health and safety legislation,
regulations, and official advice with the aim of ensuring a consistent
approach to occupational PFAS exposure—and actively encourages states
and territories to do the same;
 amends the Australian Code for the Transport of Dangerous Goods by
Road & Rail to classify PFAS as a 'dangerous good'; and
 develops a publicly available online resource to provide state regulators,
employers, and employees with information about PFAS as a workplace
hazard, including how to initiate and progress remediation of
contamination.
11.118Where necessary, work health and safety regulations should also be
amended to extend provisions to employees of private companies working on
PFAS remediation, such as for Defence.
Recommendation 9
11.119The committee recommends the Australian Government:
 works with relevant state and territory regulators to ensure that all
veterans and firefighters exposed to PFAS—including members of the
Australian Army Fire Service and Retired Firefighters' Group—are
256
considered and eligible as part of any remediation, research, testing, and
support programs; and
 includes Airservices Australia Emergency Vehicle Technicians in its
workers compensation scheme for firefighters, by amending the Safety,
Rehabilitation and Compensation Act 1988 (Cth).
Recommendation 10
11.120The committee recommends the Australian Government works with state
and territory work health and safety regulators to develop a national
regulatory framework which enables state and territory WorkSafe agencies to
issue notices and progress regulatory responses to PFAS exposure in
Australian workplaces.
Chapter 12
Committee views on other matters
Introduction
12.1 The Australian Government was first warned about the risks of PFAS
(specifically, PFOS and PFOA) in May 2000 by the United States Environmental
Protection Agency (US EPA). Commonwealth government agencies began
working to reduce the use of PFAS-containing firefighting foams from 2002.
12.2 It took until 2017 for Australian governments to establish a national PFAS
position statement, promoting a collaborative approach to managing legacy
contamination and preventing future releases.
12.3 Finally, in 2025 a number of long-chain PFAS chemicals, including PFOS, PFOA
and PFHxS, were prohibited from import, manufacture and export under the
Industrial Chemicals Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS).
12.4 Despite this 25-year history, inquiry participants noted with disappointment
that PFAS are still described as 'emerging contaminants' by many regulators.
12.5 Like asbestos and DDT in previous decades, PFAS have been caught up in
'regulatory lag', with restrictions and control measures taking decades to
achieve.
12.6 It is encouraging to see that Australian governments are now taking decisive
action to 'turn off the tap' of PFAS entering the environment and our bodies, but
there is more that can be done.
12.7 This chapter presents the committee's views and recommendations on matters
raised in the report other than health and related issues, which was covered in
the previous chapter.
12.8 Specifically, this chapter addresses:
 dealing with the legacy of PFAS-containing firefighting foams, including
remediation of current and former fire stations and fire training grounds;
 addressing social and environmental impacts of PFAS contamination;
 addressing impacts on First Nations communities, lands and waters;
 remediation programs and technologies;
 PFAS in water supplies;
 PFAS in consumer goods, waste streams, and agriculture; and
 PFAS policy, regulation and national coordination.
12.9 As with Chapter 11, the committee has summarised evidence received and
drawn conclusions where members could reach agreement. Any additional
views and recommendations relating to these matters are incorporated in
senators' additional comments.
257
258
PFAS firefighting foams—a terrible legacy
12.10 The largest source of significant PFAS contamination in Australia has been the
use of PFAS-containing firefighting foams. These foams were used all around
Australia from the 1970s until the 2000s, at high volumes, and were allowed to
simply run off into the environment, polluting waterways, soil, plants and
wildlife.
12.11 Firefighters and others in associated jobs were regularly drenched in these
foams and believed at the time that the products were safe. Indeed, the
committee heard that children and firefighters were even sprayed with PFAS-
containing foams during celebrations and that the foams ran into rivers and
creeks, creating a blanket of bubbles on the surface that children would play in.
12.12 Highly exposed workers, including firefighters, emergency vehicle technicians
