and those who work (or worked) at contaminated sites, now live with the
impacts of this PFAS exposure—including elevated levels of PFAS in their
blood—and the uncertainty of future impacts on their health and longevity.
12.13 The committee appreciates this can be highly stressful and has economic and
social impacts for them and their families.
12.14 Responsibility for the heavy use of these foams rests primarily with the
Commonwealth (Department of Defence (Defence) and Airservices), though
state and territory firefighting organisations were also heavy users.
12.15 Defence has been phasing out legacy firefighting foams containing PFOS and
PFOA since 2004. Airservices did not cease using PFAS-containing foams until
2010. Even now, PFAS-containing foams are still available for use in a number
of specific circumstances.
12.16 Firefighters and their unions have played a leading role in advocating for
domestic and international bans, regulation, and remediation of PFAS. They
have commissioned and volunteered for research, developed and tested cleanup
technologies and remediation techniques, and are keen to share the results of
their research and development for the good of the nation.
12.17 Assistant Chief Fire Officer at Fire Rescue Victoria, Mr Mick Tisbury, has been
working to eliminate the use of PFAS-containing foams and support impacted
firefighters for decades. Mr Tisbury told the committee that governments and
regulators have known for the last 25 years that PFAS is dangerous and
therefore action is required, not more talk.
12.18 The committee heard that only three states have banned PFAS-containing
foams—South Australia, Queensland, and New South Wales. Firefighters were
also concerned that the national import and export ban that came into place in
2025 (the IChEMS listing) provides a five-year transition period for those who
are still using the foams and permits accepted uses, including 'emergencies'.
259
12.19 Committee members acknowledge the frustration felt by Mr Tisbury,
Commander Dave Hamilton, and the other firefighters who gave evidence
during the inquiry. We note the widespread calls from these and other exposed
workers for regulatory change to improve the safety of their work, protect
Australia's environment, and better support those who were heavily exposed in
the past.
12.20 Phasing out all remaining uses of PFAS-containing foams and safely disposing
of legacy stores of these products should be a priority.
12.21 Fire Rescue Victoria provided compelling evidence that existing PFAS-free
(fluorine-free) firefighting foams are effective on all kinds of fires. Indeed, three
Australian states are now completely fluorine-free. The committee also notes the
work being done at Charles Darwin University to develop even better fluorine-
free foams.
12.22 Where policies or standards maintain the use of legacy, PFAS-containing foams,
these must be amended as a priority, and the cost of replacing hardware and
cleaning out storage tanks must not be used as an excuse for inaction.
Recommendation 11
12.23 The committee recommends the Australian Government:
 fast-tracks the deployment of PFAS-free firefighting foams to replace all
legacy PFAS-containing foams;
 moves to quickly replace all legacy firefighting equipment that still
utilises or stores PFAS-containing foams, and ensures the safe disposal of
existing stocks;
 bans or severely restricts the use of firefighting foams containing PFAS
by Commonwealth entities; and
 works through cross jurisdictional bodies to achieve an urgent national
ban on the use of PFAS-containing firefighting foams, including by
private sector entities, major hazard facilities and B Class foam
extinguishers in Australia.
12.24 It is critical that legacy PFAS-containing firefighting foams are not replaced with
new products that also contain toxic chemicals.
12.25 The Commonwealth must do its due diligence to ensure that replacement
fluorine-free foams are safe.
Recommendation 12
12.26 The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides
funding for toxicological studies of fluorine-free foams so as to ensure that
legacy PFAS-containing foams are not replaced by the next harmful products.
260
12.27 Firefighting organisations are working to remediate contamination at fire
training facilities, current and former fire stations, airports, and surrounding
areas, including waterways. While remediation of firefighting facilities would
generally be a state and territory responsibility, the committee agrees that
Commonwealth oversight and grant funding would enable more effective and
targeted remediation of these PFAS 'hotspots'.
12.28 Firefighters also noted a lack of readily available resources and information for
fire services and other employers wanting to take positive steps to remediate
contaminated worksites.
12.29 Governments, led by the Commonwealth, need to leverage the research and
development work already undertaken by state-based organisations including
Fire Rescue Victoria to enable greater resource-sharing across jurisdictions.
Recommendation 13
12.30 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
states and territories to establish a fund and taskforce to support the
remediation of severely PFAS-contaminated sites, including current and
former fire stations (and surrounds), firefighting training facilities, and
nearby water catchments that have been impacted, and ensures the
availability of uncontaminated drinking water at all fire stations.
12.31 To complement this initiative, the Australian Government should establish
an expert tripartite panel comprised of government, private sector and unions,
to:
 advise on reducing PFAS exposure risk to workers;
 advise on effective remediation of PFAS-contaminated sites;
 advise on new and established techniques and technologies; and
 encourage further innovation through supporting site remediation
research and development.
12.32 The committee understands that there are high levels of PFAS contamination at
Norfolk Island Airport Fire Station and responsibility for remediating this area
is unclear.
Recommendation 14
12.33 The committee recommends the Department of Infrastructure, Transport,
Regional Development, Communications, Sports and the Arts assumes full
responsibility for remediation and management of PFAS contamination on
and around Norfolk Island airport.
Social and environmental impacts of PFAS
12.34 Inquiry participants, including environmental organisations, scientists,
community members, sports and recreation groups highlighted their concerns
261
with the impacts of PFAS contamination on Australia's environment, wildlife
and ecology.
12.35 Research organisations provided compelling evidence that PFAS contamination
of Australia's surface water and groundwater is negatively impacting many
terrestrial and aquatic species, leading to reduced reproduction and species
decline. This includes species of national significance, such as platypuses.
12.36 Addressing the impacts of PFAS on the environment is complicated by factors
including:
 patchy and incomplete data on the prevalence and distribution of PFAS in
the environment;
 interplay of PFAS with other environmental contaminants and pollutants;
 limited options for remediation, storage, and management of legacy PFAS
contamination;
 questions around jurisdictional responsibility; and
 challenges with national coordination in a federation.
12.37 While there is isolated high-quality research on environmental impacts of PFAS
in Australia, research organisations including crcCARE, CSIRO, and the
Australian Academy of Science highlighted the lack of baseline data for PFAS
levels in the Australian environment. These organisations recommended
national PFAS environmental monitoring, with standardised testing
methodologies, be conducted to better inform regulatory responses and future
research and focus remediation efforts.
12.38 Committee members support calls for targeted national PFAS environmental
monitoring to establish a baseline, identify trends, and better inform PFAS
policy and investment decisions.
12.39 Committee members also note that the only publicly available map of PFAS
contaminated sites in Australia is currently maintained by volunteers at Friends
of the Earth, which collates information from across Australia using media
reports, freedom of information requests, and government sources.
12.40 Commonwealth government agencies with data about environmental PFAS
contamination should work together with state and territory environment
protection authorities, and local government authorities, to produce an official
map, which includes PFAS 'hotspots', contamination levels, and information
about remediation activity.
Recommendation 15
12.41 The committee recommends that the Australian Government funds and
coordinates a strategic national PFAS environmental monitoring program to
track contamination levels and trends in the Australian environment,
262
including key waterways and native wildlife species, and publish emerging
data.
Recommendation 16
12.42 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with state
and territory and local governments to improve data-sharing on PFAS
contamination and remediation activity, and develop a publicly available,
interactive online PFAS map which identifies PFAS contamination locations
and remediation activity.
12.43 Along with the risk posed by PFAS contamination to wildlife and ecology,
inquiry participants noted impacts on lifestyle and recreation, land
conservation, and the use and enjoyment of Australia's land and waterways.
12.44 A striking example was provided by Field & Game Australia, which purchased
and is working to restore the Heart Morass in Victoria. Field & Game Australia
made this investment without knowing about the significant PFAS
contamination of the site and the impacts it would have on their plans to restore
and rejuvenate the land for environmental and recreational purposes.
12.45 Inquiry participants recommended that the Commonwealth provides
compensation and funding for conservation of environments impacted by
heavy PFAS contamination, such as the Heart Morass.
12.46 Committee members agree that where PFAS contamination has impacted
environments, waterways and wetlands of cultural and environmental
significance, the Commonwealth Government should work with states and
territories to fund and support cleanup and remediation of these areas.
12.47 Recreational use of these areas should still be able to be conducted safely. For
instance, bushwalking, hunting and sightseeing may be low-risk activities as
long as plants and wildlife are not consumed. However, there is currently a lack
of information and guidance material to enable organisations to provide
reassurance and advice to potential users of the space.
12.48 The Commonwealth Environment Department should work with its state and
territory counterparts to develop user-friendly guidance and public information
to assist organisations that are undertaking remediation activities to do so
safely. This could include information for volunteers and workers engaged in
remediation activities, and safety information for school groups and other
recreational users of PFAS-contaminated environments.
Recommendation 17
12.49 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with state
and territory government entities to establish a mechanism to fund and
support conservation and remediation of PFAS-contaminated environments
263
that have significant social, cultural, recreational, heritage and/or
environmental value.
12.50 This initiative should include the development and provision of information
to assist organisations to undertake remediation activities safely, and safety
information for school groups and other recreational users.
Impacts on First Nations communities, lands and waters
12.51 A number of significant sites of PFAS contamination are geographically located
in or near First Nations communities.
12.52 Inquiry participants highlighted the unique impacts of PFAS contamination on
First Nations communities, lands and waters, where contamination can
interrupt cultural practices, restrict consumption of traditional foods, and
complicate communities' connection with the land.
12.53 The committee was privileged to be welcomed onto Aboriginal lands at Wreck
Bay by the local community, where we saw firsthand the devastating impacts of
PFAS contamination on the land and people, their lives and cultural practices.
12.54 The committee would like to have travelled to other Aboriginal communities
impacted by PFAS contamination to hear their concerns firsthand but was
unable to do so due to the timing of the 2025 Federal election.
12.55 Drawing on evidence from Aboriginal health organisations, the ANU PFAS
Health Study, and the Independent Review of land uses around key Defence bases
impacted by PFAS contamination (PFAS Independent review), the committee
identified several key issues relating to Commonwealth government
engagement with First Nations people affected by PFAS.
12.56 The committee also considered evidence provided by the Defence throughout
the course of the inquiry, and statements in the Government's response to the
PFAS Independent review, before forming its views, which are outlined in this
section.
Commonwealth government engagement with Wreck Bay
12.57 PFAS contamination has had a devastating impact on the lands and waters, and
the community at Wreck Bay. Wreck Bay people's connection to their lands and
waters, their spiritual and cultural practices, access to foods and medicines, and
sense of happiness and well-being have been dramatically disturbed.
12.58 A profound sense of loss and grief is evident in the testimonies provided by
community members. The committee thanks community members for their
honesty and courage in sharing their loss and concerns for the future.
12.59 Governments must understand that for the community in Wreck Bay and other
First Nations communities, contamination of the land and waters means more
than a reduction in land value, an inconvenience, or a loss of income.
264
Contamination of the land and waters impacts every aspect of the community's
identity, culture, lifestyle and story.
12.60 That loss cannot be quantified in dollar terms.
12.61 The committee understands that the $22 million settlement paid by the
Commonwealth to the Wreck Bay community for cultural loss was deeply
disappointing to many residents. We also recognise that no settlement could
have made up for the loss that Wreck Bay people have endured and continue to
endure.
12.62 The committee was encouraged to see the strength and perseverance of the
community as it works to find new ways to practice culture, preserve cultural
history, and tell important stories. The heritage and land management work of
the Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community Council (WBACC) and the Bherwerre
Rangers is to be commended and supported.
12.63 It is the committee's view that delays and inadequate communication and
engagement from Defence in the past have damaged the community's trust in
the investigation and remediation process, despite Defence's public apology to
the Jervis Bay community, including the people of Wreck Bay, in 2023.
12.64 In spite of significant trepidation and distrust, WBACC and many community
members remain willing to engage in the remediation process and want to play
a bigger role. They are also seeking support to find new ways to record, pass on,
and continue cultural practices that are restricted due to the contamination.
12.65 The committee observes that Wreck Bay is Aboriginal land, with the village and
surrounds returned to the Aboriginal community via a land grant in the late
1980s, and the Booderee National Park and Botanic Gardens returned in 1995.1
12.66 Despite this, community members indicated feeling that they have no control
and no say over how the PFAS contamination is managed.
12.67 It is critical that the community is intimately involved in developing and
implementing solutions for managing PFAS contamination now, and into the
future. Community members have a wealth of knowledge around land
management and are well-placed to take a leading role in the monitoring and
remediation efforts on their land, going forward.
12.68 It is unfortunate that it has taken so long for Defence to begin establishing a
meaningful partnership with the community. In its interim report, the
committee recommended that Defence and the Department of Infrastructure
work with WBACC and other stakeholders to improve signage, messaging and
information provided to the community.
1 For details, see the committee's Interim report, Appendix 2: Wreck Bay community visit, pp. 102–
103.
265
12.69 Since the committee's visit to Wreck Bay in January and the tabling of its interim
report in March 2025, evidence suggests Defence has increased the frequency
and quality of its engagement, is installing temporary signage, and has
commenced work to develop better, permanent signage. Defence must continue
to prioritise building a genuine partnership with the community, while
supporting and empowering Wreck Bay people to manage and heal their land.
12.70 With critical sites of PFAS contamination, including Mary Creek and Mary
Beach (Bullocks Hoof), located on Aboriginal land, the Commonwealth should
seek to develop a formal agreement with Wreck Bay people for co-management
of remediation and restoration in these areas.
Recommendation 18
12.71 The committee recommends that the Australian Government commences
negotiation with the aim of formalising its partnership with the Wreck Bay
Aboriginal community for ongoing remediation and restoration of PFAS-
affected lands and waters at Wreck Bay. The agreement should be designed
to provide self-determination for Wreck Bay people in managing and
remediating PFAS contamination on their land.
12.72 The committee also reiterates its previous recommendation—Recommendation
3 in the committee's Interim report—which suggested the Parliament considers
referring an inquiry to the Joint Standing Committee on the National Capital
and External Territories which examines governance, representation, and
service provision in the Jervis Bay Territory (including the Wreck Bay
Aboriginal community settlement).
12.73 It is critical that the Commonwealth begins to speak with 'one voice' in its
dealings with the Wreck Bay community, and better coordinates governance
and service provision in the Jervis Bay Territory, to reduce the risk that
community needs will fall through the cracks.
12.74 Finally, in relation to Wreck Bay, the committee understands that a pipeline is
being built to bring cleaner drinking water to the people at Wreck Bay.
12.75 Despite the fact that PFAS levels in Wreck Bay's drinking water are well within
guideline limits, the water is brown, and people understandably feel
uncomfortable and unsafe drinking it, bathing in it, and using it around their
homes.
12.76 The committee notes that some community members, including Elders, have
been purchasing bottled water at significant personal expense.
12.77 The committee considers that the Commonwealth should provide immediate
access to clean drinking water for community members as a stopgap measure.
This could be achieved through providing water tanks or by provision of bottled
water.
266
Recommendation 19
12.78 The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides
immediate access to clean drinking water for the community at Wreck Bay as
a temporary measure until the pipeline connecting Wreck Bay to the
Shoalhaven City Council water supply is completed and operational.
Australian government engagement with other impacted communities
12.79 While the timing of the 2025 Federal Election meant that the committee was
unable to travel to any other First Nations communities during the inquiry,
members of other First Nations communities have raised similar concerns about
the impacts of PFAS, and the quality of Defence and other government agencies'
engagement.
12.80 The National Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Organisation
(NACCHO) highlighted the failure of Commonwealth PFAS management plans
to consider the complex and disproportionate impact of PFAS contamination on
First Nations communities, including impacts on food security, financial well-
being, psychological and social health.
12.81 Despite the central role of contaminated lands and waters in their lives,
Indigenous stakeholders have often been excluded from decision-making and
have had to fight for recognition of what they have lost.
12.82 The committee was concerned to hear from Aboriginal Medical Services
Alliance NT (AMSANT) and Wurli Wurlinjang Health Service that initial
collaboration with Aboriginal stakeholders in Katherine does not appear to have
fed into the creation of culturally appropriate warning materials and signage for
local communities. This is a lost opportunity and must be rectified.
12.83 The committee notes evidence that some local people, as well as itinerant
Aboriginal people who utilise the river for essential nutrition, may be exposed
to PFAS at much higher than recommended levels through their continued
consumption of fish and other bush foods. However, it is equally concerning
that removing these sources of nutrition may leave already disadvantaged
groups and individuals without access to previously reliable, healthy sources of
food, contributing to an already high chronic disease burden.
12.84 As discussed in Chapter 11, the Commonwealth's approach to remediation has
largely been environmentally focused, rather than focused on health and mental
health impacts, or social and cultural impacts.
12.85 Compensation schemes—largely focused on landowners—have not accounted
for the broader impacts of restrictions on fishing, gathering bush foods and
medicinal plants, or cultural uses of the land by Aboriginal people (with the
exception of the settlement between the Commonwealth and Wreck Bay
community).
267
12.86 First Nations submitters maintained that Commonwealth efforts must look
beyond the initially apparent priorities of stopping further contamination and
providing safe drinking water. Efforts in Katherine, for instance, should include
direct support for food security, culturally appropriate health promotion, and
engagement facilitated through genuine partnerships with Aboriginal
community groups and community-controlled organisations.
12.87 Protecting individuals from the risk of PFAS exposure cannot come at the
expense of access to healthy and culturally important food sources. Nor is it
acceptable that communities be expected to simply abandon cultural practices
such as fishing and gathering bushfoods.
12.88 The committee encourages Defence and other relevant governments and
agencies to review their PFAS management plans to ensure these plans align
with the National Agreement on Closing the Gap and the United Nations Declaration
on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.2 PFAS management plans must incorporate
actions to safeguard the cultural, social, nutritional, and economic wellbeing of
First Nations people around Katherine and other areas impacted by significant
PFAS contamination.
Recommendation 20
12.89 The committee recommends that Australian Government agencies involved
in remediation efforts, including the Department of Defence, work together
with local First Nations community and language groups, and Aboriginal
Community Controlled Organisations, to review PFAS management plans
and update them to incorporate actions to safeguard the cultural, social,
nutritional, and economic wellbeing of First Nations people and communities
that are significantly impacted by PFAS contamination.
12.90 PFAS management plans for sites with significant Aboriginal and Torres
Strait Islander communities should be aligned with the priority reform areas
in the National Agreement on Closing the Gap and the United Nations
Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples.
Recommendation 21
12.91 The committee recommends that the Australian Government urgently
considers options to improve and maintain ongoing access to healthy and
traditional food sources for Aboriginal communities around Katherine. This
2 Relevant UNDRIP Articles include: Article 11, regarding the right to cultural practices; Article 24,
regarding traditional medicines and highest standards for health and mental health; Article 28,
regarding redress for damaged land; Article 29, regarding protection of their lands, including no
hazardous substances and effective measures to monitor people's health; and Article 32, on self-
determination over their land. See: Australian Human Rights Commission, UN Declaration on the
rights of Indigenous peoples, Friday, 10 September 2021 (accessed 11 November 2025).
268
may include facilitating safer fishing, subsidising, and/or improving the
supply of affordable uncontaminated seafood.
12.92 The Katherine focus groups held as part of the ANU PFAS Health Study in 2018
revealed fear and confusion among community members about PFAS. The
researchers identified psychological stress and anxiety; worries about impacts
on Country, culture, and traditional diets; and uncertainty about the future. As
with Wreck Bay, communities around Katherine expressed a desire for clearer,
more appropriate communications and more transparent information.
12.93 These communities were critical of Defence for relying on largely one-way
communication, holding sporadic, often inaccessible community engagement
sessions, and publishing material on its website that is dense, highly technical
and lacks clarity. Researchers also noted a sense of distrust towards Defence and
government more broadly that negatively impacted community engagement
and collaboration.
12.94 The committee notes the ANU researchers recommended better engagement
with Aboriginal communities through community visits and the provision of
tailored, plain English information. Five years later, the Independent review
made similar recommendations, suggesting that Defence has still not done
enough.
12.95 For instance, committee members were concerned to read that—five years after
the ANU PFAS study—the independent reviewer again observed a lack of
understanding around PFAS exposure pathways among Aboriginal people in
the Katherine area.
12.96 We note that the independent reviewer praised Defence for increasing its
engagement capability through employing local Indigenous Liaison Officers.
However, the success of this initiative appears to have varied by location, with
First Nations communities around Williamtown seeming to have benefited the
most.
12.97 The committee understands that Defence is making efforts to improve its
engagement with First Nations stakeholders. Along with increased engagement
with the community at Wreck Bay, the committee notes recent engagement
activities held with the Kalano Aboriginal Community Board and a cultural
activity held with members of the Binjai community to discuss PFAS
contamination of the Katherine River. The committee is also encouraged to hear
that Defence has surveyed the Wreck Bay community to better understand how
community members use the land, waters, and biota.
12.98 Expanding and strengthening this work must be a priority for Defence.
12.99 For instance, if similar surveys have not been conducted with communities
around Katherine, and other First Nations communities, this should be
remedied.
269
12.100The committee understands that engagement with communities around
Katherine may be more complex than in other communities impacted by PFAS,
because there are a number of distinct language groups involved. The
committee would suggest that Defence increases its investments in Indigenous
engagement across the board, with investment proportionate to the complexity
of the engagement challenge.
Recommendation 22
12.101The committee recommends that the new PFAS National Coordinating Body
prioritises establishing community-based working groups, and/or other
collaboration mechanisms, to facilitate the involvement of affected
communities in decision-making processes relating to remediation efforts.
Recommendation 23
12.102The committee recommends that the Australian Government, through the
Department of Defence, reaches out to local health services, such as Wurli
Wurjlinjang in Katherine, to codesign culturally appropriate and effective
signage and educational material for Aboriginal communities impacted by
the PFAS contamination of the Katherine River.
12.103Finally, the committee notes First Nations research centres at La Trobe
University submitted a proposal for government to support the development of
Indigenous-led field-deployable PFAS detection technologies, which could
enable faster environmental and health monitoring in Aboriginal communities
impacted by PFAS contamination.
12.104The committee did not have an opportunity to hear more about this proposal
during the inquiry. However, committee members strongly support the direct
involvement of First Nations people in monitoring and assessing PFAS
contamination and remediation outcomes. As such, the committee encourages
the government to investigate this proposal.
Recommendation 24
12.105The committee recommends that the Australian Government investigates the
proposal made by La Trobe University to develop Indigenous-led field-
deployable PFAS detection technologies.
Remediation programs and technologies
12.106This section provides the committee's views on the remediation programs
being undertaken by Defence, Airservices and the Department of Infrastructure.
12.107It then looks at how the Australian Government could better support the
ongoing development of PFAS remediation technologies and techniques.
270
Defence program
12.108The committee heard that Australia is a world leader in PFAS remediation—
most notably, the remediation program undertaken by the Department of
Defence. This billion-dollar program, established in 2015, utilises robust science
and technology to detect, analyse, remove, and contain environmental PFAS
contamination at sites all around Australia.
12.109The program incorporates investigation and remediation at 28 Defence sites,
and has made significant progress since commencement, with all projects now
moving into the remediation phase, and remediation complete at two locations.
12.110Nevertheless, the committee notes that the Joint Standing Committee on
Foreign Affairs, Defence and Trade (JSCFADT) took evidence from members of
communities impacted by contamination from these sites and found that many
were angry and disillusioned with the slow pace of the program, limited scope
of remediation, confusing and unsatisfactory communication, and ongoing
impacts on their land and their lifestyles.
12.111Parliamentary scrutiny by the JSCFADT between 2018 and 2022 contributed to
improvements in the program over time. However, the committee notes the
government has yet to formally respond to the JSCFADT final report, tabled in
2022, which made 8 recommendations.
12.112The committee encourages the government to formally respond to that report
and notes there are a number of recommendations in this report aimed at further
improving Defence's engagement—which is essential to the success of Defence's
remediation program going forward.
Airservices program
12.113While Defence has commenced remediation at most or all of its program sites,
Airservices' National PFAS Management Program—which commenced in
2009—is still largely at the detailed site investigation stage, with a limited range
of preliminary management and remediation activities taking place across the
airports involved.
12.114The committee acknowledges the frustration felt by airport operators about the
slow pace of this program and lack of clarity around who is responsible for costs.
12.115North Queensland Airports Group provided a striking example of the slow
pace of the program, saying that PFAS contamination of soil and groundwater
was confirmed at Cairns Airport in 2008, but it was not until 2019 that
Airservices conducted 'preliminary site investigations and targeted sampling'.
The draft version of the detailed site investigation report for Cairns Airport was
not received until 2024.
12.116While it is the first program to commence, airport operators described the
Airservices program as 'too little, too late'.
271
12.117The committee acknowledges that PFAS testing and investigation is complex
and resource intensive. However, it should not take 11 years between the initial
detection of PFAS contamination and the commencement of preliminary site
investigations, or five years to move from preliminary investigations to a final
report and management plan. The committee also notes that Airservices is only
just beginning the remediation part of its program, and only at a few sites.
12.118The committee recommends that Airservices devotes more resources to this
program in order to expedite the investigation phase and move more quickly to
remediation and ongoing, long-term management of sites.
Recommendation 25
12.119The committee recommends that Airservices devotes additional resources to
its National PFAS Management Program to expedite the remediation phase
of the program.
Department of Infrastructure program
12.120Airservices' program focuses exclusively on PFAS within Airservices lease
areas on airports. In contrast, the Australian Government PFAS Airports
Investigation Program—which commenced following completion of a pilot
program in June 2023—investigates the whole airport site for PFAS
contamination, regardless of who is responsible.
12.121The primary outcome of these investigations is the development of PFAS
Management Plans, in collaboration with airports, which will outline an
approach to managing and remediating PFAS pollution at relevant airports.
12.122The committee notes the update provided by the Department of Infrastructure
on 1 August 2025, which indicated that detailed site investigations and/or mass
flux fieldworks are underway at 10 airports, with another 14 either commencing
investigations or at the preliminary stage. The committee also notes a further
seven airports may participate in the program, and six will not be participating
or are included in another airport's program.
12.123The committee is aware that some stakeholders, including the Australian
Airports Association, are concerned that both the Airservices and Department
of Infrastructure programs are too heavily focused on federally leased airports,
leaving other airports to fall through the cracks.
12.124The committee notes, however, that the Department of Infrastructure program
encompasses more than the 21 existing federally leased airports, with a total of
37 airports 'in scope'. Regardless, this does not account for all of Australia's
many small, regional, and general aviation airports. Depending on the historical
use of PFAS at smaller, regional and general aviation airports, there may be a
need for Commonwealth coordination of a broader program, working with
272
state, territory and local government entities, to identify and remediate
impacted airports.
Recommendation 26
12.125The committee recommends that the Department of Infrastructure,
Transport, Regional Development, Communications, Sport and the Arts
works with the Australian Airports Association, Airservices, and the
Department of Defence to identify airports that may have been exposed to
significant PFAS contamination which fall outside the scope of the three
Commonwealth PFAS investigation and remediation programs.
12.126Options for remediating these airports should then be considered by the
Australian Government, in collaboration with the relevant state, territory, and
local government authorities.
Need for national coordination
12.127In 2024, the independent reviewer observed that Defence had taken a 'strong
national leadership role' in PFAS investigation and remediation, as well as in its
engagement with impacted communities. Nevertheless, Mr Varghese also
argued for better national coordination of PFAS remediation.
12.128The committee shares this view and sees the need for a coordinated national
approach that applies to all contaminated sites, not just Defence facilities.
12.129The committee was encouraged by its visit to Launceston Airport, where site
remediation commenced in late 2024 and was completed in June 2025. The
airport operator, Asia Pacific Airports (Launceston) praised the risk-based
approach taken to the remediation project, which was led by Airservices in
conjunction with the Department of Infrastructure and a number of other
Commonwealth and state government bodies. However, nearby landowners
were concerned that PFAS contamination has spread from the airport to their
land and that these areas were not remediated.
12.130Airservices argue they are not responsible for the pollution of this nearby land,
with contamination originating from the Former Wastewater Treatment Ponds
on Launceston Airport. This may be true. However, it does not leave nearby
landowners with a satisfactory solution.
12.131This example demonstrates the need for nationally coordinated,
intergovernmental management of PFAS contamination and remediation. It
echoes views expressed in Wreck Bay, where the committee heard that Defence's
remediation activity 'stopped at the fence line'—unlike the PFAS contamination
which moves freely through the water table and environment.
12.132Landowners and communities should not be caught up in an endless argument
between different government agencies around who is responsible. Nor should
contamination that falls just outside of the jurisdiction of a particular agency fail
273
to be remediated because of a fence line or jurisdictional border. Governments
must work together to remediate contaminated land and waterways as
required, taking a risk-based approach.
12.133National coordination of PFAS investigation and remediation should
encompass all relevant Commonwealth agencies, state and territory
governments, private landholders including airport operators, and—critically—
local government organisations, in order to ensure a fairer, more consistent and
more strategic approach to remediation.
12.134The committee supports the recommendation from crcCARE that governments
need to work together to take a more holistic approach to site assessment and
remediation, with a view to remediating the highest risk areas as a priority and
minimising long-term environmental impacts from contamination sources.
Recommendation 27
12.135The committee recommends that the Australian Government works through
the new PFAS National Coordinating Body, the Heads of Environmental
Protection Authorities Australia and New Zealand, and other relevant bodies
to:
 conduct a national audit of PFAS contamination sites and remediation
activity;
 develop a strategic, risk-based national remediation plan;
 develop a common national framework for remediation; and
 develop national standards for reporting on remediation activity and
outcomes.
Funding remediation
12.136The Commonwealth Government has already committed over almost $900
million to investigation and remediation of Defence sites contaminated by
PFAS-containing firefighting foams. A further $130 million has been committed
for the Department of Infrastructure's PFAS Airports Investigation Program.
The committee notes that remediation of these airports is not included in that
figure and may be many millions more.
12.137Considering Airservices has spent almost $130 million and is now seeking
support from the Commonwealth to remediate individual airports, committee
members note that the costs are likely to be significant and that the majority of
this spending may not yet have been calculated or allocated.
12.138The committee understands the frustration of airport operators, who argued
that existing Commonwealth programs do not include a mechanism to prevent
airport operators from shouldering the costs for contamination they did not
cause. However, we also observe that Australian taxpayers are currently
shouldering the majority of the burden for PFAS removal.
274
Recommendation 28
12.139The committee recommends that the Australian Government produces a
detailed cost estimate for the remediation of Australia's airports impacted by
PFAS contamination and works with stakeholders to develop a cost-sharing
model.
12.140The question of financial responsibility for cleaning up PFAS contamination is
a complex one. Further discussion on the costs of PFAS remediation is included
in a subsequent section of this chapter—'PFAS in water supplies'.
Remediation technologies
12.141While PFAS treatment technologies have improved significantly over the past
10 years, destructive options remain limited and expensive. Most treatment
technologies currently in use cannot destroy PFAS. Instead, they reduce the
movement of PFAS into the environment by containing it; or remove PFAS from
water or soil to below regulatory levels.
12.142Non-destructive technologies, such as activated carbon filters and ion exchange
resins, are well established and commercially available. These technologies are
important to remove PFAS from the environment and water catchments, but
they generate a concentrated PFAS waste stream that needs to be managed.
12.143Destructive technologies do exist that can breakdown PFAS into small,
naturally occurring molecules, such as the volatilisation technique employed at
SOLVE. However, PFAS destruction technology like this is energy intensive and
only available at a small number of facilities in Australia.
12.144The committee agrees with crcCare and other witnesses who maintained that
Australia requires a combination of technologies and techniques to achieve
optimal remediation outcomes. This is likely to include on-site containment,
established and novel removal and reduction technologies, along with
hazardous waste storage and destruction.
12.145The committee considered a number of technologies and techniques that may
contribute to Australia's remediation challenge. Some of these are already in use
and some were seeking support to undertake trials or pursuing government
investment to become part of Australia's remediation 'toolbox'.
12.146Technology providers argued there is a need for dedicated funding to support
research and development of Australian solutions, along with adoption of a
wider variety of PFAS treatment technologies in government remediation
programs. Inquiry participants urged greater support for field trials, including
support for research and development into innovative, nature-based PFAS
remediation technologies, such as hemp.
12.147Committee members are not experts and were not in a position to individually
evaluate the remediation technologies presented during the inquiry. However,
committee members agree that Australian government remediation programs
275
should aim to support the development of a variety of local innovative
technologies and techniques for PFAS remediation.
12.148Committee members also encourage government agencies to assess
remediation technologies on the basis of both cost and environmental impact;
for instance, to take into consideration how many grams of PFAS are removed
per dollar spent along with per unit of carbon dioxide emitted.
Recommendation 29
12.149The committee recommends that the Australian Government establishes an
interdisciplinary joint council, comprising regulators, industry experts, and
researchers, to evaluate PFAS treatment and remediation technologies.
Recommendation 30
12.150The committee recommends that the Australian Government continues to
invest in a variety of PFAS remediation, removal and destruction technologies
and techniques as part of its remediation programs. Where possible,
Australian Government PFAS remediation programs should seek to support
locally developed solutions and invest in environmentally responsible
remediation technologies.
PFAS in water supplies
Revised Australian Drinking Water Guidelines
12.151The committee considered a range of evidence on the adequacy of the new
values in the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines. Critics primarily argued
that the guidelines should be stricter, in accordance with international
jurisdictions such as the United States. Other witnesses argued that the United
States' regulatory PFAS levels were established based on a different scientific
approach and could not be translated directly into an Australian context.
12.152The committee notes that the new guidelines are consistent with the draft
proposal, with the exception of PFOS. The draft guidelines proposed a level of
4 ng/L (nanograms per litre) for PFOS but eventually settled on 8ng/L. The
committee notes this is still an almost tenfold reduction from the previous 2018
guideline value.
12.153Some committee members believe the revised guideline values strike a good
balance and represent a responsible precautionary approach to controlling
PFAS in water supplies. However, some were concerned that there is no safe
level of PFAS and therefore the revised values may not be sufficiently protective
of human health.
12.154Where senators have further comments on this matter, these are included in
additional comments.
276
Implementation and testing
12.155The committee notes that the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines are not
enforceable unless and until they are incorporated into regulations in each
jurisdiction.
12.156Inquiry participants, including state and local governments, were concerned
about increased costs in complying with the revised Drinking Water Guidelines,
particularly given many water treatment facilities do not currently possess the
appropriate infrastructure and testing regimes to meet the revised levels.
12.157While a small number of water utilities routinely test for PFAS, testing has
historically been completed only when there is a suspected PFAS source or
'hotspot'. The committee also notes that testing is generally limited to those
PFAS types listed under the Guidelines.
12.158Numerous submitters and witnesses were concerned about the piecemeal
approach to water testing that currently exists. When testing has occurred,
drinking water in Australia has generally been found to be compliant with PFAS
levels prescribed in the Drinking Water Guidelines. However, the committee
notes that testing has been limited, and some historic testing results would be
higher than the revised guideline levels.
12.159These inquiry participants advocated for a consistent national approach to
PFAS water testing. They also questioned the transparency of reporting results,
noting that Australia lacks an official, centralised database for water quality.
12.160The committee observes that implementation of Recommendation 13 in this
report would provide a potential solution for this issue, as a national PFAS
contamination map could be used to report instances where PFAS is detected in
water supplies.
12.161The committee understands complying with the revised Australian Drinking
Water Guidelines will require investment on the part of state and local
governments and water service providers, and that the Australian Government
will need to make consequential amendments to other PFAS regulations.
12.162The committee encourages the Australian Government to be proactive in
providing support and guidance to state and territory governments and water
service providers to assist in implementing the revised guidelines.
12.163The committee notes calls by water service providers for the National Water
Initiative (NWI)—a national plan for water reform created in 2004—to be
revised to incorporate a coordinated approach to pollutants and contaminants,
including PFAS. The NWI is an intergovernmental framework designed to
establish water sharing and water management plans. It is the committee's view
that a better mechanism to achieve this would be the National Water Quality
Management Strategy (NWQMS), which provides a nationally agreed approach
to managing water quality.
277
12.164In addition, government should consider creating a taskforce comprising
regulatory agencies and relevant peak industry bodies to deal with the
management of PFAS and implementation of the revised water guidelines.
Recommendation 31
12.165The committee recommends that the Australian Government amends the
National Water Quality Management Strategy to incorporate a coordinated
approach to pollutants and contaminants, including PFAS. As part of this
initiative, governments should create a taskforce, comprising regulatory
agencies and industry bodies, to:
 plan and coordinate the management of PFAS in water supplies around
Australia;
 develop standardised PFAS monitoring protocols and a national register
of results; and
 progress the implementation of the revised Australian Drinking Water
Guidelines.
Costs and who pays for remediation and removal of PFAS
12.166The committee is aware of media reports that have estimated the total cost of
testing, cleaning up PFAS contamination, and removing PFAS from drinking
water systems in the United States to be several billion dollars a year. In the
United Kingdom, it is estimated that substantial remediation and removal costs
will be incurred over the next 20 years.
12.167The committee was unable to locate a similar estimate for Australia. However,
the Water Services Association of Australia told the committee that the
advanced water treatment infrastructure required could cost $100–200 million
per facility and be energy intensive to run. Water service providers would have
to recoup these costs through higher consumer costs and fees.
12.168Acutely aware of these potential costs, state and local governments and water
service providers advocated strongly for regulations to stem the 'flow' of PFAS
contamination into water and waste systems in the first place—so-called, 'source
control'.
12.169The committee notes evidence that PFAS only costs around $150 per kilogram
to buy, but costs somewhere between $4 and $25 million per of kilogram to treat
in wastewater. Committee members agree that preventing PFAS entering
Australia's environment is more effective and efficient than trying to remediate
and remove it.
12.170The committee supports calls by the water services sector for governments to
assist with the cost of upgrading water testing and treatment and makes
relevant recommendation in subsequent parts of this chapter.
278
Recommendation 32
12.171 The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
state and territory governments and the Water Services Association of
Australia to develop funding mechanisms that will support water service
providers to meet upgraded PFAS testing and removal.
12.172Like airport operators, utilities urged the Commonwealth to consider partially
funding PFAS removal and remediation by pursuing litigation against
manufacturers and suppliers of firefighting foams containing PFAS. The
committee notes that the PFAS Independent review also suggested legal actions
by the Commonwealth against manufacturers of PFAS could help fund
remediation.
12.173The committee notes that the Department of Defence suggested the
government is considering potential legal action against 3M, the manufacturer
of most PFAS-containing firefighting foams historically used in Australia. We
also note the New South Wales Environment Protection Authority (NSW EPA)
recently ordered 3M Australia to address PFAS contamination at Brogans Creek
Quarry.
12.174The committee supports calls from the United Firefighters Union of Australia,
water utilities, and others to pursue compensation from these companies on
behalf of the Australian government and the Australian people.
Recommendation 33
12.175The committee recommends that the Australian Government actively
pursues legal action against 3M and any other manufacturer of PFAS-
containing firefighting foams used in Australia, with any settlement used to
help fund remediation of contaminated sites.
PFAS in consumer goods, waste streams, and agriculture
12.176The committee understands PFAS are present in a broad range of consumer
products. Common examples include everyday household items, personal care
products, cleaning products, outdoor wear, cookware, grease-resistant food
packaging, stain-resistant furniture and repellents, plastics and paint. However,
it is not possible to quantify how many, and which consumer products contain
PFAS.
12.177Evidence to the inquiry was focused on: potential PFAS exposure pathways
from consumer goods; the problem of PFAS ending up in waste streams; PFAS
in food packaging; managing PFAS in imported goods; and questions about
regulation and labelling.
279
Exposure pathways
12.178PFAS from consumer goods may enter our bodies through skin absorption,
inhalation or ingestion, and enter the environment through water treatment
facilities and waste management infrastructure. Research on pathways other
than ingestion, such as dermal uptake, is relatively new. However, evidence
indicates PFAS are absorbed through the skin.
12.179The committee also heard that indoor exposure to PFAS from household dust
is an underrecognized pathway, and that dust containing PFAS can also enter
the environment and water supplies during rain events. Further research in
these areas would be beneficial.
12.180The committee notes that three of the nine studies funded under the Australian
government's PFAS Targeted Call for Research Scheme include consideration of
alternative pathways. The committee was also interested to hear about research
recently commissioned by Fire Rescue Victoria to measure dermal absorption of
PFAS from Firefighter Turnout Gear. While noting that the context in which
firefighters wear this gear is extreme and results may not translate to the broader
population, committee members eagerly anticipate the outcomes of this
research, along with the outcomes of the Targeted Call for Research—most of
which are expected to be published during 2025 and 2026.
12.181Should research indicate that inhalation and/or dermal exposure are bigger
contributors to overall PFAS exposure than Australia's regulators currently
presume, the committee would encourage a review of Australia's PFAS health
advice that reflects any new information.
12.182The committee shares concerns raised by the Circular Textile Working Group
Australia (WA) that PFAS are commonly used in textiles imported to Australia,
and that the textiles sector is estimated to be the biggest contributor to PFAS
pollution in Europe.
12.183The growing availability and consumer preference for cheap 'fast fashion'
exacerbates the problem of PFAS from textiles flooding global waste streams.
The committee agrees that clothing, textiles and furnishings, including carpets,
must be considered priority areas for addressing PFAS contamination in the
environment and potential human exposure from consumer goods.
Recommendation 34
12.184The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
stakeholders from the textiles, clothing, floor coverings, and furniture sectors,
and other relevant stakeholders, to:
 measure the scale and distribution of PFAS in clothing, textiles, floor
coverings, and furniture across Australia;
 identify key sources of PFAS-contaminated clothing, textiles, carpets and
furniture, including import pathways;
280
 develop strategies for reducing the import of PFAS-contaminated
clothing, textiles, floor coverings, and furniture; and
 develop mechanisms for more safely disposing of legacy PFAS-
contaminated textiles waste.
Food packaging
12.185Submitters were particularly concerned about the levels of PFAS in food
contact packaging. In an effort to make food packaging more environmentally
sustainable, manufacturers have moved from plastic to fibre-based packaging.
However, many producers intentionally add PFAS to this packaging for its
water-repellent qualities, which can migrate into food, and ultimately ends up
in the waste stream.
12.186The Australian Packaging Covenant Organisation (APCO) implemented an
action plan between 2021 in 2023 to phase out PFAS in food contact packaging,
which has had some success. The committee commends industry for taking this
initiative. However, the committee is concerned that this voluntary plan has not
removed all PFAS from food contact packaging—industry's own results suggest
10 per cent of food contact packaging still contains PFAS. As such, committee
members believe that regulation is likely to be required.
12.187The committee understands that Australia imports most of its packaging, so
regulating PFAS in food packaging is largely a matter for the Commonwealth
government. Committee members also note that the Department of
Environment is working with industry to determine methods for identifying
PFAS in packaging items and is considering regulatory options.
Recommendation 35
12.188The committee recommends that the Australian Government fast-tracks
regulatory reforms to remove PFAS from all food contact packaging imported
into and/or used in Australia and considers encouraging the establishment of
a domestic, toxic-free packaging industry.
Other imported goods
12.189Submitters highlighted the unique challenge of PFAS in imported goods. While
Australia has recently instituted an industrial ban on a number of PFAS
chemicals of concern (discussed further below), many importers are not aware
whether the goods they import and sell in Australia contain PFAS. Moreover,
there is no clear mechanism by which this information can be ascertained or
made generally available.
12.190Several local and state government entities echoed this concern, expressing
frustration at the lack of national oversight and efforts to prevent the circulation
of consumer goods that contain PFAS.
281
12.191The committee appreciates that state and local governments face a
disproportionate burden from PFAS, as they are required to manage disposal
and regulation of PFAS-contaminated waste but cannot 'turn off the tap' of
PFAS-containing products entering the country.
12.192The committee notes a majority of inquiry participants argued for banning non-
essential uses of PFAS in Australia and some argued for compulsory labelling
of PFAS in consumer goods.
12.193The committee supports calls from state and local governments and other
submitters for the Commonwealth to take the lead in reducing the incidence of
PFAS in imported products.
12.194To support the recent industrial chemicals import ban, one option for reducing
PFAS in imported consumer goods would be to introduce labelling
requirements. The committee understands that some types of products are
already covered by labelling requirements in Australia, such as cosmetics.
However, there is no broad requirement for labelling of products containing
PFAS, such as clothing, furniture, floor coverings, textiles, paint, and general
household items.
12.195While other Commonwealth entities suggested the Australian Competition
and Consumer Commission (ACCC) enforces relevant labelling standards and
may have a role in enforcing the recent ban, the committee was told that the
ACCC does not undertake testing and surveillance to monitor imported
consumer goods for the presence of PFAS or other chemicals, and would not
have a role in enforcing an importation ban on consumer goods containing
PFAS.
12.196Compulsory labelling of PFAS in consumer products should be considered,
particularly for those products with a higher risk of chemical leaching, such as
personal care products. The committee agrees with proposals that mandatory
labelling would discourage manufacturers from including PFAS variants of
concern in their products, would help Australian importers to be compliant with
Australia's PFAS regulations, and may help individuals make informed choices
about the goods they purchase.
12.197Australian government agencies, including the Department of Environment,
Australian Border Force, and the ACCC, need to work together to develop
mechanisms for identifying imported products that contain PFAS, and
restricting their import into Australia.
12.198Further views on enforcing PFAS regulation are outlined below, under 'Policy
and coordination'.
282
PFAS in waste streams
12.199The committee notes compelling evidence that costs associated with PFAS
removal and wastewater treatment are much greater than the costs required to
produce PFAS chemicals and the products that contain them. Considering that
many consumer products end up in waste streams, it is likely to be easier and
cheaper to reduce the influx of PFAS-containing products, rather than dealing
with them once they enter waste streams.
12.200Preventing PFAS entering waste streams should be the priority. However, even
if PFAS-containing products stopped entering the Australian economy
immediately, there would still be a large amount of legacy PFAS-contaminated
waste to be managed.
12.201Submitters from the waste management sector explained that current
approaches to managing PFAS contamination in waste streams involves setting
'end-of-pipe' guideline values for PFAS in soil, water, and biosolids.
12.202Limits on PFAS in biosolids are recent, with allowable limits only stipulated in
version three of the PFAS National Environmental Management Plan (NEMP 3.0),
released in March 2025. Most states have not yet adopted regulatory limits for
PFAS in biosolids. This was a concern for a number of submitters, who argued
that reducing PFAS in biosolids and compost products should be a priority, and
that government should consider imposing requirements to label these products
when they contain PFAS (including the concentration).
12.203State and local authorities also called for more resources to be provided by the
Commonwealth to assist in managing the legacy PFAS burden and meeting
stricter requirements for end-of-pipe management of PFAS waste.
Recommendation 36
12.204The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with
state and territory governments, local councils, and the waste management
sector to develop a plan for dealing with legacy PFAS contamination in
Australian waste streams, including consideration of funding, options to
reduce disposal of PFAS contamination in landfill facilities, and cost-sharing
mechanisms.
Agriculture
12.205PFAS contamination directly affects some land that is or has been used for food
production. In addition, the committee heard that the application of PFAS-
contaminated biosolids to uncontaminated agricultural land poses significant
risks for subsequent contamination of the food chain and the natural
environment.
12.206Committee members note that regulatory limits for PFAS in biosolids form part
of the update to the latest version of the PFAS NEMP, discussed below.
283
12.207Witnesses noted there are no regulatory limits for PFAS in stock water,
meaning livestock may be drinking contaminated water. However, regulators
argued that farming practices often minimise or reduce PFAS levels in stock,
and that regulatory limits for stock water are not required.
12.208The committee did not receive enough information to form a concrete view on
this matter, but supports efforts to ensure livestock meant for consumption are
not raised on heavily contaminated land.
12.209We also note that a recommendation of the PFAS Independent Review was for
the proposed national coordinating body to address risks associated with food
production in the context of PFAS contamination, including by developing new
Health Investigation Levels to inform risk assessments for the suitability of
relevant land uses. The committee supports this recommendation and would
support the development of further mechanisms to ensure that farmers and
others who produce food from the land are informed of relevant PFAS
contamination.
Recommendation 37
12.210The committee recommends that the Australian Government fast-tracks the
development and publication of new Health Investigation Levels to inform
risk assessments for the suitability of PFAS contaminated land for
agricultural purposes, and publishes advice on the threshold over which
produce containing PFAS should not be sold.
Recommendation 38
12.211The committee recommends that, in line with Recommendation 16, the
Australian Government provides publicly available information identifying
the presence of PFAS contamination on farming land, and proactively
informs landholders.
12.212Another issue related to PFAS and agriculture was around the presence of
PFAS in pesticides. The committee received some evidence. However, this
evidence was limited.
12.213Some submitters claimed that PFAS may be used as an active ingredient in
pesticides, may be included in packaging or containers, or that organofluorine
compounds used in pesticides (which do not meet the definition of PFAS) may
nevertheless degrade into short-chain PFAS in the environment.
12.214Other evidence disputed these suggestions. For instance, CropLife and the
Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines Authority insisted that
pesticides used in Australia are rigorously assessed to ensure they are safe for
humans, animals, and the environment.
12.215The committee understands that Australia briefly had a scheme that required
review and re-registration of agricultural and veterinary (agvet) chemicals, to
284
ensure their ongoing safety. That scheme was repealed in 2014 by an incoming
government, shortly after it was established.
12.216Some committee members indicated support for reinstating this scheme.
12.217Where senators have formed a view on this matter, it is included in their
additional comments.
Policy and coordination
12.218This section includes the committee's views on regulation of PFAS chemicals in
Australia, including the recent Schedule 7 listing. It then discusses the PFAS
National Environmental Management Plan (NEMP), including national
coordination, funding and support. The section finishes by discussing PFAS
research and policy development, and PFAS public information.
Regulating PFAS—individually or as a group?
12.219Australia has recently instituted a ban on the import, industrial use and
manufacture of a number of PFAS chemicals by listing these chemicals under
the Industrial Chemicals Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS)
Schedule 7. Schedule 7 contains the highest risk chemicals, which should be
phased out, and can be subject to strict management conditions.
12.220The Schedule 7 listing includes PFOA, PFOS and PFHxS (and related
chemicals). Other PFAS variants are also being considered for listing. While
PFAS variants may be assessed in groups, PFAS chemicals must still be listed
(regulated) individually.
12.221A number of inquiry participants argued that PFAS should be assessed and
regulated as a group, rather than individually, to take a precautionary approach
and speed up the process and reduce the likelihood that manufacturers will
replace existing, restricted PFAS chemicals with new ones.
12.222Many inquiry participants recommended phasing out, or banning the use of,
all PFAS in Australia. However, others noted that there are many different kinds
of PFAS chemicals, some may be less toxic than others, and a blanket ban would
have unintended consequences.
12.223Under existing processes, Australian regulators may test and consider PFAS in
groups where they have similar characteristics. However, the committee was
told it is unlikely that Australia would move to ban all PFAS as a group.
12.224In fact, the committee notes advice from the Department of Environment that
there are 'chemicals of lower concern' that can often be substituted for the PFAS
that have been banned. Part of the role of the IChEMS is to provide a source of
advice on safer alternative chemicals to those listed in the more heavily
restricted schedules.
285
12.225Committee members hold a variety of views on whether PFAS should be
banned as a group or class of chemicals, or assessed and regulated individually,
as they are now.
12.226The views of individual senators on this matter are included in their additional
comments.
12.227That being acknowledged, committee members agree that chemical regulation
is an increasingly complex field which may require additional resourcing for
Australia's chemical regulators.
Recommendation 39
12.228The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides
additional resources to the Australian Industrial Chemical Introduction
Scheme to fast-track chemical assessment, including for PFAS and other
persistent organic pollutants (POPs) to ensure toxic chemicals are not allowed
on the market.
Enforcing the Schedule 7 listing
12.229The Schedule 7 listing allows the Minister to prohibit 'articles' (products)
containing the specific PFAS that have been scheduled. However, the listing of
PFOA, PFOS and PFHxS (and related chemicals) will not, by itself, stem the flow
of PFAS into Australia.
12.230Implementation of this ban requires adoption by states and territories, and
enforcement of chemical regulation is generally considered to be a state and
territory responsibility. However, as discussed above, states and territories note
that they have limited capacity to prevent pollution resulting from imported
products containing PFAS.
12.231The committee notes that the IChEMS listing includes no detection or
disclosure mechanisms, and there is not currently any legislation, policies or
procedures in place to give effect to the ban at the border. Further, while the
listing applies to articles (products), it is unclear how it would apply to
'individual imports'—products purchased by individuals from overseas online
retailers.
12.232Inquiry participants made a number of recommendations aimed at improving
Australia's regulation of PFAS, including:
 better harmonisation of chemicals regulation at Commonwealth, state and
territory levels;
 increased powers and resources for Commonwealth entities to enforce the
ban on PFAS in imported products;
 consideration of mechanisms to reduce the instance of PFAS in products
purchased online by individual Australians; and
 mandatory reporting on the importation, storage and use of PFAS.
286
12.233The Department of Environment argued that interventions on PFAS must be
risk-based, evidence-based, proportionate and pragmatic, and make efficient
and effective use of available resources.
12.234Committee members agree that the Schedule 7 listing is a good start, but needs
to be supported by adequate, risk-based and proportionate detection, disclosure
and enforcement mechanisms.
Recommendation 40
12.235The committee recommends that the Australian Government prioritises
work to develop legislative and policy mechanisms to enable the Schedule 7
listing of PFOA, PFOS and PFHxS (and related chemicals) to be effectively
monitored and enforced, especially in relation to imported products.
12.236Detection, disclosure and monitoring mechanisms should include internal
assessments and self-reporting by businesses along with independent
product testing where appropriate.
12.237Enforcement mechanisms should be risk-based, focussing on industries and
sectors where businesses may be unintentionally or unwittingly importing
products containing restricted PFAS.
Recommendation 41
12.238The committee recommends that, as a priority, the Australian Government
works to enforce the PFAS ban in relation to cosmetics and personal care
products, including period products.
Recommendation 42
12.239The committee recommends that the Department of Climate Change, Energy,
the Environment and Water works with the Department of Health, Disability
and Ageing and the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission to
develop a mandatory product labelling regime aimed at retailers, wholesalers,
distributors, importers and manufacturers who import consumer products
containing PFAS chemicals.
Ratification of the Stockholm Convention PFAS listings
12.240Numerous submitters argued that Australia needs to urgently ratify the three
Stockholm Convention PFAS listings.
12.241The committee notes evidence from the Department of Environment that it is
working on the necessary architecture to enable ratification, and that adoption
of the IChEMS into state and territory regulations is the next step. The
committee also notes, however, that departmental witnesses stated there is no
timeframe for ratification, and the department has other pressing priorities,
suggesting this may not be a core priority of the government.
287
12.242The committee would suggest that ratification remains a priority for the
government and that, if further resourcing is required in order to enable the
Environment Department to continue this work, that resourcing be provided.
12.243The committee was encouraged to hear that adoption of the IChEMS
framework into state and territory regulatory regimes should enable a more
efficient and streamlined process for ratifying future amendments to the
Stockholm Convention.
Recommendation 43
12.244The committee recommends that, as a priority, the Australian Government:
 provides adequate resourcing for the development of legislation and
associated regulations to give effect to the ban on PFAS;
 works with states and territories to encourage and support adoption of the
Industrial Chemicals Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS)
into state and territory chemical regulations;
 urgently progresses the treaty making process to enable the ratification of
the Stockholm Convention listings of PFOS, PFOA and PFHxS; and
 works across government to develop a clear and simplified framework to
enable faster ratification of future amendments to the Stockholm
Convention and domestic implementation of toxic chemical bans.
Recommendation 44
12.245The committee recommends that the Australian Government develops a
standardised approach to domestic chemical classification and regulatory
changes for new hazard classifications made by the International Agency for
Research on Cancer.
PFAS NEMP
12.246The NEMP provides a highly detailed, evidence-based framework for the
community, environmental regulators, governments and industry to address
PFAS contamination.
12.247The most recent version includes new guideline values for investigation and
risk assessment; guidance around remediation of contaminated land; guidance
on re-use of resource recovery products; and risk-based criteria for re-use of
biosolids contaminated with PFAS.
12.248Inquiry participants highlighted the critical role played by the NEMP in
forming the basis of many state and territory policies and regulations,
remediation practices, and environmental management approaches. However,
some were concerned about the inconsistent application of the NEMP's
standards and practices across different states and territories.
288
12.249Inquiry participants argued that:
 thresholds and standards in the NEMP should be consistently applied in all
jurisdictions;
 PFAS contamination should be monitored and publicly reported by an
independent national body;
 jurisdictions should better share information and data in order to facilitate
better coordination and more strategic, risk-based management of PFAS in
the environment;
 a map of PFAS-contaminated sites, along with contamination data, should
be publicly available and easy to use; and
 local governments should be better supported to incorporate new waste
management standards and requirements into their practices, including
being supported to remediate legacy PFAS contamination.
12.250Committee members agree that states and territories should look to incorporate
consistent standards, practices and regulatory limits as outlined in the NEMP.
While this is a matter for states and territories, the Commonwealth has a role in
providing encouragement and support.
12.251Committee members believe states and territories are committed to the NEMP,
including the latest iteration—NEMP 3.0. However, we note that a number of
jurisdictions are calling for more resources and assistance from the
Commonwealth to help interpret and incorporate the new requirements.
12.252The committee was concerned by evidence from the Department of
Environment that it does not monitor jurisdictional implementation, and that
there are no departmental resources dedicated to overseeing or supporting the
implementation of the NEMP.
12.253As a minimum, the Commonwealth should support the NEMP, providing
information, resources, and assisting with interpretation and integration into
state and territory regulatory regimes.
Recommendation 45
12.254The committee recommends that the Australian Government works through
intergovernmental decision-making forums, such as National Cabinet and
the Heads of Environmental Protection Authorities Australia and New
Zealand, to encourage and support jurisdictions to incorporate the standards,
practices and regulatory limits outlined in the PFAS National Environmental
Management Plan (NEMP) into their own regulatory regimes, including new
limits for biosolids.
12.255The Australian Government should consider providing additional
resourcing and support as jurisdictions implement NEMP 3.0, including by
funding the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and
289
Water to provide departmental staff to oversee and support the
implementation.
Research and policy development
12.256There is no doubt that research has a pivotal role to play in the approach taken
by governments and regulatory agencies to the management of PFAS in
Australia. The highly technical nature of the issue means that governments rely
heavily on experts in a number of fields, including health, epidemiology and
biology, chemical engineering, and waste management technologies.
12.257Evidence considered throughout the inquiry demonstrated that progress in
Australia's management of PFAS has been achieved through strong leadership
and collaboration between governments, research organisations and industry.
12.258A number of industries have an interest and a contribution to make to defining
and measuring the problem, as well as designing and implementing solutions.
However, governments need to be aware of the potential impact of conflicts of
interest and put in place robust measures to manage these conflicts.
12.259Investments in PFAS research should be focused on areas of highest need,
where research outcomes will best serve the public interest. It is incumbent upon
governments to provide leadership in this area, ensuring public funding is
directed towards PFAS research that benefits the community.
12.260Inquiry participants from all sectors acknowledged the critical role of scientific
data and research in the development of PFAS policy and suggested future
research needs to be better coordinated and focused, with a view to supporting
a more strategic approach to managing the problem of PFAS in Australia.
12.261The committee supports a number of suggestions made by inquiry participants,
including that:
 governments should partner with research organisations, industry, and
technology sectors to develop unified regulatory frameworks, provide
better public information, and fast-track the development of remediation
technologies;
 governments should prioritise supporting longitudinal studies; and
 government should work together to streamline ethics approval processes
for critical PFAS research to ensure this research is not unnecessarily held
up.
Recommendation 46
12.262The committee recommends that the Australian Government works with:
research organisations including the CSIRO, Australian Academy of Science
and Australian Academy of Health and Medical Sciences; industry bodies;
and independent research centres like crcCARE, to develop a strategic PFAS
research strategy and roadmap.
290
12.263A national PFAS research strategy should focus investments towards
developing novel remediation technologies, supporting longitudinal
