research—particularly in relation to health and environmental impacts—and
streamlining ethics approval for research involving multiple jurisdictions.
PFAS public information
12.264Inquiry participants were critical of Australia's existing public information on
PFAS. The Australian Government PFAS Taskforce website has been a primary
source of information about PFAS until recently. This legacy website was
inconsistently updated since the taskforce was disbanded in 2022 and appears
to have now been replaced with a revised website, hosted by the Department of
Environment.
12.265While this is a positive step, the committee notes that the 'Contact us' page on
the website still lists five separate Commonwealth departments and suggests
that members of the public contact the 'relevant' Australian Government
department 'directly', if they have a query. This is likely to be confusing to the
average Australian seeking information about PFAS.
12.266The committee supports calls from inquiry participants for the Commonwealth
to coordinate the provision of a simple, easy to understand website, which
functions as a single source of information on PFAS for the public. A revised
PFAS website must provide a single point of contact for members of the public
who are concerned about PFAS and should aim to eventually include a map of
PFAS contamination 'hotspots', and information about remediation in these
areas (as proposed in Recommendation 16).
Recommendation 47
12.267The committee recommends that the Australian Government provides user–
friendly, up-to-date public information on PFAS which incorporates:
 a 'single source of truth' on PFAS, updated regularly according to the most
recent evidence, with publication dates included on all pages;
 a single point of contact for members of the public to ask questions about
PFAS; and
 in due course, a map showing PFAS contamination 'hotspots' and
remediation activity (see Recommendation 16).
12.268This information could be provided on a new website or the existing website
hosted by the Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and
Water (pfas.gov.au).
Senator Lidia Thorpe
291
Chair

Additional comments from Senator Thorpe
Introduction
1.1 In August 2024, I initiated this Select Committee and inquiry, after having
become aware of the ever-increasing health and environmental impacts of PFAS
chemicals. I found it particularly devastating to hear from members of the
Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community about how PFAS have poisoned their lands,
waters, totem, and people. This community has suffered a lot from the
contamination resulting from PFAS-containing firefighting foams used at the
nearby military base. It became clear to me that, as a country we do not yet
sufficiently address the health and environmental impacts from these 'forever'
chemicals, which threaten to be the 'asbestos of the 21st century'.
1.2 We need a well-considered and coordinated national approach to effectively
support affected communities, undertake remediation of soils and water, and
prevent these chemicals from causing even more harm.
1.3 I am grateful for the unanimous support of the Senate in establishing this
committee and the work it has been able to undertake over the last year.
1.4 The committee collected a vast amount of evidence, completed eight public
hearings and conducted three important site visits, gathering critical evidence
for two substantial reports.
1.5 While there are further hearings we could have held and more communities I
would like to have visited, the committee can be proud of the work we have
done to dig deeper into this pressing issue.
1.6 Committee members come from across the political spectrum. We all
acknowledge that PFAS is a complex issue, and we may not always agree on the
way forward. In that context, I am pleased with what we were able to achieve
as a committee and the report we are presenting as a result.
1.7 Committee members engaged in earnest with a substantial amount of evidence,
considered a wide range of possible recommendations, and were able to achieve
consensus in many areas.
1.8 Most of all, however, I would like to thank affected communities, groups and
individuals who provided evidence and invited the committee to see and hear
for themselves the impacts PFAS chemicals have had on them—something that
can be very challenging to talk about. I would also like to thank the experts who
gave evidence as part of this inquiry—all of which formed the basis for the
extensive report and recommendations we are now able to present. I very much
hope that the recommendations, at least to some extent, address your concerns
and that they can result in real action.
293
294
1.9 If implemented, the recommendations in this report would improve Australia's
response to PFAS and other hazardous chemicals and bring this country closer
to world's best practice in PFAS regulation and response.
1.10 But there is more that can and should be done.
1.11 The committee received evidence to support a number of additional, important
recommendations that were not included in the majority report; those
recommendations are outlined here.
1.12 I sincerely hope that this and future governments will consider these and the
main report's recommendations seriously and in an unbiased manner and do
what is right for this country and its people.
PFAS are toxic to living things
1.13 The committee weighed up evidence from Australia and overseas on the science
of PFAS and human health. Overwhelmingly, scientists—including
toxicologists—told the committee sufficient evidence already exists to indicate
with reasonable scientific certainty that PFAS are harmful to human health.
1.14 Scientists from Australia, the United States (US), and Denmark explained how
PFAS impact the immune and reproductive systems, increase cholesterol levels,
impact the endocrine system, are strongly associated with kidney and testicular
cancer, and are implicated in numerous other cancers. PFAS build up in the
body and are poisonous to living creatures, including human beings.
1.15 Early PFAS products like Teflon were hailed as miraculous and safe, echoing
the way tobacco products were sold in the first half of the 20th Century.
Meanwhile, chemical companies including DuPont and 3M had evidence that
early types of PFAS—and later, PFOA and PFOS—were toxic decades ago, based
on the results of their own animal studies and studies on their own workers.
They chose to suppress that evidence, cherry-picked results, left adverse
findings unpublished, and failed to conduct follow-up studies where evidence
pointed to a problem.
1.16 Although some types of PFAS have been produced for over 70 years, the
findings of independent research on their toxicity would not be published for 50
years. Despite this lag, there are now many thousands of high-quality studies
establishing strong links between PFAS exposure and significant health impacts.
1.17 Australian governments have been very cautious in their acknowledgement of
the health impacts of PFAS, and often inconsistent in their messaging around
encouraging precaution while officially stating that there is no reason to worry
about potential health impacts of PFAS. This advice is confusing to the public
and has led to a lag in action to address the health impacts of PFAS, particularly
in highly affected communities and professions.
295
1.18 It seems like governments have been scared of the implications acknowledging
the health impacts of PFAS could have, but inaction based on this is
unacceptable and neglects the government's duty of care towards the people.
1.19 Australian governments and authorities must take a precautionary approach to
protecting people, as well as the environment, from these dangers. Australian
regulators should be more consistent and definitive in their language:
 PFAS are toxic to living things.
 PFAS are immunosuppressive.
 Most Australians have low levels of PFAS exposure.
 Exposure to PFAS may impact your health, especially higher levels of exposure.
 Pregnant women and young children are especially vulnerable.
1.20 If people are concerned about their exposure to PFAS, blood tests can tell them
their PFAS levels in relation to average or typical levels. There are options to
reduce the amount of PFAS in a person's blood, such as through regular blood
or plasma donations. And there are low-risk health interventions—from simple
blood tests to more regular cancer screening—which may be of benefit to those
with high exposure levels.
1.21 At the moment, this information and these options are only available to a small
subset of those exposed (some firefighters), and only because they took the
initiative to set it up for themselves. This is not fair and it is not right. The
government insists that testing is not required, but without testing, it is hard to
build a further evidence-base and for people to seek proactive healthcare.
Having access to testing and blood and plasma donations has also shown to
reduce people's anxiety levels as they feel less helpless and start taking the
situation into their own hands.1
1.22 In the case of Wreck Bay, a small but highly affected Aboriginal community, it
was clear that the health impacts on the community have been devastating, with
many—even young—people passing away in recent years. The situation has
been described as an obvious cancer cluster, yet due to the small size of the
community, this is scientifically hard to prove. This argument has been used by
governments to avoid providing much needed health screening and support to
the community.
1.23 The Wreck Bay class action reached a settlement over cultural loss, and did not
cover health impacts on the community, despite the community still dealing
with the ongoing health impacts. It is clear that, despite the community crying
out for help for years, government action has been lacking and that the health
impacts on the Wreck Bay community urgently need to be addressed and
publicly acknowledged so that healing can start to happen.
1 See for instance: Ms Jayne Newgreen, Private capacity, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 3; Mr
Christopher Howe, Submission 91, p. 6.
296
1.24 The committee made a number of recommendations designed to improve
Australia's response to PFAS as a health hazard. The committee has
recommended a national chemicals biomonitoring program, clearer health
messaging, targeted longitudinal research, enhanced health monitoring, and
increased cancer screening for heavily exposed cohorts.
1.25 I reiterate those recommendations and urge the government to take
responsibility—and act now—for the relatively small number of people in this
country who have been heavily exposed to PFAS.
1.26 I welcome the recent developments where the United Firefighters Union
Aviation Branch achieved presumptive cancer workers compensation coverage
for their members on Norfolk Island. This was one of the recommendations in
their submission to this inquiry, and I hope this inquiry has been helpful in
achieving this change, which will provide important cover for Norfolk Island
firefighters, and should be the absolute minimum that can be done for people
who have put their lives on the line to protect the wider community.
1.27 It is not good enough to tell the firefighters, residents of heavily contaminated
areas, like Williamtown, Katherine and Wreck Bay, and others exposed to high
levels of PFAS, that there is nothing that can be done for them.
1.28 In addition to the recommendations made by the committee, I therefore
recommend the following:
Recommendation 1
1.29 That Food Standards Australia New Zealand urgently establishes regulatory
limits on PFAS in food, taking into account a holistic quantification of
population PFAS exposure and the presence of PFAS in takeaway and other
food packaging.
Recommendation 2
1.30 That the Australian Government adds liver cancer to the list of cancers
covered by the Safety Rehabilitation and Compensation Act 1988 and other
work health and safety legislation and works cooperatively with state and
territory governments to facilitate the inclusion of liver cancer in their
presumptive workers compensation legislation.
Recommendation 3
1.31 That the Australian Government:
 extends eligibility for access to Medicare benefit item number 13750 for
firefighters and others with PFAS levels in excess of 5ng/ml PFOS/PFOA
and 2ng/ml PFHxS to access therapeutic plasma/blood treatment in order
to reduce their PFAS blood levels; and
297
 works with the Norfolk Island Regional Council to facilitate and fund
regular plasma donations for firefighters and community members who
have experienced significant PFAS exposure on Norfolk Island.
1.32 The committee has recommended that the Australian Government considers
providing subsidised PFAS blood testing, enhanced health and cancer
screening, and targeted mental health support for those people with the highest
levels of PFAS exposure (Recommendation 3).
1.33 While I support this recommendation, it could be stronger. As such, I
recommend:
Recommendation 4
1.34 That the Australian Government urgently provides subsidised PFAS blood
testing, enhanced health and cancer screening, and targeted mental health
support for those people with the highest levels of PFAS exposure, including
some firefighters, people exposed at work, and residents of heavily
contaminated areas (especially those with exposure to home-grown produce,
contaminated seafood, plants and animals, river and creek waters, and/or bore
water).
Recommendation 5
1.35 In addition to subsidising blood testing and health monitoring, the
Australian Government should provide additional funding to the South
Coast Medical Service Aboriginal Corporation to increase primary care access
for members of the Wreck Bay Aboriginal community.
Industry interference led to delays
1.36 The fact that countries like Australia did not move to ban PFAS until 2025 is a
direct result of the choices made by companies like DuPont and 3M to suppress
and ignore early scientific evidence that their products were harmful.
1.37 The committee was privileged to hear from American attorney, Mr Robert Bilott,
who fought for decades to expose the dangers of PFAS. Mr Bilott explained how
legal action against DuPont eventually brought this hidden research to light.
Courts then compelled DuPont to fund and participate in a large-scale,
independent study of the impacts of PFAS on a heavily exposed population in
West Virginia. Around 69 000 people participated in the study, which
conclusively found in 2013 that exposure to PFOA in the water supply had
probable links with six different diseases, including testicular and kidney
cancer.
1.38 DuPont and related companies have since settled dozens of lawsuits, paying out
billions in damages and compensation, including a 2023 settlement with water
298
providers for US$1.19 billion, and a recent 2025 settlement with the State of New
Jersey for US$875 million.2
1.39 Along with the findings of the West Virginia study and other independent
scientific studies, chemical company data and records were provided to
American regulators who eventually set regulatory limits. Some states in the US
have gone further than others, with the state of Maine passing the first national
law (to take effect in 2030) that will prohibit intentionally added PFAS in
products.
1.40 I share Mr Bilott's frustration that regulators in countries like Australia have
been so slow to act, insisting on Australian data and Australian research, when
masses of high-quality international evidence already exists.
Regulators were too slow to act
1.41 Action from Australian regulators has been patchy, slow and inconsistent. The
Department of Defence may have known about the dangers of PFAS as early as
the late 1980s when its own consultants' reports advised that PFAS were
environmental hazards and should not be released into waterways.3
1.42 There is no doubt that the Australian Government had credible advice—
including written warnings from US regulators—that PFAS may be dangerous
to human health from at least the year 2000. As outlined in the committee's
report, the forerunner to the Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction
Scheme (AICIS), the National Industrial Chemicals Notification and Assessment
Scheme, called for governments to cease using PFAS-containing firefighting
foams in 2003. Yet action was slow and way too little.
1.43 Regulators in some states took years to pass on this warning to their fire services,
allowing millions more litres of toxic firefighting foam to flow into Australia's
environment and waterways. Airservices continued using these foams until
2010 and, while Defence has worked to phase these foams out over a number of
years, it still uses them in a limited number of circumstances, causing further
health and environmental impacts. The committee also heard that a significant
proportion of fixed firefighting infrastructure—including at airports—is still
loaded with PFAS-containing foams.
1.44 This must be addressed before these legacy stores end up in the environment
and water supply.
2 Ben Casselman, Ivan Penn and Matthew Goldstein, 'Three "Forever Chemicals" Makers Settle Public
Water Lawsuits', The New York Times, 2 June 2023; CHEMTrust, 'DuPont, Chemours and Corteva to Pay
$875 Million to New Jersey over PFAS Pollution Claims', 14 August 2025 (accessed 6 November 2025).
3 Christopher Knaus, 'Australian defence force warned about toxic firefighting foam 30 years ago',
The Guardian Australia, 9 October 2017 (accessed 6 November 2025).
299
1.45 Even more concerning are the delays between when government first detected
significant PFAS contamination around Defence bases and when it began
notifying impacted residents.
1.46 We know Defence started phasing out these firefighting foams in 2004 and
conducted various early environmental tests in subsequent years. Previous
parliamentary inquiries into PFAS reveal that Defence began routinely testing
for PFAS contamination around its bases in 2011, and that it detected
contamination around RAAF BASE Williamtown in 2012.4
1.47 Defence wrote to a number of stakeholders about this detection in 2012,
including local water service providers. However, impacted residents were not
informed until three years later. That is irresponsible and unacceptable.
1.48 In 2015, the New South Wales Environment Protection Authority (NSW EPA)
notified Defence that it had decided to take a precautionary approach by
informing local residents and issuing precautionary advice. The NSW EPA
advised locals to 'not drink bore water and to not eat fish caught in the nearby
area or eggs from backyard chickens that have been drinking bore water in the
area'. The EPA also temporarily closed commercial and recreational fisheries,
pending further investigations.5
1.49 Defence has publicly admitted that it should have told the community in 2012.6
1.50 If the NSW EPA had not acted, how long would the Department of Defence have
taken to inform local residents?
1.51 We see the same thing in other parts of Australia, including Wreck Bay, where
contamination was detected in 2016, but health warnings were not issued until
2018—and even then, not by the Commonwealth. When the committee visited
in January this year, it was obvious that public signage and warnings were still
inadequate and that the community had many questions that remained
unanswered.
1.52 In fact, all around Australia, we see that First Nations communities were often
the last to know about the contamination, despite being more heavily exposed
due to traditional diets, fishing, hunting, swimming and consumption of bush
foods.
4 Senate Foreign Affairs, Defence and Trade References Committee, Inquiry into firefighting foam

contamination: Part A—RAAF Base Williamtown, February 2016, pp. 8–14.

300
Another injustice for First Peoples
1.53 One of the most eye-opening and important parts of this inquiry was the
committee's trip to Wreck Bay and its public hearing in Nowra.
1.54 I am immensely grateful to the Wreck Bay community, including the Elders and
Traditional Owners who welcomed me and the other committee members onto
their beautiful land. It was a privilege to learn a little bit about Wreck Bay's
history and culture and see some of the significant sites that have been damaged
by this contamination.
1.55 I was heartbroken to hear the stories of loss and suffering and see for myself
how the water has been poisoned and the animals (totems) are suffering.
Nothing can make up for what Wreck Bay people have endured, certainly not a
few thousand dollars of compensation.
1.56 At the same time, I was struck by the strength and resilience of the community
and impressed by the knowledge and dedication of the Bherwerre Rangers, who
continue caring for Country despite the devastating impacts of PFAS leaching
onto the land and into the water from nearby Defence facilities.
1.57 Community members described finding out about the PFAS, and the restrictions
it would suddenly place on their lifestyle and culture, as an abrupt and
profound shock, causing deep pain. Even more distressing was being told that
this poison is forever.
1.58 Wreck Bay people will never again be able to use their lands and waters in the
ways they have done for thousands of years. It is no longer safe to eat many of
the traditional foods, gather plants for medicine and cultural purposes, for
children to swim in local creeks and rock pools, or for women to birth on
Country in sacred waters.
1.59 Thousands of years of cultural practice, customs and traditions were stopped,
practically overnight, with little recognition or acknowledgement from the
authorities responsible for this disaster.
1.60 This failure of the Commonwealth is disappointingly typical and a symptom of
the continued cultural genocide on First Peoples, violating their inherent rights.
The rights of Indigenous peoples
1.61 As First Peoples, we have suffered from historic injustices as a direct result of
colonisation and dispossession of our lands and resources. The United Nations
(UN) recognises these injustices have prevented Indigenous peoples from
exercising their right to development in accordance with their own needs and
interests.
The right to practice culture and maintain health
1.62 Recognising this history of dispossession and injustice, the UN Declaration on the
Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP), adopted by the UN General Assembly on
301
13 September 2007, declares that First Peoples have a protected right to practise
their culture and customs. Article 11 states that Indigenous peoples have the
right to 'practise and revitalize their cultural traditions and customs', including
'past, present and future manifestations of their cultures'.7
1.63 Related to this, Article 25 outlines the right of Indigenous peoples to:
… maintain and strengthen their distinctive spiritual relationship with their
traditionally owned or otherwise occupied and used lands, territories,
waters and coastal seas and other resources and to uphold their
responsibilities to future generations in this regard.8
1.64 Article 24 states that Indigenous peoples 'have the right to their traditional
medicines and to maintain their health practices', as well as the right to access
all social and health services without discrimination. Article 24 compels states
to 'take the necessary steps' to ensure Indigenous individuals are able to enjoy
'the highest attainable standards of physical and mental health'.9
1.65 Defence and other Commonwealth agencies, along with state health and
environment protection authorities, have issued precautionary advice that
substantively interferes with First Nations communities' right to practice their
culture and customs in PFAS-contaminated areas. Traditional food sources have
been suddenly prohibited, with no alternative provided, and no compensation
paid. Access to lands, waters, flora and fauna, integral to traditional and cultural
practices have been severely restricted.
1.66 First Peoples have not received an acknowledgement, an apology, or
compensation from the Commonwealth or any other government for these
injustices, nor has sufficient action been taken to address their substantive
nature.
1.67 The majority of litigated and non-litigated claims for damages against the
Commonwealth relating to PFAS have been limited to landowners in the
affected communities. These claims have been paid on the basis of diminution
of property values, perceived degradation of land quality, and loss of
'enjoyment' of land (considered a 'nuisance-style' claim in the courts).
1.68 The Wreck Bay class action settlement was unique among PFAS class actions in
Australia because the claim was commenced on behalf of the Aboriginal
community and sought damages for cultural loss—the first case of its kind in
Australia.
7 United Nations declaration on the rights of indigenous peoples, resolution adopted by the General
assembly on 13 September 2007, p. 11 (accessed 6 November 2025).
8 United Nations declaration on the rights of indigenous peoples, 2007, p. 19.
9 United Nations declaration on the rights of indigenous peoples, 2007, p. 18.
302
1.69 It may have been a first, but it was also disappointing and disheartening for
many in the community.
1.70 As the committee heard in Nowra, Australia's colonial legal system has no way
of measuring, valuing, or fairly compensating First Peoples for the loss of
culture and traditions caused by chemical contamination of their lands and
waters.
1.71 Lawyers told the committee that, in their view, the $22 million settlement was
the best that could be achieved in a legal system that has no precedent or
mechanism to measure or value cultural loss.
1.72 If this is right, then the law needs to change.
1.73 Most community members received a few thousand dollars in compensation for
what amounts to a permanent, profound disruption to their culture, traditions,
food supply, and use of the land. This is grossly inadequate.
1.74 Along with culture and traditions, First Peoples have the right to maintain
health practices and enjoy the highest standards of physical and mental health.
Like non-Indigenous people, First Peoples in PFAS-affected areas—including
Wreck Bay Aboriginal people, and Jawoyn, Dagoman, Wardaman and other
First Peoples around the Katherine River—have been denied that right. Their
health has been impacted in numerous ways, with governments failing to
acknowledge those impacts, or provide compensation.
1.75 I was deeply concerned to hear, for example, about the failure of government
agencies to take into account how restrictions on fishing are impacting the diets
of Aboriginal people around the Katherine River. What might be regarded as
small, administrative changes by the authorities has real impacts on peoples'
lives, their health and their ability to practice culture.
1.76 The committee's recommendations—including Recommendations 18 to 24,
which deal with specific impacts on First Nations communities—aim to address
some of the damage that has been done to the physical and mental health of
people in areas heavily affected by PFAS contamination, including First Peoples.
1.77 I also note advice from Shine Lawyers that it remains open for community
members to raise health-related personal injury claims in the future.
1.78 People whose lives have been turned upside down by PFAS deserve
acknowledgement, support and compensation. That said, it is expensive and
stressful to go through these legal processes, and people should not have to take
the government to court to be compensated for the health impacts of chemical
contamination, such as PFAS.
The right to conserve and protect the environment
1.79 Globally, many Indigenous peoples feel connected in a profound way to their
lands, waters, and the environment. Environmental destruction and
303
contamination are a continuation of the violence of dispossession and
colonisation.
1.80 Article 29 of the UNDRIP states that Indigenous peoples 'have the right to the
conservation and protection of the environment and the productive capacity of
their lands or territories and resources'. It commits states to 'take effective
measures to ensure that no storage or disposal of hazardous materials shall take
place on the lands or territories of indigenous peoples without their free, prior
and informed consent'.10
1.81 All of Australia's Defence bases are on stolen land. The First Peoples Clans and
Nations impacted by these bases did not choose for these facilities to be built on
their land and have no say in the activities that take place on these bases,
including the use of toxic chemicals, even though, according to the UNDRIP,
they should have the right to Free, Prior and Informed Consent (see next section
for further detail) on what impacts their lands and waters.
1.82 The Australian Government and the Department of Defence failed to take
effective measures to ensure that PFAS were not stored, used, or disposed of on
these lands. PFAS, and many other hazardous substances, have been allowed to
contaminate Defence sites all over this country—and surrounding lands and
waters—taking away the right of First Peoples to conserve and protect their
environments.
1.83 Australia's current environmental laws exclude First Peoples from decision-
making and disrespect their connection to the land. Protection of First Peoples'
Country and cultures, and their right to Free, Prior and Informed Consent, must
be embedded in Australia's environmental legislation, and all laws relating to
the management of pollution, including PFAS.
1.84 In the absence of environmental laws that protect First Peoples' rights, the
committee's recommendations urge the Commonwealth to form meaningful,
formal relationships with First Nations communities which empower those
communities to make decisions about the future management of their lands and
waters.
The right to make decisions on the development and use of lands and waters
1.85 Article 32 declares that Indigenous peoples 'have the right to determine and
develop priorities and strategies for the development or use of their lands or
territories and other resources'. It commits states to 'consult and cooperate in
good faith' with First Peoples, to 'obtain their free and informed consent' before
approving any project affecting their lands, and to provide 'just and fair redress'
for such incursions. Critically, the declaration also states that 'appropriate
10 United Nations declaration on the rights of indigenous peoples, 2007, p. 21.
304
measures shall be taken to mitigate adverse environmental, economic, social,
cultural or spiritual impact'.11
1.86 The tokenistic communication and consultation efforts of Commonwealth
agencies, such as Defence, have not met the government's responsibility to
consult and cooperate in good faith or provide just and fair redress for permanently
damaging culturally significant lands and waters.
1.87 After the committee's visit to Wreck Bay in January 2025, it appears that the
Department of Defence immediately increased its consultation efforts and acted
on the inadequate signage that had been in place for many years.
1.88 It is unfortunate that it took the visit of a parliamentary committee to motivate
Defence to improve its response in Wreck Bay. It is also unfortunate that the
Wreck Bay Aboriginal community had to take the government to court to get
the small amount of redress that it got, while other Aboriginal communities
whose land has been poisoned by PFAS have not received any compensation for
cultural loss.
1.89 These failures represent a broader system of injustice that touches every aspect
of First People's lives in this country. Addressing these systemic issues may have
been outside the scope of this inquiry, however, implementation of the
committee's recommendations would go some way to restoring the right to self-
determination for First Peoples whose land is impacted by PFAS.
1.90 In addition to the recommendations made in the majority report, evidence from
the Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community Council (WBACC) (discussed in the
committee's interim report) indicated there may be an opportunity to review the
Wreck Bay land claim in light of the PFAS contamination. Noting that this
contamination was not understood at the time of the original land claim, there
may be scope for a review, and an updated agreement which takes into account
the damage caused by PFAS.
1.91 WBACC also suggested consideration could be given to providing
uncontaminated land on which the community could build new housing for
community members who no longer wish to live in Wreck Bay Village.
1.92 The government should seriously consider these suggestions and engage in
good faith with WBACC and the community. I recommend:
Recommendation 6
1.93 That the Australian Government consults with the Wreck Bay Aboriginal
Community Council and community members on:
 reviewing and updating the Wreck Bay land claim in light of the impacts
of PFAS contamination; and
11 United Nations declaration on the rights of indigenous peoples, 2007, p. 23.
305
 providing uncontaminated land on which the community could build
new housing for community members.
Recommendation 7
1.94 That the Australian Government, in any development or implementation of
legislation affecting land and waters, adheres to First Peoples rights as
outlined in the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous
Peoples, including their right to Free, Prior and Informed Consent.
Stronger environmental management of PFAS
1.95 The committee made a number of recommendations designed to improve the
way in which PFAS as an environmental contaminant is managed. However,
over the course of the inquiry, the committee received some additional
suggestions which warrant consideration.
1.96 For example, the Environmental Defenders Office suggested reforming the
National Environment Protection Measures Framework to better regulate PFAS
and recommended development of a water quality national environment
protection measure.
1.97 These recommendations merit serious consideration by the government.
1.98 I note that proposed changes to Australia's national environmental laws are
currently being debated in the Parliament and may impact on the applicability
of these recommendations as drafted. Nevertheless, I recommend:
Recommendation 8
1.99 That the Australian Government:
 reforms relevant national environment protection measures to better
regulate PFAS; and
 prioritises the development of a water quality national environment
protection measure which establishes enforceable water quality
standards, objectives and goals to protect our water systems from PFAS
and other contaminants.
1.100 The committee heard evidence that Australia's PFAS National Environmental
Management Plan (NEMP 3.0) was once a groundbreaking document but would
benefit from being reviewed.
1.101 Experts on the NEMP argued that it should undergo an independent review to
ensure it remains risk-proportionate, clear and actionable. As such, I
recommend:
306
Recommendation 9
1.102 The Australian Government leads a review of the PFAS National
Environmental Management Plan to ensure that the guidance it provides
around remediation of contaminated land, re-use of resource recovery
products, and criteria for re-use of biosolids, as well as the guideline values
for investigation and risk assessment, are risk-proportionate, clear and
actionable.
Landfill disposal of PFAS
1.103 It was concerning to hear that landfill disposal is a common practice for PFAS
contaminated waste. While acknowledging that currently available destructive
technologies are expensive and energy intensive, simply moving PFAS
contamination off-site and into landfill is not a long-term solution.
1.104 Recommendations 29 and 30 of the committee's report are designed to increase
the availability of a variety of different remediation technologies, including
more environmentally sustainable options, and reduce Australia's dependence
on landfill disposal of PFAS-contaminated waste. In addition, I recommend:
Recommendation 10
1.105 That the Australian Government pursues a coordinated approach to phasing
out PFAS landfill disposal as a routine practice and focuses on developing a
greater range of destructive technologies and supporting the development of
innovative emerging destruction methods.
The problem of artificial turf
1.106 Evidence received by the committee about artificial, or synthetic, turf indicates
that it is ubiquitous around Australia and poorly regulated. NSW alone has over
200 synthetic turf fields. Concerningly, artificial turf is also often used on school
grounds, where children have daily contact with it.
1.107 It was disturbing to hear that research from the University of Technology
Sydney indicates a single artificial turf field can lose from 800kg to 3200kg of
PFAS-contaminated particulate directly into the environment.
1.108 As these artificial turf fields reach their 'end of life', there are potentially
hundreds of thousands of kilograms of PFAS-contaminated pollutants that will
be left to enter the environment, contaminating soil and leaching into waterways
for decades to come.
1.109 To reduce this damage, I recommend:
Recommendation 11
1.110 That the Australian Government works with states and territories to progress
national regulation that would ban PFAS in synthetic turf, artificial ground
307
coverings and associated products, and responsibly manage the impacts of
PFAS contamination from legacy artificial turf products.
PFAS in pesticides
1.111 Inquiry participants, including Dr Matt Landos and Dr Mariann Lloyd-Smith
highlighted concerns with Australia's regulatory regime for pesticides and the
risks PFAS in pesticides might pose to the food chain.
1.112 Dr Landos revealed that the Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines
Authority (APVMA) often relies on manufacturers' claims about what is in their
products, rather than conducting tests. This approach is flawed.
1.113 We have seen that manufacturers cannot be blindly trusted to do the right thing
when it comes to disclosing PFAS in their products. In fact, the committee heard
from Victorian firefighters that firefighting foams which manufacturers claimed
were PFAS free, did in fact contain PFAS, as discovered when the firefighters
undertook independent testing.
1.114 It is the Australian Government's responsibility to ensure that chemicals
released on the Australian market are safe to humans and the environment. This
responsibility cannot be passed on to manufacturers with little to no oversight.
1.115 In line with the Schedule 7 listing of several PFAS, and in anticipation of the
listing of many more, I recommend:
Recommendation 12
1.116 That the Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines Authority (AVPMA)
bans the use of existing and new pesticides which contain PFAS.
1.117 Dr Landos also pointed out that Australia has no re-registration scheme for
pesticides. A pesticides re-registration scheme would require periodic
examination (every seven to 15 years) of active constituents and products in
pesticides to ensure safety, based on consideration of current science.
1.118 This need was previously recognised when a re-registration scheme for
agricultural and veterinary chemicals was introduced in 2013. Unfortunately, it
was reversed just a year later.
1.119 However, it is important that pesticide products that were thought to be safe at
the time they were approved for use in Australia, need to be reviewed
periodically to ensure they do not contain emerging contaminants.
1.120 When we know better, we must do better. As such, I recommend:
Recommendation 13
1.121 That the Australian Government legislates a re-registration scheme for
pesticides, requiring the Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines
Authority (AVPMA) to review pesticide products approved for use in
308
Australia periodically, to ensure they remain safe and free from hazardous
chemicals.
1.122 The legislated review periods should be risk-based and developed in
conjunction with independent scientific and environmental experts.
PFAS on farming land
1.123 While the committee made recommendations designed to improve public access
to information about areas where PFAS contamination exists and remediation
activity is occurring, I am concerned about the environmental impacts of
contamination remaining unaddressed. Additionally, in the absence of any
suitable alternatives or compensation, farmers are put under significant
financial and ethical pressure, while contaminated foods risk being released
onto the market. I recommend:
Recommendation 14
1.124 That the Department of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry establishes a
compensation scheme for farmers whose land and produce is affected by
PFAS contamination.
Better, faster chemical regulation
1.125 The committee made a number of recommendations that, if implemented,
would vastly improve Australia's chemical regulation. Recommendations 39 to
44 address regulator resourcing issues, enforcing the Schedule 7 listing of PFAS,
mandatory product labelling, and faster domestic integration of international
chemical conventions.
1.126 In addition to these critical recommendations, I recommend:
Recommendation 15
1.127 That, given the growing evidence of long-term harm to the environment and
human health, the Environment Minister takes a precautionary approach,
using their discretion under Schedule 7 of the Industrial Chemicals
Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS) to make a scheduling
decision to include all PFAS chemicals—including new and short-chain PFAS
chemicals—in Schedule 7 of the IChEMS register, and, as a consequence,
progresses a general ban on the import, export, manufacture, and use of PFAS
chemicals, with the exception of 'essential uses' (see Chair's Recommendation
12).
'Essential use' framework
1.128 I acknowledge that there may be some essential uses for PFAS in Australia.
1.129 The committee heard, for instance, that some medical devices and medications
require the addition of PFAS to be effective, and that effective fire-retardant
309
coatings for protective clothing worn by firefighters has not yet been produced
without PFAS.
1.130 That said, I believe the chemical industry vastly inflates the essentiality of PFAS
in most products and has used this argument to oppose wider phasing out of
PFAS chemicals.
1.131 Australia should move to ban all PFAS as a default position and establish an
independent, scientifically rigorous mechanism for determining true essential
uses—something that was supported by a large number of inquiry participants.
I recommend:
Recommendation 16
1.132 That the Australian Government works with experts to develop an
'essentiality framework' for PFAS applications, with an independent
scientific body being responsible for evaluating what constitutes an
'essential' use.
Evidence required to bring chemicals to market
1.133 As discussed in the majority report, the way in which Australia regulates
chemicals puts the burden of proof for evidence of harm onto regulators,
academic researchers and environmental bodies, instead of chemical
manufacturers.
1.134 Chemical manufacturers should instead have to demonstrate safety before
bringing new chemicals into the market in the same way pharmaceutical
companies do before introducing new drugs. I recommend:
Recommendation 17
1.135 That the Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme (AICIS)
requires complete toxicological data before allowing chemicals to come to
market.
Product labelling
1.136 In its report, the committee recommended that the Environment and Health
Departments work with the Australian Competition and Consumer
Commission (ACCC) to develop mandatory product labelling.
1.137 The ACCC has a role to play in enforcing Australia's PFAS ban and stemming
the flow of products containing PFAS into Australia. As Australia's consumer
protection and product safety regulator, the ACCC can recommend that the
Minister makes an information standard in relation to goods of a particular kind
(that is, the Minister propose product labelling requirements) and should do so
in relation to PFAS.
310
1.138 The committee wrote to ACCC Chair, Ms Gina Cass-Gottlieb, on 5 November
2025 to encourage the ACCC to work with the government to actively explore
options for giving effect to the Schedule 7 listing of PFAS, including the option
of mandatory product labelling.
1.139 In addition to the recommendation on product labelling included in the majority
report, I recommend:
Recommendation 18
1.140 That the Australian Government introduces a requirement for manufacturers
to make publicly available a full list of product ingredients for each product,
including packaging, and that the government develops a public register for
this purpose.
Safe, clean, accessible drinking water
1.141 The right to safe, clean drinking water is an internationally recognised human
right which derives from the right to an adequate standard of living under
Article 11(1) of the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights.12
1.142 It is well-established that First Nations communities in Australia are
significantly less likely to have access to good quality drinking water. PFAS
contamination has predominantly affected drinking water in rural and remote
communities and has disproportionately impacted First Peoples.
1.143 All people deserve access to sufficient, safe, acceptable, physically accessible,
and affordable water, including drinking water.
1.144 While acknowledging that the provision of drinking water is currently a state
and territory responsibility, there is a role for the Commonwealth to take
leadership in ensuring equality of access across the nation. Also, as the entity
responsible for much of the PFAS contamination that impacts drinking water
supplies, the Commonwealth cannot shirk its responsibility in this area.
1.145 As such, in addition to the recommendations around drinking water made in
the majority report, I recommend:
Recommendation 19
1.146 That all drinking water sources be regularly tested for PFAS and other
hazardous chemical contamination.
12 United Nations, About water and sanitation, undated (accessed 7 November 2025).
311
Recommendation 20
1.147 That the National Health and Medical Research Council establishes
guidelines and limits for the much wider variety of PFAS chemicals which
are already found in drinking water in Australia.
Recommendation 21
1.148 That the National Health and Medical Research Council reviews and updates
the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines every three years to ensure they
capture emerging scientific findings on potentially harmful substances in
drinking water.
1.149 The committee heard a range of views on the management of drinking water in
Australia. In short, a dispersed system of regulation and governance has led to
inconsistent results, where some states and territories are ahead of others in
terms of water quality monitoring and testing for PFAS.
1.150 Dr Stuart Khan from the University of Sydney argued that the best way to
achieve national consistency in managing water quality and addressing the
challenges of PFAS would be for the Commonwealth to take a more active role
in setting drinking water quality and risk management requirements.
1.151 Specifically, Dr Khan proposed the development of an Australian Safe Drinking
Water Act. A national safe drinking water act would enable the Commonwealth
to mandate best practices for managing drinking water quality, as defined by
the Australian Drinking Water Guidelines.
1.152 State and territory management of drinking water has led to inequality,
inconsistency, and poor outcomes in rural and remote communities, including
First Nations communities. As such, I recommend:
Recommendation 22
1.153 That the Senate establishes an inquiry into safe drinking water, including
access to and management of drinking water, water security, contaminants
and other relevant matters.
Recommendation 23
1.154 That, pending the findings of a Senate inquiry into safe drinking water
(Recommendation 22), the Australian Government introduces an Australian
Safe Drinking Water Act to enable the Commonwealth to set consistent
standards for safe drinking water around the nation.
Make the polluters pay
1.155 Cleaning up and dealing with the damage and pollution caused by the use of
PFAS-containing firefighting foams in Australia has already cost taxpayers over
312
$1 billion, not even taking into account contamination resulting from a wide
range of other PFAS-containing products.
1.156 In the US, companies responsible for producing and selling PFAS—even when
they knew it was hazardous—have paid out billions to settle legal actions. A
number of these claims have been brought by water utility providers and state
governments.
1.157 Recently, in Australia, a small number of PFAS-related actions have been
launched—most notably, a consumer-led PFAS class action against 3M
Australia Pty Ltd by Qenos Olefins Pty Ltd, filed in the Victorian Supreme Court
in December 2024.13
1.158 Evidence heard by the committee suggested that the Department of Defence
may be actively considering legal action. It is surprising that it has taken this
long, given there is no advantage for governments here to foot the large bills for
contamination these private companies have caused.
1.159 Why have Australian authorities been so slow to act to hold 3M and other
chemical companies to account?
1.160 The committee recognised that this injustice must be addressed and
recommended that the Commonwealth government explores avenues for
remediation costs to be recovered through litigation.
1.161 It is critical that 3M, and other producers and retailers of PFAS-containing
products, be held to account for the harm their products have caused to
firefighters, communities, and the environment.
1.162 Many of these companies knowingly produced harmful PFAS-containing
products for decades, while suppressing adverse scientific evidence and falsely
marketing these products as safe. As such, I recommend:
Recommendation 24
1.163 That the Australian Government launches litigation against 3M and any other
relevant chemical company as soon as possible, seeking restitution on behalf
of the Australian people for the misleading and deceptive conduct of these
companies, breaching their duty of care, and causing damage to communities,
culture, heritage, food sources, lifestyles, and the environment.
Concluding comments
1.164 I would like to sincerely thank my fellow committee members—including
former committee members—for their time and commitment to this inquiry.
13 Amanda Beattie, Zoe Jones and Nick Salagaras, PFAS litigation: A deep dive into the rise of consumer-
led claims and its implications, Wotton Kearney, 12 March 2024 (accessed 10 November 2025).
313
1.165 It was a privilege to conduct this inquiry, and I sincerely thank every witness
who made a submission or appeared at a hearing.
1.166 Again, I thank the community at Wreck Bay for graciously hosting the
committee. Our visit there is an experience I will always remember, for sharing
your experiences and showing us your beautiful Country.
1.167 I also thank Enviropacific for showing the committee around the SOLVE
hazardous waste facility, and Airservices and Australia Pacific Airports for the
tour of the remediation site at Launceston Airport.
1.168 This report contains critical evidence about PFAS from a vast array of different
sources, and I would encourage all who are involved in regulation and/or
remediation of PFAS, the provision of water and other services to regional and
remote communities, and anyone with an interest in chemical contamination to
read it. As such, I recommend:
Recommendation 25
1.169 That the Australian Government reports the findings of this inquiry and
publishes the committee's report on the PFAS website (www.pfas.gov.au).
1.170 In closing, this country has been inactive and silent about PFAS chemicals for
too long. It is time for governments on all levels to take this seriously and
actively implement the important recommendations of this report, for the sake
of human health and the health of our lands and waters. There can be no more
excuses, no more delay. We owe it to current and future generations to act.
Senator Lidia Thorpe
Chair

Australian Greens additional comments
1.1 PFAS chemicals, or 'forever chemicals', build up in the environment and our
bodies, causing serious health risks. Australia lags behind other countries in
banning these harmful substances, risking our nation becoming a dumping
ground for dangerous chemicals.
1.2 Urgent national action is needed to protect people, food systems, and the
environment from PFAS, or 'forever' chemicals.
1.3 The recommendations made in this report are extensive and the Australian
Greens support the recommendations listed in the committee's report.
1.4 While the Greens support the recommendations made in the report as positive
steps towards limiting the ongoing impact of PFAS and forever chemicals, the
Australian Greens believe action should be more significant.
1.5 The Greens ultimately believe the Federal Government should take a leadership
role through the Environment Minister's meeting, or at an executive level
through National Cabinet, to coordinate national action across federal and state
jurisdictions and support the phase-out of all non-essential PFAS uses.
1.6 The Australian Greens would like to also acknowledge the work of our NSW
Greens colleague Cate Faerhmann MLC, who chaired the NSW Parliament's
recent Select Committee on PFAS Contamination in Waterways and Drinking
Water Supplies Throughout New South Wales. This Select Committee handed
down its final report on 11 September 2025 and provided a number of important
recommendations for state and federal parliaments to act on to reduce health
and environmental harm from PFAS and forever chemicals.
1.7 In addition to this report and the recommendations contained within it, and our
stated position that urgent government intervention is needed to minimise the
current and future impacts of PFAS, the Australian Greens would like to
highlight that in the lead up to the May 2025 federal election, the Greens took a
comprehensive plan to the Australian public aimed at effective intervention on
PFAS and forever chemicals.
1.8 The Australian Greens' May 2025 federal election plan to tackle PFAS and
forever chemicals included the following commitments:
 Ban all types of PFAS across all uses, including in consumer packaging,
agrichemicals, and cosmetic products.
 Adopt a design-first approach by regulating harmful chemicals and
contaminants out of packaging to prevent pollution at the source.
 Harmonise national frameworks for managing Persistent Organic Pollutants
(POPs), including PFAS, in the production of organic compost.
315
316
 Develop a timeline for eliminating contaminants from compostable food
packaging to support food waste recycling and ease the burden on the
waste and resource recovery sector.
1.9 We acknowledge that across this inquiry some witnesses and submitters have
provided evidence and testimony that there are some essential uses of PFAS.
However, these are highly specific and the Greens believe much stronger
regulation and oversight must be enacted to monitor when and how these
chemicals are used. This would provide greater transparency to the public, who
rightly have concerns about the health and environmental impacts of PFAS and
forever chemicals.
1.10 The Australian public can be assured the Greens will continue to advocate
strongly for urgent action on PFAS and forever chemicals across all levels of
government.
Senator Peter Whish-Wilson
Senator for Tasmania
Australian Greens Spokesperson for Waste and Recycling
Dissenting report from Labor Senators
Introduction
1.1 The Senate Select Committee on PFAS (per and polyfluoroalkyl substances)
(Committee) was appointed on 22 August 2024 by the 47th Australian Parliament
and reappointed by the 48th Australian Parliament on 28 July 2025. The
appointment and conduct of the inquiry are set out in Chapter 1 of the
committee's report.
1.2 Labor Senators would like to thank all submitters and witnesses who provided
evidence during the inquiry, including those who provided personal testimony.
1.3 We thank the Wreck Bay Aboriginal community, Enviropacific, Australia Pacific
Airports (Launceston), and Airservices Australia for hosting the committee as
we conducted informative site visits. We also thank Assistant Chief Fire Officer
(ACFO) Mick Tisbury and Commander Dave Hamilton for travelling to
Canberra to present the findings of decades of work to the committee.
1.4 Labor Senators note the wide scope of this inquiry's terms of reference and the
depth and breadth of submissions and oral testimony received. We
acknowledge the depth of feeling and the personal experiences of those who
have been exposed to PFAS at higher levels, including firefighters and others
with occupational or environmental exposure.
1.5 The assessment and regulation of chemicals is a complex and highly technical
area, involving expert scientific research in environmental science, toxicology,
epidemiology, and other disciplines.
1.6 Governments and regulatory agencies are required to manage a variety of
potential hazards, including hazardous chemicals. There are also other
persistent organic pollutants that governments must manage. Authorities must
take a careful, responsible, and risk-based approach to managing all
environmental contaminants, including PFAS.
1.7 Labor Senators greatly appreciate the volume of evidence and the discussion in
the committee's report, and support several of its recommendations (although
some in amended form). Across Chapters 2 to 10, the committee's report
primarily represents the evidence received throughout the inquiry. However,
Labor Senators have reservations about some of the conclusions that have been
reached from this evidence, and with some of the recommendations that have
been made in Chapters 11 and 12.
1.8 The issues raised by the use and presence of PFAS in Australia and its impact
on human health and the environment are complex and far-reaching. The
evidence makes clear the significant community and scientific concern about the
317
318
presence and impact of PFAS in Australia and these concerns need to be
addressed in a holistic, coherent, evidence-based, and logical way.
1.9 Further, there is a growing body of research, literature, and evidence in
Australia and around the world about the potential impacts of PFAS on human
health and the natural environment and biota and ways to manage the PFAS-
related problems.
1.10 The matters considered by this dissenting report are based on submissions and
evidence received by the inquiry, which necessarily reflects information and
scientific guidance that is available now. As further information or evidence
becomes available, existing policies, guidance, and regulations must respond
and adapt.
1.11 Labor Senators' views on the evidence and our recommendations are outlined
below.
Size and complexity of PFAS (and persistent pollutant) problem
1.12 The per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) encompass a family of synthetic
chemicals with carbon-fluorine bonds, which give them characteristics
(including resistance to heat, water, and other substances) that have led to their
use in a range of different contexts including firefighting foams, cookware,
furniture, food packaging, clothing, electronics, agriculture, pharmaceuticals,
and medical devices.1
1.13 PFAS are near ubiquitous in the environment. The persistence and mobility of
PFAS mean that most people will encounter PFAS in their lives, are likely to
have PFAS in their bodies, and PFAS is thought to be present all over the world.2
1.14 There are thousands of different chemical compounds within the PFAS
classification.3 The term 'PFAS' itself does not inform as to the level of risk or
harm that a particular compound may present. Rather, it communicates that the
compounds under this term share the same trait for having a fully fluorinated
methyl or methylene carbon atom.4
1.15 The differences in the chemical character of PFAS mean that different
compounds tend to have different characteristics and present different levels of
1 Australian Academy of Technological Sciences and Engineering, Submission 18, p 1; Department of
Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33, p. 3.
2 CSIRO, Submission 22, p. 8; Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water,
Submission 33, p. 3.
3 Chemistry Australia, Submission 101, p. 1.
4 Department of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease Control, Submission
100, p. 3.
319
risk.5 PFAS are sometimes classified as long-chain or short-chain compounds.
Long-chain PFAS are thought to have higher potential risk.6
1.16 PFAS have been extensively used for a wide range of different purposes and
have varying degrees of necessity depending on the use case. PFAS are
considered critical or essential to the efficacy of some products or processes for
which where there are no known viable or safer substitutes, including in the
semiconductors industry as well as in the medical, pharmaceutical, and
agricultural industries.7
1.17 The widespread presence of PFAS in the environment, as well as the range of
risk profiles and use cases, make the development of appropriate and effective
regulation a complex and layered public policy problem.
1.18 The inquiry heard differing views about the appropriateness of broad-based
bans on the use, manufacture, and import of PFAS. Concerns were raised that
chemical innovation has often outpaced regulation from a safety perspective
and that the risk assessment process for managing chemicals is slow and time
consuming.8 Concerns were also raised that broad-based banning of PFAS as a
class would prevent their use in essential cases, including the production of
certain vital medical devices and pharmaceutical products.9
1.19 In assessing the impact of PFAS on human health, the picture is no less
complicated. There is a growing body of research, literature and evidence
regarding potential health risks associated with PFAS exposure, and this had
led to justified concerns about the long-term impacts on public health.10 There is
evidence that exposure to types of PFAS is associated with a number of health
5 Crop Life, Submission 88, p. 3.
6 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33, p. 3. Long-
chain PFAS are defined as perfluorosulfonates with six or more carbons, perfluorocarboxylic acids
with seven or more carbons, and their precursors. Short-chain PFAS are defined as
perfluorosulfonates with less than six carbons, perfluorocarboxylic acids with six or less carbons,
and their precursors. National PFAS Position Statement, 21 October 2019, p. 2. Australian Industrial
Chemicals Introduction Scheme, Submission 65, p. 4.
7 CSIRO, Submission 22, pp. 9–11; Chemistry Australia, Submission 101, p. 2.
8 Dr Bradley Clarke, Associate Professor in Analytical Chemistry and Environmental Science,
University of Melbourne, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 58.
9 Professor Oliver Jones, Professor of Chemistry, RMIT University, Australian Academy of Science,
Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 10; Dr Nathan Kilah, Senior Lecturer in Chemistry, University
of Tasmania, Australian Academy of Science, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 11; Chemistry
Australia, Submission 101, p. 2; Professor Denis Michael O'Carroll, Professor, University of New
South Wales, Committee Hansard, 13 November 2024, p. 22.
10 Australian Academy of Technological Sciences and Engineering, Submission 18, p 1; Cancer Council,
Submission 62, pp. 3 and 5.
320
impacts.11 However, a causative relationship between health effects and PFAS
exposure has not been definitively established to date.12
1.20 A number of other issues emerged from the evidence and submissions to the
inquiry, including:
(a) the evolving and developing 'best practice' approaches to quarantining and
remediation of PFAS-contaminated land and water;
(b) the effectiveness and sustainability of destructive and non-destructive
remediation methods and technologies; and
(c) the availability, effectiveness and safety of PFAS replacements or
substitutes for firefighting and other critical uses (such as medical
treatment).
1.21 A further challenge was the acknowledgement that there were gaps in
knowledge, research and data about PFAS, particularly in Australia.13
Principles and methods of evaluation
1.22 The level of complexity and concern make it particularly important to be
rigorous in developing holistic, coherent, practical, and evidence-based
responses to the impact of PFAS in Australia.
1.23 In a complex and evolving landscape, such policy responses inevitably involve
trade-offs and create risks associated with unintended consequences and
adverse impacts that may flow from even well-intentioned policy interventions.
1.24 The Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water
submitted that policy and regulatory interventions to address PFAS must be
proportionate to the risk, be pragmatic, and make efficient and effective use of
available resources.14
1.25 Similarly, the Intergovernmental Agreement on a National Framework for
Responding to PFAS Contamination seeks to protect the environment and
human health through actions that are effective, implementable, financially and
logistically sustainable, proportionate to risk, and support economic stability.15
11 Department of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease Control, Submission
100, p. 5; Minderoo Foundation, Submission 115, p. 5; Cancer Council, Submission 62, pp. 3 and 5;
Queensland Alliance of Environmental Health Sciences, Submission 112, p. 1.
12 Department of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease Control, Submission
100, p. 6; Per- and Polyfluoalkyl Substances. enHealth, Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS)
enHealth Guidance Statement, updated February 2024, p. 4 (accessed 18 November 2025).
13 CSIRO, Submission 22, pp. 4, 6 and 9.
14 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33, p. 9.
15 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33, p. 4.
321
1.26 However, it was noted that there may be net adverse outcomes and unintended
consequences of ill-considered policy interventions.16 For example, scientifically
inaccurate or one-size-fits-all regulatory approaches may divert scarce resources
to low-risk substances (which is inefficient and less effective at reducing harm)
and unnecessarily increase the complexity and cost of regulatory activities.17
1.27 In guiding interventions, the benefits and challenges of using risk assessments
in the context of PFAS were canvassed. Such risk assessments require risk
mitigation measures or remediation actions when the level of the chemical in
the environment is expected to be greater than the level that is considered likely
to cause harm.18
1.28 Many witnesses advocated, sensibly, for some application of the precautionary
principle or a precautionary approach to dealing with PFAS.19 That is, for
instance, if there is a reasonable suspicion a chemical could cause harm, then a
lack of scientific certainty should not be used to prevent action.20 Yet, other
witnesses also highlighted some of the challenges of taking a precautionary
approach to PFAS in broad or general terms.21
1.29 In the absence of an accepted evaluative framework or methodology, there are
significant challenges to weighing and relying upon technical or scientific
evidence across multiple fields (particularly in the context of differing and
contradictory views from experts).
1.30 Without technical expertise and given the complexity of many of the issues and
trade-offs involved and the ongoing development and evolution of the research
and data in this field, Labor Senators are cautious about recommending
prescriptive policy interventions that may not be supported by evidence when
properly considered or may have unintended and undesirable consequences.
That caution informs this dissenting report's recommendations.

17 Chemistry Australia, Submission 101, p. 2; Environmental Risk Sciences Pty Ltd, Submission 19, p. 1.
18 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33, p. 6.
19 See, for example: Department of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease
Control, Submission 100, p. 5.
20 Dr Bradley Clarke, Associate Professor in Analytical Chemistry and Environmental Science,
University of Melbourne, Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p. 59.

Council Australia, Committee Hansard, 10 June 2025, p. 30.
