Regulatory environment and existing PFAS programs and policies
1.31 The use and presence of PFAS chemicals in the environment presents national
and global issues that require a coordinated framework and approach.22 There
are a number of international agreements and initiatives that apply to the
regulation and management of PFAS.23
1.32 A range of Commonwealth and state regulatory and health policy frameworks
apply to the management of PFAS in Australia.24 An outline of PFAS-related
policy, regulation, agencies, and coordination mechanisms in Australia is set out
in Chapter 10 of the committee's report.
1.33 The approach to PFAS management in Australia is underpinned by various
laws, agreements, regulatory instruments, and management plans, including
the:
(a) Industrial Chemicals Act 2019 (Cth);
(b) Environment Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act 1999 (Cth);
(c) Food Standards Australia New Zealand Act 1991 (Cth);
(d) Intergovernmental Agreement on the Environment 1992 (COAG);
(e) Intergovernmental Agreement on a National Framework for Responding to PFAS
Contamination (2020) (COAG);
(f) National PFAS Position Statement;
(g) PFAS National Environmental Management Plan 3.0 (2025) (PFAS NEMP)
(Environment Ministers of Australia);
(h) National Environment Protection (Assessment of Site Contamination) Measure
1999 (as varied in 2013);
(i) Industrial Chemicals Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS);
(j) Australian and New Zealand guidelines for fresh and marine water quality
(Water Quality Guidelines);
(k) Australian Drinking Water Guidelines, established by the National Health
and Medical Research (NHMRC); and
(l) National Standard for Environmental Risk Management of Industrial Chemicals.
1.34 There are a number of Commonwealth departments, agencies, and bodies
involved in the development and implement of regulation and management of
PFAS in Australia, including the:
(a) Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water;
(b) Department of Health, Disability and Ageing;
(c) Department of Defence;
22 CSIRO, Submission 22, p. 4.
23 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, Submission 33, p. 5.
24 Department of Infrastructure, Transport, Regional Development, Communications and the Arts,
Submission 2, p. 4; crcCARE, Submission 36, p. 13.
323
(d) Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme (which regulates the
import and manufacture of industrial chemicals such as PFAS in
Australia);
(e) Environmental Health Standing Committee (enHealth) of the Australian
Health Protection Committee;
(f) Australian Health Protection Committee;
(g) Australian Pesticides and Veterinary Medicines Authority (APVMA)
(which regulates chemicals sought to be registered and used as
insecticides, fungicide, and herbicides in Australia);
(h) National Health and Medical Research Council (NHRMC) (which is
responsible for the development of PFAS guidelines for drinking and
recreational water);
(i) Food Standards Australia and New Zealand (FSANZ) (which is
responsible for the development of PFAS guidelines for food); and
(j) the Therapeutic Goods Association (which regulates pharmaceutical and
therapeutic products).25
1.35 The regulatory framework that applies to PFAS, and the different agencies
involved in managing the impact of PFAS in Australia, endeavour to manage
and reduce the impact of PFAS in Australia and to reduce potential harm to
human health and the environment.26 Further, Australia has a trusted and
capable complement of agencies that are responsible for public health advice
and environmental protection, and Australia's chemical regulatory agencies are
amongst the best in the world.27
1.36 Nevertheless, these agencies must adapt, and frameworks must be updated, to
reflect any new developments in research and guidance about the impact and
management of PFAS.
PFAS and human health
PFAS Health Advice
1.37 The committee's report outlines Australia's existing health-related advice, while
acknowledging evolving global standards, and emerging research in this field.
As outlined in Chapter 2 of the committee's report, high-quality Australian
research, undertaken by the National Centre for Epidemiology and Population
Health at the Australian National University from 2016–2021, found limited
evidence for adverse health outcomes associated with significant PFAS
25 CSIRO, Submission 22, p. 8.
26 See, for example: Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme, Submission 65, p. 5.
27 Chemistry Australia, Submission 101, p. 3.
324
exposure, other than psychological distress and higher cholesterol levels.28
Specific PFAS may contribute to other health effects, but research has not yet
established causation, and research has not identified strong links with any
disease.
1.38 Understanding the relationship between environmental exposure and health
outcomes is complicated. Research can be slow and yield mixed results, which
can be frustrating, however it is essential to support high quality systematic
research, rather than drawing conclusions from inconclusive data.
1.39 Labor Senators acknowledge emerging international research outlined in the
committee's report, including the International Agency for Research on Cancer's
(IARC) finding that perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) is carcinogenic to humans.
We also acknowledge community concern around the potential health impacts
of PFAS.
1.40 The field of health policy relies heavily on technical and scientific evidence, and
the use of robust evidence-to-decision frameworks, when translating scientific
evidence into policy solutions and regulatory action. As outlined in Chapter 7
of the committee's report, Australia's health policy—including policies relating
to PFAS—is developed using best practice models which are also used in
numerous other countries around the world.29
1.41 The Environmental Health Standing Committee (enHealth) and the Australian
Health Protection Committee have developed guidance statements on key
health issues in relation to PFAS contamination.30 The enHealth PFAS Guidance
Statement provides evidence-based advice on the risks associated with PFAS
exposure. The current version was published in February 2024.
1.42 As a precaution, enHealth continues to recommend exposure to PFAS be
minimised wherever possible. This precautionary advice considers the current
scientific evidence, including the lack of causation data on human health effects
and the ability of these chemicals to persist in humans and in the environment.31
1.43 In light of the IARC advice, the revised Australian Drinking Water Guidelines,
and other developments since February 2024, enHealth is currently conducting
a thorough review of new international evidence on PFAS, which will feed into
the formulation of revised enHealth guidance on PFAS. This revised guidance
28 Professor Martyn Kirk, Dr Kayla Smurthwaite and Ms Susan Trevenar, Submission 85 (47th
Parliament), pp. 1–2.
29 Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe
(received 23 September 2025), [p. 1].
30 Department of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease Control, Submission
100, p. 6.
31 Department of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease Control, Submission
100, p. 5.
325
will be based on expert analysis of the most up-to-date evidence, will be
carefully considered, and will be risk-based.32
1.44 Australian regulators will also continue to monitor international trends and
remain informed regarding any emerging evidence. Should new evidence
indicate Australia's official advice needs to change, it will be updated through
these processes. As a result, Labor Senators do not support Recommendations
1, 2 or 5 in the committee's report, which pre-empt the findings of the current
review.
1.45 However, Labor Senators agree that there may be scope for FSANZ to more
regularly review Tolerable Daily Intakes for PFAS and other chemicals of
concern, and we recommend:
Recommendation 1
1.46 That the Australian Government considers the need to establish periodic
reviews of the Tolerable Daily Intakes for PFAS and other chemicals of
concern.
Monitoring PFAS levels in the population
1.47 Monitoring levels of PFAS and other potentially hazardous substances in the
population helps regulators understand the scale and distribution of
contamination, as well as to track the results of regulatory interventions.
1.48 Labor Senators note results from the Australian Bureau of Statistics' National
Health Measures Survey 2022–2025, which tested for 11 types of PFAS in people
aged 12 and over. While the three most common PFAS were detected in the
blood of most of the test subjects (over 85 per cent), levels were generally lower
than in comparable countries and are reducing over time.33
1.49 This research should be repeated to track the bioaccumulation of PFAS, and
other chemical contaminates, in the Australian population, over time.
1.50 Labor Senators recommend:
Recommendation 2
1.51 That the Australian Government considers commissioning future research to
assess long-term contamination levels and trends in any potential health
impacts of PFAS, and improves data sharing and coordination between
32 Department of Health, Disability and Ageing, response to written questions from Senator Thorpe—
blood testing and enHealth guidance (received 6 August 2025), p. 1.
33 Australian Bureau of Statistics, Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances, Data download: Information on
serum levels of per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) for people aged 12 years and over, 27 May 2025
(accessed 17 November 2025).
326
Commonwealth, state and territory, and local entities, and enhances public
access to PFAS contamination data and information.
Health interventions and blood testing
1.52 Labor Senators acknowledge the evidence provided by firefighters and others
with occupational exposure to PFAS. We share concerns raised by firefighters,
including ACFO Mick Tisbury and Commander Dave Hamilton, about historic
high levels of PFAS exposure among firefighters and others in the field.
1.53 We acknowledge the fear and uncertainty experienced by those with especially
high levels of PFAS exposure, and the evidence these witnesses provided on the
perceived benefits of PFAS blood testing and PFAS serum reduction methods,
such as blood or plasma donations.
1.54 Labor Senators acknowledge evidence that blood and/or plasma donations may
reduce a person's PFAS blood levels, over time. However, we also note the
expert view of the enHealth committee that there is currently no evidence to
support the clinical usefulness of PFAS blood testing or serum level reduction.
Without clear evidence that PFAS exposure leads to any particular disease, there
is no framework to guide clinicians in administering PFAS blood testing, PFAS
serum reduction, or any related health interventions.34
1.55 Evidence does not currently exist to support PFAS blood testing, PFAS serum
level reduction, or specific health interventions based on PFAS blood levels. As
a result, Labor Senators are unable to support Recommendations 3, 6 and 7.
Should the current or any future review of Australia's PFAS health advice
determine that widespread testing should be undertaken, a holistic and
consistent approach should be developed by the enHealth committee or another
appropriate agency.
1.56 Labor Senators do agree there is a need for further research, and we recommend:
Recommendation 3
1.57 That the Australian Government increases its investment in PFAS research
and development, including in relation to epidemiological research in the
general population and in specific groups at high risk of exposure, such as
firefighters.
Workplace Exposure
1.58 Labor Senators support mechanisms to eliminate unnecessary and harmful
workplace exposure to PFAS. A precautionary approach must be taken in
relation to PFAS in all workplaces and high-risk sectors should have in place
comprehensive procedures to avoid exposure and support employees. In
34 enHealth, Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS) enHealth Guidance Statement, updated February
2024, pp. 5–6 (accessed 18 November 2025).
327
principle, Labor Senators support measures which aim to ensure consistent
hazard reduction policies across all jurisdictions and support employers and
employees.
1.59 Labor Senators recommend:
Recommendation 4
1.60 That the Australian Government:
 works with states and territories to ensure that workers are protected from
unnecessary and harmful exposure to contaminants, such as PFAS, in all
states and territories; and
 reviews relevant Commonwealth work health and safety legislation,
regulations, and official advice with the aim of ensuring a consistent
approach to occupational PFAS exposure.
Environmental impacts
1.61 Chapter 4 of the committee's report considers how PFAS contamination may
affect ecological environments. Labor Senators note evidence from the CSIRO
and others that research on precise impacts is limited, and further work is
required. We also note evidence that suggests PFAS may have negative impacts
on animal life. The government is supporting this work through targeted
research grants and ongoing support of Australia's scientific research
organisations, including the CSIRO.
1.62 Labor Senators recognise that PFAS contamination is also impacting perceptions
of ecosystems and consider that there are potential benefits of clearer public
information and guidance in this area.
1.63 Labor Senators support proposals for a more strategic approach to further
research, monitoring and testing of PFAS in the environment, and note evidence
provided by Australian scientific organisations on the potential benefits of a
national monitoring program.
1.64 These proposals merit consideration by the government, as such, we
recommend:
Recommendation 5
1.65 That the Australian Government considers ways to improve and accelerate
the integration, mapping, and presentation of Australian data about PFAS
(including establishing anthropogenic baseline levels for PFAS in Australia),
to enable improvements in human and environmental risk assessments and
facilitate informed and effective PFAS management decisions at local and
regional levels.
328
Recommendation 6
1.66 In addition to Recommendation 5, the Australian Government should:
 support longitudinal research which examines the effects of PFAS on
aquatic and terrestrial biota across their lifetimes; and
 implement a standardised national framework with consistent data
requirements for PFAS monitoring and measurement.
Engagement with affected communities
1.67 People living in PFAS-affected communities are more likely to experience
psychological distress, due to a range of factors.35 The mental health toll of PFAS
exposure may be very significant for individuals.36 It is vital that information
and assistance that is place-based and culturally appropriate is provided to
communities in a timely manner.37 More generally, transparent, up-to-date and
reliable risk information should be communicated to the Australian public on
PFAS contamination and associated risks.38
1.68 Communities also face higher costs associated with managing PFAS
contaminated water sources, reduction in property and housing values,
reputation harm and income loss (for instance, difficulties in selling or exporting
agricultural products).39
1.69 First Nations communities, including those at Wreck Bay and Katherine, can feel
a disconnection to Country, uncertainty around long-term health outcomes, and
loss of access to culturally significant sites, waters, and foods.40
1.70 Chapter 5 of the committee's report presents valuable evidence on the
disproportionate impact that PFAS contamination and associated restrictions
are having on First Nations communities.
1.71 Labor Senators acknowledge the devastating impacts described by First Nations
communities in Wreck Bay and Katherine and sincerely thank the individuals
and community organisations who gave evidence—your testimony helped the
committee to better understand these impacts.
1.72 We were concerned to hear that precautionary advice for communities to limit
their dietary intake of seafood and bushfoods is having unintended

consequences for some communities, where it is ultimately leading to unhealthy
changes to people's diets. These impacts warrant further investigation and
illustrate the need to take a responsible, evidence-based approach to managing
PFAS contamination, based on a holistic view of environmental and human
health.
1.73 Evidence in Chapter 5 of the committee's report demonstrates the need for better
engagement and consultation with all communities impacted by PFAS
contamination, including First Nations communities.
1.74 Australian government agencies, including the Department of Defence, have
been working to improve consultation with affected communities for some time
now. For instance, we note evidence that the Department of Defence has
increased its engagement with First Nations communities and is conducting
surveys to increase its understanding of contamination in local soil, surface
water, groundwater, and biota, and is working to improve the availability of
culturally appropriate information materials.
1.75 In relation to Wreck Bay, we note evidence from the Department of Defence that
it has established a partnership with the community to enable community
members to conduct PFAS monitoring and is working with the community on
new, permanent precautionary signage.
1.76 Noting this evidence, Labor Senators agree that PFAS management should
incorporate actions to safeguard the cultural, social, nutritional, and economic
well-being of First Nations people and communities.
1.77 We agree that the Australian government should consider options to improve
and maintain ongoing access to healthy and traditional food sources for
Aboriginal communities around Katherine (Recommendation 21). We also
agree that the Department of Defence should establish partnerships with local
health services, and other Aboriginal organisations, to codesign culturally
appropriate signage and educational material for Aboriginal communities
impacted by PFAS contamination (Recommendation 23).
1.78 In addition, Labor Senators note actions arising from the recent PFAS
Independent review.
1.79 The Independent Review of land uses around key Defence bases impacted by PFAS
contamination (PFAS Independent review) highlighted a need for better
coordination across the Commonwealth, states and territories, and local
governments, in managing PFAS contamination. In addition, it recommended
mechanisms be developed to improve engagement and collaboration with
affected communities, including First Nations communities.41 The Government

is acting in this area, having set up the National Coordinating Body and
Williamtown working group over the last 12 months.
Remediation technologies
1.80 In relation to remediation, discussed in Chapter 6 of the committee's report,
Labor Senators acknowledge evidence presented on the importance of further
developing remediation technologies—including emerging technologies.
1.81 Evidence received throughout the course of the inquiry suggested emerging
technologies show promise, however, it is critical that remediation techniques
are rigorously evaluated.
1.82 Labor Senators agree there is a role for the Commonwealth, as a major PFAS
remediator in Australia, to help facilitate testing and evaluation of new and
emerging technologies. Labor Senators support the intent of Recommendations
29 and 30, but do not want to be prescriptive about the best mechanism to
support remediation technology development.
PFAS in water supplies
1.83 Evidence indicates the new PFAS guideline values established in the revised
Australian Drinking Water Guidelines are responsible and proportionate. In
addition, most of Australia's water supplies already meet the revised
guidelines.42
1.84 Labor Senators note that the supply of drinking water is a state and territory
responsibility, and that most water service providers are owned by state and
territory governments. The Australian Government is actively encouraging
national implementation of the revised guidelines.
1.85 Labor Senators note that construction is underway on a major water
infrastructure project to deliver a new water source to the Jervis Bay Territory.
The project will deliver a water pipeline connecting the Shoalhaven City
Council-owned Vincentia Reservoir to the Stoney Creak Reservoir in the Jervis
Bay Territory, ensuring the community has access to high-quality drinking
water and modern infrastructure that meets current and future demand.
Construction commenced on the New South Wales side of the border at the end
of August and is now under way with the Jervis Bay Territory, with expected
completion early in early 2026.
1.86 Labor Senators also note that the Australian Government has consulted with the
community and local stakeholders throughout the pipeline project, including
the Wreck Bay Aboriginal Community Council and the Booderee National Park
Board of Management to ensure it aligns with community needs and
environmental standards.
42 See also: Committee Hansard, 4 June 2025, p 38.
331
Agricultural impacts
1.87 Chapter 9 of the committee's report considers PFAS in waste streams and
agricultural impacts, including PFAS in pesticides.
1.88 Labor Senators acknowledge concerns raised by some inquiry participants
about potential impacts of PFAS on agriculture and the need for clear and
consistent messaging. However, PFAS contamination in Australia's food supply
is very low.43
1.89 It is important that regulators take a measured and appropriate response to any
risks associated with PFAS contamination of agricultural land—for instance,
where biosolids or pesticides applied to land may contain traces of PFAS.
1.90 The PFAS Independent review recommended several measures designed to
reduce the risks associated with contamination on land used for food
production. The government agreed, or agreed in principle, with these
recommendations and stated that it will work with states and territories—
through the National Coordinating Body—to address the need for improved
policy guidance on PFAS and food production.44
1.91 Labor Senators note concerns from some inquiry participants about PFAS in
pesticides. However, evidence from the Australian Pesticides and Veterinary
Medicines Authority revealed that, out of around 9700 agricultural chemical
products in Australia, fewer than ten contain ingredients that meet the United
States Environmental Protection Agency (US EPA) PFAS definition.45
Chemical regulations
1.92 Labor Senators note evidence on PFAS in consumer goods, presented in Chapter
8 of the committee's report and evidence on chemical regulation, presented in
Chapter 10.
1.93 PFAS are not manufactured in Australia, and it is now prohibited for Australian
manufacturers and retailers to import, export, manufacture, or use certain PFAS
chemicals (PFOA, perfluorooctane sulfonate (PFOS), and perfluorohexane
sulfonate (PFHxS), and related substances).
1.94 The new standards for the three groups of PFAS listed on the Stockholm
Convention (PFOS, PFOA and PFHxS) were created in December 2023,
43 Food Standards Australia New Zealand, Submission 70 (47th Parliament), p. 3. See also: Department
of Health and Aged Care interim Australian Centre for Disease Control, Submission 100 (47th

including bans on their import, manufacture and use that came into effect on
1 July 2025 and now apply to around 500 PFAS.46
1.95 In addition, higher risk new PFAS chemicals not listed on the Australian
Inventory of Industrial Chemicals require AICIS assessment and authorisation
prior to introduction into Australia.47
1.96 Australia's regulatory approach—contained in the Industrial Chemicals
Environmental Management Standard (IChEMS) Minimum Standards—
establishes baseline requirements for the environmental management of
industrial chemicals. The Minimum Standards place the responsibility on
businesses to:
(a) obtain, share, and use information on the environmental risks of PFAS and
other Schedule 7 listed chemicals;
(b) identify potential risks and apply practicable control measures;
(c) store and contain these chemicals in an environmentally safe manner;
(d) plan for and respond effectively and promptly to industrial chemical
incidents; and
(e) implement responsible waste management.48
1.97 Labor Senators understand that these regulations also apply to 'articles',
including consumer products containing these PFAS (with some exceptions,
including articles already in the market).
1.98 Labor Senators note the recent Schedule 7 listing of PFOA, PFOS, and PFHxS,
and evidence that the government is currently developing mechanisms to
enforce the ban.
1.99 At this stage, Labor Senators do not consider that all PFAS should be banned 'as
a class', noting this could lead to unintended consequences. Such a ban may
make essential products unavailable in Australia, may lead to the substitution
of PFAS for other harmful chemicals, or may result in the use of greater volumes
of other, unregulated chemicals to achieve the same effect.
1.100 However, Labor Senators note that several jurisdictions around the world have
begun to implement restrictions, bans, or phase outs of the use of types of
PFAS.49 It is important that Australia keeps pace with the evolving regulatory
46 CSIRO, Submission 22, p. 7.
47 Australian Industrial Chemicals Introduction Scheme, Submission 65, pp. 4–5.
48 Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water, The IChEMS Minimum

landscape internationally to avoid becoming an export destination for goods
containing high concern PFAS.50
1.101 We also note evidence from CSIRO indicated that there are consumer products
that contain PFAS, but where PFAS may be readily replaced while maintaining
desired properties.51
1.102 As a result, Labor Senators recommend:
Recommendation 7
1.103 That the Australian Government considers restricting or phasing out uses of
PFAS in consumer products where PFAS is not essential to the product and is
readily replaceable. Regulation facilitating these restrictions or phase outs
should also consider and endeavour to mitigate against the risk of
substitutions for other PFAS-like chemicals or potentially hazardous
alternatives.
1.104 Further, Labor Senators note evidence, outlined in Chapter 10, that the
government is working to develop further mechanisms to assist industry to
identify and avoid importing consumer products containing PFAS.
Funding remediation
1.105 Persistent organic pollutants, including PFAS, can negatively impact the
Australian environment and pose a potential risk to human health.
1.106 In line with the precautionary approach, Australian governments have invested
hundreds of millions of dollars testing and remediating environments that were
contaminated through the historic use, by Australian government entities, of
PFAS-containing firefighting foams.
1.107 The cost of remediation should be sought to be recovered in accordance with
the principles set out in the PFAS NEMP, including that:
(a) polluter pays; that is, those who generate pollution and waste should bear
the cost of containment, avoidance or abatement; and
(b) the users of goods and services should pay prices based on the full life
cycle costs of providing goods and services, including the use of natural
resources and assets and the ultimate disposal of any wastes.
1.108 Labor Senators note that governments in the United States and other
jurisdictions have used legal action to recover some of these costs from
manufacturers of PFAS.

51 CSIRO, Submission 22, p. 4.

Launceston Airport site inspection
1.1 On Tuesday 12 March 2025, a sub-committee comprising PFAS Committee
Chair, Senator Lidia Thorpe, and Deputy Chair, Senator Varun Ghosh,
conducted an inspection of the PFAS remediation site at Launceston Airport in
Tasmania.
1.2 Officials from both Australia Pacific Airports (Launceston) (APAL), a subsidiary
of Australia Pacific Airports Corporation, and Airservices Australia
(Airservices), welcomed the Chair and Deputy Chair and gave a short
presentation on the history of Launceston Airport and the contaminated site.
1.3 Officials explained that the per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS)
contamination at Launceston airport was caused by firefighting foam containing
PFAS that had been used for firefighting and fire training exercises prior to 2010.
1.4 There was discussion of a map of Launceston airport and the key areas of PFAS
contamination, namely:
 The Aviation Rescue Fire Fighting Fire Station (built in the 1960s).
 The Aviation Rescue Fire Fighting area.
 The former Fire Training Ground.

Auger Locations 2019
Source: Airservices Australia (prepared by AECOM Australia), Preliminary Site Investigation and Limited
Sampling, Launceston Airport, 2019, [p. 65] (accessed 10 November 2025).
1.5 While environments such as the former Fire Training Ground and the Fire
Station were directly impacted by PFAS contamination, the committee was told
that lower levels of PFAS have been detected across the wider airport estate and
also in drainage lines and creeks that subsequently cross agricultural properties.
1.6 APAL and Airservices explained to the committee that they have been working
to remediate the former Fire Training Ground at the northern end of the airport's
premises.
1.7 Airservices is required to undertake this remediation work in order to meet
requirements under the Airports (Environment Protection) Regulations 1997,
administered by the Department of Infrastructure, Transport, Regional
Development, Communications, Sport and the Arts (the department). After
investigations spanning several years, the department issued Airservices with
an Environmental Remediation Order in 2023, as Airservices were found to have
caused PFAS pollution of soil and groundwater at the former Fire Training
Ground and surrounding area.
1.8 Committee members had an opportunity to ask questions and were given a
safety briefing prior to conducting the site inspection. Committee members and
337
officials then took a bus to the former Fire Training Ground remediation site via
Kelly’s Creek.
1.9 Officials from Airservices explained that the PFAS remediation works at the
former Fire Training Ground began in January 2025 and are scheduled to finish
in June 2025. The remediation work is priced at $20 million, with another $5
million for the pre-works.
Figure 1.2 Committee site inspection at Launceston Airport
Source: Secretariat photograph.
Senator Lidia Thorpe, Senator Varun Ghosh, and staff from Airservices Australia, APAC and the department.
1.10 Officials from Airservices explained the on-site remediation process. It is
estimated the entire works will involve removing 19 000 cubic meters of
contaminated soil and rocks (approximately 18 to 20 Olympic swimming pools).
1.11 However, before any digging could begin, the entire surface area was divided
into a grid pattern with samples taken from each grid to test for PFAS
concentrations (if the PFAS concentrations in samples from certain grids are too
high, remediation of soil from those squares cannot occur onsite).
1.12 Officials emphasised that the machinery used for excavation is relatively small
because precision and appropriate care is more important than speed.
Importantly, all machinery is fully sealed, and full-face breathing and dust
masks are provided.
1.13 Approximately 500 cubic metres of soil and rock is excavated each day to a
depth of 1.6m. While base rock has been hit in places before a depth of 1.6 metres
has been reached, testing has revealed that the base rock did not contain
338
detectable levels of PFAS. In other words, the base rock acted as a low
permeability barrier.
1.14 Once the soil has been dug up, it is placed into 100 cubic metre stockpiles on a
treatment pad. Then, 2 per cent powdered activated carbon (a binding agent) is
added to each 100 cubic metres of soil to ensure a consistent mix and good
results. The carbon powder is stored under cover in a large onsite shed.
Figure 1.3 Exposed bedrock after contaminated soil was removed from part
of the site
Source: Secretariat photograph.
1.15 Next, the remediated soil is put back in the hole on a layer of clean fill. Only one
layer is put back at a time and each layer is compacted. Officials advised that no
membrane is needed because the carbon binding treatment means that the PFAS
in the soil is no longer as mobile and any leaching is greatly reduced.
1.16 The treated soil is then topped with clean fill and compacted to render it almost
impervious to rain as a further safety measure. At the time of the committee’s
visit in early March, officials explained that there had been very little rain at the
remediation site in 2025 which made the work easier. However, in the event of
a big rain, the hole would need pumping out.
1.17 New access roads, a large storage shed, and the reorganisation of security were
all required to facilitate the remediation works. Airservices employed AECOM
as the main contractor with minor works being completed by Ternary Group,
339
an Indigenous, family-owned company that has experience with civil works and
environmental remediation. At the time of the visit, Ternary Group had four
trainees from the Torres Strait Islands.
Figure 1.4 Extent of PFAS removal works
Source: Secretariat photograph.
1.18 Finally, officials noted that some highly PFAS contaminated soil is being sent to
the facility at Altona in Victoria where it undergoes thermal destruction (see
Appendix 2, SOLVE Altona site inspection).
1.19 Airservices told the committee that it is using the learnings from the Launceston
Airport project to guide planning for remediation works at Canberra and
Rockhampton airports.
1.20 On behalf of the committee, Senators Thorpe and Ghosh thank officials from
APAL and Airservices for hosting the site inspection.

Appendix 2
SOLVE Altona site inspection
1.1 On Tuesday 3 June 2025, a sub-committee comprising PFAS Committee Chair,
Senator Lidia Thorpe, and committee member, Senator Perin Davey conducted
a site inspection at SOLVE Altona—one of Australia's largest hazardous waste
treatment facilities. Committee members heard that SOLVE is one of only three
sites in Australia where PFAS-contaminated soil can be remediated on an
industrial scale, using thermal treatment technologies.
1.2 The SOLVE thermal desorption plant at Altona, Victoria, is run by Australian
environmental remediation and hazardous waste management company,
Enviropacific. Committee members met at the site and were welcomed by CEO
and Managing Director of Enviropacific, Mr David Tucker; Manager of SOLVE,
Mr Dino Adikaram; and General Manager—Resource Recovery, Mr Brayden
Rees.
1.3 Committee members received a presentation on SOLVE's operations, had an
opportunity to ask questions, and were given a safety briefing and equipment
prior to conducting a site inspection.
Figure 2.1 Committee members conducting an inspection of the SOLVE
thermal desorption plant
Source: Chair's photograph, 3 June 2025. Left to right: Dino Adikaram, David Tucker, Senator Lidia Thorpe,
Senator Perin Davey, Brayden Rees.
341
342
Background—Enviropacific
1.4 Mr Tucker introduced Enviropacific, an environmental remediation company
which was established in Sydney's inner-west in 2001. With over 360 employees,
Enviropacific now operates in all parts of Australia, with offices in Sydney,
Brisbane, Melbourne, Adelaide, Perth, Darwin, Hobart and Newcastle.1
1.5 Mr Tucker explained that Enviropacific has three divisions and around 250
projects on the go at any one time. According to Mr Tucker, government
contracts represent around 60 per cent of Enviropacific's work. Enviropacific is
involved in remediation projects at many PFAS-contaminated Defence sites,
including RAAF Base Tindal, RAAF Base Darwin, and Defence sites in the
Shoalhaven. Enviropacific is also providing remediation services to airport
operators, such as Australia Pacific Airports (Melbourne), for civil rehabilitation
works at the Arundel Creek dams, among other projects.2
1.6 Enviropacific also handles remediation work for water service utilities, local
councils, state government entities, etc., with about one third of its work
allocated to PFAS remediation, and the other two thirds focused on removing
asbestos, hydrocarbons, heavy metals and other environmental contaminants.
1.7 Enviropacific is contracted to manage, stabilise, treat, and/or dispose of PFAS,
and other hazardous substances. Around three quarters of Enviropacific's work
relates to on-site remediation around Australia with destruction of PFAS at
SOLVE only representing around 10 per cent of Enviropacific's work.
1.8 Enviropacific has been successfully treating PFAS-contaminated waste for more
than 10 years, including operating Australia's first plant destroying PFAS
molecules in soil utilising thermal desorption. Treatment technologies for solid
PFAS waste include:
 Chemical Immobilisation
 Thermal Desorption
 Soil Washing
 Sludge Dewatering
 Design and construct of containment cells and repositories.3
Thermal desorption of PFAS at SOLVE
1.9 SOLVE was established in 2018 and is segregated into two distinct processing
streams—thermal desorption, and non-destructive digging (NDD). SOLVE has
received material from across Australia, with over 370 000 tonnes thermally
1 Enviropacific, 'Our story', Company website (accessed 10 November 2025).
2 Enviropacific, 'Dam stabilisation and remediation—APAM', Company website (accessed 10
November 2025).
3 Enviropacific, 'PFAS treatment', Company website (accessed 10 November 2025).
343
destroyed and 100 000 tonnes of NDD spoil remediated via segregation and
chemical treatment.
Figure 2.2 SOLVE thermal desorption plant, Altona, VIC
Source: SOLVE presentation, June 2025.
1.10 Thermal desorption is a destructive PFAS remediation technique that is only
employed for the most heavily contaminated waste. It is effective in completely
removing and destroying PFAS, leaving a soil-like product which can be safely
used in a number of applications. The process utilises controlled high-
temperature treatment to ensure complete PFAS destruction, using natural gas
for permanent contamination removal. Material that is treated at SOLVE is
transported from remediation sites across Australia, where local destruction is
unavailable.
1.11 The committee noted that, while effective, the process is energy intensive,
requiring large amounts of gas to be burned to produce extremely high
temperatures and some material to be transported thousands of kilometres to
be remediated.
1.12 Transporting PFAS-contaminated waste is potentially hazardous and follows
established logistics protocols using standard truck and rail transport methods.
The transport process allows contaminated material to be moved to SOLVE's
facility for permanent destruction, removing the PFAS problem from source
locations. SOLVE operates with transporters licensed by state and territory
environmental protection authorities, and with Commonwealth and state
approvals, maintaining strict chain of custody throughout. It is ultimately able
to issue a Certificate of Destruction, helping remediators to meet their
responsibilities in relation to PFAS contamination and remediation.
344
Thermal treatment of soil
1.13 Heavily PFAS-contaminated soil is put through a Rotary Dryer, at a rate of up
to 35 tonnes per hour. The temperature inside the Rotary Dryer heats soil up to
550°C for approximately 15 minutes, which volatilises PFAS, and any other
organic compounds, in the soil. Volatilise means to turn substances into a
gaseous form. The solids are then cooled with recycled water to a temperature
around 80°C, while gases released during the volatilisation process are treated
in the thermal oxidiser, which reaches temperatures between 950°C and 1100°C,
breaking down harmful gases into their harmless base elements.
Figure 2.3 Thermal treatment of soil
Source: SOLVE presentation, June 2025.
1.14 Treated soil can be used for landfill rehabilitation and capping, structural fill for
VicRoads and industrial developments, and bulking material for crushed
products. Enviropacific is working with regulators to try to identify more uses
for this material and has, for instance, successfully grown native plants in a mix
of this material with added compost.
345
Figure 2.4 Remediated product
Source: Chair's photograph, 3 June 2025.
NDD process
1.15 The NDD plant treats PFAS-contaminated sludge and other materials using a
two-part treatment system, consisting of the following:
 A pre-treatment system first separates the liquid from the NDD solids
volume from the incoming loads via CDE’s plant; segregation of NDD
material into the following components aggregates (>5mm), sand (5mm–
65μm) and clay (<65μm).
 A second water treatment system then treats the separated liquids via
Enviropacific's designed Water Treatment Plant.
1.16 Enviropacific's processes are highly efficient—for instance, making maximum
use of reclaimed water—and environmentally responsible, with continuous
emissions monitoring, batch testing, and strict safety protocols.

other staff members at SOLVE for supporting the committee's visit and
providing an informative and interesting tour.
